11The one-loop coefficient and stationary paths

11.1A uniform analytic bound for the bare plaquette energy

The path construction uses uniform analytic bounds for the unperturbed Wilson energy, before it compares that energy with a general path. This section records the finite-volume estimate and its proof. All constants are independent of the torus side.

Write \(\mathcal{A}\) for the finite set of positive oriented plaquette types, \(N_{\mathcal{A}} = |\mathcal{A}|\), and let \(d_G\) be the dimension of the fixed matrix representation. For a link field \(U\) on a torus of side \(m\), put

\(\displaystyle W_m (U) = \sum_{x \in (\mathbb{Z}/ m\mathbb{Z})^4} \sum_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left( 1 - \frac{\text{Retr} \hspace{0.17em} U (\partial (x, a))}{d_G} \right) .\)

Here \(U (\partial (x, a))\) is the ordered four-edge holonomy. The estimate below is a coefficientwise bound uniform in \(m\); the action itself is extensive. We use Lie-algebra coordinates \(z\) at the identity and write \(\tilde{W}_m (z) = W_m (\exp z)\); every derivative below means the derivative of this coordinate function at \(z = 0\). The norm on link-coordinate arrays and on their local Fourier-mode restrictions is the supremum norm.

For \(t \in \mathbb{N}\), set \(n = t + 1\). A coefficient picture of order \(t\) assigns a function of \(t\) independent complex momenta \(q_1, \ldots, q_t\) to every list of \(n\) colors and oriented-link directions; the zeroth momentum is fixed by \(q_0 = - \sum_{j = 1}^t q_j\). The width-one strip is the set where every coordinate of each independent momentum has imaginary part of absolute value at most one. Let \(\text{Cauchy} (R, 0) = 1\) and \(\text{Cauchy} (R, k + 1) = \text{Cauchy} (R, k) / (R / 2^{k + 1})\).

Proposition 11.1. (Uniform bare-energy coefficient bound) There are constants \(R_{\ast} > 0\) and \(A_{\ast} < \infty\), depending only on the compact gauge representation and plaquette convention, and for each \(t\) there is a single picture \(\mathcal{W}_t\) that works for every torus side \(m\). Its components are holomorphic on the width-one momentum strip and satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sup_{q \text{ in the strip}} |\mathcal{W}_t (q) | \leqslant A_t := N_{\mathcal{A}} A_{\ast} \exp (16 (t + 1)^2) \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R_{\ast}, t + 1)}{(t + 1) !} .\)

Moreover, let \(p_0, \ldots, p_t\) be dual-torus momenta, \(c_0, \ldots, c_t\) colors, and \(b_0, \ldots, b_t\) oriented-link directions. Let \(e_{p, b, c}\) denote the corresponding Fourier-mode variation of the link field, and put \(q = (p_1, \ldots, p_t)\). Then

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{n!} D^n \tilde{W}_m (0) [e_{p_0, b_0, c_0}, \ldots, e_{p_t, b_t, c_t}] = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} | (\mathbb{Z}/ m\mathbb{Z})^4 | \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{W}_t (\mathbf{c}, \mathbf{b}; q), & \sum_{j = 0}^t p_j = 0,\\ 0, & \sum_{j = 0}^t p_j \ne 0. \end{array} \right.\)

Thus the volume factor in the zero-total-momentum coefficient is exactly the torus cardinality, and every nonzero-total-momentum coefficient vanishes.

Proof. First construct a single plaquette extension. Let \(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}\) be the complexification of the represented Lie algebra and let \(\mathcal{E}: \mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}} \to \text{Mat}_{d_G} (\mathbb{C})\) be the linear representation map. For a complex link-coordinate array \(z\), the two oriented link matrices are \(\exp (\mathcal{E}z_e)\) and \(\exp (-\mathcal{E}z_e)\). These depend holomorphically on \(z\), since the matrix exponential is entire. Multiplication along the four edges, trace, and the average of the forward and reverse loop traces are holomorphic too. On real coordinates that average is the real trace of the holonomy, because for the unitary holonomy \(H\) one has \(\text{tr} (H^{- 1}) = \overline{\text{tr} (H)}\). Thus this extension restricts to the real plaquette energy summand.

Continuity of the exponential at zero gives \(R_{\ast} > 0\) such that complex link coordinates in the radius-\(R_{\ast}\) ball give oriented matrices within \(\text{rad} (1)\) of their physical unitary matrices, distance being measured in the Frobenius norm \(\| \cdot \|_F\). This implies the product estimate as follows. If \(M\) is within \(r = \text{rad} (1)\) of a unitary \(V\), then for every matrix \(X\),

\(\displaystyle \|MX\|_F \leqslant \|VX\|_F + \|(M - V) X\|_F \leqslant (1 + r) \|X\|_F,\)

since left multiplication by \(V\) is an isometry and the operator norm of \(M - V\) is at most its Frobenius norm. Iterating this inequality through four factors, starting with \(X = I\), gives \(\|M_1 M_2 M_3 M_4 \|_F \leqslant (1 + r)^4 \|I\|_F\). The reverse loop has the same bound. The continuous pulse

\(\displaystyle (M, N) \longmapsto 1 - \frac{\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M + \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} N}{2 d_G}\)

is bounded on the product of the two closed Frobenius balls of radius \(T = (1 + r)^4 \|I\|_F\) about zero, since both four-edge products lie there. Compactness in finite dimension gives a common bound \(A_{\ast}\). Together these facts give a uniform holomorphic extension bounded by \(A_{\ast}\) on a fixed ball, at every plaquette anchor and every torus size.

Each plaquette is supported in the radius-four coordinate box about its anchor: every link in its four-edge boundary has an anchor at max-distance at most four. Hence its real local action is unchanged when the cover configuration is changed outside that box.

To define the common local symbol, place each radius-four box in the auxiliary torus \(\Lambda_9 = (\mathbb{Z}/ 9\mathbb{Z})^4\), with \(9 = 2 d + 1\) and \(d = 4\). The box embeds in this torus without identifications. For each plaquette type, evaluate its local Taylor form there on the restricted Fourier modes \(y \mapsto \boldsymbol{1}_{(b, c)} \exp (i \langle p, \text{lift} \hspace{0.17em} y \rangle)\), and sum these local symbols over the plaquette types; this defines \(\mathcal{W}_t\) at the corresponding independent momenta. For a period of any side \(m\), pull a period-\(m\) field \(z\) to the auxiliary torus by

\(\displaystyle (P_m z) (y, b, c) = z (\text{wrap}_m (\text{lift} \hspace{0.17em} y), b, c) .\)

Then the local plaquette function satisfies \(\text{local}_m (z) = \text{local}_9 (P_m z)\). The pullback of a period-\(m\) Fourier mode is exactly the restricted Fourier mode used above. This identity holds even when \(m < 9\) and distinct auxiliary sites wrap to the same period-\(m\) link: evaluating \(\text{local}_9\) on \(P_m z\) retains all repeated-link contributions. Thus the local symbol is the same for every period, including the small periods. The picture variables are momenta, rather than link coordinates. To obtain the strip bound, evaluate the order-\(n\) Taylor multilinear form of the local plaquette extension on the Fourier-mode vectors \(e_{p_j, b_j, c_j}\). The Cauchy derivative estimate on successively shrinking balls of radii \(R_{\ast} / 2, R_{\ast} / 2^2, \ldots, R_{\ast} / 2^n\) bounds its order-\(n\) derivative by \(A_{\ast} \text{Cauchy} (R_{\ast}, n)\). The Taylor coefficient is normalized by \(1 / n\)!. For a momentum in the width-one strip, a Fourier mode restricted to the radius-four stencil has supremum norm at most \(\exp (16 n)\): the dependent momentum has imaginary coordinates bounded by \(t\), and a stencil displacement has four coordinates, each of absolute value at most four. There are \(n\) input modes in the multilinear form, so their product costs at most \(\exp (16 n^2)\). This proves the claimed bound for each plaquette type. Summing over the finite set \(\mathcal{A}\) multiplies it by \(N_{\mathcal{A}}\) and yields a single picture \(\mathcal{W}_t\), independent of the torus side.

It remains to check the finite-volume coefficient identity. Expand the translated plaquette sum and apply the multilinear coefficient to an ordered momentum/color/direction tuple. Translating a local plaquette coefficient by an anchor multiplies its Fourier mode by the character at total momentum \(\sum_{j = 0}^t p_j\). The sum over all anchors is zero unless that total momentum is zero; in that case each anchor contributes the same local coefficient, so the sum is exactly \(| (\mathbb{Z}/ m\mathbb{Z})^4 |\) times the local symbol \(\mathcal{W}_t\) evaluated at the output phase. This proves the displayed identity, including its volume normalization.\(\Box\)

Remark 11.2. (Use in the selected path estimate) The one-step path estimate uses the pictures \(\mathcal{W}_2\) and \(\mathcal{W}_3\) of Proposition 11.1, that is, the Taylor coefficients of degrees three and four. Only the finitely many plaquette types enter, each with support in a box of radius four, so the finite sum over \(\mathcal{A}\) in the proof is all that is needed; no summation over infinitely many interaction terms occurs. The two pictures are uniform over torus sides; no limit in the volume is used here.

11.2The weighted quadratic row estimate

The part of the one-step action quadratic in the row field has a uniform coefficient bound. Its cost is the square of the exponentially weighted row budget. The proof is an exact two-index expansion followed by a summable Cauchy estimate; no finite-volume truncation of the row family is needed.

Let \(\text{Cauchy}\) be the iterated Cauchy derivative factor defined in Subsection 11.1. Let \(\mathcal{A}\) be the finite set of plaquette types. Write \(d_{\text{col}}\) for the number of color coordinates and \(d_{\text{mat}}\) for the dimension of the matrix representation; these constants have different roles below. For each \(a \in \mathcal{A}\) and \(i \in \mathbb{N}\), let \(s_{a, i} (U ; x)\) be a color vector obtained by evaluating one path slot at the translated anchor \(x\), and let \(\ell_{a, i}\) be that slot's length. Let \(c_{a, i} \in \mathbb{R}\) be its row coefficient, and assume \(\ell_{a, i} \in \mathbb{N}\). The color vector takes values in the finite-dimensional real Euclidean color space on physical fields; its complex extension applies the complex-linear extension of the real color projection to half the difference between the forward slot word and its reversed word. On real fields this is the original skew-color slot. The quadratic extension is bilinear, \(\sum_c s_c s_c\), not a Hermitian norm. Assume the row budget

\(\displaystyle \sum_i |c_{a, i} | e^{\sigma \ell_{a, i}} \leqslant C \quad (a \in \mathcal{A}), \qquad \sigma > 0.\) (11.1)

We use the following elementary path bound. For each \(\sigma > 0\) there are a common complex coordinate radius \(R_{\sigma / 2} > 0\) and a constant \(S_G\) such that, on the radius-\(R_{\sigma / 2}\) ball about the identity, every color component of a slot of length \(\ell\) has a holomorphic extension bounded by \(S_G e^{(\sigma / 2) \ell}\). More explicitly, if \(H_a (z)\) is the matrix product along the slot walk and \(H_a^{\text{rev}} (z)\) the product along the reversed slot walk, the extension is

\(\displaystyle s_a^{\mathbb{C}} (z) = \Pi_{G, \mathbb{C}} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \frac{H_a (z) - H_a^{\text{rev}} (z)}{2} \right),\)

where \(\Pi_{G, \mathbb{C}}\) is the complex-linear extension of the real skew-color projection. On real links, the reversed full slot walk is the inverse walk; its string matrix is the adjoint of the forward string matrix. Their half-difference is skew-adjoint, and the physical color projection of this skew matrix is exactly the transported-curvature slot. Thus the complex extension agrees with the original real slot on physical fields. The two words each contain at most \(2 \ell\) edge factors. Choose the radius so that each complexified edge matrix and its inverse has operator norm at most \(e^{\sigma / 4}\); this is possible by continuity near the compact unitary group. Each word then has norm at most \(e^{(\sigma / 2) \ell}\). Apply the bounded complex-linear projection; its norm and the fixed factor from the difference are absorbed in \(S_G\). This proves the bound uniformly in location and length.

Define the quadratic row action on a torus of side \(m\) by

\(\displaystyle Q^{(2)}_m (U) = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{x \in (\mathbb{Z}/ m\mathbb{Z})^4} \sum_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \left| \sum_i c_{a, i} s_{a, i} (U ; x) \right|^2 .\)

The sum over \(i\) converges absolutely on the real compact configuration space by (11.1) and the path bound. On the common complex coordinate ball, the two-index expansion below also converges uniformly: its absolute majorant is the product of the two row sums with exponent \(\sigma / 2\), which is bounded by the assumed row budget. Thus the complexified action is holomorphic there before any derivatives or momentum coefficients are taken.

Proposition 11.3. (Uniform bound of the quadratic row action) For each \(t \in \mathbb{N}\), put \(n = t + 1\) and

\(\displaystyle \eta_t = \min \hspace{-0.17em} \left( 1, \frac{\sigma}{8 n^2} \right) .\)

There is one coefficient picture \(\mathcal{Q}_t\) valid at every torus side, holomorphic for \(t\) independent complex momenta of width \(\eta_t\), whose components are bounded by

\(\displaystyle \frac{|\mathcal{A}| d_{\text{col}}}{2} C^2 S_G^2 \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R_{\sigma / 2}, n)}{n!} .\)

For each ordered list of \(n\) Fourier-mode variations, its coefficient is zero unless their total momentum vanishes; in the zero case it is the torus cardinality times the value of \(\mathcal{Q}_t\) at the independent momenta.

Proof. For each \(a, i, j\), expand the squared norm into the two-slot term

\(\displaystyle \Gamma_{a, i, j} (U ; x) = \frac{1}{2} c_{a, i} c_{a, j} \sum_{c = 1}^{d_{\text{col}}} s_{a, i, c} (U ; x) s_{a, j, c} (U ; x) .\)

It has support length at most \(\ell_{a, i} + \ell_{a, j}\) and total absolute coefficient \(d_{\text{col}} |c_{a, i} c_{a, j} | / 2\). Therefore its total weighted activity satisfies, by Tonelli and (11.1),

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \sum_{a, i, j} \frac{d_{\text{col}}}{2} |c_{a, i} c_{a, j} | e^{\sigma (\ell_{a, i} + \ell_{a, j})} & = \frac{d_{\text{col}}}{2} \sum_a \left( \sum_i |c_{a, i} |e^{\sigma \ell_{a, i}} \right)^2\\ & \leqslant \frac{|\mathcal{A}| d_{\text{col}}}{2} C^2 . \end{aligned}\)

We now estimate the coefficient of each two-slot term. On the common radius-\(R_{\sigma / 2}\) ball, the product of its two holomorphic slot extensions is bounded by \(S_G^2 e^{(\sigma / 2) (\ell_{a, i} + \ell_{a, j})}\). The Cauchy derivative estimate on the nested radii \(R_{\sigma / 2} / 2, \ldots, R_{\sigma / 2} / 2^n\) contributes \(\text{Cauchy} (R_{\sigma / 2}, n) / n\)! to the normalized order-\(n\) coefficient. Restricting a width-\(\eta_t\) Fourier mode to a support of length \(\ell\) costs at most \(e^{4 n \eta_t \ell}\); for \(n\) inputs the total additional factor is at most \(e^{4 n^2 \eta_t \ell}\). Since \(4 n^2 \eta_t \leqslant \sigma / 2\), this factor is at most \(e^{(\sigma / 2) \ell}\). Consequently the slot-extension and Fourier-mode factors together are bounded by \(S_G^2 e^{\sigma (\ell_{a, i} + \ell_{a, j})}\).

The weighted activity sum just computed is thus a summable majorant for the series of coefficient pictures, uniformly on the smaller momentum strip. The Weierstrass \(M\)-test gives uniform convergence on compact subsets; its sum is holomorphic, and Cauchy's integral formula permits termwise extraction of each Taylor coefficient. On the real field, absolute convergence also permits expanding the row square and interchanging the sums, so this summed picture represents exactly \(Q^{(2)}_m\).

Finally, translating each local two-slot term over all anchors multiplies its Fourier coefficient by the character of the total momentum. The character sum on the finite torus is zero at nonzero total momentum and equals the torus cardinality at zero total momentum. For each pair atom, its finite support lies in a max-distance box of radius \(d = \ell_{a, i} + \ell_{a, j}\). Compute its local symbol on the auxiliary torus \(\Lambda_{2 d + 1}\); this box has no internal identifications there. Pull any period-\(m\) field back to this auxiliary torus by \(z (y) = z_m (\text{wrap}_m (\text{lift} \hspace{0.17em} y))\). The identity holds even when \(m < 2 d + 1\), so repeated links are retained. Summing the local symbols over atoms gives one picture independent of \(m\), with the asserted volume factor and bound.\(\Box\)

Corollary 11.4. (The averaged path-kernel coefficient bound) Suppose the unaveraged path kernel has the exact decomposition

\(\displaystyle Q_{p, R, m} = \lambda W_m + Q^{(2)}_m, \qquad 0 \leqslant \lambda \leqslant Z,\)

with \(Q^{(2)}_m\) as above. Then the unaveraged order-\(n\) picture has width \(\eta_t\) and bound at most

\(\displaystyle A_{raw} = ZA_t + \frac{|\mathcal{A}| d_{\text{col}}}{2} C^2 S_G^2 \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R_{\sigma / 2}, n)}{n!}, \qquad n = t + 1.\)

Let \(\mathcal{S}\) be the finite signed-coordinate group and define the average \(Q^{\sharp} (U) = |\mathcal{S}|^{- 1} \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} Q_{p, R, m} (\rho_s U)\), where \(\rho_s\) is the corresponding signed coordinate action on link fluctuations. For this averaged kernel, the common picture has width \(\eta_t\) and bound at most

\(\displaystyle A_{avg} =\mathcal{B}_{\text{refl}} (t, \eta_t) A_{raw}, \qquad \mathcal{B}_{\text{refl}} (t, \eta) = \exp ((t + 1)^2 \eta) .\)

Both coefficient identities hold at every torus side, with the exact zero-total-momentum volume factor.

Proof. The unaveraged assertion follows by adding the bare-action picture and the quadratic picture, after restricting the former to width \(\eta_t \leqslant 1\). For completeness, the signed action on a picture entry is

\(\displaystyle (\text{rot}_s \hspace{0.17em} L) (x, (j_r)_{r = 1}^n, p) = \left( \prod_{r = 1}^n \text{reflectFee}_s (\text{plotted} (p, r), k_r) \right) L (x, (k_r)_{r = 1}^n, \text{rotatePhase}_s (p)),\)

Here \(n = t + 1\) and \(j_r \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}\) is the coordinate axis attached to the \(r\)th slot and \(k_r = s_{\text{perm}}^{- 1} (j_r)\) is the corresponding input axis, used both in the fee and in the original picture's axis argument. Thus the original picture is evaluated on the tuple \((k_1, \ldots, k_n)\). Each factor \(\text{reflectFee}_s\) is either \(1\) or minus the Fourier character \(\exp (i \langle p, - e_{s (k_r)} \rangle)\) of one unit direction. On the width-\(\eta_t\) strip, each independent momentum has imaginary coordinates bounded by \(\eta_t\), and the plotted momentum (including the dependent \(p_0 = - \sum_{r = 1}^t p_r\)) has imaginary coordinates bounded by \(n \eta_t\). Thus each such factor has bound at most \(e^{n \eta_t}\). The product of the \(n\) factors therefore costs at most \(e^{n^2 \eta_t}\). The map \(p \mapsto \text{rotatePhase}_s (p)\) applies the signed coordinate action \(s^{- 1}\) independently to each independent momentum; the dependent momentum transforms by the same action because it is minus their sum. This preserves the coordinate strip and incurs no additional exponential bound. This is precisely the picture transformation formula: the character factors account for the translated base point in the signed coordinate action, and the remaining coefficient is the original picture evaluated on the reindexed directions and transformed momenta. Hence each rotated picture has bound at most \(\mathcal{B}_{\text{refl}} (t, \eta_t) A_{raw}\). The normalized finite signed average preserves the same bound by the triangle inequality. Linearity of the coefficient identity shows that the averaged picture represents the averaged kernel and retains the same zero-momentum volume factor. This is the exact orientation-average step needed for the input kernel.\(\Box\)

11.3Soft response of the cubic and quartic curvature terms

This section supplies the order-two and order-three response estimates used by the selected one-loop route. The argument has two parts. First, the absolute path budgets give common analytic pictures for the cubic and quartic terms, uniformly over the volume sequence. Second, a finite gauge-variation identity removes the unwanted low-momentum coefficients. The latter step is needed: analyticity and an absolute activity bound alone do not imply the extra powers of momentum.

11.3.1Summable local atoms and their coefficient pictures

An atom of degree \(r\) consists of \(r\) transported curvature slots and a finite coefficient array \(c_{\alpha}\), where \(\alpha\) records one color coordinate for each slot. Write \(d (a)\) for the sum of the slot lengths and

\(\displaystyle b_{\sigma} (a) = \sum_{\alpha} |c_{\alpha} | e^{\sigma d (a)} .\)

The corresponding local function is the polynomial in those \(r\) slot values. For a sequence \(a_i\), its absolute activity budget is

\(\displaystyle \sum_i b_{\sigma} (a_i) \leqslant A.\)

Gauge invariance means that simultaneous adjoint rotation of all slot arguments leaves each polynomial unchanged. These are precisely the two properties of the cubic and quartic streams used below.

Proposition 11.5. (Uniform coefficient picture for an atom stream) Fix \(r, t \in \mathbb{N}\) and \(\sigma > 0\). There are constants \(\eta_{r, t} > 0\) and \(C_{r, t} < \infty\), depending only on \(G, r, t, \sigma\), such that the following holds. If \(\sum_i b_{\sigma} (a_i) \leqslant A\), then the translation sum of the local atom functions has an exact order-\((t + 1)\) Fourier coefficient picture \(P_{r, t}\). Its components are holomorphic on the independent momentum strip of width \(\eta_{r, t}\), have uniform bound

\(\displaystyle \|P_{r, t} \|_{\eta_{r, t}} \leqslant AC_{r, t},\)

and represent the finite-volume Taylor coefficient on every torus. At nonzero total momentum the coefficient vanishes; at zero total momentum it is the torus cardinality times the picture value. The bound is independent of the torus side and of the number of atoms.

If the atoms are gauge invariant, the same conclusion holds for their orientation-averaged action, with the bound multiplied by

\(\displaystyle \exp ((t + 1)^2 \eta_{r, t}) .\)

Proof. The one-slot estimate in the proof of Proposition 11.3 gives, on one common complex ball about the physical identity, a bound

\(\displaystyle |s_{\nu, \alpha_{\nu}} (z) | \leqslant S_G e^{(\sigma / 2) \ell_{\nu}} .\)

It was obtained from the complexified skew-color projection of the forward slot string minus its reverse; thus it applies to the real color coordinates and their complex-linear extension. Multiplying the \(r\) slot bounds and summing the coefficient magnitudes gives

\(\displaystyle |a_i^{\mathbb{C}} (z) | \leqslant S_G^r b_{\sigma / 2} (a_i) .\)

The complex extension is entire in the finitely many link variables in the atom's stencil. Cauchy's derivative estimate on nested balls of radii \(R, R / 2, \ldots, R / 2^{t + 1}\) bounds the normalized order-\((t + 1)\) derivative by

\(\displaystyle b_{\sigma / 2} (a_i) S_G^r \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R, t + 1)}{(t + 1) !} .\)

Here \(\text{Cauchy} (R, 0) = 1\) and \(\text{Cauchy} (R, k + 1) = \text{Cauchy} (R, k) / (R / 2^{k + 1})\); the factorial is the Taylor coefficient normalization.

The local coefficient picture inserts one Fourier character for each of the \(t + 1\) differentiated slots. A slot support lies in the four-dimensional box of radius at most \(d (a_i)\). On a momentum strip of width \(\eta\), the imaginary part of each plotted momentum is bounded coordinatewise by \((t + 1) \eta\). Hence the product of the \(t + 1\) characters is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \exp (4 (t + 1)^2 \eta d (a_i)) .\)

Choose \(\eta = \sigma / (8 (t + 1)^2)\), so \(4 (t + 1)^2 \eta = \sigma / 2\). The resulting term bound is at most

\(\displaystyle b_{\sigma / 2} (a_i) S_G^r \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R, t + 1)}{(t + 1) !} e^{(\sigma / 2) d (a_i)} = C_{r, t} b_{\sigma} (a_i),\)

where \(C_{r, t} = S_G^r \text{Cauchy} (R, t + 1) / (t + 1)\)!. Thus the coefficient pictures form a normally convergent series on every smaller strip. The same majorant on the common complex link ball permits termwise differentiation of the atom series before extracting any momentum coefficient. This proves analyticity and the claimed uniform bound.

For the finite-volume identity, translate the local atom over all anchors. The sum of its characters is the finite-torus character sum: it is zero unless the total momentum is zero, and equals the number of sites when the total momentum is zero. The local picture is defined on a cover box large enough to contain the stencil, then pulled back by the wrapping map. This keeps the identity exact even on small tori where different cover points wrap to the same link. Absolute summability just proved permits exchanging the atom sum, derivative, and finite anchor sum.

For an orientation \(s\), the picture transforms by multiplying the \(t + 1\) reflected-character fees and evaluating the original picture at the inverse-permuted direction labels and transformed independent momenta. Each fee has strip bound at most \(e^{(t + 1) \eta}\), because its plotted momentum has coordinatewise imaginary parts bounded by \((t + 1) \eta\). The product therefore costs at most \(e^{(t + 1)^2 \eta}\). The normalized finite orientation average preserves this bound by the triangle inequality, and its coefficient identity is the average of the exact finite-volume identities. This proves the final assertion.\(\Box\)

11.3.2The finite gauge-variation identity

A coefficient picture of order \(t\) records the normalized \((t + 1)\)st Taylor coefficient. For an external color word \(v\), color run \(a\), and independent momentum tuple \(p\), its one-leg contraction at leg \(j\) is

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{C}_j P)_{v, a} (p) = \sum_{\mu \in \mathcal{A}} q (p_j, \mu) P_{v, \text{update} (a, j, \mu)} (p), \qquad q (p_j, \mu) = e^{ip_{j, \mu}} - 1,\)

where \(\mathcal{A}\) is the finite set of spatial axes. The color word \(v\) is held fixed; the sum is over the spatial difference axis. Here \(j\) ranges over all \(t + 1\) legs and \(p_0, \ldots, p_t\) denote the external momenta of the tuple: \(p_1, \ldots, p_t\) are the independent momenta and \(p_0 = - \sum_{i = 1}^t p_i\).

A function of the independent momenta has ray order at least \(s\) if, for every real tuple \(p\), its value at \(\lambda p\) is \(O (| \lambda |^s)\) as the real parameter \(\lambda\) tends to zero.

Gauge invariance of an action \(F\) on link fluctuations is used in the following form. For a site field \(\theta\) and a link fluctuation \(u\), both Lie-algebra valued, let \(g (\theta, u)\) be the gauge transform of \(\exp u\) by \(\exp \theta\) in logarithmic link coordinates: on the link from \(x\) in direction \(\mu\),

\(\displaystyle g (\theta, u)_{x, \mu} = \log (\exp (\theta_x) \exp (u_{x, \mu}) \exp (\theta_{x + e_{\mu}})^{- 1}) .\)

Then \(F (g (\theta, u)) = F (u)\) for all \((\theta, u)\) near zero. The map \(g\) is given on every link of every torus by one and the same analytic function \(\omega\) of the three arguments \((\theta_x, u_{x, \mu}, \theta_{x + e_{\mu}})\). Its first derivative at zero is \(D_u g (0, 0) = I\) and \(D_{\theta} g (0, 0) \theta = - d_0 \theta\), where \((d_0 \theta)_{x, \mu} = \theta_{x + e_{\mu}} - \theta_x\).

Lemma 11.6. (Ward partition identity) Let \(m_k \to \infty\), and let \(F_k\) be real analytic, gauge invariant actions with \(DF_k (0) = 0\). Suppose an order-\(t\) picture \(P\) is holomorphic and uniformly bounded on a momentum strip and records the \((t + 1)\)st coefficient of \(F_k\) exactly on every torus of the sequence. Suppose further that for each \(b = 2, \ldots, t\) there is one order-\((b - 1)\) picture \(P_{b - 1}\), holomorphic and bounded on a momentum strip, which records the \(b\)th coefficient of \(F_k\) for every \(k\) and each of whose entries has ray order at least \(s\). Then each contraction \(\mathcal{C}_j P\) has ray order at least \(s\).

Proof. Fix the leg \(j\), the color word \(v\) and the run \(a\). On the torus of side \(m_k\) take mesh momenta \(p_0, \ldots, p_t\) with zero sum, and differentiate the identity \(F_k (g (\theta, u)) = F_k (u)\) once in each of \(t + 1\) directions: in slot \(j\) the direction is the site wave of momentum \(p_j\) and color \(v_j\), and in every other slot \(i\) it is the link wave of momentum \(p_i\), axis \(a_i\) and color \(v_i\). The right side gives zero, because its slot \(j\) has no link component. The left side is, by the chain rule, the finite sum over the set partitions \(\pi\) of the \(t + 1\) slots, the blocks being ordered by their least slot, of \(D^b F_k (0)\) applied to the block derivatives of \(g\); here \(b\) is the number of blocks of \(\pi\).

The all-singleton partition contributes the first derivative of \(g\) in each slot. In slot \(j\) this is \(- d_0\) of the site wave, whose link components are \(- q (p_j, \mu)\) times the link waves of momentum \(p_j\) and axis \(\mu\); in the other slots it is the link wave itself. By the exact coefficient record this term equals \(- | \Lambda_{m_k} | (t + 1) ! \hspace{0.17em} (\mathcal{C}_j P)_{v, a}\) at the independent momenta. The one-block term vanishes since \(DF_k (0) = 0\).

Now let \(\pi\) have \(b\) blocks \(\pi_1, \ldots, \pi_b\) with \(2 \leqslant b \leqslant t\). Since \(g\) is given by the one local function \(\omega\) on every link, the derivative of \(g\) of order \(| \pi_r |\) applied to waves of momenta \((p_i)_{i \in \pi_r}\) is the link wave of momentum \(\sum_{i \in \pi_r} p_i\) whose amplitude, at axis \(\mu\) and color \(c\), is the corresponding component of \(D^{| \pi_r |} \omega (0)\) evaluated on the triples \((\alpha_i, \upsilon_i, e^{ip_{i, \mu}} \alpha_i)\) formed from the site and link amplitudes of the inserted waves. Call this amplitude \(c_{\pi, r} (\mu, c ; p)\). It is a polynomial in the phases \(e^{ip_{i, \mu}}\), so it is a continuous function of real momenta, and it does not depend on the period. The lower picture \(P_{b - 1}\) records the \(b\)th coefficient of every \(F_k\), and the block momenta sum to zero. Hence the contribution of \(\pi\) is \(| \Lambda_{m_k} | \hspace{0.17em} g_{\pi} (p_1, \ldots, p_t)\), where

\(\displaystyle g_{\pi} (p) = b! \sum_{\boldsymbol{\mu}, \mathbf{c}} \left( \prod_{r = 1}^b c_{\pi, r} (\mu_r, c_r ; p) \right) (P_{b - 1})_{\mathbf{c}, \boldsymbol{\mu}} \left( \left. \sum_{i \in \pi_2} p_i, \ldots, \sum_{i \in \pi_b} p_i \right) \right.\)

is one function for all \(k\); in these sums \(p_0\) stands for \(- \sum_{i \geqslant 1} p_i\). It is defined and continuous at every real tuple. Its arguments in \(P_{b - 1}\) are linear in \(p\), so along a real ray through zero the picture factor is \(O (| \lambda |^s)\), while the continuous multipliers stay bounded. Thus \(g_{\pi}\) has ray order at least \(s\).

Dividing the differentiated identity by the positive factor \(| \Lambda_{m_k} |\), which is common to the top and to every lower term, we obtain on the period mesh

\(\displaystyle (t + 1) ! \hspace{0.17em} (\mathcal{C}_j P)_{v, a} (p) = \sum_{\pi} g_{\pi} (p),\)

the sum being over the partitions with \(2 \leqslant b \leqslant t\) blocks. Every real momentum tuple is a limit of mesh tuples along \(m_k \to \infty\): round the \(t\) independent momenta to the period mesh and let the remaining momentum \(p_0\) be their negative sum. The mesh momenta are determined modulo \(2 \pi\), and both sides are evaluated at this real representative; periodicity of the pictures makes the choice immaterial. Both sides are continuous at real tuples, the left one because \(P\) is holomorphic on a strip. The identity therefore holds at every real tuple. The finite sum on the right has ray order \(s\), proving the claim.\(\Box\)

Lemma 11.7. (Recovering powers from the contracted kernel) Assume the picture entries are analytic at zero. For every fixed color word and run with at least one independent momentum variable, contractions of ray order at least two imply that each entry has ray order at least one. If the contractions have ray order at least three and there are at least two independent momentum blocks (equivalently, at least three external legs), then each entry has ray order at least two.

Proof. The first-order expansion \(q (\lambda p_j, \mu) = i \lambda p_{j, \mu} + O (\lambda^2)\) shows, after dividing a contraction of order two by \(\lambda\), that

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\mu \in \mathcal{A}} p_{j, \mu} P_{v, \text{update} (a, j, \mu)} (0) = 0.\)

Choose \(p\) supported at the unique axis which updates \(a_j\) back to itself. Then \(P_{v, a} (0) = 0\). Analyticity gives ray order one.

For a contraction of order three, the next coefficient, using that constant terms vanish, is

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\mu \in \mathcal{A}} p_{j, \mu} DP_{v, \text{update} (a, j, \mu)} (0) [p] = 0. \) (11.2)

Fix leg \(j\) and a vector \(w\) whose \(j\)th block is zero. Let \(q\) be the coordinate unit vector on leg \(j\) at the axis that updates \(a_j\) back to itself. Evaluate (11.2) at \(q + w\) and subtract its value at \(q\). Only the selected axis remains, giving \(DP_{v, a} (0) [w] = 0\). The equation at \(q\) also gives \(DP_{v, a} (0) [q] = 0\). Thus the derivative vanishes on all vectors transverse to leg \(j\). With two distinct legs, split any vector into a part supported on the first and a part with that leg zero; apply the transverse conclusion using the other leg for the first part and the first leg for the second. The full differential vanishes. Together with the vanishing constant term, this gives ray order two.\(\Box\)

11.3.3Cubic and quartic terms

Let \(A^{(3)}_i\) and \(A^{(4)}_i\) be gauge-invariant atom streams of degrees three and four, each with activity budget at most \(A\) at width \(\sigma\). Write \(T_r\) for their finite signed-orientation-averaged translation sums.

Proposition 11.8. (Two soft pictures for the curvature sum) If \(m_k \to \infty\), there are \(\eta_{\ast} > 0\) and \(K_{\ast} < \infty\), depending only on \(G, \sigma\), such that

\(\displaystyle F_k = T_3 (m_k) + T_4 (m_k)\)

has exact recorded pictures \(P_2\) of order \(2\) and \(P_3\) of order \(3\) with uniform bound at most \(AK_{\ast}\). The first picture has vanishing order \(2\); the second has vanishing order \(1\). The functions \(F_k\) are analytic, gauge invariant, and vanish to order at least three at zero.

Proof. Apply Proposition 11.5 to each of the four choices

\(\displaystyle (r, t) = (3, 2), (3, 3), (4, 2), (4, 3) .\)

For each fixed \(t\), add the degree-three and degree-four pictures. Their recording identities add exactly, and their bounds add. Choose the minimum of their four positive widths and the sum of their four finite bound constants; this yields one \(\eta_{\ast}\) and one \(K_{\ast}\), uniform over the stream and all periods.

The individual atom series vanish to at least their degree at zero, because each transported curvature slot vanishes there. Absolute convergence of the differentiated series preserves these vanishing orders, so \(F_k\) has order at least three. Gauge invariance is preserved by the finite orientation average. The zero picture records its second Taylor coefficient, and the zero first derivative is automatic. Thus the order-two picture supplies the lower-order coefficient needed for the order-three Ward argument.

Apply Lemma 11.6 with \(t = 2\) to the order-two picture. The zero lower coefficient and the order-three vanishing of \(F_k\) give contractions of order three. Lemma 11.7 then upgrades the order-two picture to vanishing order two. For the order-three picture, apply Lemma 11.6 with \(t = 3\); the zero second coefficient and the order-two picture just obtained supply the lower-order data at block lengths two and three. The contractions vanish to order two, and Lemma 11.7 gives vanishing order one for the order-three picture. These are the two claimed Ray orders.\(\Box\)

11.3.4The curvature stream and the Wilson subtraction

We fix the objects. On the torus of side \(m\) a link fluctuation \(u\) assigns a Lie-algebra element to every positively oriented link, and \(U = \exp u\) is the corresponding configuration. Let \(\varrho\) be the defining unitary representation, of dimension \(d_G\), and normalize the inner product on the Lie algebra by \(\| \varrho_{\ast} (c)\|^2 = d_G |c|^2\), with the Hilbert–Schmidt norm on the left. The plaquette energy is \(\varpi (g) = 1 - \text{Retr} \hspace{0.17em} \varrho (g) / d_G = \| \varrho (g) - 1\|^2 / (2 d_G)\), and the Wilson action is \(W_m (u) = \sum_f \varpi (U_f)\), summed over the plaquettes \(f\) of the torus with holonomies \(U_f\). The curvature of a group element is \(\kappa (g) = \Pi_G \left( \tfrac{1}{2} (\varrho (g) - \varrho (g)^{- 1}) \right)\), where \(\Pi_G\) is the projection onto the Lie algebra, so that \(\kappa (\exp c) = c + O (|c|^2)\). A slot consists of a lattice walk from the origin and a plaquette type; its value \(s (U ; x)\) at the anchor \(x\) is the curvature of the plaquette of that type at the endpoint of the translated walk, transported back to \(x\) by the adjoint action of the holonomy along the walk.

A path family \(R\) consists of rows, a loop stream, and cubic and quartic atom streams \(R^{(3)}, R^{(4)}\). For each plaquette type \(b\) the row is a sequence of real coefficients \(c_{b, i}\) and slots \(s_{b, i}\). Its row field at the plaquette \(f = (x, b)\) is the sum \(\ell_R (U ; f) = \sum_i c_{b, i} s_{b, i} (U ; x)\); it converges absolutely when \(R\) satisfies the path bound stated below, which is assumed whenever the row field is used. A clip \(p\) supplies the direct coefficient \(p. \text{direct} > 0\). The unaveraged quadratic path action is

\(\displaystyle Q_{p, R, m} (U) = \sum_f \left( p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} \varpi (U_f) + \tfrac{1}{2} | \ell_R (U ; f) |^2 \right) = p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} W_m + Q^{(2)}_m,\)

in the notation of Corollary 11.4. Let \(\mathcal{S}\) be the finite signed-coordinate group acting on configurations by \(U \mapsto \rho_s U\). For a period sequence \(m_k\) put

\(\displaystyle J_k (u) = \frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|} \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} Q_{p, R, m_k} (\rho_s \exp u), \qquad C_k = T_3 (m_k) + T_4 (m_k), \qquad W_k = W_{m_k},\)

where \(T_3, T_4\) are the signed-orientation-averaged translation sums of the atom streams \(R^{(3)}, R^{(4)}\), as in the preceding subsection. The selected action is

\(\displaystyle I_k = \text{intended} (p, R ; m_k) = J_k + C_k .\)

The loop stream of \(R\) is not part of \(I_k\). The path bound \(R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda)\), recalled in Subsection 11.21, provides the row budgets \(\sum_i |c_{b, i} | e^{\tau \ell_{b, i}} \leqslant A\), where \(\ell_{b, i}\) is the length of the slot \(s_{b, i}\), the cubic and quartic activity budgets at most \(A\) at the same width \(\tau\), and the invariance of every atom.

Proposition 11.9. (Curvature stream pictures) Fix \(\tau > 0\). There are constants \(\eta_c > 0\), \(K_c < \infty\), depending only on \(G\) and \(\tau\), such that the following holds. If \(m_k \to \infty\) and the cubic and quartic streams are invariant with activity budgets at most \(A\) at width \(\tau\), then for each \(j = 1, 2, 3\) the sequence \((C_k)_k\) has an exactly recorded picture of order \(j\), holomorphic on the strip of width \(\eta_c\), of ray order at least \(4 - j\), with bound at most \(AK_c\).

Proof. Proposition 11.8, applied at width \(\tau\), supplies the order-two and order-three pictures. For order two its vanishing order is two, as required. For order three its vanishing order is one. The order-one picture is identically zero: \(C_k\) vanishes to third order, so its second Taylor coefficient is zero. The zero picture records it exactly, its bound is zero, and it has every ray order. Taking the common minimum width and the maximum of the two bounds proves the assertion.\(\Box\)

We next describe the metric hypotheses. A bed \(B\) consists of five real coefficient arrays on \(\mathbb{Z}^4\), each indexed by pairs of plaquette types: the metric \(H\), the diagonal metric \(\tilde{H}\), designated inverses of these two, and the root \(r\). For such an array \(E\) write \(E_m\) for its periodization to the torus of side \(m\), a matrix acting on two-form fields, whose entries are the sums of \(E\) over residue classes modulo \(m\). Since the arrays of a bed are arbitrary, these sums, and the value \(\hat{H} (0) = \sum_x H (x)\) occurring in the floor condition, are defined by the following convention: the sum of a countable family of real numbers is its unconditional sum if the family is summable, which for real families is the same as absolutely summable, and is \(0\) otherwise. The floor and sworn conditions below are conditions on the matrices so defined. In this section the symbol of \(H\) is used only under a strip bound on \(H\), where all these series converge absolutely. If \(E\) is entrywise absolutely summable, \(\sum_x |E_{bb'} (x) | < \infty\) for every pair of types, write \(\hat{E} (p) = \sum_x e^{ip \cdot x} E (x)\) for its symbol; then \(E_m\) multiplies a Fourier wave of mesh momentum \(k\) by \(\hat{E} (k)\). The strip bound \(E. \text{strip} (\sigma, M)\) means \(\sum_x e^{\sigma |x|_1} |E_{bb'} (x) | \leqslant M\) for every pair of types. For \(\sigma > 0\) it implies absolute summability, and that every entry of \(\hat{E}\) is holomorphic and bounded in modulus by \(M\) on the strip of width \(\sigma\).

  • \(B\) is attached to \(R\) if, for all plaquette types \(b, b'\) and every \(y \in \mathbb{Z}^4\), the coefficients \(c_{b, i}\) of the slots of row \(b\) with plaquette type \(b'\) and walk endpoint \(y\) sum to \(r_{bb'} (y)\).

  • \(\text{Floor} (B, p, m, \varepsilon, M)\) is the conjunction of the following conditions at the side \(m\): the designated inverses are inverse to \(H_m\) and \(\tilde{H}_m\); \(H_m = p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} I + r_m^{\textsf{T}} r_m\); \(\hat{H} (0) = I\); \(H_m d_1 = \tilde{H}_m d_1\), with \(d_1\) the lattice curl from one-forms to two-forms; \(H_m\) and \(\tilde{H}_m\) are symmetric; and both satisfy \(\varepsilon |v|^2 \leqslant \langle v, H_m v \rangle \leqslant M |v|^2\), respectively the same with \(\tilde{H}_m\), for every real two-form \(v\).

  • The sworn condition at the side \(m\) says that the quadratic form

    \(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}_m (x, y) = \sum_f \langle (d_1 x)_f, (H_m d_1 y)_f \rangle\)

    on Lie-algebra valued one-forms is invariant under the signed-coordinate group: \(\mathcal{K}_m (x^s, y^s) =\mathcal{K}_m (x, y)\) for \(s \in \mathcal{S}\), where \(x \mapsto x^s\) is the linear action for which \(\rho_s \exp x = \exp x^s\).

Here \(H_m\) acts on each color component, in an orthonormal basis of the Lie algebra, and \(\langle \cdot, \cdot \rangle\) is the color inner product. Of the floor conditions, this section uses only the root identity, the normalization \(\hat{H} (0) = I\) and the symmetry of \(H_m\).

Lemma 11.10. (Quadratic coefficients of the Wilson and path actions) Let \(m\) be a torus side.

  1. On every torus, \(W_m (0) = 0\), \(DW_m (0) = 0\) and

    \(\displaystyle D^2 W_m (0) [x, y] = \sum_f \langle (d_1 x)_f, (d_1 y)_f \rangle .\)

  2. Let \(R\) satisfy the path bound with parameters \((p, \tau, A, \Lambda)\) and let \(B\) be attached to \(R\). Suppose that \(\text{Floor} (B, p, m, \varepsilon, M)\) and the sworn condition hold at the side \(m\). Then the signed average \(J\) of \(Q_{p, R, m}\) satisfies

    \(\displaystyle J (0) = 0, \qquad DJ (0) = 0, \qquad D^2 J (0) [x, y] =\mathcal{K}_m (x, y) .\)

Proof. Both functions are real analytic near zero, by the holomorphic extensions constructed for Proposition 11.1 and Proposition 11.3. We use the following elementary fact. Let \(f\) be twice continuously differentiable near zero in a finite-dimensional space, and let \(\mathcal{Q}\) be a symmetric bilinear form such that \(f (\lambda a) / \lambda^2 \to \mathcal{Q} (a, a) / 2\) as \(\lambda \to 0\), for every \(a\). Then \(f (0) = 0\), \(Df (0) = 0\) and \(D^2 f (0) =\mathcal{Q}\). Indeed the second-order Taylor formula along the line through \(a\) forces \(f (0) = 0\), then \(Df (0) a = 0\), then \(D^2 f (0) [a, a] =\mathcal{Q} (a, a)\), and polarization of the two symmetric forms gives the last claim.

Fix a one-form \(a\) and put \(U (\lambda) = \exp (\lambda a)\). The holonomy \(V_f (\lambda)\) around a plaquette \(f\) is a product of four factors \(\exp (\pm \lambda a_e)\), so \(V_f (0) = 1\) and \(\frac{d}{d \lambda} \varrho (V_f (\lambda)) |_{\lambda = 0} = \varrho_{\ast} ((d_1 a)_f)\). By the normalization of the color inner product,

\(\displaystyle \lambda^{- 2} \varpi (V_f (\lambda)) = \frac{\| \lambda^{- 1} (\varrho (V_f (\lambda)) - 1)\|^2}{2 d_G} \longrightarrow \tfrac{1}{2} | (d_1 a)_f |^2 .\)

Summing over the finitely many plaquettes and applying the elementary fact with \(\mathcal{Q} (x, y) = \sum_f \langle (d_1 x)_f, (d_1 y)_f \rangle\) proves the first part.

For the second part consider one slot \(s\) at an anchor \(x\), and let \(f'\) be the torus plaquette it reads. Its value at \(U (\lambda)\) is \(\kappa (T (\lambda) V_{f'} (\lambda) T (\lambda)^{- 1})\), where \(T (\lambda)\) is the holonomy along the walk and \(T (0) = 1\). The derivative at zero of the conjugated holonomy is that of \(V_{f'}\), hence

\(\displaystyle \lambda^{- 1} s (U (\lambda) ; x) \longrightarrow (d_1 a)_{f'} .\)

Moreover, the adjoint action is isometric and \(V_{f'}\) ranges over the finitely many plaquettes of the torus. Hence there is a constant \(b_a\), for instance \(1 + \sum_f | (d_1 a)_f |\), such that \(| \lambda^{- 1} s (U (\lambda) ; x) | \leqslant b_a\) for all sufficiently small \(\lambda \neq 0\), simultaneously for all slots and anchors. The row budget gives \(\sum_i |c_{b, i} | \leqslant A\). Dominated convergence therefore yields, for every plaquette \(f = (x, b)\),

\(\displaystyle \lambda^{- 1} \ell_R (U (\lambda) ; f) \longrightarrow \ell_{\text{path}} (f, a) := \sum_i c_{b, i} \hspace{0.17em} (d_1 a)_{f_{b, i} (x)},\)

where \(f_{b, i} (x)\) is the plaquette read by the slot \(s_{b, i}\) at the anchor \(x\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle \lambda^{- 2} Q_{p, R, m} (U (\lambda)) \longrightarrow \tfrac{1}{2} \sum_f \left( p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} | (d_1 a)_f |^2 + | \ell_{\text{path}} (f, a) |^2 \right) .\)

The right side equals \(\tfrac{1}{2} \mathcal{K}_m (a, a)\). This is the path-to-root computation of Subsubsection 3.9.3: grouping the absolutely summable row by the plaquette read and using attachment gives \(\ell_{\text{path}} (f, a) = [r_m (d_1 a)] (f)\) in each color component, and the root identity \(H_m = p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} I + r_m^{\textsf{T}} r_m\) of the floor then gives

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}_m (a, a') = \sum_f \left( p. \text{direct} \hspace{0.17em} \langle (d_1 a)_f, (d_1 a')_f \rangle + \langle \ell_{\text{path}} (f, a), \ell_{\text{path}} (f, a') \rangle \right) .\)

Finally \(\rho_s \exp (\lambda a) = \exp (\lambda a^s)\), so the same limit applies to each term of the signed average, with \(a^s\) in place of \(a\):

\(\displaystyle \lambda^{- 2} J (\lambda a) \longrightarrow \frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|} \sum_{s \in \mathcal{S}} \tfrac{1}{2} \mathcal{K}_m (a^s, a^s) = \tfrac{1}{2} \mathcal{K}_m (a, a),\)

the last equality being the sworn condition. The form \(\mathcal{K}_m\) is symmetric because \(H_m\) is. The elementary fact proves the second part.\(\Box\)

The Hessian formulas of the lemma become coefficient pictures as follows. Let \(d_1 (p)\) be the symbol of the lattice curl, a matrix from link directions to plaquette types whose entries are \(0\) or \(\pm q (p, \mu)\). Let \(E\) be one of the arrays above, assumed entrywise absolutely summable, or the unit array \(I\), which is the identity matrix at the origin and zero elsewhere; its symbol is the identity and it has strip bound \((\sigma, 1)\) for every \(\sigma\). Define the order-one picture

\(\displaystyle \textsf{S} [E]_{\mathbf{c}, \mathbf{a}} (p) = \tfrac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{c_0 = c_1 \}} \left[ d_1 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} \hat{E} (p) \hspace{0.17em} d_1 (p) \right]_{a_0 a_1},\)

where \(p\) is the one independent momentum, \(\mathbf{c}\) the pair of colors and \(\mathbf{a}\) the pair of link directions. The transpose is algebraic; for real \(p\), \(d_1 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} = d_1 (p)^{\ast}\). The factor \(\tfrac{1}{2}\) is the Taylor normalization of the second coefficient. Inserting two Fourier waves of mesh momenta \(k_0, k_1\) into the complex-bilinear extensions of the forms of Lemma 11.10, with colors \(c_0, c_1\) and link directions \(a_0, a_1\), gives zero unless \(k_0 + k_1 = 0\) and \(c_0 = c_1\), and otherwise the number of sites times the displayed bracket at \(p = k_1\), with \(\hat{E} = I\) for the Wilson action and \(\hat{E} = \hat{H}\) for \(J\); the normalized second coefficient is half of this. Thus \(\textsf{S} [I]\), the unit leader, records the second Taylor coefficient of \(W_m\) exactly on every torus. Under the hypotheses of the lemma, and assuming in addition that the metric \(H\) is entrywise absolutely summable, the metric-slot picture \(\textsf{S} [H]\) records that of \(J\) exactly on the torus of side \(m\). In the applications below this additional condition follows from \(H. \text{strip} (\sigma, C)\) with \(\sigma > 0\). If \(E. \text{strip} (\sigma, D)\), then each entry of \(\textsf{S} [E]\) is bounded on the strip of width \(\sigma\) by

\(\displaystyle \text{quadBound} (\sigma, D) = 36 \cdot (4 (e^{\sigma} + 1))^2 D,\)

the product of the two counts of plaquette types and the elementary strip bounds for the two curl factors; it is independent of the torus side. Each curl factor is \(O (|p|)\) along real rays, so \(\textsf{S} [E]\) has ray order at least two.

Now impose the selected metric hypotheses: \(B\) is attached to \(R\); its metric satisfies \(H. \text{strip} (\sigma, C)\); the floor holds at every torus side; and the sworn condition holds at each selected side \(m_k\). The following are the only uses of these assumptions. The metric-slot picture \(\textsf{S} [H]\) has bound \(b_0 = \text{quadBound} (\sigma, C)\) and records the quadratic coefficient of \(J_k\) for every \(k\). The unit leader has bound \(b_1 = \text{quadBound} (\sigma, 1)\) and records the Wilson coefficient. The floor gives the zero-momentum normalization \(\hat{H} (0) = I\), and analyticity then gives \(\hat{H} (p) - I = O (|p|)\). The difference

\(\displaystyle \textsf{S} [H] - \textsf{S} [I] = \tfrac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{\{c_0 = c_1 \}} \left[ d_1 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} (\hat{H} (p) - I) \hspace{0.17em} d_1 (p) \right]\)

therefore has ray order three. This is the order-one picture for \(J_k - W_k\), with bound \(b_0 + b_1\).

The higher pictures of \(J_k\) are supplied by Corollary 11.4, whose averaged kernel \(Q^{\sharp}\) is \(J_k\) in logarithmic coordinates. Apply it with its row budget equal to \(A\), its width equal to the path width \(\tau\), and the bound \(Z\) for the direct coefficient. For \(j = 2, 3\) it gives one picture of order \(j\), recording the \((j + 1)\)st coefficient of \(J_k\) on every torus, holomorphic on the strip of width \(\eta_j = \min (1, \tau / (8 (j + 1)^2))\), with bound

\(\displaystyle P_j = e^{(j + 1)^2 \eta_j} \left( ZX_j + \frac{|\mathcal{A}_{\text{pl}} |d_{\text{col}}}{2} A^2 S_G^2 \frac{\text{Cauchy} (R_{\tau / 2}, j + 1)}{(j + 1) !} \right),\)

where \(|\mathcal{A}_{\text{pl}} |\) is the number of plaquette types, \(d_{\text{col}}\) the number of color coordinates, and \(X_j\) the bound of the Wilson picture of order \(j\) in Proposition 11.1. That proposition supplies the Wilson pictures of orders two and three themselves, with bounds \(X_2, X_3\) and width one. Subtracting pictures preserves exact recording and adds their bounds.

Lemma 11.11. (Soft Wilson pictures) Let \(m_k \to \infty\). For \(j = 1, 2, 3\) the Wilson actions \(W_{m_k}\) have an exactly recorded picture of order \(j\), holomorphic and bounded on the strip of width one, of ray order at least \(3 - j\). The sum of the three bounds is at most \(P_W = \text{quadBound} (1, 1) + X_2 + X_3\), which does not depend on the sequence.

Proof. For \(j = 1\) take the unit leader \(\textsf{S} [I]\). It records the second coefficient by Lemma 11.10, has bound \(\text{quadBound} (1, 1)\) at width one, and has ray order two. For \(j = 2\) take the picture of Proposition 11.1. The Wilson action is real analytic and gauge invariant, and \(DW_m (0) = 0\) by Lemma 11.10. Apply Lemma 11.6 with \(t = 2\); its single lower picture is the unit leader, of ray order two. The contractions therefore have ray order two, and the first part of Lemma 11.7 gives ray order one. For \(j = 3\) take the order-three picture of Proposition 11.1; ray order zero only requires boundedness near zero.\(\Box\)

Proposition 11.12. (The selected Wilson-subtracted response) Fix \(\sigma, \tau > 0\), \(C, Z, A \geqslant 0\) and \(\varepsilon \in \mathbb{R}\). There are \(s > 0\) and \(M < \infty\) such that the following holds for every divergent period sequence \(m_k\), bed \(B\), clip \(p\), path family \(R\) and \(\Lambda\). Assume that \(R\) has path bounds \((p, \tau, A, \Lambda)\), that \(B\) is attached to \(R\) with metric strip bound \((\sigma, C)\), that \(\text{Floor} (B, p, n, \varepsilon, C)\) holds at every torus side \(n\), that the sworn condition holds at each \(m_k\), and that

\(\displaystyle p. \text{direct} \leqslant Z.\)

Then \((I_k - W_k)_k\) has exactly recorded pictures of orders \(j = 1, 2, 3\), each holomorphic on the strip of width \(s\) with bound at most \(M\), and of ray order at least \(4 - j\). One valid bound is

\(\displaystyle M = \text{quadBound} (\sigma, C) + \text{quadBound} (\sigma, 1) + P_2 + P_3 + X_2 + X_3 + AK_c,\)

where \(P_2, P_3\) are the two row-action bounds displayed above, \(X_2, X_3\) the Wilson bounds of Proposition 11.1, and \(K_c\) is from Proposition 11.9 at the width \(\tau\). One valid width is the minimum of \(\sigma\), \(1\), the two row widths \(\eta_2, \eta_3\), and \(\eta_c\).

Proof. Set \(F_k = J_k - W_k\). Let \(S_1 = \textsf{S} [H] - \textsf{S} [I]\) be the metric-slot picture minus the unit leader picture. It records the second Taylor coefficient of \(F_k\) for every \(k\), has bound \(b_0 + b_1\), and has vanishing order three by the floor normalization \(\hat{H} (0) = I\). In the notation of the Ward lemma it is the lower picture of order one for \(F_k\). Let \(S_2\) be the order-two picture of \(J_k\) from Corollary 11.4 minus the order-two Wilson picture, and let \(S_3\) be the corresponding order-three picture minus the order-three Wilson picture. They exactly record the third and fourth Taylor coefficients of \(F_k\) and have bounds at most \(P_2 + X_2\) and \(P_3 + X_3\).

The action \(F_k\) is real analytic and has zero first derivative by Lemma 11.10. It is gauge invariant: a gauge transformation conjugates every plaquette holonomy and rotates every slot value, hence every row field \(\ell_R (U ; f)\), by the adjoint action of the gauge element at the anchor, which preserves \(\varpi\) and the color norm; and the signed-coordinate action carries gauge transformations to gauge transformations. For \(t = 2\), apply Lemma 11.6 to \(S_2\), with the single lower picture \(S_1\); the resulting contraction has vanishing order three, and Lemma 11.7 gives vanishing order two for \(S_2\). For \(t = 3\), apply the partition lemma to \(S_3\), using \(S_1\) and the now improved \(S_2\) at its two lower block counts. The contraction has vanishing order two, so the same recovery lemma gives vanishing order one for \(S_3\). This supplies the exact three records and bounds required for \(F_k\).

The cubic and quartic terms \(C_k\) have the order-one, order-two, and order-three pictures of Proposition 11.9. Add the order-\(j\) picture of \(C_k\) to the order-\(j\) picture of \(F_k\) for each \(j = 1, 2, 3\). The sum records \(F_k + C_k = I_k - W_k\) exactly. Its bound is at most the displayed \(M\), and its vanishing order is at least \(4 - j\), since the order of a sum is at least the minimum order of its summands. Taking the common minimum strip width gives \(s\).\(\Box\)

11.3.5From coefficient pictures to local response arrays

We state the finite conversion in its concrete two-sided form. For \(j = 1, 2, 3\), a coefficient picture \(P_j\) records exactly the normalized \((j + 1)\)st derivative of \(U_m\) on every finite torus. It is uniformly priced at width \(s\) if each entry is holomorphic and bounded by the same constant on the independent-momentum strip of width \(s\). It has ray order \(r\) if, on every real ray through zero, each entry is \(O (| \lambda |^r)\). For an analytic entry this means every Taylor homogeneous polynomial below degree \(r\) vanishes: evaluating that polynomial on each real direction gives zero, hence the polynomial is identically zero. Thus ray order \(r\) supplies exactly the Taylor-vanishing hypothesis used in Lemma 11.13. The required input at level \(j\) is ray order \(3 - j\).

For \(n = 0, 1, 2\), an external color address is an \((n + 2)\)-tuple of one-link color-coordinate labels, each a pair consisting of a spatial axis and a Lie-color basis index. The multilinear test fields \(w_r\) carry the corresponding link and site dependence. The stitch address set \(\textsf{St}_n\) is the set of ordered \((2 - n)\)-tuples from \(\{0, \ldots, n\} \times \mathcal{A}\). The fixed rule \(\ell_n\) routes each key \((r, \mu)\) to its assigned response leg and axis: for \(n = 0\) the two keys are routed to legs zero and one; for \(n = 1\) its single key \((r, \mu)\) is routed to leg \(r + 1\); and for \(n = 2\) there are no keys. Let \(\textsf{Shift}_n\) be the integer lattice on the \((n + 1)\) independent momentum-axis coordinates. A response array is a map

\(\displaystyle F_n : \textsf{Color}_{n + 2} \times \textsf{St}_n \times \textsf{Shift}_n \longrightarrow \mathbb{C}.\)

Its width at \(\eta\) is the supremum, over the finite color and stitch addresses, of

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \textsf{Shift}_n} e^{\eta |z|_1} |F_n (b, i, z) | .\)

The stitched Fourier picture is

\(\displaystyle \tilde{Q}_n (b, p) = \sum_{i \in \textsf{St}_n} \left( \prod_{r = 0}^{n + 1} \prod_{e \in \ell_n (i)_r} (e^{i \hspace{0.17em} \text{plot}_r (p) \cdot e} - 1) \right) \sum_z F_n (b, i, z) e^{ip \cdot z} . \) (11.3)

Here \(\text{plot}_r (p)\) are the \(n + 2\) external momenta: the first is minus the sum of the \(n + 1\) independent momenta, and the remaining ones are those independent momenta in order. For \(n = 0\) the two listed differences are distributed one per leg; for \(n = 1\) there is one difference, and for \(n = 2\) there are none.

To state the response identity, split a link fluctuation into two species \(x_+, x_-\) and let \(\textsf{D}_m (x)\) be the logarithmic link coordinate of the product of the two exponentials: on every link \(e\),

\(\displaystyle \textsf{D}_m (x_+, x_-)_e = \log (\exp (x_{+, e}) \exp (x_{-, e})),\)

where \(\log\) is the local inverse of \(\exp\) at the identity and \(x_{\pm}\) are near zero. Let \(v_n\) put species \(-\) in the first \(n\) slots and species \(+\) in the final two. Let \(\mathcal{R}_{n, m} (U_m)\) be the \((n + 2)\)-linear derivative of \(U_m \circ \textsf{D}_m\) at zero, restricted to those species in that order; it is a tensor whose components are indexed by external color addresses \(b\). For a list of axes \(\ell = (e_1, \ldots, e_r)\), let \(\Delta_{\ell} = (T_{e_1} - I) \cdots (T_{e_r} - I)\) be the corresponding product of translated one-link differences. For a test tuple \(w = (w_0, \ldots, w_{n + 1})\), define

\(\displaystyle \text{Local}_{m, \ell_n (i)} (F_n (b, i, \cdot)) [w] = \sum_z F_n (b, i, z) \sum_{x \in \Lambda_m} \prod_{r = 0}^{n + 1} (\Delta_{\ell_n (i)_r} w_r) (x + \text{wrap}_m (\text{lane}_r (z)), b_r) .\)

Here \(\text{lane}_0 (z) = 0\), while \(\text{lane}_r (z)\) for \(r \geqslant 1\) is the shift in the \((r - 1)\)st independent momentum coordinate. The width bound makes the Laurent sum absolutely convergent. The response identity required below is componentwise and multilinear:

\(\displaystyle (\mathcal{R}_{n, m} (U_m))_b [w] = \sum_{i \in \textsf{St}_n} \text{Local}_{m, \ell_n (i)} (F_n (b, i, \cdot)) [w] \qquad (b \in \textsf{Color}_{n + 2}) . \) (11.4)

Lemma 11.13. (Finite difference division) Let \(E\) be a finite set of phase coordinates. Suppose \(f\) is \(2 \pi\)-periodic in each coordinate and holomorphic, bounded by \(A\) on a strip of width \(s > 0\), and its Taylor expansion at zero has no terms of degree below \(q\). Then on a narrower strip it has a finite representation

\(\displaystyle f (p) = \sum_{(a_1, \ldots, a_q) \in E^q} \prod_{r = 1}^q (e^{ip_{a_r}} - 1) \hspace{0.17em} g_{a_1, \ldots, a_q} (p),\)

where the residual functions are holomorphic on width at least \(s / 2^q\) and uniformly bounded there by \(AC (E, q, s)\). For \(q = 0\) take the one-term representation \(g = f\).

Proof. For \(J \subseteq E\), write \(p^{[J]}\) for \(p\) with the coordinates in \(J\) set to zero. For each \(a\), periodicity implies that \(f (p) - f (p^{[\{a\}]})\) vanishes whenever \(p_a \in 2 \pi \mathbb{Z}\). Consequently it is divisible, as a holomorphic periodic function, by \(d_a (p) = e^{ip_a} - 1\). The quotient is removable at every zero. On a fundamental real period cell, away from fixed neighborhoods of these zeros the denominator is bounded below; inside those neighborhoods the quotient is bounded by Cauchy's estimate on a narrower strip. Thus

\(\displaystyle f (p) - f (p^{[\{a\}]}) = d_a (p) g_a (p)\)

with a uniform bound for \(g_a\). In the telescoping identity over \(J\), replace \(f\) at each step by the function with the previously selected coordinates staged to zero. Each corresponding quotient lowers the vanishing order by one. Induction on \(q\) applied to these residuals therefore yields the displayed finite sum. At most \(|E|^q\) terms occur; at each division, Cauchy's estimate on the current strip of width \(r\) bounds the quotient on width \(r / 2\); after \(q\) divisions the residual strip has width \(s / 2^q\). Each step multiplies the bound by a constant depending only on \(E, q, s\). The fully staged term is zero by the order-\(q\) hypothesis. This proves the result.\(\Box\)

For the local response array, the number of factors is \(q = 2 - n\), not \(n\). If \(n = 1, 2\), apply the factorization to every picture entry with \(q = 2 - n\) differences and place them on the prescribed stitch legs. For \(n = 0\), two factors are required, one on each of the two response legs. The division first gives the two factors in the single independent momentum; the second is moved to the other leg by the exact identity

\(\displaystyle q (p, \mu) = - \text{mode} (p, \text{step} \hspace{0.17em} \mu) q (- p, \mu) .\)

The phase is absorbed into the Laurent coefficient, with its strip cost included in the constant. Thus this construction yields precisely the three stitch patterns: two factors for \(n = 0\), one for \(n = 1\), and none for \(n = 2\).

To control the Laurent width, let \(g\) be one residual, bounded by \(B\) on a strip of width \(\eta\). Shifting the contour in each periodic coordinate gives \(| \hat{g} (\nu) | \leqslant Be^{- \eta | \nu |_1}\). At width \(\eta / 2\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\nu} e^{(\eta / 2) | \nu |_1} | \hat{g} (\nu) | \leqslant B \left( \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} e^{- (\eta / 2) |k|} \right)^{|E|} = B \left( 1 + \frac{2}{e^{\eta / 2} - 1} \right)^{|E|} .\)

Thus each residual defines a Laurent entry with a quantitative width bound; summing the at most \(|E|^q\) residuals multiplies it by that finite factor. This is the coefficient-norm estimate used in the array construction.

We now verify the response identity and its bound using the actual mixed-field composition. Let \(D (x)\) be the local map which forms the product of the two split fields and then takes its logarithmic link coordinate. For a Boolean word \(v\) on the external slots, the response of \(U_m\) is the corresponding mixed derivative of \(U_m \circ D\) at zero. At level \(n\), the word has \(n\) entries of the first species followed by two entries of the second species; its length is \(n + 2\).

For a set partition \(\pi\) of these \(n + 2\) slots into \(b\) nonempty blocks, order the blocks canonically by their least slot. The chain rule gives the derivative of \(U_m\) of order \(b\) evaluated at the sums of external momenta in the blocks, multiplied by the derivatives of \(D\) on each block. The single-block term is zero because \(DU_m (0) = 0\). For \(2 \leqslant b \leqslant n + 2\), the action coefficient has picture order \(b - 1\). Its available ray order is \(3 - (b - 1) = 4 - b\), which is at least \(2 - n\). Thus every term in the mixed response picture has ray order \(2 - n\) and is analytic on a common narrower strip.

More explicitly, for a partition \(\pi\) and a basis phase assignment \(w\), the corresponding mixed coefficient is

\(\displaystyle b! \sum_{a_1, \ldots, a_b} \left( \prod_{r = 1}^b c_{\pi, r} (a_r ; w_r) \right) P_{b - 1} \left( a_1, \ldots, a_b ; \left. \sum_{j \in \pi_1} p_j, \ldots, \sum_{j \in \pi_b} p_j \right), \right.\)

where \(c_{\pi, r} (a_r ; w_r)\) is the coordinate coefficient of the derivative of \(D\) on block \(r\). The factorial is the normalization between the symmetric derivative picture and the raw \(b\)th action derivative; it does not enumerate permutations of the blocks. If the input pictures are bounded by \(A\), its absolute bound is bounded by

\(\displaystyle Ab! \sum_{a_1, \ldots, a_b} \prod_{r = 1}^b |c_{\pi, r} (a_r ; w_r) | .\)

Summing the absolute coefficient multiplier (the displayed sum without the input bound \(A\)) over partitions, color basis labels, and phase basis labels defines the response constant \(P_n\). These coefficients are uniformly finite: the map \(\textsf{D}_m\) is the same pointwise product-and-log map on each link. On a fixed identity neighborhood in the finite-dimensional gauge group, this map is analytic and bounded. Cauchy's estimate on a smaller ball bounds every derivative of order at most four by a constant depending only on the group and the order. The number of partitions and color/phase labels is finite, so \(P_n < \infty\) independently of \(m\). The common analytic strip for the mixed picture can be chosen as

\(\displaystyle s_n = \frac{s}{(n + 3)^2} .\)

Every independent external momentum has imaginary coordinates at most \(s_n\), and the missing momentum, their negative sum, has coordinates at most \((n + 1) s_n\). A block sum of at most \(n + 2\) external momenta therefore has imaginary coordinates at most \((n + 2) (n + 1) s_n < s\). Thus the input pictures can be evaluated at every block sum. The mixed picture is bounded by \(AP_n\) on width \(s_n\) when the input picture bounds are at most \(A\).

For each entry of the mixed picture \(\tilde{Q}_n\), finite difference division now produces the prescribed \(2 - n\) stitch factors and residual functions on width \(d_n = s_n / 2^{2 - n}\). Shifting each contour within the smaller strip of width \(d_n / 2\) gives Laurent coefficients bounded by \(Be^{- (d_n / 2) |z|_1}\) when the residual supremum is \(B\). At array width \(d_n / 4\), their weighted sum is bounded by

\(\displaystyle B \left( 1 + \frac{2}{e^{d_n / 4} - 1} \right)^{D_n},\)

where \(D_n\) is the finite number of independent Laurent coordinates. The number of partitions and addresses is finite, so all residuals assemble into a response array of width at most \(AC_n\) for a constant \(C_n\) depending only on the finite labels and input width.

To check exactness, evaluate the factorization on a finite torus. Each Laurent term is absolutely summable by its weighted width. Its spatial translation sum is a finite character sum; orthogonality makes it zero unless total momentum is zero, in which case it equals the number of sites. The coefficient picture recording identity then identifies the sum with the mixed derivative of \(U_m \circ D\). This is the exact finite-volume response identity, not only equality of Taylor polynomials.

Proposition 11.14. (Three-level response arrays) Let \(U_m\) be real analytic, with \(U_m (0) = 0\) and \(DU_m (0) = 0\). Suppose it has exact coefficient pictures \(P_j\) at orders \(j = 1, 2, 3\), whose entries are \(2 \pi\)-periodic in each phase coordinate, bounded on one common strip of width \(s > 0\), and have ray orders at least \(3 - j\). Then there are a common positive strip width \(\eta_{\ast}\) and a finite constant \(C_{\ast}\) such that the response arrays at levels \(n = 0, 1, 2\) satisfy (11.3), the exact finite-period response identity, and width at most \(C_{\ast}\) times the sum of the three input picture bounds, uniformly in \(m\).

Proof. For each \(n\), first form the mixed picture \(\tilde{Q}_n\) by the ordered-partition chain rule. Every term is the input picture \(P_{|b| - 1}\) at the block-summed momenta, multiplied by the block coefficients. The one-block term is zero. Since \(P_{|b| - 1}\) has ray order \(4 - |b| \geqslant 2 - n\), the mixed picture has ray order \(2 - n\). Apply the finite difference lemma to \(\tilde{Q}_n\) with \(q = 2 - n\) and assign the factors to the prescribed legs; for the two-leg split use the phase identity above. The finite character sum proves the response identity. The Laurent estimate bounds the width. For each \(n = 0, 1, 2\), let \(C_n\) be the finite bound multiplier from the ordered-partition and division construction, including the Laurent coefficient sum. For the common input strip \(s\), the mixed-picture strip at level \(n\) is \(s_n = s / (n + 3)^2\), and the residual strip is \(d_n = s_n / 2^{2 - n}\). Choose

\(\displaystyle \eta_{\ast} = \min_{0 \leqslant n \leqslant 2} (d_n / 4), \qquad C_{\ast} = C_0 + C_1 + C_2 .\)

Then all arrays have width at most \(C_{\ast}\) times the sum of the three input picture bounds. These finite constants and the positive width are independent of the period and action.\(\Box\)

The division and response construction above outputs an array of exactly this type. The factorization proves (11.3) entrywise. The finite-volume character identity proves (11.4); Laurent absolute convergence justifies the sum over shifts. Thus the exact response is supplied at each finite period, not merely in the limiting Taylor series.

Apply the conversion to \(I_k = (I_k - W_k) + W_k\) along a divergent period sequence. Proposition 11.12 supplies pictures with ray orders \(3, 2, 1\) at orders \(1, 2, 3\). By Lemma 11.11 the Wilson pictures have ray orders \(2, 1, 0\), so the sums have the required orders \(2, 1, 0\). Their exact records and bounds add. The value and first derivative of \(I_k\) vanish at zero by Lemma 11.10 and the third-order vanishing of the curvature part \(C_k\). We obtain three uniformly priced response arrays. Let \(M_g\) denote the displayed bound in Proposition 11.12, and let \(P_W\) be the bound of Lemma 11.11. Each of the three input pictures has bound at most \(M_g + P_W\) on the common strip of width \(\min (1, s)\), so the common array width is bounded by \(K = 3 C_{\ast} (M_g + P_W)\), where \(C_{\ast}\) is the multiplier of Proposition 11.14 for that strip.

Define the mockup width property by the common Laurent bound above at each level \(n \leqslant 2\), and define its response property to be (11.4) at every such level. Then the theorem below supplies both properties, conventionally called the width and exact-response requirements for the three mockups.

Theorem 11.15. (Selected soft-response arrays) Fix \(\sigma > 0\), \(M, Z, A \geqslant 0\), \(\tau > 0\), and \(\varepsilon \in \mathbb{R}\). There are \(\eta > 0\) and \(K < \infty\) such that the following holds for every divergent period sequence \(m_k\), clip \(p\), path family \(R\), bed \(B\), and \(\Lambda \geqslant 0\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle p. \text{direct} \leqslant Z, \quad R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda), \quad B \text{ attached to } R, \quad B. \text{metric.strip} (\sigma, M),\)

with \(\text{Floor} (B, p, n, \varepsilon, M)\) for every torus side \(n\), and the sworn condition at each selected side \(m_k\). Put \(U_{m_k} = \text{intended} (p, R ; m_k)\). Then there is one family of arrays \(F_0, F_1, F_2\), not depending on \(k\), such that each \(F_n\) has width at most \(K\) at \(\eta\), and such that the exact response identity (11.4) holds for \(U_{m_k}\) at every level \(n \leqslant 2\) and for every \(k\). Set \(F_n = 0\) for \(n > 2\). By construction the stitched pictures (11.3) of these arrays are the mixed response pictures of the sequence. One may take \(K = 3 C_{\ast} (M_g + P_W)\), where \(M_g\) is the bound displayed in Proposition 11.12 (with \(C = M\)), \(P_W\) is the Wilson bound of Lemma 11.11, and \(C_{\ast}\) is the response-array multiplier above. The constants \(\eta, K\) depend only on the listed scalar bounds and the fixed gauge and finite coordinate sets. The identity (11.4) is asserted only on the tori of the selected sequence, where the sworn condition is assumed.

Proof. Proposition 11.12 supplies pictures of \((U_{m_k} - W_k)_k\) of orders \(j = 1, 2, 3\) and ray orders \(4 - j\). Lemma 11.11 supplies Wilson pictures of ray orders \(3 - j\). Their sums are pictures of \((U_{m_k})_k\) with the required ray orders \(3 - j\), on the common strip of width \(\min (1, s)\). Bounds and exact recording identities add. Each \(U_{m_k}\) is real analytic, and Lemma 11.10 together with the third-order vanishing of the curvature part gives \(U_{m_k} (0) = 0\) and \(DU_{m_k} (0) = 0\). Apply Proposition 11.14 to the sequence \((U_{m_k})_k\). Its finite constants depend only on the listed bounds and the finite color, phase, and stitch sets, so the common width and bound are uniform in the path, the bed, the period sequence, and the selected action.\(\Box\)

Scope.
This section proves the uniform atom, Ward, and Wilson-subtracted coefficient estimates, together with the finite division and the exact stitched response identity. The dependence of downstream applications on the displayed metric, floor, and sworn hypotheses remains as stated.

11.4From Ward identities to invariant curvature coefficients

For the algebraic Poincaré lemma on continuum local jet forms, see [BBH00, Theorem 4.2]. Here the contraction is constructed on periodic momentum symbols, with quantitative strip and coefficient bounds used in the lattice comparison.

The leading Taylor terms of a gauge-invariant action satisfy a Ward identity in every field argument. We now turn these identities into an exponentially summable expansion in curvature variables. The momenta of the arguments are constrained to sum to zero, so one cannot simply apply independent one-variable divisions to all of them. The following construction resolves that dependence before choosing the invariant, real coefficients of the path expansion.

11.4.1A single exterior division with auxiliary constraints

Put \(q_a (p) = e^{ip_a} - 1\). On alternating coordinate tensors in four dimensions let \(E_a\) denote exterior multiplication by the \(a\)th basis vector and let \(\iota_a = E_a^{\textsf{T}}\). Write

\(\displaystyle \delta (p) = \sum_{a = 1}^4 q_a (p) \iota_a .\)

Thus \(\delta\) lowers exterior degree by one and \(\delta^2 = 0\). Tensor factors other than the one on which this operator acts are coefficient spaces. Operators on distinct factors commute. All transposes here are algebraic transposes, without complex conjugation. This convention is appropriate for the multilinear Taylor coefficients of a scalar action.

A periodic coefficient on \(d\) complex momentum coordinates has strip bound \((\sigma, A)\) if it is holomorphic on \(| \text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} p_j | < \sigma\) and has absolute value at most \(A\) there. Tensor bounds mean this entry by entry. Say that it has order \(s\) at zero if its restriction to each real ray \(p = tu\) is \(O (t^s)\) as \(t \to 0\). For an analytic function this is equivalent to the vanishing of all homogeneous Taylor polynomials of degree less than \(s\): restrict the Taylor expansion to each ray and use that a polynomial vanishing on every real vector is zero.

The division from Subsection 3.10 has the following strip version. For each coordinate \(j\), there is a periodic holomorphic \(D_j f\) such that

\(\displaystyle (e^{ip_j} - 1) D_j f (p) = f (p) - f (p|_{p_j = 0}), \qquad |D_j f|_{\sigma / 4} \leqslant A \hspace{0.17em} d_{\sigma},\) (11.5)

where one may take

\(\displaystyle d_{\sigma} = \frac{4}{\sigma} \left( \frac{2 (1 + \sigma / 4)}{\sigma / 4} \right)^d .\)

Indeed, Fourier expansion gives coefficients \(\hat{f} (z)\) with

\(\displaystyle \sum_z e^{(\sigma / 2) |z|_1} | \hat{f} (z) | \leqslant A \left( \frac{2 (1 + \sigma / 4)}{\sigma / 4} \right)^d .\)

Replace each monomial in coordinate \(j\) by the finite geometric sum displayed in that earlier section. It has at most \(|z|_1\) terms, all with displacement norm at most \(|z|_1\). On the smaller strip its absolute value is at most \(|z|_1 e^{(\sigma / 4) |z|_1}\), which is bounded by \((4 / \sigma) e^{(\sigma / 2) |z|_1}\). This proves normal convergence, the bound, and the identity, including the zeros of the divisor. If \(f\) has order \(s\), this division has order at least \(\max (s - 1, 0)\). To verify this, the right side of (11.5) has order \(s\), while the lowest nonzero homogeneous term of the divisor is \(ip_j\). A nonzero lowest homogeneous term of the quotient therefore gains exactly one degree upon multiplication.

Suppose a tensor \(f\) of exterior degree \(r\) is a cycle, \(\delta f = 0\). Set \(f_0 = f|_{p_a = 0}\) and

\(\displaystyle B_a = E_a D_a f, \qquad R_a = (I - E_a \iota_a) f_0 .\)

The Cartan identity \(\delta E_a + E_a \delta = q_a I\) gives

\(\displaystyle f = \delta B_a + R_a .\) (11.6)

For example, where \(q_a \ne 0\) the cycle identities for \(f\) and \(f_0\) imply \(\delta D_a f = - \iota_a f_0\); substitution gives the displayed formula. Continuity extends it to \(q_a = 0\). The residual is still a cycle, has zero entries on faces containing \(a\), and evaluates all other entries at \(p_a = 0\). Successive residual operations in the four coordinates therefore leave zero when \(r > 0\). When \(r = 0\), they leave the value at \(p = 0\), so the same conclusion holds if that value vanishes. Adding the four lifts proves the following fact.

Lemma 11.16. Let \(r, s \in \mathbb{N}\). Let \(f\) have strip bound \((\sigma, A)\), order \(s\), and exterior degree \(r\). Assume \(\delta f = 0\) and either \(r > 0\) or \(f|_{p = 0} = 0\). Then \(f = \delta B\), where \(B\) has degree \(r + 1\), strip bound \((\sigma / 4, 4 Ad_{\sigma})\), and order at least \(\max (s - 1, 0)\). Here other momentum coordinates may remain as parameters, and \(p = 0\) in the degree-zero hypothesis means only that the four coordinates of the divided momentum vanish.

If, in addition, for each exterior component the vector of coefficients in the other tensor factors belongs to a fixed linear subspace of that other-factor coefficient space, depending only on the other momenta, the same membership holds separately for every exterior component of \(B\) at every real momentum.

Proof. Only the last assertion remains. Away from \(q_a = 0\), the quotient in (11.5) is a scalar multiple of the difference of two vectors in that subspace. On \(q_a = 0\), vary just \(p_a\) and take the limit. The subspace is fixed during this limit and is closed, since the coefficient space is finite dimensional. Exterior multiplication, subtraction, and coordinate restriction preserve membership as well. Thus each of the four lifts and their sum preserve the constraint. Each residual retains the original strip bound and order; summing four lifts accounts for the factor four, with only one loss of Taylor order.\(\Box\)
