11.4.2Restoring the dependent momentum constraint

There are \(t\) independent momenta \(p_1, \ldots, p_t \in \mathbb{C}^4\), and we set \(p_0 = - \sum_{j = 1}^t p_j\). Write \(\delta_j = \delta (p_j)\) acting on tensor factor \(j\). A Ward identity on that factor is \(\delta_j f = 0\). We first allow the zeroth factor to have degree \(r + 1\) and all independent factors to have degree one.

One can solve the zeroth-factor cycle while preserving all Ward identities except, provisionally, the first. Exchange \(p_1\) and \(p_0\) by the linear involution

\(\displaystyle (p_1, p_2, \ldots, p_t) \longmapsto (- p_1 - \cdots - p_t, p_2, \ldots, p_t) .\)

It preserves the integer periodicity lattice and sends a sufficiently narrow strip into the original strip; width \(\sigma / (t + 1)\) is sufficient for \(t \geqslant 1\). Apply Lemma 11.16 in the new first variable and exchange back. The Ward subspaces for factors \(2, \ldots, t\) do not depend on this variable, so they are retained. The cost is a fixed multiple of the old cost, the width remains strictly positive, and Taylor order drops by at most one. Denote this operation simply as a zeroth-factor lift. Likewise, if a coefficient has no first tensor factor and vanishes when \(p_1 = 0\), the degree-zero case of the lemma adds a degree-one first factor whose contraction is the original coefficient, while retaining the remaining Ward identities.

Lemma 11.17. (Dimension shift) Fix \(t, r \geqslant 0\) with \(r + 1 + 2 t > 4\) and \(\sigma > 0\). There are \(\eta > 0\) and \(K < \infty\), depending only on these data, such that the following holds for every \(A \geqslant 0\). If \(u\) has strip bound \((\sigma, A)\), degrees \((r + 1, 1, \ldots, 1)\), and \(\delta_j u = 0\) for \(j = 0, \ldots, t\), there is \(v\) of degrees \((r + 2, 1, \ldots, 1)\) with strip bound \((\eta, KA)\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle \delta_0 v = u, \qquad \delta_j v = 0 \quad (1 \leqslant j \leqslant t) .\)

Proof. Induct on \(t\), allowing \(r\) to vary. At \(t = 0\) the degree \(r + 1\) exceeds four, so \(u = 0\) and one takes \(v = 0\). For \(t \geqslant 1\), first lift the zeroth cycle, temporarily forgetting the first Ward identity. This gives \(X\) with \(\delta_0 X = u\) and all remaining Ward identities. Contract its first factor:

\(\displaystyle V = \delta_1 X, \qquad \delta_0 V = \delta_1 u = 0.\)

A second zeroth-factor lift gives \(Y\) of zeroth degree \(r + 3\) with \(\delta_0 Y = V\) and the remaining Ward identities. Restrict \(Y\) to \(p_1 = 0\), obtaining \(Z\). Since \(q (0) = 0\), \(V|_{p_1 = 0} = 0\), so \(Z\) is a zeroth-factor cycle in \(t - 1\) independent variables. Its zeroth degree is \(r + 3\), and

\(\displaystyle r + 3 + 2 (t - 1) = r + 1 + 2 t > 4.\)

The induction hypothesis gives \(W\) of degree \(r + 4\) with \(\delta_0 W = Z\), retaining every remaining Ward identity. Extend \(W\) constantly in \(p_1\), writing the extension as \(W'\), and set

\(\displaystyle L = Y - \delta_0 W' .\)

Here \(\delta_0\) uses the full momentum \(- p_1 - \cdots - p_t\). Thus \(\delta_0 L = V\) by \(\delta_0^2 = 0\), while \(L|_{p_1 = 0} = 0\) by the induction identity. The degree-zero first-factor lift now gives \(F\) with \(\delta_1 F = L\) and all remaining Ward identities. Finally put \(v = X - \delta_0 F\). Commutation on distinct factors gives

\(\displaystyle \delta_0 v = u, \qquad \delta_1 v = V - \delta_0 L = 0.\)

The other identities hold term by term. All operations are finite sums, fixed linear changes of variables, restrictions, exterior contractions, and the lifts already bounded above. At each stage choose a smaller positive common width. Their number is determined by \(t, r\), and every bound remains linear in \(A\). This gives \(\eta\) and \(K\) before the particular input tensor is chosen.\(\Box\)

Take now a tensor \(f\) with \(t + 1 \geqslant 3\) degree-one factors and all Ward identities. The dimension-shift lemma with \(r = 0\) lifts factor zero while preserving the independent identities. Lift each independent factor in turn by Lemma 11.16. At each step the identities in the remaining factors are auxiliary subspaces independent of the divided momentum; the already lifted indices are just finite coefficient indices. We obtain \(g\) with all factors of degree two such that

\(\displaystyle f (p_1, \ldots, p_t) = \delta_0 \delta_1 \cdots \delta_t g (p_1, \ldots, p_t) .\) (11.7)

Its strip width and cost multiplier depend only on the original width and the number of factors. In particular the cubic and quartic applications have constants independent of the torus.

11.4.3The quadratic case and retained moments

For two factors the dimension inequality fails. Suppose instead that \(f (p)\) has both Ward identities at momenta \((- p, p)\) and order \(s + 2\) at zero. The preceding construction can be shortened in a way that retains precisely \(s\) orders after both lifts. Lift factor zero to obtain \(X\) of degrees \((2, 1)\) and order \(s + 1\), with \(\delta_0 X = f\). The contraction \(V = \delta_1 X\) has order \(s + 2\), since the contraction symbol vanishes to first order. It is a zeroth-factor cycle. Lift it to obtain \(Y\) of zeroth degree three and order \(s + 1\) with \(\delta_0 Y = V\).

There are no other momenta now. Hence \(Y|_{p = 0} = 0\), and the degree-zero first-factor lift gives \(D\) of degrees \((3, 1)\), order \(s\), and \(\delta_1 D = Y\). Set \(Z = X - \delta_0 D\). Then

\(\displaystyle \delta_0 Z = f, \qquad \delta_1 Z = 0, \qquad Z \text{ has order } s + 1.\)

One last first-factor lift gives \(g\) of degrees \((2, 2)\) and order \(s\), with \(\delta_0 \delta_1 g = f\). All bounds remain linear in the initial strip cost, with a positive width chosen before \(f\) and \(s\). For the loop remainder we use \(s = 2\): fourth-order vanishing of the potential coefficient yields second-order vanishing of its curvature coefficient.

11.4.4Real coefficients and invariance at each placement

Fix a real orthonormal basis of the finite-dimensional Lie algebra. The tensors now also have one colour index for each field factor. Assume the original tensor satisfies

\(\displaystyle \overline{f (- p)} = f (p) \quad \text{for real independent momenta } p,\)

and that its real and imaginary colour tensors are invariant under the simultaneous adjoint action of the gauge group. The exterior lifts need not respect these properties, so we restore them explicitly.

For each entry of \(g\), take the real part of its Fourier coefficients. The resulting real coefficient array has symbol

\(\displaystyle g_{\mathbb{R}} (p) = \tfrac{1}{2} (g (p) + \overline{g (- p)}) .\)

Since \(\overline{q (- p)} = q (p)\) for real \(p\), contraction of \(g_{\mathbb{R}}\) is still \(f\). If \(g\) has order \(s\), so does \(g_{\mathbb{R}}\). Fourier coefficient estimates on a smaller strip give an exponentially weighted summation bound. More precisely, from strip width \(w\) and entry bound \(B\) one obtains width \(w / 2\) and bound

\(\displaystyle B \left( \frac{2 (1 + w / 4)}{w / 4} \right)^{4 t} .\)

Let \(\mathcal{I}\) be the subspace of invariant real colour tensors, and choose a fixed linear projection \(P\) onto \(\mathcal{I}\). Apply \(P\) separately at each tuple of face directions and each tuple of lattice displacements. This gives real coefficients \(R_{\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{c}} (z)\) that are invariant at every such placement. Exterior contractions do not act on colour indices, so they commute with \(P\). Since \(P\) fixes the real and imaginary parts of \(f\), the reconstructed potential coefficient remains \(f\). The weighted bound is multiplied by at most

\(\displaystyle \sum_{i, j} |P_{ij} | < \infty .\)

This is a fixed finite-dimensional projection; no assertion that its norm is one is needed. It also preserves each retained order of vanishing. Thus, for some \(\eta > 0\) and \(C < \infty\) fixed by width, arity, and gauge group, every face and colour entry obeys

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^{4 t}} e^{\eta |z|_1} |R_{\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{c}} (z) | \leqslant CA.\) (11.8)

For two factors with retained order two, these coefficients have both zero total mass and zero first moments, entry by entry:

\(\displaystyle \sum_z R (z) = 0, \qquad \sum_z z_a R (z) = 0 \quad (a = 1, \ldots, 4) .\) (11.9)

Indeed the symbol at zero is the first sum. Exponential summability implies \(\sum_z |z|_1 |R (z) | < \infty\), so its derivative along the \(a\)th real momentum coordinate is \(i \sum_z z_a R (z)\). The symbol's value and first derivatives vanish by its retained second order. This proves both identities with absolute convergence.

11.4.5The invariant path expansion

For each tuple \((\mathbf{a}, z)\) of oriented coordinate plaquettes and displacements, put the zeroth plaquette at the origin and plaquette \(j\) at \(z_j \in \mathbb{Z}^4\) for \(1 \leqslant j \leqslant t\). Use the coefficient colour tensor \(R_{\mathbf{a}, \cdot} (z)\) for this atom. It is invariant by the preceding construction. In the path convention of Subsection 11.21, the atom's length is exactly

\(\displaystyle |z|_1 + 4 (t + 1) .\)

The four edges of every plaquette, including the anchored one, contribute to this length. Summing the weighted absolute coefficients over the finite face and colour index sets and over all displacements gives total atom budget at most

\(\displaystyle CA \hspace{0.17em} \# \{ \text{face tuples} \} \hspace{0.17em} \# \{ \text{colour tuples} \} \hspace{0.17em} e^{4 \eta (t + 1)} .\)

An enumeration of this countable family changes neither its sum nor its budget. In the two-factor case, filtering the enumeration by a fixed pair of face directions and a fixed colour tuple turns (11.9) into the required zero total loop coefficient and zero first moment of the relative displacement of the two legs. The latter displacement is \(z_1 - 0\).

The potential coefficient reconstructed from these atoms is exactly (11.7), with the two-factor version proved separately above. Absolute convergence permits periodization and evaluation on every finite torus. This result constructs the invariant coefficients from the specified analytic Ward data. The subsequent stationary action calculation must still supply those data, their vanishing orders, and the scalar normalization of the recorded Taylor terms.

11.5Weighted derivatives of the centered block frame

The penalty estimate in Subsection 11.11 requires uniformity as the block side increases. Here the averaging in the frame construction is essential. We prove the weighted derivative bound at the identity, retaining the same minimizing frame and predictor as in Subsection 8.8.

11.5.1A calculus on finite probability spaces

For a probability measure \(\nu\) on a finite set, define

\(\displaystyle \|f\|_{\nu, 0} = \max |f|, \qquad \|f\|_{\nu, 1} = \left( \sum \nu |f|^2 \right)^{1 / 2}, \qquad \|f\|_{\nu, 2} = \sum \nu |f| .\)

An input direction has charge zero, one or two. Fix, for each charge \(b \in \{0, 1, 2\}\), a set \(\mathcal{A}_b\) of input directions, called the admissible directions of charge \(b\); the calculus below is valid for any such choice. A family of smooth maps \(f_i\), indexed by the points \(i\) of the probability space, has derivative bound \(C\) through order \(n\) if, for every \(1 \leqslant k \leqslant n\) and all directions \(u_1 \in \mathcal{A}_{b_1}, \ldots, u_k \in \mathcal{A}_{b_k}\) with \(\sum b_j \leqslant 2\), the function \(i \mapsto D^k f_i (0) [u_1, \ldots, u_k]\) has norm at most \(C\) in the output norm of charge \(\sum b_j\). All derivatives are taken at the origin. Zeroth derivatives are specified separately. In the application of this section, let \(L\) be the blocking side and put \(\mu = L^{- 1}\). A direction consists of fine-link fields on a padded patch together with finitely many coarse and gauge coordinates. It is admissible of charge \(b\), with constant \(C\), if each fine-link field has norm at most \(\mu C\) in the norm \(\| \cdot \|_{\nu, b}\) of normalized counting measure on the patch and each coarse and gauge coordinate has norm at most \(C\). These are the bounds verified at the end of this section. Products add charges. The only nontrivial product rule for these norms is \(L^2 \cdot L^2 \subset L^1\), which is Cauchy–Schwarz. Averaging over one variable contracts each norm by the triangle inequality or Jensen's inequality. If \(\nu' \leqslant H \nu\) with \(H \geqslant 1\), changing the measure costs at most \(H\) in all three norms.

These observations give quantitative product and composition rules. For a fixed smooth finite-dimensional map \(F\) at \(z\), set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{C}_{n, F, z} (C) = \sum_{k = 1}^n \sum_{\pi \in \mathcal{P}_k} \|D^{| \pi |} F (z)\| C^{| \pi |},\)

where \(\mathcal{P}_k\) is the set of partitions of \(\{1, \ldots, k\}\). If \(f (0) = z\) and \(f\) has derivative bound \(C\) through order \(n\), the partition chain rule and the preceding product inequalities bound \(F \circ f\) by \(\mathcal{C}_{n, F, z} (C)\). Each differentiated direction appears once, so the sum of charges is unchanged. If \(DF (z) = 0\), the same chain rule bounds the derivative of order \(n + 1\) using derivatives of \(f\) only through order \(n\): the excluded single-block partition is the only one that would contain \(D^{n + 1} f\).

11.5.2Long paths with small link derivatives

Suppose each link matrix equals \(1\) at the origin and has derivative bound \(\mu C\) through a fixed target order \(N\), where \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\). A path contains at most \(K / \mu\) links, with \(K\) fixed. Its ordered product has a bound depending on \(K, C, N\) only. The following induction proves this without an exponential loss in the number of links.

For \(0 \leqslant n < N\), assume all partial products have derivatives through order \(n\) bounded by \(Q_n\), independently of path length. Write a new factor as \(1 + v\) and a partial product as \(1 + u\), where \(u (0) = v (0) = 0\). In the derivative of order \(n + 1\) of \(uv\), both factors have positive derivative order. Thus derivatives of \(u\) only through order \(n\) occur, while every term has a factor \(\mu\) from a derivative of \(v\). The finite Leibniz sum is bounded by \(\mu R_n (Q_n, C)\), for a polynomial \(R_n\) depending only on \(n\) and the matrix multiplication norm. The new factor alone contributes at most \(\mu C\). Starting with the empty product and adding at most \(K / \mu\) factors bounds the derivative of order \(n + 1\) by \(K (C + R_n (Q_n, C))\). Taking its sum with \(Q_n\) defines a common bound \(Q_{n + 1}\). The induction begins at order zero, where every partial product has value \(1\).

Apply this on the enlarged block \(\mathcal{B}\) of radius \((J + 3) L\). For pairs of endpoints, a coordinate path has four straight pieces, each of length at most \(2 (J + 3) L\). At a fixed position along one piece, the link read is a translate of the coordinatewise splice of the two endpoints. Swapping the corresponding endpoint coordinates is a bijection of \(\mathcal{B} \times \mathcal{B}\) and preserves its uniform measure. A translate restricted to reads inside \(\mathcal{B}\) is injective; reads outside the path contribute the constant identity. Splitting positive and negative directions costs at most two; inverse link matrices are adjoints and have the same derivative norms. Consequently the preceding link estimate applies on the pair space, with a constant independent of \(L\). Multiplying the four pieces and averaging the finitely many coordinate orders gives uniform derivative bounds for \(E_{xy}\), defined as the average of the adjoint (equivalently, inverse) of the transport along \(x \to y\), with \(E_{xy} (0) = 1\).

11.5.3Eliminating the center of the frame

Let \(F_x\) be the matrix of the minimizing frame after recentering by the nearest group element to its weighted mean. Let \(\nu\) be the uniform measure on \(\mathcal{B}\), and \(\omega\) the actual smoothing probability used for this recentering. The latter is the distribution of the sum of \(J\) independent points, each uniform on the centred box \(\{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4 : |z|_{\infty} \leqslant (L - 1) / 2\}\) of side \(L\); the block side \(L\) is odd (Subsection 19.2), so this box is a complete residue system modulo \(L\). The reduction of the sum modulo \(L\) is therefore uniform: fix all summands except the first, and let the first run over its complete residue system. Every residue class thus has \(\omega\)-probability \(L^{- 4}\), and in particular \(\omega (x) \leqslant L^{- 4}\). Moreover \(\omega\) is supported in the box of radius \(J (L - 1) / 2 < (J + 3) L\), hence in \(\mathcal{B}\), where \(\nu = |\mathcal{B}|^{- 1}\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \omega (x) \leqslant L^{- 4}, \qquad \omega (x) \leqslant H_J \nu (x),\)

where \(H_J\) is a fixed upper bound for \(|\mathcal{B}| / L^4\). This proves the measure comparison needed below with no assumption that \(\omega\) itself is uniform on \(\mathcal{B}\).

Let \(\mathcal{F}\) denote the matrix-valued nearest-group map near \(1\), let \(\Pi = D\mathcal{F} (1)\) be the orthogonal projection onto the Lie algebra, and put \(R (z) =\mathcal{F} (z) - \Pi z\). Then \(DR (1) = 0\). Define

\(\displaystyle J_0 = \sum_y \nu (y) F_y, \qquad B_x = \sum_y \nu (y) F_y (E_{xy} - 1), \qquad T_x = J_0 + B_x .\)

The frame equation and its normalization are

\(\displaystyle F_x =\mathcal{F} (T_x), \qquad \sum_x \omega (x) \Pi F_x = \Pi 1.\) (11.10)

Indeed, minimization at each frame coordinate says that it is the nearest group element to the average of the transported other frame coordinates. At the identity this average is \(1\), so the smooth nearest map applies in a neighborhood. Common left multiplication by the recentering group element preserves the minimizing equation. The nearest-point normal equation for that recentering element gives the second equality in (11.10). Existence and smoothness near the identity are those of the selected minimizer already constructed; only its uniform derivatives are at issue here.

Put \(S_x = \Pi B_x + R (T_x)\). Since \(\Pi^2 = \Pi\), the two equations imply the exact identity

\(\displaystyle F_x = \Pi 1 - \sum_y \omega (y) \Pi S_y + S_x .\) (11.11)

To check it, first write \(F_x = \Pi J_0 + S_x\). Apply \(\Pi\), average with \(\omega\), and solve for the constant \(\Pi J_0\) using the normalization. This is the step that removes the derivative of the unknown center.

We now induct on derivative order. Suppose derivatives of \(F\) through order \(n\) have uniform bound \(A_n\). The transport \(E\) has derivatives through order \(n + 1\) bounded by a fixed \(D_{n + 1}\). In differentiating \(F_y (E_{xy} - 1)\) to order \(n + 1\), the term with \(D^{n + 1} F_y\) vanishes because \(E_{xy} (0) - 1 = 0\). Every remaining term is bounded by the product rules, and averaging in \(y\) gives a bound for \(B_x\) through order \(n + 1\). It follows that \(T_x\) is bounded through order \(n\). Since \(DR (1) = 0\), the composition rule above bounds \(R (T_x)\) through order \(n + 1\) using only those derivatives. Thus \(S_x\) has a bound \(A'_n\), depending only on \(A_n, D_{n + 1}, G, n\). Equation (11.11) now bounds \(F_x\) through order \(n + 1\) by \((1 + H_J) A'_n\). The induction starts at order zero, with \(F_x (0) = 1\). Every constant depends on \(G, J, n\) and the incoming link bound, but not on \(L\). In particular no inverse of a rooted-frame counting-norm Hessian has been inserted into this argument.

11.5.4The predictor and penalty

The connector for a coarse direction consists of the centered frame at one endpoint, a straight path of length \(L\), and the inverse centered frame at the translated endpoint. Use a padded box of radius \((J + 5) L\) for the link fields. Translation from either endpoint patch into this box is injective, and the ratio of the two normalized counting measures is bounded in terms of \(J\) only. The long-path argument and the frame induction therefore give uniform derivative bounds for the connector. Changing its average to the smoothing probability costs \(H_J\). Composing the resulting matrix average, whose value is \(1\), with \(\mathcal{F}\) bounds the actual predictor through order \(n\). The strict local predictor test identifies this expression with every selected tag near the identity, as in Subsection 10.1.

Next apply the fixed group exponential, multiplication, adjoint and nearest-group maps. The fine-link directions have norm at most \(\mu C\) on the padded box, while the finitely many coarse and gauge coordinates have norm at most \(C\). Composition with an exponential preserves a factor \(\mu\) in every positive fine-link derivative: each chain-rule term contains \(\mu^k\), \(k \geqslant 1\), and \(\mu^k \leqslant \mu\). Thus these are precisely the link bounds used above. The penalty in one direction is the fixed smooth function

\(\displaystyle Z \longmapsto \frac{k}{2 c_G^2} \|Z - 1\|^2\)

of the coarse group element times the inverse predictor; \(k\) is the penalty coefficient and \(c_G\) the fixed color normalization. The finite composition rule now gives its derivative bound \(B_n\). All constants were selected before \(L\). Summing the four directions and complexifying gives (11.30).

11.5.5Verification for the Fourier legs

We finally check the weighted stencil hypotheses just used. Write \(k_l (p) = (p + 2 \pi l) / L\), and let \(P (p)\) be the normalized block Fourier matrix, whose entries have magnitude \(L^{- 2}\) at real \(p\). The weighted norm of a fine block vector \(u\) is

\(\displaystyle \|u\|_p^2 = \sum_l h_l (p)^2 | (P (p)^{- 1} u)_l |^2, \qquad h_l (p) = \left( |q (\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k_l (p)) |^2 + \mu^2 \right)^{1 / 2} .\)

Here \(q_a (k) = e^{ik_a} - 1\); color components are included in the sum. On the strip of width one, \(P (p)\) and its inverse have Euclidean operator norms at most \(e^4\): factor them into the unitary real Fourier matrix and diagonal imaginary phases. Since \(h_l \geqslant \mu\), a unit weighted vector satisfies \(\|u\|_{\ell^2} \leqslant e^4 \mu^{- 1}\). Dividing by \(L^2\) gives its normalized block \(L^2\) bound \(e^4 \mu\). Also

\(\displaystyle |q_a (k_l (p)) | \leqslant 5 h_l (p) .\)

For this last inequality, split the symbol into its real-momentum value and its change in the imaginary direction. Put \(x = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k_l (p)\) and \(w = k_l (p) - x = i \text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} p / L\). On the strip, \(|w_a | \leqslant \mu \leqslant 1\) for every coordinate \(a\), since \(\mu = L^{- 1}\). Because \(x\) is real,

\(\displaystyle q_a (k_l (p)) - q_a (x) = e^{ix_a} (e^{iw_a} - 1), \qquad |e^z - 1| \leqslant \sum_{n \geqslant 1} \frac{|z|^n}{n!} \leqslant (e - 1) |z| \leqslant 2 |z| \quad (|z| \leqslant 1) .\)

Hence each coordinate changes by at most \(2 \mu\), and the Euclidean norm on \(\mathbb{C}^4\) by at most \(4 \mu \leqslant 4 h_l (p)\). The real-momentum value is at most \(h_l (p)\), which gives the factor \(5\). Fourier multiplication therefore bounds one fine difference in unnormalized \(\ell^2\) by \(5 e^4\), and in normalized \(L^2\) by \(5 e^4 \mu^2\). These are exactly the one-internal-leg bounds used in Subsection 11.11. The auxiliary scalar part of the internal norm is ordinary Euclidean, so its coordinates are bounded by one. Smooth bridge coordinate legs satisfy the required supremum bounds by (11.29), with a fixed color-dimension factor when components are combined into a vector.

To extend a block Fourier vector to the padded stencil, write \(z = \bar{z} + Lr (z)\), where \(\bar{z}\) is the chosen residue representative. Throughout the padded box, \(|r (z) |_1 \leqslant 4 + 4 (J + 5)\). The extension is \(e^{ip \cdot r (z)} u (\bar{z})\), whose phase is bounded by \(\exp (4 + 4 (J + 5))\). The map \(z \mapsto (r (z), \bar{z})\) is injective, and \(r (z)\) ranges over a fixed finite set depending only on \(J\). Comparing normalized counting measures therefore costs another constant depending only on \(J\). The fine field retains its factor \(\mu\) in the appropriate supremum or normalized \(L^2\) norm. Coarse stencil shifts are at most one step and cost at most \(e\); taking real or imaginary parts only decreases the coordinate norms. This verifies every fine, coarse and gauge stencil bound used to obtain \(B_n\) and closes the weighted penalty and internal Fourier inputs of the preceding section. The quantitative smooth bridge bound (11.29) is the remaining independent input.

11.6Uniform inverse bounds for the stationary matrices

We use the block Fourier norm of Subsection 11.5. Its weight at alias \(l\) is \(h_l (p) = (|q (\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k_l (p)) |^2 + \mu^2)^{1 / 2}\). The internal space is the direct sum of the fine one-form space in this norm and the coarse scalar space in its Euclidean norm. We denote it by \(W_p\). The dual always pairs opposite momenta. The weights are not holomorphic functions of \(p\), so a complex inverse estimate requires more than a direct application of Cauchy's inequality to a weighted matrix.

11.6.1Continuing a real lower bound to a complex strip

Use the maximum norm on the four momentum coordinates throughout. Let \(X_p, Y_p\) be two weighted norms, represented by invertible matrices \(S_X (p), S_Y (p)\): \(\|u\|_{X_p} = |S_X (p)^{- 1} u|_2\). Suppose that, at each real momentum \(x\), these matrices admit holomorphic replacements on the ball \(|z - x| < \sigma\). The replacements agree with the actual matrices on the vertical slice \(\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} z = x\) and compare to the actual weighted norms in both directions by a fixed factor \(H\), and first restrict \(0 < \sigma \leqslant 1\). Let \(K\) be periodic and holomorphic, with weighted operator bound \(A\) on the strip of width \(\sigma\), and suppose its weighted real matrix has least singular value at least \(a > 0\). Then there are \(\eta > 0\) and \(F < \infty\), depending on \(\sigma, H, A, a\) only, such that \(K^{- 1} : X_p \to Y_p\) has norm at most \(F\) for \(p\) in the strip of width \(\eta\).

Here is a quantitative proof. At a real anchor \(x\), transform \(K (z)\) using the holomorphic replacement matrices. The resulting matrix \(Q_x (z)\) is holomorphic and has Euclidean operator norm at most \(H^2 A\) on the ball of radius \(\sigma\). Cauchy's estimate on complex lines, tested against unit vectors on both sides, gives

\(\displaystyle \|Q_x (p) - Q_x (x)\| \leqslant k |p - x|, \qquad k = \frac{H^2 A}{\sigma / 2}, \quad |p - x| < \sigma / 2.\)

Choose \(\eta = \min \{\sigma / 2, a / (2 (1 + k))\}\). For \(x = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} p\), the actual and replacement norms coincide, and

\(\displaystyle |Q_x (p) v| \geqslant (a - k|p - x|) |v| \geqslant (a / 2) |v| .\)

Thus \(Q_x (p)\) is invertible and its inverse has norm at most \(2 / a\). The same is true of the weighted inverse of \(K (p)\). Holomorphy of the inverse follows from the adjugate formula; periodicity follows from uniqueness of the inverse. No dimension enters this argument.

For the block Fourier weights the required replacement is explicit: freeze \(h_l\) at the real anchor \(x\), while retaining the holomorphic Fourier matrix \(P (z)\). The inequality

\(\displaystyle h_l (z) \leqslant 6 h_l (x), \qquad h_l (x) \leqslant 6 h_l (z) \quad (|z - x| \leqslant 1)\)

follows from \(h_l \geqslant \mu\), the triangle inequality and the Lipschitz bound \(|q (\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k_l (z)) - q (k_l (x)) | \leqslant 4 \mu |z - x|\). The Fourier matrices cancel in the comparison of the two norms; the remaining matrix is diagonal with these ratios. Hence \(H = 6\) works, uniformly in \(L\). Direct sums with the ordinary scalar norm and dual norms have the same property.

11.6.2The scalar and gauge-completed fine matrices

Put \(\omega (k) = \sum_a q_a (- k) q_a (k)\) and define

\(\displaystyle s_{\mu} (k) = \omega (k) + \mu^2, \quad d_{\mu} (k) = \frac{\mu^2}{s_{\mu} (k)}, \quad c_{\mu} (k) = \mu^2 d_{\mu} (k)^9, \quad \gamma_{\mu} (k) = \omega (k) + c_{\mu} (k) .\)

At real \(k\), \(\omega (k) = |q (k) |^2\). The sign convention in this display uses \(q_a (- k) q_a (k) = |q_a (k) |^2\) on the real slice. For \(x \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle x + \mu^2 \left( \frac{\mu^2}{x + \mu^2} \right)^9 \geqslant 2^{- 10} (x + \mu^2) .\) (11.12)

If \(x \geqslant \mu^2\), its first term alone suffices. If \(x < \mu^2\), the ratio is greater than \(1 / 2\), so the second term is at least \(2^{- 9} \mu^2 \geqslant 2^{- 10} (x + \mu^2)\). On the alias strip \(| \text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} k|_{\infty} < \mu / 128\), comparison with the real symbol gives \(|s_{\mu} (k) | \geqslant \tfrac{1}{2} (|q (\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k) |^2 + \mu^2)\). Indeed the change of \(q\) has norm at most \(4 \mu / 128\) and the change of its quadratic product is bounded by twice this times the real norm plus its square; \(2 ab \leqslant a^2 + b^2\) absorbs this into less than half the real denominator. In particular all displayed rational symbols are holomorphic there, \(|d_{\mu} | \leqslant 2\), and \(|c_{\mu} | \leqslant 2^9 \mu^2\).

Fix \(\varepsilon > 0\), \(\Theta \geqslant 0\) and a coarse width \(0 < \sigma \leqslant 1\). Let \(H (k)\) be the symbol of the fine two-form metric, holomorphic and bounded by \(\Theta\) on the fine strip \(| \text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} k|_{\infty} < \mu \sigma\), which contains the aliases \(k_l (p)\) of the coarse strip of width \(\sigma\). Write \(K_f = d_1 (- k)^T H (k) d_1 (k)\) and \(d_0 (k) v = q (k) \wedge v\), and suppose that the real kinetic form satisfies the all-period lower bound

\(\displaystyle \langle v, K_f (k) v \rangle \geqslant \varepsilon |d_1 (k) v|^2\)

at every momentum \(k\) of every finite-period Fourier grid, with the same \(\varepsilon\) for all periods. In the application this is a consequence of the floor condition of Subsection 11.3, which gives \(\langle w, H_m w \rangle \geqslant \varepsilon |w|^2\) for every real two-form \(w\) at every period \(m\). The positivity of \(\varepsilon\) is used twice: the weighted floor of the gauge-completed matrix \(D_{\mu}\) and the constant \(a_0\) of the quotient bound (11.13) below are positive only because \(\varepsilon > 0\). The scalar matrix \(s_{\mu} I\) and the scalar matrix \(\gamma_{\mu} I\) have weighted real floors \(1\) and \(2^{- 10}\). The gauge-completed one-form matrix

\(\displaystyle D_{\mu} (k) = K_f (k) + d_0 (k) d_0 (- k)^T + c_{\mu} (k) I\)

has weighted floor \(2^{- 10} \min \{\varepsilon, 1\}\). This follows from

\(\displaystyle |d_1 v|^2 + |d_0^{\ast} v|^2 = |q|^2 |v|^2\)

and (11.12). The exterior identity is obtained by expanding the wedge and contraction in the coordinate basis. The all-period kinetic bound applies at every real momentum by density of finite-torus frequencies and continuity.

For these scalar and fine symbols restrict further to \(\sigma \leqslant 1 / 128\). Their complex weighted upper bounds are also uniform. Each exterior matrix costs at most a fixed color-direction constant times \(|q|\), \(|q (k_l) | \leqslant 5 h_l\), and \(|c_{\mu} (k_l) | \leqslant 2^9 h_l^2\). Thus division by \(h_l\) at both ends leaves a fixed bound depending only on \(G, \Theta\). The block Fourier conjugations cancel between opposite momenta, leaving a unitary alias permutation and these diagonal weights. The preceding continuation argument gives inverse bounds for all three matrices on one coarse strip, before \(L\) and before any higher-order interaction budget are chosen.

11.6.3A real lower bound modulo gauge directions

Let \(Q (p)\) be the linear block mean on one-forms and \(J (p)\) its scalar counterpart, so \(Qd_0 = d_{0, c} J\). Set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}a = (- d_0 a, - Ja), \qquad N (u, r) = d_{0, c} r - Qu, \qquad \mathcal{G} (u, r) = \langle u, K_f u \rangle + \kappa |N (u, r) |^2,\)

where \(\kappa > 0\) and all operators are expressed in the same block Fourier coordinates. The curl and \(N\) annihilate \(\mathcal{R}a\), so \(\mathcal{G}\) is unchanged when a gauge vector is subtracted. Recall that \(\varepsilon > 0\) and \(\kappa > 0\). We claim that, for some \(a_0 > 0\) depending only on \(G, J, \kappa, \varepsilon\),

\(\displaystyle \text{for every } v \text{ there is } a \text{ such that } a_0 \|v -\mathcal{R}a\|_{W_p}^2 \leqslant \mathcal{G} (v) .\) (11.13)

Choose the real coarse momentum in \([- \pi, \pi]^4\). For every nonzero alias, \(|q (k_l) | \geqslant 2 \mu\). Subtract its longitudinal component by solving \(d_0 (k_l)^{\ast} (u_l + d_0 (k_l) a_l) = 0\); the scalar denominator is \(|q (k_l) |^2 > 0\). The principal alias is left available to eliminate the coarse scalar coordinate. In normalized Fourier coordinates the scalar block mean on that alias is \(\mu^2 \text{rollMean} (k_0)\), which is nonzero. No uniform bound on the eliminating gauge parameter is needed. Here \(\text{rollMean}\) is the product of the four normalized half-block geometric sums, raised to power \(J\). For \(|k_a | \leqslant \pi / L\), each sum has modulus at least \(1 / 16\); this follows from the geometric-sum formula and \(\sin t \geqslant 2 t / \pi\) for \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant \pi / 2\), with the value at zero understood by continuity. Thus \(| \text{rollMean} | \geqslant c_J = (1 / 16)^{4 J}\). We obtain a representative \((P (p) b, 0)\) with all nonprincipal \(b_l\) transverse.

Put \(A_h = \sum_{l \ne 0} h_l^2 |b_l |^2\) and \(B_0 = |QP (p) b|^2\). The kinetic bound and transversality give

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{G} (v) \geqslant (\varepsilon / 2) A_h + \kappa B_0 .\)

The principal part of \(QP (p)\) has smallest singular value at least \((c_J / 16) \mu\): the straight connector contributes a geometric sum of size at least \(L / 16\), and \(P\) contributes \(\mu^2\). The full block mean has weighted norm at most a constant \(C_J\). To verify this upper bound directly, each fixed-position read has coefficients bounded by \(C_J \mu^4\) on \(O_J (L^4)\) sites, hence Euclidean row norm at most \(C'_J \mu^2\); sum the \(L\) positions and use \(h_l \geqslant \mu\). The same argument gives the scalar averaging norm \(\|J\| \leqslant C'_J \mu^2\). Separating the principal and nonprincipal aliases and squaring the triangle inequality now gives

\(\displaystyle ((c_J / 16) \mu |b_0 |)^2 \leqslant 2 B_0 + 2 C_J^2 A_h .\)

Finally \(h_0^2 \leqslant ((2 \pi)^2 + 1) \mu^2\). It follows that \(\|(Pb, 0)\|_{W_p}^2 = A_h + h_0^2 |b_0 |^2\) is bounded by a fixed multiple of \((\varepsilon / 2) A_h + \kappa B_0\), proving (11.13). This proof includes \(p = 0\).

11.6.4Adding the gauge-fixing square

Let \(F\) be the actual linear gauge-fixing map, periodic and holomorphic on the chosen strip, and satisfying \(F (- p)^T = F (p)^{\ast}\) on real momenta. Its required identities and estimate are

\(\displaystyle F\mathcal{R}= \Gamma_{\mu}, \qquad |Fv| \leqslant D \|v\|_{W_p},\) (11.14)

where the bound holds throughout that complex strip, and \(\Gamma_{\mu}\) is the block scalar operator with symbol \(\gamma_{\mu}\). These are exact same-frame identities; they remain inputs to this subsection until the gauge-fixing construction is identified with these symbols. The bound on \(J\), the definition of \(\mathcal{R}\) and (11.12) give

\(\displaystyle \|\mathcal{R}a\|_{W_p} \leqslant C \hspace{0.17em} | \Gamma_{\mu} a| = C |F\mathcal{R}a|,\)

with \(C\) independent of \(L\). For the fine component this is the pointwise inequality \(h_l |q_l | \leqslant h_l^2 \leqslant 2^{10} \gamma_{\mu} (k_l)\); for the coarse component use \(\mu^2 \leqslant h_l^2\) and the scalar mean bound above. Given \(v\), take \(a\) from (11.13) and put \(b = \|v -\mathcal{R}a\|_{W_p}\), \(c = |Fv|\). Then

\(\displaystyle \|v\|_{W_p} \leqslant Cc + (CD + 1) b, \qquad a_0 b^2 + c^2 \leqslant \mathcal{G} (v) + |Fv|^2 .\)

Squaring the first inequality proves a uniform positive lower bound for the full quadratic form \(\mathcal{G}+ F^{\ast} F\); for example its inverse floor can be taken as \(\max \{2 C^2, 2 (CD + 1)^2 / a_0 \}\). On the complex strip its weighted upper bound is the sum of the kinetic bound, \(\kappa\) times the square of the bound for \(N\), and \(D^2\). The holomorphic opposite-momentum transpose replaces \(F^{\ast}\) off the real slice. The continuation lemma therefore gives a common inverse width and cost for this actual stationary fluctuation matrix.

Once the common matrix is identified on the Fourier grids of an unbounded family of tori, the identification extends to the entire real slice by density, and then to the strip by analytic uniqueness. Consequently any two stationary matrix presentations representing that same family inherit this one inverse estimate. This last statement does not identify arbitrary independently chosen matrices: the all-period representation and the exact identities (11.14) must refer to the same construction.

11.7The selected bridge and gauge-fixing map

The preceding estimates must be supplied on the actual retained horizon. We verify two identifications: the fine-difference bounds of its linear stationary bridge, and the exact gauge identity of its fluctuation matrix.

11.7.1The unchanged one-form transport

Let \(A_0\) be the one-form transport of the retained tower at a fixed adjacent pair of scales. The construction in Subsection 3.10 retains this row unchanged; only the curvature completion rows are replaced. Apply Theorem 3.21 with \(e = 1\). The schedule has \(j \geqslant 4\), so every difference word \(u\) of length at most two is covered. Its actual Laurent row satisfies

\(\displaystyle \sum_z e^{w |z|_1} | (\nabla^u A_0) (z) | \leqslant DL^{- 1 - |u|} .\)

The width \(w > 0\) and the cost \(D\) are those of the stage of this adjacent pair. They are common to every terminal cutoff, every level at that stage and every finite period, but, as stated at the end of Subsection 3.10, no common positive width or common cost over all stages is asserted. The hypotheses of the cited theorem for the retained rows, and the resulting difference bounds, are part of the horizon supplier of that section; here they are an input. The bound enlargement used in the retained construction can only increase \(D\) and narrow \(w\).

Define \(\Phi (p)\) by Fourier evaluation of \(A_0\), with the color identity on its color factor. Fourier evaluation commutes with a cover difference, including its centered carry: this is exactly the residue-difference identity in the proof of the cited theorem. Thus, for a coordinate leg,

\(\displaystyle | \nabla^u \Phi (p) e_a (z) | \leqslant DL^{- 1 - |u|} \qquad (p \in \text{strip} (w), |u| \leqslant 2) .\)

Absolute exponential summability gives holomorphy and periodicity on the strip. Restriction to a smaller width preserves every bound. This proves (11.29) for the selected bridge and supplies the identical hypothesis in the inherited-loop estimate.

We must also identify this row with the derivative of the stationary representative used below. Write \(W = A_0\) in finite-volume coordinates. In the original tree gauge chart the positive internal Hessian gives a unique local stationary branch. Its fine tangent is not \(W\) itself: straightening up the tree adds an infinitesimal gauge transformation, so the expected tangent is

\(\displaystyle E = W + d_0 K,\)

where \(K\) is the linear tree-straightening map: at a fine site, \(K\) integrates \(W\) along the tree path to the block root and subtracts the root value \(\text{fixRoot} \hspace{0.17em} W\). Existence and uniqueness of the branch do not by themselves show that its derivative is this particular matrix. The identification is the following input, which is the installed-tangent identity proved in Subsubsection 11.21.2.

Stationary tangent input. On each finite torus of the selected family, for the same forest, incoming path, fine and coarse beds and the same installed matrix \(W\) of the retained tower, the stationary branch \(\rho\) of the tree chart satisfies

\(\displaystyle D \rho (0) x = (Ex - \iota x) |_{\text{free}}\)

for every coarse direction \(x\). Here the tree chart records the fine field on the free (non-tree) links relative to the linear copy \(\iota x\) of the coarse field, which equals the coarse value on fine links joining two different blocks and zero on links inside a block.

This is the precise form of the statement that the fine tangent of the branch is \(E\). The cited section derives it by differentiating the vertical stationarity equation: by the finite-volume Hessian identity the bilinear form in that equation is the fine bed kinetic form plus the penalty pairing of the predictor residual, the candidate \(E\) solves the same linear equation by the two matching identities \(K_f W = Q^{\textsf{T}} K_c\) and \(QW = I - \kappa^{- 1} K_c\) of the retained tower for these two beds, and positivity of the vertical Hessian gives uniqueness. Its premises (the Hessian identity, the Hessian gap and the two matching identities for the same beds and the same \(W\)) are therefore premises of everything that follows. Gauge this branch back by the group exponential of \(Kx\). The derivative of the resulting fine field is \(E - d_0 K = W\). At a block root the straightening identity reads \(K_{\text{root}} = - \text{fixRoot} \hspace{0.17em} W\). The derivative of the rooted frame is \(- \text{fixRoot} \hspace{0.17em} W\) as well; multiplication by the inverse root gauge factor cancels it. Thus recentering the gauged-back branch gives the joint derivative \((W, 0)\). Gauge covariance preserves its stationarity. We use uniqueness only in the original tree chart: the centered merit on the full joint space has gauge null directions. Finite Fourier inversion now identifies this actual derivative on every torus grid with \(\Phi\). Hence the difference bounds above concern the selected stationary representative.

11.7.2The exact gauge identity

Work first on one finite torus. Let \(d\) be the infinitesimal fine gauge action at the identity, in the sign convention of the joint action, and let \(\mathcal{R}\) be its expression in centered block coordinates. Its two components are \((- d_0 a, - Ja)\) as in the preceding section. Let \(X\) be the negative right logarithmic derivative of the frame in these coordinates, and let \(C_{\mu}\) be the positive regulator with scalar symbol \(c_{\mu}\). The linear gauge-fixing map is

\(\displaystyle F = d^{\ast} \text{pr}_{\text{fine}} + C_{\mu} X.\)

For a gauge curve through the identity, frame covariance gives its frame ratio as the inverse gauge transformation. Applying the negative right logarithm therefore returns exactly the gauge parameter near zero. Differentiating this identity gives \(X\mathcal{R}= I\), after expressing the gauge tangent in the same coordinates. Also the fine component of the gauge tangent is \(d\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle F\mathcal{R}= d^{\ast} d + C_{\mu} = \Gamma_{\mu} .\) (11.15)

There is a coordinate issue in this calculation. The rooted gauge coordinates and the centered coordinates are related by a triangular change whose derivative is

\(\displaystyle (u, r) \longmapsto (u, r + K_0 u),\)

where \(K_0\) is the derivative of the recentering frame. The frame covariance identity gives \(K_0 d_0 a = a_{\text{root}} - Ja\). It follows by substitution that this triangular derivative sends the centered gauge tangent to the rooted gauge tangent. The fine projection is unchanged. The chain rule sends \(X\) to its product with that same triangular derivative, so both sides of (11.15) remain identical. This verifies the identity in the centered chart used for the estimates, including its sign; it would be incorrect to combine the rooted \(X\) with a centered \(\mathcal{R}\) without this change.

Fourier transformation yields the identity on every torus grid. The symbols are common to the unbounded family of periods, by the local stencil construction. They therefore agree at every real momentum by density and at every complex momentum in their common strip by analytic uniqueness. For real momentum, the Fourier symbol of each real finite-volume matrix satisfies \(F (- p) = \overline{F (p)}\). This applies to the frame linearization, the fine difference, and the real regulator, hence to \(F\) itself. Density of the common finite Fourier grids gives this identity at every real momentum, and therefore \(F (- p)^T = F (p)^{\ast}\) there. Thus the bilinear holomorphic continuation used below agrees with the positive Hermitian square on the real slice. The same argument identifies the constant stationary matrix with \(\mathcal{G}+ F (- p)^T F (p)\): the fine action contributes its attached kinetic form, the block penalty contributes \(\kappa N (- p)^T N (p)\), and the gauge-fixing term contributes the displayed square. Each is the Hessian of the corresponding term of the same action at its identity.

11.7.3The uniform bound for the gauge-fixing map

For a unit vector in \(W_p\), the fine derivative term \(d^{\ast} u\) is bounded in unnormalized Euclidean norm by a constant depending only on \(G\), by the Fourier multiplier estimate in Subsection 11.5. The frame derivative \(Xv\) has normalized block \(L^2\) norm at most a constant, by the centered-frame induction, restriction to one residue block, and the finite coordinate operations defining its logarithm. Restriction from the padded patch to this block costs a bounded ratio of volumes. Its unnormalized Euclidean norm is therefore at most \(CL^2\). On the fixed small coarse strip the regulator has Euclidean operator norm at most \(C' \mu^2\): its alias symbol is \(\mu^2 (\mu^2 / (\omega + \mu^2))^9\), with the denominator estimate of the preceding section, and the two Fourier conjugations have uniformly bounded norms. Hence

\(\displaystyle |C_{\mu} Xv| \leqslant C' C \mu^2 L^2 = C' C.\)

Adding the fine derivative term gives the required bound \(|Fv| \leqslant D\), with \(D\) fixed before \(L\). The same stencil construction is holomorphic and periodic in the external momentum, since it uses only linear Fourier legs in finite real derivatives at the identity and the holomorphic regulator symbol. This proves both parts of (11.14) for the selected data.

The inverse estimates of Subsection 11.6 now apply to the actual common stationary matrix. At the origin the two stationary matrix presentations used for the action and its comparison have this same matrix. Their inverse bounds consequently use the same width and cost; no bound for their positive-order derivatives is asserted by this constant-matrix identification.

11.8The kinetic response on the selected bridge

We supply the fine kinetic matrix bound used in Subsection 11.9, and identify its three derivative kernels on the selected finite periods. Let \(F_n\), \(0 \leqslant n \leqslant 2\), be the response arrays of Theorem 11.15, with width at most \(C \geqslant 0\) at the Laurent weight \(t_{\ast} > 0\) (the constants \(K\) and \(\eta\) of that theorem). Retain all the theorem's hypotheses on the incoming path and metric; in particular the response identity (11.4) is available on the tori of the selected period sequence. Let \(\Phi\) be the actual bridge of Subsection 11.7, with fine differences through order one bounded by \(D \mu^{1 + j}\). Fix \(0 < \sigma \leqslant 1\) in its strip and require \(\mu \sigma \leqslant t_{\ast}\). This last condition is imposed by increasing \(L\) after \(t_{\ast}\) and \(\sigma\) have been chosen.

11.8.1Contracting the response arrays

For external momenta \(p_1, \ldots, p_n\) and coarse coordinate legs \(a_1, \ldots, a_n\), put \(v_j = \Phi (p_j) e_{a_j}\). For an internal input momentum \(q\) the output momentum is \(q' = q + \sum_j p_j\). Define a matrix \(K_n (p_1, \ldots, p_n ; q)\) by its bilinear pairing with arbitrary internal vectors \(u, w\):

\(\displaystyle u^T K_n (p_1, \ldots, p_n ; q) w =\mathcal{B}_n (p_1, \ldots, p_n, - q', q) [v_1, \ldots, v_n, u, w] .\)

Here \(\mathcal{B}_n\) is the block-periodized multilinear form obtained by inserting the array \(F_n\) in (11.4) and summing the base site over one fine residue block. Translation of a residue across a block boundary carries the corresponding Bloch phase. This defines the pairing on coordinate vectors, hence defines the matrix uniquely. We only use momenta for which \(p_j, q, q'\) belong to the width-\(\sigma\) strip.

Every stitch uses words of length at most one, and its lengths obey

\(\displaystyle (n + 2) + \sum_{j = 1}^{n + 2} | \ell_j | = 4.\) (11.16)

This is the precise derivative count: two differences for the value, one for the first background derivative, and none for the second. Set \(R = D + 5 e^4\). In normalized counting measure on a residue block, the external leg with word \(\ell_j\) has supremum norm at most \(\mu^{1 + | \ell_j |} R\). A unit internal leg in the weighted Fourier norm has normalized \(L^2\) norm at most the same expression, by the multiplier estimate of Subsection 11.5. There are exactly two such internal legs. Hölder's inequality therefore bounds the normalized sum of their product and all external factors by

\(\displaystyle \prod_{j = 1}^{n + 2} \mu^{1 + | \ell_j |} R.\)

Restoring the unnormalized residue sum multiplies by \(L^4\). Equation (11.16) gives the cancellation

\(\displaystyle L^4 \prod_{j = 1}^{n + 2} \mu^{1 + | \ell_j |} R = R^{n + 2} .\)

Thus neither a count of fine sites nor a conversion of a full matrix to entrywise bounds introduces an extra power of \(L\).

We also check complex translation factors. For a shift \(z\) of a residue leg, its Bloch extension differs from the permuted residue vector by a phase with modulus at most \(\exp (\sigma (8 + \mu |z|_1))\). To see the bound, write the translated site as its residue plus \(L\) times its block quotient. The difference between the two residues has coordinate sum at most \(8 L\); the remaining displacement contributes \(|z|_1\). Multiplying the phases of the \(n + 2\) legs costs at most

\(\displaystyle e^{8 \sigma (n + 2)} e^{\mu \sigma |z|_1},\)

where now \(z\) is the whole array shift. The last factor is paid by its Laurent width. Summing shifts, color addresses and stitch addresses therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \|K_n \| \leqslant A_n, \qquad A_n = N_n e^{8 \sigma (n + 2)} R^{n + 2} C, \qquad 0 \leqslant n \leqslant 2,\) (11.17)

where \(N_n\) is the product of the numbers of color and stitch addresses at level \(n\). These numbers depend only on the fixed group and \(n\). The norm is the operator norm between the weighted fine space and its bilinear dual at the two indicated momenta. Taking \(A_K = A_0 + A_1 + A_2\) supplies the common bound required in the determinant comparison.

Absolute Laurent convergence, uniformly on every strictly smaller strip, also proves holomorphy of these matrices. Moving a momentum by a reciprocal lattice vector changes its residue phases and its block quotient phases by inverse factors, so the matrices are periodic. This verifies the common analytic extension directly from the arrays and the bridge.

11.8.2Identification with the actual Hessian derivatives

Let \(I_m\) be the incoming action on a fine torus, as a function of logarithmic link coordinates, and let \(\mathcal{D}= \textsf{D}_m\) be the split-field map of Subsection 11.3: on every link \(e\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{D} (x_+, x_-)_e = \log (\exp (x_{+, e}) \exp (x_{-, e})) .\)

Near zero, \(I_m (\mathcal{D}(x_+, x_-))\) is exactly the action at the background configuration \(\exp (x_-)\) after the left increment \(\exp (x_+)\). Its Hessian in \(x_+\) is therefore the fine kinetic Hessian in the left coordinates used for the stationary determinant. Differentiating it \(n\) times in the background \(x_- = Wx\), and testing with two arbitrary internal vectors, gives precisely the mixed response with \(n\) minus-species legs followed by two plus-species legs. Analyticity permits this reordering of derivatives. This is the species order in (11.4).

On a coarse Fourier wave, the actual installed matrix \(W\) produces the block wave with residue amplitude \(\Phi (p) e_a\), by the finite Fourier identity of Subsection 11.7. Insert these waves and the two internal block waves into (11.4). The sum over coarse base sites is zero unless the total coarse momentum vanishes; otherwise it is the coarse site cardinality. In the latter case the remaining residue and shift sum is exactly the defining pairing of \(K_n\) above. Fourier waves span the full finite-volume space, and testing against every outgoing wave determines a matrix. Thus \(K_0, K_1, K_2\) are the actual value, first derivative and second derivative kernels of the same kinetic Hessian on every selected torus, including nonzero momentum transfer. They are not only limiting coefficient pictures.

Finally the local bridge legs used by the frame and penalty estimates obey their required common pointwise bound: at a residue the color Euclidean norm is at most \(\sqrt{d_1} \hspace{0.17em} D \mu\), because there are \(d_1\) one-form color coordinates. Enlarging the local bound to \(\sqrt{d_1} D + e^4\) also covers the fixed coordinate legs. This bound is independent of \(L\). Consequently the local estimates, the kinetic triple and all finite-volume identifications in the determinant comparison refer to one and the same selected bridge and path.

11.9Uniform bounds for the determinant comparison

The absolute one-loop term counts all fine variables in a block and therefore has a bound proportional to \(L^4\). Its comparison with the gauge-completed fine determinant has a better bound. We prove that bound before extracting the quadratic coefficient. Throughout this section \(L\) is odd, \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), and all constants are chosen from the fixed group, patch size, bridge bound and penalty coefficient, before \(L\). The fine kinetic response is an additional input: on a positive common strip of width \(\sigma\), assume its value and first two background kernels satisfy \(\|K_0 \|, \|K_i \|, \|K_{ij} \| \leqslant A_K\) in the weighted fine one-form norms, at every admissible set of intermediate momenta. Subsection 11.8 constructs that response and its bound for the selected actions.

11.9.1Differentiated Fourier matrices

We need the value and the first two background derivatives of a matrix. An external leg of momentum \(p\) changes an input momentum \(q\) to \(q + p\). Write these three kernels as \(M_0 (q)\), \(M_i (p ; q)\) and \(M_{ij} (p, r ; q)\). All momenta that occur as intermediate inputs or outputs must lie in the specified strip. A bound on these three kernels always uses the weighted norms at their respective input and output momenta. It does not mean an entrywise bound. The holomorphic rear reverses the endpoints while retaining the external momenta:

\(\displaystyle (M^{\ast})_0 (q) = M_0 (- q)^T, \quad (M^{\ast})_i (p ; q) = M_i (p ; - q - p)^T, \quad (M^{\ast})_{ij} (p, r ; q) = M_{ij} (p, r ; - q - p - r)^T .\)

This convention is used in all products below.

Products obey the ordinary Leibniz rule with these intermediate momenta. Thus common bounds \(A, B\) for two triples give the bound \(4 AB\) for their product: there is one value term, two first-derivative terms, and four second-derivative terms. If \(M_0^{- 1}\) has bound \(F\), its derivative kernels follow by differentiating \(M^{- 1} M = 1\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} (M^{- 1})_i & = - M^{- 1} M_i M^{- 1},\\ (M^{- 1})_{ij} & = M^{- 1} M_i M^{- 1} M_j M^{- 1} + M^{- 1} M_j M^{- 1} M_i M^{- 1}\\ & \quad - M^{- 1} M_{ij} M^{- 1} . \end{array}\)

Intermediate momenta in these formulas are determined by composition. In particular a common inverse-jet bound is \(F + F^2 A + 2 F^3 A^2 + F^2 A\). These formulas use only the inverse at zero background; they impose no uniform neighborhood of invertibility in the full background space.

Here are the local inputs to this calculus. Let \(d (U)\) be the fine covariant difference at background \(U\). On each edge its difference from \(d (1)\) is the adjoint action of the edge variable minus the identity, followed by the fixed endpoint shift. The identity

\(\displaystyle d (U) = d (1) + (d (U) - d (1))\)

is therefore a fixed shift operator multiplied by local smooth functions of one fine link. Positive derivatives through order two of these functions have a factor \(\mu\): every differentiated fine link has size at most \(\mu C\), and products of more such factors are smaller since \(\mu \leqslant 1\). Passing from Euclidean norms to a single weighted fine norm costs at most \(e^4 \mu^{- 1}\), by Subsection 11.5. The factor \(\mu\) pays this cost. The constant term \(d (1)\) has a uniform weighted bound directly from its multiplier \(q\): on the real slice \(|q (k_l) | \leqslant h_l\), and throughout the complex strip of width at most one \(|q (k_l (p)) | \leqslant 5 h_l (p)\), by the multiplier estimate of Subsection 11.5. The weight \(h_l\) is defined with the real part of the momentum, so the inequality without the factor five does not extend to complex momenta. Consequently the first two background derivatives of \(d (U)\) are uniformly bounded in both placements needed for \(d^{\ast} d\) and \(dd^{\ast}\).

The derivative of the centered frame, denoted by \(X (U)\), has bound \(CL^2\) from the joint weighted space to the ordinary fine scalar space, through two background derivatives. Indeed these are at most third derivatives of the frame, with one internal direction of charge one and the remaining external directions of charge zero. The frame calculus bounds the normalized \(L^2\) output; removing that normalization multiplies by \(L^2\). The logarithm and the finite chart changes preserve this estimate. The block constraint derivative \(Q (U)\) and its coarse penalty form \(H (U)\) have uniformly bounded kernels. The actual penalty Hessian \(B (U)\) has the same property: its first two background derivatives require derivatives through order four, with two internal directions of charge one. Their total charge is two, exactly the \(L^1\) case of the weighted penalty estimate.

These estimates apply on one common smaller strip. The external directions are the actual stationary bridge of Subsection 11.7. The same finite stencils define the kernels on every torus. Thus the preceding bounds apply to their common holomorphic Fourier matrices, rather than to separately chosen extensions of finite-grid matrices.

11.9.2Why the regulator has a uniform trace bound

For a map between weighted Hilbert spaces, let \(\| \cdot \|_1\) denote the nuclear norm after transforming to orthonormal coordinates. We use

\(\displaystyle \|ABC\|_1 \leqslant \|A\| \|B\|_1 \|C\|, \qquad | \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} B| \leqslant \|B\|_1 .\)

The second inequality is applied to endomorphisms with the same input and output momentum. Both statements follow from a singular value decomposition; the first also follows by decomposing \(B\) as a sum of rank-one operators. They introduce no dimension factor.

Set

\(\displaystyle P (U) = \mu^2 (d (U)^{\ast} d (U) + \mu^2 I)^{- 1}, \qquad C (U) = \mu^2 P (U)^9 .\)

Here and below the star means transpose with opposite Fourier momentum in the holomorphic formulas. On the real slice it is the usual adjoint. The scalar inverse estimate of Subsection 11.6, the preceding product and inverse rules, and the two weight conversions show that all three kernels of \(P\) have a common Euclidean bound independent of \(L\). The factor \(\mu^2\) cancels the two costs \(O (\mu^{- 1})\). It follows that the three kernels of \(C\) have operator bound \(C_0 \mu^2\).

We need the stronger nuclear bound, for which the ninth power is useful. At zero background, \(P_0\) has alias eigenvalues \(d_{\mu} (k_l) = \mu^2 / (\omega (k_l) + \mu^2)\). On the strip of width \(1 / 128\), after choosing a real fundamental cube, the alias distance \(n = \text{dist}_{\infty} (l, 0)\) satisfies

\(\displaystyle |d_{\mu} (k_l) | \leqslant \frac{2}{(1 + n)^2} .\)

For \(n = 0\) this is the scalar denominator bound. For \(n > 0\), the real alias difference has size at least \(2 \mu n\); the same denominator comparison gives the displayed estimate. The number of residues with distance \(n\) is at most \((2 n + 1)^4\). Hence

\(\displaystyle \sum_l |d_{\mu} (k_l) |^3 \leqslant 8 \sum_{n \geqslant 0} \frac{(2 n + 1)^4}{(n + 1)^6} \leqslant 128 \sum_{n \geqslant 0} (n + 1)^{- 2} < \infty .\)

The fixed color multiplicity and the bounded Fourier conjugations only multiply this constant. Thus \((P^3)_0\) has a uniform nuclear bound, although \(P_0\) itself need not have one.

If a triple \(V\) has operator bound \(a\) and its value \(V_0\) has nuclear bound \(b\), the triple for \(V^3\) has nuclear bound \(20 a^2 b\). To see this, distribute at most two derivatives among three factors. Every term has an undifferentiated factor, which can be estimated in nuclear norm; all other factors use operator norm. The value, first derivative and second derivative have at most one, three and nine terms, respectively. The larger constant 20 also accommodates the grouped product estimates above. Apply this to \(V = P^3\). We obtain

\(\displaystyle \|C_0 \|_1, \|C_i \|_1, \|C_{ij} \|_1 \leqslant C_1 \mu^2 .\) (11.18)

This argument needs only the value-level spectral calculation for \(P^3\); derivatives of its eigenvectors are not used.

11.9.3Cancellation of the fine kinetic form

Let \(b\) project the joint space onto its fine one-form part, and let \(e\) project it onto its coarse scalar part. Let \(s_a\) be the fixed map selecting the one-form component in direction \(a\). Denote the fine kinetic Hessian triple by \(K\). Define

\(\displaystyle X = d^{\ast} b, \qquad T = CX_{\text{fr}}, \qquad F = X + T,\)

where \(X_{\text{fr}}\) is the centered frame derivative denoted by \(X (U)\) above. The change of notation separates it from \(d^{\ast} b\). The three matrix triples to be compared are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} M_{\text{act}} & = b^{\ast} Kb + B + F^{\ast} F,\\ M_{\text{core}} & = b^{\ast} Kb + Q^{\ast} HQ + F^{\ast} F,\\ M_{\text{rank}} & = b^{\ast} \left( K + dd^{\ast} + \sum_{a = 1}^4 s_a^{\ast} Cs_a \right) b + e^{\ast} e. \end{array}\)

The first uses the full penalty Hessian; the second retains its constraint pullback. Their distinction will matter in the next comparison of low-momentum coefficients.

The weighted norm bounds above, including the bound for \(K\), give a common operator bound \(A\) for all three triples and for \(d^{\ast} d + C\). Their zero-background inverses have one common bound \(F_0\) on a smaller strip of width \(\eta\), by the previous two sections. Indeed the zero-background penalty Hessian equals its constraint pullback, and the gauge-completed fine matrix is precisely the auxiliary matrix treated there. Take the minimum of the finitely many widths and the maximum of their costs.

The difference of the second and third triples is exactly

\(\displaystyle M_{\text{core}} - M_{\text{rank}} = Q^{\ast} HQ + T^{\ast} F + X^{\ast} T - b^{\ast} \sum_a s_a^{\ast} Cs_a b - e^{\ast} e.\) (11.19)

This is an identity at all three derivative levels. In particular the entire \(K\) triple cancels, not just its value at the origin. It follows by expanding \(F = X + T\) and using \(X^{\ast} X = b^{\ast} dd^{\ast} b\).

Every term on the right has a nuclear bound independent of \(L\). For \(Q^{\ast} HQ\), take the nuclear bound on the fixed-dimensional coarse space before multiplying by \(Q^{\ast}\) and \(Q\); the estimate \(\|H_j \|_1 \leqslant \dim (\text{coarse one-forms}) \|H_j \|\) works for each derivative. The same argument applies to \(e^{\ast} e\). For \(T = CX_{\text{fr}}\), (11.18) cancels the \(L^2\) frame cost because \(\mu^2 L^2 = 1\). Multiplication by \(F\) or \(X\) preserves its nuclear bound. Finally, converting the regulator term to weighted fine norms costs at most \(e^8 \mu^{- 2}\), paid by its factor \(\mu^2\); the four component maps \(s_a\) have fixed bounds. Leibniz's rule proves the same estimates through order two. Thus there is \(k\), independent of \(L\) and of the fine kinetic bound, with

\(\displaystyle \|(M_{\text{core}} - M_{\text{rank}})_j \|_1 \leqslant k, \qquad j = 0, 1, 2.\) (11.20)

The inverse bound \(F_0\) and absolute bound \(A\) may still depend on the stated kinetic hypotheses. That dependence has not been removed by this cancellation.

11.9.4The second logarithmic derivative

For any of the invertible triples define its two-leg trace density by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{T}_M (i, j ; p, q) = \text{tr} [M_0 (q)^{- 1} M_{ij} (- p, p ; q) - M_0 (q)^{- 1} M_i (- p ; q + p) M_0 (q + p)^{- 1} M_j (p ; q)] .\)

This is the second derivative of \(\log \det M\), obtained by differentiating \(D \log \det M = \text{tr} (M^{- 1} DM)\). It requires no choice of a global logarithm on the strip. The formula itself is holomorphic wherever the constant inverses exist. Restrict both independent momenta to width \(\eta / 2\), so every intermediate momentum lies in width \(\eta\).

If the fiber dimension is \(d\), the operator bounds give

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{T}_M | \leqslant d (F_0 A + F_0^2 A^2) .\) (11.21)

Here \(d \leqslant L^4 (d_0 + d_1)\), with \(d_0, d_1\) the fixed scalar and one-form color dimensions. This explains the absolute \(L^4\) cost.

For \(M = M_{\text{core}}\) and \(N = M_{\text{rank}}\) put

\(\displaystyle D = F_0 kF_0, \qquad E = DA + F_0 k.\)

The resolvent identity gives \(\|M_0^{- 1} - N_0^{- 1} \|_1 \leqslant D\). The elementary telescoping identity \(UV - U' V' = (U - U') V + U' (V - V')\) then bounds the difference of \(M_0^{- 1} M_{ij}\) and \(N_0^{- 1} N_{ij}\) by \(E\) in nuclear norm. For the four-factor term, first compare the initial two factors, at cost \(E\); compare the next inverse, at additional cost \(F_0 AD\); and finally compare the last derivative. Keeping the remaining factors in operator norm gives

\(\displaystyle \|M_0^{- 1} M_i M_0^{- 1} M_j - N_0^{- 1} N_i N_0^{- 1} N_j \|_1 \leqslant (EF_0 + F_0 AD) A + F_0 AF_0 k.\)

All these products respect the displayed intermediate momenta. Taking traces proves

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{T}_{M_{\text{core}}} -\mathcal{T}_{M_{\text{rank}}} | \leqslant E + (EF_0 + F_0 AD) A + F_0 AF_0 k.\) (11.22)

There is no dimension factor in this estimate.

The one-loop densities are \(\frac{1}{2} \mathcal{T}_{M_{\text{act}}} -\mathcal{T}_{d^{\ast} d + C}\), \(\frac{1}{2} \mathcal{T}_{M_{\text{core}}} -\mathcal{T}_{d^{\ast} d + C}\) and \(\frac{1}{2} \mathcal{T}_{M_{\text{rank}}} -\mathcal{T}_{d^{\ast} d + C}\). Their individual bounds may all be enlarged to twice the right-hand side of (11.21). In the difference of the latter two densities the scalar determinant cancels exactly, leaving half the difference in (11.22). This proves the required uniform comparison bound. Identification with the thermal stationary coefficient, and the disappearance of the remaining action-versus-core quadratic difference, are separate steps below.

11.10The thermal determinant and its contact term

We now relate the determinant matrices to the stationary integral and show that replacing the full penalty Hessian by its constraint pullback does not alter the leading quadratic coefficient. We retain the actual stationary branch, predictor and tree gauge from the preceding sections. The group has nonabelian simple real Lie algebra. The finite-volume stationary Hessian gap and the predictor branch hypotheses remain in force. We also retain the exact matching of the incoming and outgoing quadratic forms with the selected bridge; in particular its constraint map obeys \(QW = I - \kappa^{- 1} K_c\) for the selected outgoing kinetic matrix \(K_c\). Stationarity and the gap alone do not identify an independently chosen outgoing matrix. All identities below concern germs at the trivial field.

11.10.1The Haar factor in tree coordinates

Let \(y\) denote the free tree-gauge coordinates of a stationary point, and let \(j (y) > 0\) be the product, over these coordinates, of the Haar density ratio \(J\) of Subsection 8.18; it is analytic and positive near zero, with \(j (0) = 1\), and by (8.138) the probability Haar measure in exponential coordinates has density a positive constant times \(J\). Define the left-increment coordinate map, componentwise, by

\(\displaystyle s_y (z) = \log (\exp (z) \exp (y)^{- 1}), \qquad s_y (y) = 0.\)

It is a local diffeomorphism. Right invariance of probability Haar measure and the ordinary change-of-variables formula give

\(\displaystyle j (z) = | \det Ds_y (z) | j (s_y (z))\)

on the common coordinate neighborhood. For completeness, apply both change-of-variables formulas to any compact subset of that neighborhood. The two continuous positive densities have equal integrals over every such set. If they differed at a point, continuity would give a closed ball of positive Lebesgue measure on which one density is strictly larger, contradicting that equality. Evaluating at \(z = y\) yields

\(\displaystyle j (y) = | \det Ds_y (y) | .\) (11.23)

Thus the Haar density cannot be discarded when the stationary point moves away from zero.

Let \(H\) be the Hessian of the joint action in left coordinates at a stationary configuration, let \(R\) be its fine gauge-tangent matrix, and let \(P\) insert the free tree increments. Gauge invariance and stationarity give \(HR = 0\); \(H\) is symmetric. The square matrix \(S = [RP]\) has \(\det (S)^2 = 1\) in the physical counting bases. To verify this last assertion, order the fine vertices by tree height, assigning to a nonroot vertex its upward tree edge and to a root its coarse scalar coordinate. On the tree edges the gauge variation is the signed difference between the vertex and its parent, because those edge variables equal the identity. At a root it is minus the root gauge parameter. The resulting gauge block is triangular, with diagonal entries \(\pm 1\). The free-increment block is the identity, and the other upper block is zero. Hence the squared determinant is one, regardless of the free edge variables.

Let \(L\) be the gauge-fixing derivative and \(\Gamma = LR\) the positive scalar gauge matrix. Set \(C = P^T HP\) and \(B = LP\). Direct multiplication gives

\(\displaystyle S^T (H + L^T L) S = \left( \begin{array}{cc} \Gamma & B\\ 0 & I \end{array} \right)^{\hspace{-0.17em} T} \left( \begin{array}{cc} I & 0\\ 0 & C \end{array} \right) \left( \begin{array}{cc} \Gamma & B\\ 0 & I \end{array} \right) .\)

Consequently

\(\displaystyle \det (H + L^T L) = \det (\Gamma)^2 \det C.\) (11.24)

The tree-chart Hessian \(H_{\text{tree}}\) is \(Ds_y (y)^T CDs_y (y)\): the additional second-derivative term in the chain rule vanishes by stationarity. Combining this with (11.23) proves

\(\displaystyle j (y)^2 \det M_{\text{act}} = \det H_{\text{tree}} \det \Gamma^2 .\)

The tree gap, positive Haar density, and positive scalar determinant show that every determinant inside the logarithms is positive near the origin. Thus

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{2} \log \det H_{\text{tree}} - \log j (y) = \frac{1}{2} \log \det M_{\text{act}} - \log \det \Gamma .\) (11.25)

Adding the inherited loop gives the thermal term of the stationary Gaussian integral with exactly its original normalization.

11.10.2Replacing the stationary graph by its linear tangent

Write \(\zeta (x)\) for the actual centered stationary representative. Subsection 11.7 gives \(\zeta (0) = 0\) and, under the stationary tangent input stated there, \(D \zeta (0) x = (Wx, 0)\). Each scalar logarithmic determinant under consideration is invariant under simultaneous constant color rotation in the coarse, fine and scalar coordinates. Indeed these rotations act orthogonally; they conjugate the Hessian and the scalar gauge matrix. The centered-frame change is equivariant because group multiplication, the nearest-group map and the logarithm are equivariant under conjugation.

An invariant linear functional on one copy of the Lie algebra is zero. Its derivative along \(\text{Ad} (\exp ta) b\) annihilates every bracket \([a, b]\). The derived algebra is a nonzero ideal and therefore is the whole algebra by nonabelian simplicity. Applying this argument to each coordinate proves that the first derivative of each invariant scalar function vanishes at zero. The second-derivative chain rule therefore gives

\(\displaystyle D^2 (f (x, \zeta (x)))_{x = 0} [u, v] = D^2 f (0) [(u, Wu, 0), (v, Wv, 0)] .\)

This is why the linear external legs used in the Fourier matrix calculus compute the thermal Hessian of the actual nonlinear branch. There is no assertion that the nonlinear branches themselves coincide.

Rooted and centered internal increments are related by a triangular coordinate change with determinant one. On shell the full Hessians transform by congruence; the constraint-pullback Hessian does so even off shell. Hence this change preserves their determinants in the calculations below. Gauge changes preserve the same determinants by orthogonal congruence. These observations justify working in rooted coordinates for the contact calculation and centered coordinates for the uniform estimates.

11.10.3The finite-volume contact identity

Let \(w_x (v)\) be the logarithmic change of the block predictor when an internal left increment \(v\) is applied at background \(x\); \(w_x (0) = 0\). Let \(b (x)\) be the gradient, with respect to this predictor increment, of the coarse penalty. Write \(A (x) = M_{\text{core}} (x)\) in rooted coordinates and define

\(\displaystyle S_x (a)_{ij} = \langle a, D_v^2 w_x (0) [e_i, e_j] \rangle, \qquad Z (x) = S_x (b (x)) .\)

The Hessian chain rule for the penalty says exactly

\(\displaystyle M_{\text{act}} (x) = A (x) + Z (x) .\)

It separates the pullback of the penalty Hessian from the gradient of the penalty paired with the second derivative of its constraint. At zero \(b (0) = 0\), and hence \(Z (0) = 0\).

Define a coarse vector \(Y (x)\) by

\(\displaystyle \langle a, Y (x) \rangle = \text{tr} (A (x)^{- 1} S_x (a)) .\)

It is analytic for this fixed finite volume. Gauge covariance follows directly from this formula: the internal matrices transform by the same congruence and the predictor output by the coarse adjoint action, so cyclicity of trace gives the coarse adjoint action on \(Y\). At zero each component of \(Y (0)\) is fixed by constant color rotations and is therefore zero. This also follows by differentiating the adjoint action: an invariant vector is central, and a nonabelian simple Lie algebra has zero center.

Let \(K_c\) be the coarse kinetic matrix and put \(J = - K_c\). The exact linear bridge identity is

\(\displaystyle QW = I - \kappa^{- 1} K_c .\)

The quadratic penalty at the identity is \(\frac{\kappa}{2} |x - z|^2\). Its gradient in \(z\) has derivative \(\kappa (z - x)\). Applying the bridge identity therefore gives

\(\displaystyle Db (0) = J.\) (11.26)

Along the stationary graph this remains the same derivative, because its tangent is \(W\). In particular \(DZ (0) u = S_0 (Ju)\).

The identity \(\det (A + Z) = \det A \det (1 + A^{- 1} Z)\) and \(Z (0) = 0\) give, through second order,

\(\displaystyle \log \det (A + Z) - \log \det A = \text{tr} (A^{- 1} Z) - \tfrac{1}{2} \text{tr} (A^{- 1} ZA^{- 1} Z) + O (|x|^3) .\)

Since \(\text{tr} (A^{- 1} Z) = \langle b, Y \rangle\) and \(b (0) = Y (0) = 0\), differentiation gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} D^2 (\log \det M_{\text{act}} - \log \det M_{\text{core}}) (0) [u, v] & = \langle Ju, DY (0) v \rangle + \langle Jv, DY (0) u \rangle\\ & \quad - \text{tr} (A (0)^{- 1} S_0 (Ju) A (0)^{- 1} S_0 (Jv)) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.27)} \end{array}\)

The first two terms include the derivative of the inverse matrix; it has not been dropped. The vanishing of \(Y (0)\) is what removes the term involving the second derivative of \(b\).

There is also a Ward identity

\(\displaystyle DY (0) d_c a = 0\) (11.28)

for every coarse scalar field \(a\). To prove it on the actual branch, gauge the trivial stationary configuration by \(\exp (ta)\) at coarse sites, then restore tree gauge. Gauge invariance preserves stationarity; uniqueness in the tree chart identifies the restored configuration with the selected stationary branch on this curve. The coarse field has tangent \(- d_c a\). Covariance of \(Y\) and \(Y (0) = 0\) show that \(Y\) vanishes along the curve. Differentiation gives (11.28). Replacing the branch by its linear tangent does not alter \(DY (0)\), by the first-derivative chain rule. This proves the identity including the zero modes of the coarse difference.

11.10.4Passing the identity to the common momentum picture

Fix \(L\) for this paragraph. The rooted frame and its change to centered coordinates are analytic finite stencils. The inverse of \(A_0\) is holomorphic on a fixed strip. These facts construct a common holomorphic matrix kernel for \(A (x)^{- 1} S_x (a)\) and for its square, with two kinds of external legs: a coarse background leg and a separate coarse test leg \(a\). Indeed express \(S_x (a)\) using the second derivative of the local predictor, conjugate the centered inverse by the triangular change, and multiply. Every factor has the same finite-volume Fourier representation; products and inverse derivatives preserve it. Only a finite bound for this fixed \(L\) is required here.

Take normalized traces per coarse site. Finite Fourier inversion identifies them with averages of the common kernels over the internal momentum grid. Holomorphy on a smaller strip bounds the Fourier coefficients exponentially, so these averages converge to the torus integral, uniformly for real external momentum. The same convergence holds when an external momentum varies along grid points approaching a prescribed real momentum: subtract the limiting integral at that grid point and use its continuity. Thus both the finite-volume contact identity and its Ward identity pass to all real momenta along any divergent family of coarse periods.

Denote the resulting mixed background/test trace kernel by \(M_{ij} (p)\), and the second test/test derivative kernel of the trace of the square by \(B_{ij} (p)\). Explicitly, at zero background its finite-volume bilinear value on test fields \(a, b\) is

\(\displaystyle 2 \text{tr} (A (0)^{- 1} S_0 (a) A (0)^{- 1} S_0 (b)) .\)

Thus \(B\) includes both ordered cross terms. They are analytic near zero. The Ward identity says

\(\displaystyle \sum_i (d_c (- p) a)_i M_{ij} (p) = 0.\)

Fix a component \(i = (\nu, \alpha)\) and take \(a\) in color \(\alpha\) and \(p = te_{\nu}\). Only that directional difference survives, giving \(q_{\nu} (- te_{\nu}) M_{ij} (te_{\nu}) = 0\). The first factor is nonzero for all sufficiently small nonzero real \(t\). Continuity gives \(M_{ij} (0) = 0\). Analyticity then implies \(M_{ij} (p) = O (|p|)\) along every ray, not just that coordinate ray.

For a fixed external component \(a\), put \(x_j (p) = (- K_c (p) e_a)_j\). Since \(K_c (p) = d_1 (- p)^T H_c (p) d_1 (p)\) with \(H_c\) analytic, \(x_j (p) = O (|p|^2)\). The infinite-volume version of (11.27), including the factor one half in the thermal determinant, has diagonal density

\(\displaystyle \frac{1}{2} \left[ W (p) + W (- p) - \frac{1}{2} V (p) \right], \qquad W (p) = \sum_j x_j (p) M_{aj} (p), \quad V (p) = \sum_{i, j} x_i (- p) x_j (p) B_{ij} (p) .\)

The factor \(1 / 2\) in front of \(V\) arises because the second derivative of the trace of the square contains the two equal cross terms. Now \(W\) has ray order at least three and \(V\) has ray order at least four. Their sum therefore has zero quadratic Taylor coefficient. Passing from a Hessian density to the normalized quadratic picture introduces one more factor \(1 / 2\) and preserves this vanishing.

We have proved that the actual and the core one-loop determinant terms,
