\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det M_{\text{act}} - \log \det \Gamma \qquad \text{and} \qquad \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det M_{\text{core}} - \log \det \Gamma,\)

have the same leading quadratic coefficient. By (11.25), the thermal term of the stationary integral is the sum of the first of these and the inherited loop evaluated on the stationary branch. The contact calculation says nothing about that inherited summand. It is treated in Subsection 11.18 (Proposition 11.30): for the same incoming path, the same beds and the same stationary bridge, and under the hypotheses stated there, the common coarse picture of the inherited loop has order four at zero, so its quadratic Taylor coefficient vanishes. With that input, the thermal term and the core determinant term have the same leading quadratic coefficient. This does not say that the inherited finite-volume Hessian vanishes; only its leading low-momentum coefficient does.

Combining the exact cancellation for the determinant terms with the uniform core-versus-rank bound from Subsection 11.9 bounds the remaining coefficient difference between the actual and the rank determinant terms by Cauchy's inequality on the common strip. The qualitative rooted-frame constants used in the vanishing argument do not enter that uniform comparison bound.

11.11Classical vertices after stationary elimination

We now construct the cubic and quartic coefficient pictures of the stationary action. Two estimates must be kept distinct. The fine action is controlled by its vanishing order in momentum; the internal contraction in the quartic term is controlled in the weighted fluctuation norm. A coordinatewise estimate for that contraction would introduce an unwanted factor equal to the number of fine variables in a block.

11.11.1The weighted vertex hypotheses

Write \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), where \(L\) is the block side. All momenta in this section are coarse momenta. Let \(\Phi (p)\) be the linear fine-field response to a coarse field. In block coordinates the full tangent vector is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{L}_p h = (h, \Phi (p) h, 0) .\)

The last component is the gauge coordinate. An internal vector is written \(\iota v = (0, v)\), with the internal space including the fine field and the auxiliary scalar variables. Let \(\| \cdot \|_p\) be its weighted fluctuation norm at coarse momentum \(p\). This is the norm defined in Subsection 11.5: writing a fine block vector \(u\) in the block Fourier basis with matrix \(P (p)\) and alias momenta \(k_l (p) = (p + 2 \pi l) / L\),

\(\displaystyle \|u\|_p^2 = \sum_l h_l (p)^2 | (P (p)^{- 1} u)_l |^2, \qquad h_l (p)^2 = |q (\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k_l (p)) |^2 + \mu^2,\)

and the auxiliary scalar component carries its Euclidean norm. The \(L\)-independent estimates below depend on this particular weight. The corresponding bilinear dual norm is

\(\displaystyle \|s\|_{p, \ast} = \sup_{\|v\|_{- p} \leqslant 1} |v^T s| .\)

The sign in this definition is dictated by momentum conservation.

Fix \(0 < \eta \leqslant 1\) and \(D \geqslant 0\). The quantitative bridge hypothesis is holomorphy and periodicity together with

\(\displaystyle \sup_z | \nabla^j \Phi (p) e_a (z) | \leqslant D \mu^{1 + j}, \qquad 0 \leqslant j \leqslant 2, \quad p \in \text{strip} (\eta) .\) (11.29)

Here \(e_a\) is a unit color-direction coordinate and \(\nabla^j\) is any ordered string of \(j\) fine differences, with the coarse Fourier phase included at a block crossing. Choose \(D_{\ast} = 1 + D + 5 e^4\). The same bound with \(D\) replaced by \(D_{\ast}\) applies to the fine component of \(\mathcal{L}_p e_a\). For one internal vector of norm at most one, the weighted discrete Fourier estimate supplies the analogous bounds with one unit of integrability charge, through difference order one. More explicitly, if \(H_b\) denotes the normalized block norm \(L^{2 / b}\) for \(b > 0\) and \(L^{\infty}\) for \(b = 0\), the required estimates are

\(\displaystyle H_0 (\nabla^j \mathcal{L}_p e_a) \leqslant D_{\ast} \mu^{1 + j}, \qquad H_1 (\nabla^j \iota v) \leqslant D_{\ast} \mu^{1 + j} \quad (j \leqslant 1) .\)

Only the fine-field component occurs in these two inequalities. The weighted Fourier estimate and the block penalty estimate below are explicit inputs here; an ordinary bound for chart derivatives at fixed \(L\) would not imply either uniform estimate.

An \(n\)-linear vertex, a function of \(n\) independent momenta in the strip of width \(\eta\), has bound \(C_n\) if its absolute value is at most \(C_n\) on smooth coordinate legs \(\mathcal{L}_{p_i} e_{a_i}\), and also with the first leg replaced by \(\iota v\), \(\|v\|_{p_1} \leqslant 1\) for the first momentum \(p_1\), and all other legs smooth. No relation between the \(n\) momenta is imposed in this definition. These are unnormalized derivatives. Their coefficient pictures therefore carry a factor \(1 / n\)!.

For the block penalty we use the following precise receipt. For \(n = 3, 4\), its real \(n\)th derivative in each of the four coarse directions is bounded by \(B_n\), on the stencil fields induced by these legs, either all smooth or with one internal leg. The constants \(B_n\) depend on the fixed group, patch enlargement, penalty coefficient and \(D\), but not on \(L\) or the incoming interaction budget. The stencil field bounds are to hold for both real and imaginary parts. Complex multilinearity then gives bound

\(\displaystyle N_n = 2^n \hspace{0.17em} 4 B_n .\) (11.30)

Indeed, expand each complex leg into its real and imaginary parts. There are \(2^n\) terms, each with a coefficient of modulus one; sum the four directional penalties. This deduction does not replace the required real weighted stencil estimate.

11.11.2Why the residual vertices gain a scale factor

The coefficient pictures proved in Subsection 11.3 split the fine classical action into its Wilson part and its residual part. At degree three the Wilson picture vanishes to order one and the residual picture to order two; at degree four their respective orders are zero and one. Lemma 11.13 writes each as a finite sum of products of one-link difference factors and analytic coefficient arrays. The factors may be assigned to legs other than the first. Thus the leg that will later be tested in the internal norm carries no differences. For the four cases, the number of legs \(n\) and the number of differences \(q\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c|cc|c} & n & q & n + q - 4\\ \hline \text{Wilson cubic} & 3 & 1 & 0\\ \text{Wilson quartic} & 4 & 0 & 0\\ \text{residual cubic} & 3 & 2 & 1\\ \text{residual quartic} & 4 & 1 & 1. \end{array}\)

To see the significance of the last column, consider one term with \(j_i\) differences on leg \(i\). Equation (11.29) contributes \(\prod_i \mu^{1 + j_i} = \mu^{n + q}\). Summation over one fine block contributes \(L^4 = \mu^{- 4}\). The result is \(\mu^{n + q - 4}\). This count remains valid with one internal leg: use its normalized \(L^2\) bound and the supremum bounds on the other legs, then Hölder on the normalized block measure. The displaced arguments and analytic coefficient sums are bounded by the absolute exponentially weighted coefficient sum furnished by the difference factorization. Fourier phases are bounded on the chosen strip; these factors and the finite color and difference labels are included in the constant. The first leg has no differences, so its weaker derivative allowance is sufficient. This proves both parts of the vertex bound.

The order of choices and the family to which the conclusion applies are as follows. Fix the gauge group, the kernel with its penalty coefficient, the phase \(J\) of the patch, a bed strip width \(w > 0\) and bound \(Q \geqslant 0\), a floor constant \(\varepsilon\), a bound \(Z \geqslant 0\) for the direct coefficient, the bridge constant \(D\), and the coarse width \(0 < \eta \leqslant 1\). All of this is fixed before the incoming path width \(\tau > 0\) and budget \(A \geqslant 0\). The Wilson arrays and the penalty bounds then give constants \(a, b \geqslant 0\) independent of \(\tau, A, L\). For each \(\tau, A\) the residual arrays give a fine width \(\sigma' > 0\) and constants \(\ell, r \geqslant 0\); these depend on the data just listed and on \(\tau, A\), but not on the path, the bed, the loop budget \(\Lambda\), the block side or the tori. The conclusion is asserted for every sequence of fine torus sides \(n_i \to \infty\), every clip \(p\) with direct coefficient at most \(Z\), every path \(R\) with the bounds \((\tau, A, \Lambda)\) in \(p\), and every bed \(B\) such that \(R\) is attached to \(B\), the metric row of \(B\) has strip bound \((w, Q)\), the floor \((\varepsilon, Q)\) holds for \(B, p\) at every torus side, and the sworn condition holds at each \(n_i\); and for every patch of phase \(J\) satisfying the branch compatibility of the kernel, every family of block steps \(S_i\) with that patch from coarse tori of sides \(m_i\) to the fine tori of sides \(n_i = Lm_i\), every choice of block representatives \(t_i\), and every bridge \(\Phi\) satisfying (11.29). The hypotheses on \(R, B, p\) are exactly those of Proposition 11.12, which supplies the residual arrays; the Wilson arrays need none of them.

Provided \(\mu \eta \leqslant \sigma'\), addition of the three contributions gives vertices \(T_3, T_4\), holomorphic on the strip of width \(\eta\) in each momentum and periodic, with bounds

\(\displaystyle C_3 = a + \mu \ell, \qquad C_4 = b + \mu r.\) (11.31)

These vertices represent the actual block action \(\mathcal{A}_i\) of the step \(S_i\) with representatives \(t_i\), clip \(p\) and path \(R\) (the fine action of \(R\) composed with the block chart, plus the block penalty) on every torus in the family just described: for every \(i\), the \(n\)th derivative of \(\mathcal{A}_i\) at the origin on block Fourier waves of coarse momenta \(p_1, \ldots, p_n\) is zero unless \(\sum_j p_j = 0\), and is otherwise the number of coarse sites times \(T_n\) at these momenta. To verify this, insert Fourier waves into each local translation sum. Summing the coarse anchor gives zero unless the total momentum is zero, and otherwise gives the coarse volume. The remaining fine-block sum is exactly the array just estimated. The germ identity between the block action and the sum of the fine action and block penalty identifies these derivatives for the actual tags. No change of stationary branch has been made.

11.11.3The stationary contraction formula

Let \(\mathcal{A} (x, y)\) be this real analytic block action on one torus, with coarse variable \(x\) and internal variable \(y\), and let \(y (x)\) be real analytic near \(0\) with \(D_y \mathcal{A} (x, y (x)) = 0\) and \(y (0) = 0\). Put

\(\displaystyle H = D_y^2 \mathcal{A} (0, 0), \qquad E (x) =\mathcal{A} (x, y (x)), \qquad \mathcal{L}h = (h, Dy (0) h) .\)

The internal variable contains the gauge directions, and \(\mathcal{A}\) is gauge invariant. Hence the symmetric matrix \(H\) is not invertible: it annihilates the infinitesimal gauge directions. The matrix inverted in the construction is its gauge completion. The facts used are the following, in the notation of Subsection 11.7: \(\mathcal{R}\) is the matrix of infinitesimal gauge directions, \(F\) the linear gauge-fixing map with regulator \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), and

\(\displaystyle H^{\textsf{T}} = H, \qquad H\mathcal{R}= 0, \qquad F\mathcal{R}= \Gamma_{\mu} \text{invertible}, \qquad M = H + F^{\textsf{T}} F \text{invertible} .\) (11.32)

The second property follows from gauge invariance of \(\mathcal{A}\) and stationarity at \((0, 0)\): differentiating \(\mathcal{A} (0, \varphi_t (y)) =\mathcal{A} (0, y)\) along a one-parameter gauge flow \(\varphi_t\) once in \(t\) and once in \(y\) at \(t = 0\), \(y = 0\) leaves \(D_y^2 \mathcal{A} (0, 0) [\dot{\varphi}_0 (0), \hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em}] = 0\), because the other term contains \(D_y \mathcal{A} (0, 0) = 0\). The third is (11.15), where \(\Gamma_{\mu} = d^{\ast} d + C_{\mu}\) is positive definite for \(\mu > 0\). The last follows from the determinant factorization (11.24) (written there as \(\det (H + L^{\textsf{T}} L) = \det (\Gamma)^2 \det C\)), the positive stationary Hessian gap and \(\mu > 0\). All four are properties of the selected step on its finite torus; the argument of this subsection uses nothing else about \(F\) and \(\mathcal{R}\).

Differentiating stationarity once gives

\(\displaystyle D^2 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h, \iota v] = 0.\) (11.33)

Define the internal covector

\(\displaystyle s (h, k) [v] = D^3 \mathcal{A} (0) [\iota v, \mathcal{L}h, \mathcal{L}k] .\)

A second differentiation of stationarity gives, for \(u_{hk} = D^2 y (0) [h, k]\),

\(\displaystyle Hu_{hk} = - s (h, k) .\) (11.34)

Because \(H\) is degenerate this equation does not determine \(u_{hk}\), which depends on the gauge of the chosen branch. It does determine the contractions we need, by the identity

\(\displaystyle HM^{- 1} H = H.\) (11.35)

To prove (11.35), put \(N = M^{- 1} H\), so that \(MN = H\). Transposing \(H\mathcal{R}= 0\) gives \(\mathcal{R}^{\textsf{T}} H = 0\), hence \(\mathcal{R}^{\textsf{T}} M = (F\mathcal{R})^{\textsf{T}} F\) and

\(\displaystyle (F\mathcal{R})^{\textsf{T}} FN =\mathcal{R}^{\textsf{T}} MN =\mathcal{R}^{\textsf{T}} H = 0.\)

Since \(F\mathcal{R}\) is invertible, \(FN = 0\), and therefore \(HN = (M - F^{\textsf{T}} F) N = MN = H\). Consequently, by (11.34) and the symmetry of \(H\),

\(\displaystyle s (h, k)^{\textsf{T}} M^{- 1} s (l, m) = u_{hk}^{\textsf{T}} HM^{- 1} Hu_{lm} = u_{hk}^{\textsf{T}} Hu_{lm} .\) (11.36)

The resulting third and fourth derivatives are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} D^3 E (0) [h_1, h_2, h_3] & = D^3 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h_1, \mathcal{L}h_2, \mathcal{L}h_3], \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.37)} \\ D^4 E (0) [h_1, h_2, h_3, h_4] & = D^4 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h_1, \mathcal{L}h_2, \mathcal{L}h_3, \mathcal{L}h_4]\\ & \quad - \sum_{\{\{i, j\}, \{k, l\}\}} s (h_i, h_j)^T M^{- 1} s (h_k, h_l), \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.38)} \end{array}\)

where the sum runs over the three unordered pairings of four indices.

For completeness, these identities can be checked without suppressing any chain-rule terms. Along a ray, write \(y (th) = tDy (0) h + t^2 u / 2 + t^3 v / 6 + O (t^4)\). The terms involving \(D_y \mathcal{A} (0)\) vanish by stationarity. The mixed Hessian terms involving \(\mathcal{L}h\) vanish by (11.33). The cubic coefficient is therefore \(D^3 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h]^3 / 6\). The fourth-order coefficient is

\(\displaystyle \frac{D^4 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h]^4}{24} + \frac{s (h, h) [u]}{4} + \frac{u^T Hu}{8} = \frac{D^4 \mathcal{A} (0) [\mathcal{L}h]^4}{24} - \frac{s (h, h)^T M^{- 1} s (h, h)}{8} .\)

Here \(u = u_{hh}\), so that \(s (h, h) [u] = - u^T Hu\) by (11.34), and \(u^T Hu = s (h, h)^T M^{- 1} s (h, h)\) by (11.36). Polarization of these symmetric multilinear forms proves (11.37)–(11.38), including the coefficient and sign of all three pairings.

11.11.4Common analytic pictures and their bounds

Assume that the inverse \(M^{- 1}\) of the same completed matrix (11.32), on every torus of the family, has one common periodic holomorphic Fourier symbol \(V (p)\), with the weighted operator estimate

\(\displaystyle \|V (p) s\|_p \leqslant F \|s\|_{p, \ast}\) (11.39)

on the strip of width \(\eta\), where \(F \geqslant 0\) is fixed before the incoming width and budget. This is an input about the actual inverse, not an arbitrary comparison matrix. It is not proved in the present section: the inverse bound for the quadratic form \(\mathcal{G}+ F (- p)^{\textsf{T}} F (p)\) is the subject of Subsection 11.6, and the identification of that form with the Fourier symbol of \(M\) is the subject of Subsection 11.7.

With smooth legs at momenta \(p, q\), let

\(\displaystyle s_{ab} (p, q) [v] = T_3 (- (p + q), p, q) [\iota v, \mathcal{L}_p e_a, \mathcal{L}_q e_b] .\)

By the one-internal-leg estimate, \(\|s_{ab} (p, q)\|_{p + q, \ast} \leqslant C_3\). Consequently every contraction in (11.38) has modulus at most \(C_3 FC_3\). This is duality in the weighted norm; no sum of coordinatewise bounds and no dimension factor is required. The pairing uses opposite momenta on its two internal legs.

For \(n\) external legs, take the first momentum to be minus the sum of the other \(n - 1\). On the independent strip of width \(\eta / (2 n)\) all external momenta and all paired internal momenta lie in the original strip. Products, contractions and sums of the holomorphic arrays are therefore holomorphic there. Periodicity follows from that of the vertices, bridge and inverse. Equations (11.37)–(11.38), divided by the factorials, define common coefficient pictures \(P_3, P_4\) with

\(\displaystyle \|P_3 \|_{\eta / 6} \leqslant \frac{C_3}{6}, \qquad \|P_4 \|_{\eta / 8} \leqslant \frac{C_4 + 3 C_3 FC_3}{24} .\) (11.40)

They record the stationary derivatives exactly on every torus: the vertex identities already have the coarse-volume normalization, and Fourier inversion of the internal Hessian makes its contraction precisely \(V\) at the paired momentum. Thus the contraction formula preserves the same momentum-conservation rule and the same volume factor.

We collect the statement in the form in which it is used by the one-step construction.

Proposition 11.18. (Cubic and quartic pictures of the stationary value) Let the constants and the family of tori, clips, paths, beds, block steps \(S_i\), representatives \(t_i\) and bridge \(\Phi\) be as described before (11.31), with \(\mu \eta \leqslant \sigma'\), and let \(T_3, T_4\) be the vertices constructed there. For every \(i\) suppose given, on the \(i\)th coarse torus,

  1. a forest and an analytic map \(\rho_i\) into its tree chart with \(\rho_i (0) = 0\) which is stationary, near the origin, for the tested action of every choice of block representatives, these tested actions being real analytic near the origin, and a constant \(\delta > 0\), independent of \(i\), which is a lower bound for the Hessian of every tested action in the tree-chart variables at the origin;

  2. a matrix \(W_i\) such that the derivative of \(t \mapsto \rho_i (tx)\) at \(t = 0\) is the linear prediction of the tree chart from \(W_i\) for every coarse direction \(x\), and such that \(\Phi\) is the common Fourier representation of \(W_i\);

  3. a function \(V (p)\), the same for all \(i\), holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\eta\), which is the Fourier symbol of the inverse of the completed matrix (11.32) of the step \(S_i\) with representatives \(t_i\) and regulator \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), and satisfies (11.39) with a constant \(F \geqslant 0\).

Let \(E_i\) be the tested action of the representatives \(t_i\) evaluated along \(\rho_i\), a real analytic function of the coarse field near zero. Then the pictures \(P_3, P_4\) defined by (11.37)–(11.38) from \(T_3, T_4, \Phi, V\) satisfy (11.40), and for every \(i\) they record exactly the normalized Taylor coefficients \(D^3 E_i (0) / 3\)! and \(D^4 E_i (0) / 4\)!: on unit Fourier waves these coefficients vanish unless the momenta sum to zero, and otherwise equal the number of coarse sites times \(P_3\), respectively \(P_4\), at the last two, respectively three, momenta.

Proof. Fix \(i\). By Subsection 11.7, gauging the branch \(\rho_i\) back and recentering gives a stationary representative \(y_i (x)\) of the centered block action \(\mathcal{A}_i\), in all internal directions, with \(y_i (0) = 0\) and \(Dy_i (0) x = (W_i x, 0)\); hypotheses (1) and (2) are what that construction uses. The fine action and the block penalty are gauge invariant, so \(\mathcal{A}_i (x, y_i (x)) = E_i (x)\) near zero. The gap in (1) and \(\mu > 0\) give (11.32). The contraction formulas (11.37)–(11.38) therefore express \(D^3 E_i (0)\) and \(D^4 E_i (0)\) through the derivatives of \(\mathcal{A}_i\) on the legs \(\mathcal{L}h = (h, W_i h, 0)\) and through \(M^{- 1}\). Insert unit coarse Fourier waves. By (2) the leg of a wave of momentum \(p\) is the block Fourier wave with fiber \(\mathcal{L}_p e_a\); the vertex identity stated after (11.31) evaluates the derivatives of \(\mathcal{A}_i\) as the number of coarse sites times \(T_3\) or \(T_4\) at conserving momenta; and by (3) the contraction with \(M^{- 1}\) is the contraction with \(V\) at the paired momentum, which preserves momentum conservation and introduces no further volume factor. Dividing by the factorials gives the recording identities. The bounds were proved before the statement: they use the two parts of the vertex bounds, (11.39) and weighted duality.\(\Box\)

The hypotheses of this proposition are extracted from the standing data of the one-step construction. There, hypothesis (3) is not assumed directly: the identification of the inverse symbol is derived from the frame and kinetic response data of Subsection 11.8, and only the bound (11.39) is a separate estimate. The proof above uses nothing beyond (1)–(3), the path bounds, the branch compatibility and the vertex identity. The hypotheses are inputs with the following producers. The bridge bound (11.29) for the retained tower, and the identification of \(\Phi\) with the tangent of the recentered stationary representative, are proved in Subsection 11.7. The weighted Fourier and penalty estimates are proved in Subsection 11.5; the inverse estimate in (3) is the subject of Subsection 11.6. The stationary branch, its gap and its installed tangent in (1) and (2) are the chart receipts of Subsubsection 11.21.2; they are hypotheses of the one-step construction and are not produced here. Note that (1) requires one gap \(\delta\) for all \(i\), and that the path \(R\) is the incoming path, attached to the fine bed. A construction of the branch at each fixed period does not by itself give a gap that is uniform in the period; that uniformity is part of the input.

Finally choose \(L\) sufficiently large that \(\mu \ell, \mu r \leqslant 1\). Both pictures are then bounded on their common strip by

\(\displaystyle M_{\ast} = 1 + \frac{a + 1}{6} + \frac{(b + 1) + 3 (a + 1) F (a + 1)}{24} .\)

The width and \(M_{\ast}\) are chosen before \(\tau, A\); only the lower threshold for \(L\) depends on the incoming path. This order of choices is what allows the stationary output construction to be iterated. The weighted bridge, weighted penalty and common inverse estimates remain the precise supplier interfaces of this section.

11.12Priced local families give the three face estimates

We derive the upper-face bound, the full lower-face bound, and the lower-face-minus-halo bound used in the annulus estimate. The proof is the local bound calculation in the selected construction. We then verify the scale and data choices for the two populations used by the annulus: all centered configurations and the regulator-thin shell. We derive the selected row's contact identities and the scale-weighted face bounds, including the separate shell restriction required by the unregulated reference inverses.

11.12.1Derivative bounds

For analytic matrix paths \(M_i (t)\) near \(t = 0\), with values in a fixed finite-dimensional matrix space of dimension \(d\), write

\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_r (M ; C) \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad M_i \text{ is analytic near } 0 \text{ and } r_i^j \hspace{0.17em} \|M_i^{(j)} (0)\|_{\text{op}} \leqslant C \quad (j = 0, 1, 2), \) (11.41)

uniformly in the index \(i\). Here \(\| \cdot \|_{\text{op}}\) is the Euclidean operator norm; it is submultiplicative, and \(| \text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M| \leqslant d \|M\|_{\text{op}}\), where \(d\) is the matrix dimension. Constants may be enlarged by a fixed factor without changing the notation.

The elementary rules used below are as follows. If \(M, N\) have bounds \((r, C)\) and \((r, D)\), then \(M + N\) has bound \((r, C + D)\) and \(MN\) has bound \((r, 4 CD)\). For the product, the zeroth and first derivatives are the ordinary product rule; the second derivative has four terms, each bounded by submultiplicativity and the corresponding two estimates. If \(a > 0\) and \(C, D \geqslant 0\), \(M (0)\) is invertible and \(\|M (0)^{- 1} \|_{\text{op}} \leqslant D / a\), while \(M\) has bound \((r, aC)\), differentiating \(M^{- 1} M = I\) once and twice gives

\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_r (M^{- 1} ; \mathcal{I}(C, D) / a), \qquad \mathcal{I} (C, D) = D + 2 CD^2 + 2 C^2 D^3 . \) (11.42)

Indeed, \((M^{- 1})' = - M^{- 1} M' M^{- 1}\) and \((M^{- 1})'' = 2 M^{- 1} M' M^{- 1} M' M^{- 1} - M^{- 1} M'' M^{- 1}\); submultiplicativity bounds the terms by the three summands in (11.42). Finally,

\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_r (M ; Cr_i^{- 2}) \Longrightarrow | (\text{tr} \hspace{0.17em} M_i)'' (0) | \leqslant dCr_i^{- 4}, \) (11.43)

by the \(j = 2\) case of (11.41) and the trace inequality. These are the finite-dimensional bound rules underlying the estimates below.

11.12.2Upper, lower, and halo faces

For each configuration \(i\), let \(x_i \in \mathbb{R}^4\) be its background momentum, \(e_i\) its boost, \(\mu_i > 0\) its regulator and \(T_i > 0\) its scale. Norms of momenta are Euclidean. The local geometry used here is

\(\displaystyle \|e_i \| \leqslant 1, \qquad \|x_i \| \leqslant T_i \leqslant r_{\ast}, \qquad 0 < \mu_i \leqslant T_i . \) (11.44)

The proposition below uses only (11.44) and the bounds (F6). The derivation of those bounds from the selected data, given later in this section, uses in addition

\(\displaystyle x_i \in [- \pi, \pi]^4, \qquad \mu_i \leqslant 1, \qquad T_i \leqslant \|x_i \| + \mu_i . \) (11.45)

The last inequality is needed for the lower bound of the denominators: (11.44) alone allows \(x_i = 0\), \(T_i = 1\) and \(\mu_i\) arbitrarily small, in which case no bound \(T_i^2 \leqslant C (\|x_i \|^2 + \mu_i^2)\) holds. Both scale choices made below satisfy (11.45).

The matrices entering the faces are slots of a film: a triple of analytic matrix functions of momentum consisting of a tab \(a (p)\), a one-leg slot \(b_{\xi} (k, p)\) for a mark \(\xi\) inserted with momentum \(k\) on the incoming momentum \(p\), and a two-leg slot \(c_{\xi \zeta} (k, k', p)\). Sums, products and inverses of films are formed with the insertion rules recalled in the trajectory-bound paragraph below. Each configuration carries a mark \(\xi_i\), a local film and a regulator film; their sum is the total film. With \(a, b, c\) the slots of the total film, the objects in the next paragraph are

\(\displaystyle S_i (t) = a (x_i + te_i), \qquad B_i^+ (t) = b_{\xi_i} (te_i, x_i), \qquad B_i^- (t) = b_{\xi_i} (- te_i, x_i + te_i),\)

and \(J_i (t) + J_i^{\text{ref}} (t) = c_{\xi_i \xi_i} (- te_i, te_i, x_i)\), where \(J_i\) is the two-leg slot of the local film and \(J_i^{\text{ref}}\) that of the regulator film. The second leg \(B_i^-\) is evaluated at the shifted momentum \(x_i + te_i\). Thus \(S_i\) denotes the total denominator (local plus regulator film), and \(J_i\) and \(J_i^{\text{ref}}\) are the two two-leg arch insertions. Write \(K_{0, i} = S_i (0)^{- 1}\) for the inverse at the unshifted background and \(K_{1, i} (t) = S_i (t)^{- 1}\) for the inverse at the displaced background \(x_i + te_i\). Write \(D_{0, i} = (\|x_i \|^2 I)^{- 1}\) and \(D_{1, i} (t) = (\|x_i + te_i \|^2 I)^{- 1}\) for the corresponding unregulated free inverses, defined when \(x_i \ne 0\), and \(\tilde{B}_i^{\pm}\) for the legs obtained from \(B_i^{\pm}\) by replacing the slot \(b_{\xi_i}\) with its linear part \(v_{\xi_i}\), which is determined below. The unshifted inverse is constant in \(t\); the shifted inverse varies. With \(B_i^-, B_i^+\) the displaced insertion legs, define the scalar upper and lower faces by

\(\displaystyle U_i (t) = \text{tr} [K_{0, i} (J_i (t) + J_i^{\text{ref}} (t))], \qquad L_i (t) = \text{tr} [K_{0, i} B_i^- (t) K_{1, i} (t) B_i^+ (t)] . \) (11.46)

The halo is \(H_i (t) = \text{tr} [D_{0, i} \tilde{B}_i^- (t) D_{1, i} (t) \tilde{B}_i^+ (t)]\), with the four factors the corresponding free values. Thus it replaces both inverses and both legs. In this subsection and in (F6) the word “reference” and the superscript ref refer to the regulator film of the configuration; the halo factors are called free.

The selected row transfer yields the following bounds, with one constant \(C_0 \geqslant 0\) independent of \(i\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (K_{0, i} ; C_0 T_i^{- 2}), \quad \textsf{P}_{T_i} (K_{1, i} ; C_0 T_i^{- 2}), \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.47)} \\ & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (J_i^{\text{ref}} ; C_0 \mu_i^2 T_i^{- 2}), \qquad \textsf{P}_1 (J_i ; C_0), \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.48)} \\ & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (B_i^{\pm} ; C_0 T_i), \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.49)} \\ & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (K_{0, i} - D_{0, i} ; C_0 \hspace{0.17em} \delta_i T_i^{- 2}), \quad \textsf{P}_{T_i} (D_{0, i} ; C_0 T_i^{- 2}),\\ & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (D_{1, i} ; C_0 T_i^{- 2}), \quad \textsf{P}_{T_i} (K_{1, i} - D_{1, i} ; C_0 \hspace{0.17em} \delta_i T_i^{- 2}),\\ & \textsf{P}_{T_i} (B_i^{\pm} - \tilde{B}_i^{\pm} ; C_0 \hspace{0.17em} \delta_i T_i), \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.50)} \end{array}\)

where \(C_0 \geqslant 0\) and \(\delta_i = T_i + \mu_i^2 / T_i^2\). The tilded factors are the free halo factors just described. Also \(\|K_{0, i} \|_{\text{op}} \leqslant C_0 T_i^{- 2}\). The ordinary face bounds (F6a)–(F6c) apply to the all-centered population. The extra differences (F6d) are required only on the shell \(\mu_i \leqslant T_i = \|x_i \|_2\); at the center \(x_i = 0\) the unregulated halo inverse need not exist. The derivation from this same selected row and the reference field is given below; in particular, the free inverses in (F6d) are used only on the shell.

Proposition 11.19. (Face estimates) Under (11.44)–(F6c), there are constants \(A, B, C \geqslant 0\), independent of \(i\), such that

\(\displaystyle |U_i'' (0) | \leqslant \frac{A}{T_i^2} + B \frac{\mu_i^2}{T_i^6}, \qquad |L_i'' (0) | \leqslant \frac{C}{T_i^4}, \) (11.51)

and, whenever the additional shell bounds (F6d) hold,

\(\displaystyle |L_i'' (0) - H_i'' (0) | \leqslant C \frac{\delta_i}{T_i^4} . \) (11.52)

Proof. For the reference part of the upper face, (F6a)–(F6b) and the product rule give bound \(C \mu_i^2 / T_i^4\) at scale \(T_i\). By (11.43), its second derivative is at most \(C \mu_i^2 / T_i^6\). For the interacting part, \(S_i (0)^{- 1}\) is constant in \(t\) and has norm at most \(C_0 / T_i^2\); combine this with the unit-scale bound of \(J_i\) and apply the trace bound at unit scale. This gives \(A / T_i^2\). Adding proves the first inequality in (11.51).

The lower-face package supplies bound \(C / T_i^2\) at scale \(T_i\) for the four-factor product in (11.46): each inverse has bound proportional to \(T_i^{- 2}\) and each displaced leg has bound proportional to \(T_i\). Applying (11.43) yields \(C / T_i^4\), proving the second inequality.

For the difference estimate, assume in addition (F6d), as is valid on the shell. Since \(T_i \leqslant r_{\ast}\) and \(\mu_i \leqslant T_i\), we have \(\delta_i \leqslant r_{\ast} + 1\). The sum rule for bounds and (F6c)–(F6d) therefore give each tilded leg the ordinary bound \(\textsf{P}_{T_i} (\tilde{B}_i^{\pm} ; C_1 T_i)\), with \(C_1\) depending only on \(C_0\) and \(r_{\ast}\). Use the exact ordered telescoping identity

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} A_1 A_2 A_3 A_4 - B_1 B_2 B_3 B_4 & = (A_1 - B_1) A_2 A_3 A_4 + B_1 (A_2 - B_2) A_3 A_4\\ & \quad + B_1 B_2 (A_3 - B_3) A_4 + B_1 B_2 B_3 (A_4 - B_4) . \end{aligned} \ \) (11.53)

Both inverse differences, at the unshifted and shifted legs, have bound \(C \delta_i / T_i^2\) by (F6d); the leg differences have bound \(C \delta_i T_i\) by (F6d). The remaining inverse factors cost \(C / T_i^2\) and the remaining legs cost \(CT_i\). In each of the four summands the product rule therefore gives bound at most \(C \delta_i / T_i^2\) at scale \(T_i\). Apply (11.43) to the trace of their sum. Its second derivative is bounded by \(C \delta_i / T_i^4\), as claimed.\(\Box\)

11.12.3The two populations of centered records and the two scale choices

We now state the input family precisely. Let \(G\) be simple. A response is a triple of analytic coefficient pictures with two, three and four legs, \(R_0, R_1, R_2\); it determines a film whose tab, one-leg and two-leg slots are its zero-, one- and two-mark layers, with the marked momenta first, then the outgoing and the incoming momentum:

\(\displaystyle a (k) = R_0 (- k, k), \qquad b_{\xi} (p, k) = R_1 (\xi ; p, - k - p, k), \qquad c_{\xi \zeta} (p, q, k) = R_2 (\xi, \zeta ; p, q, - k - p - q, k),\)

the direction and color indices of the last two legs being the matrix indices. A response has fees \((\eta, C)\) if all components of its three pictures are holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\eta\) and bounded by \(C\) there. Fix

\(\displaystyle \eta > 0, \qquad \epsilon > 0, \qquad C, D, A \geqslant 0,\)

and one vacuum response \(V\) with fees \((\eta, C)\) which is grey: at every torus side its zero-, one- and two-mark layers record exactly, with the volume factor at conserving momenta and with no error, the corresponding marked derivatives of the Wilson action \(\mathcal{W}\) on that torus. These are the derivatives at the origin of \((y, z) \mapsto \mathcal{W} (e^y e^z)\), the product of the two exponentiated link fields being taken link by link, of order two in \(y\) and of order zero, one or two in the marked variable \(z\). The same convention defines the layers recorded by any response.

A local record consists of a strip width \(\sigma\), a regulator \(\mu\), a color \(c\), a bed \(B\), a clip \(p\) and a response \(l\), the local correction, such that

  1. \(l\) has fees \((\eta, A)\), and its zero-, one- and two-mark layers vanish at zero momentum to orders three, two and one;

  2. \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\) and \(0 < \sigma \leqslant \mu / 128\);

  3. the three layers of the combined response \(l + V\) are holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\sigma\), and the metric row of \(B\) has strip bound \((\sigma, D)\);

  4. the denominator matching (11.58) below holds on the strip of width \(\sigma\) for the vector film of \(l + V\), the bed \(B\) and the regulator \(\mu\);

  5. the floor of \(B\) in the clip \(p\) holds with constants \((\epsilon, D)\) at every torus side.

The numbers \(\eta, \epsilon, C, D, A\) and the response \(V\) are common to all records. The width \(\sigma\) belongs to the individual record and is forced to tend to zero with \(\mu\); no common strip for the regulated denominators is assumed. A centered record (an Hstar datum) is a local record together with a momentum \(x \in [- \pi, \pi]^4\). The constants obtained below depend only on \(G\), \(V\), \(\eta\), \(\epsilon\), \(C\), \(D\), \(A\): they are chosen before the record, so they are uniform over all centered records, in particular in \(\mu\), \(\sigma\), the color, the bed and the clip.

Each centered record is used in two channels, both with the mark \(\xi = (1, c)\) (direction \(1\), color \(c\)) and the unit axial boost \(e_0\). Let \(\textsf{B}\) be the fixed gauge-fixing film. It is the film of the covariant difference: for a link configuration \(U\) and a Lie-algebra valued function \(h\) on sites,

\(\displaystyle (D_U h) (x, a) = h (x) - \text{Ad}_{U (x, a)} \hspace{0.17em} h (x + e_a),\)

and the tab and the one- and two-leg slots of \(\textsf{B}\) are the Fourier symbols of \(D_1\) and of the first and second derivatives of \(D_{\exp z}\) in the background \(z\) at \(z = 0\), with the routing of momenta displayed above. In particular its tab is \(- \textsf{T} (P_0)\), the color-diagonal extension of minus the zero-form symbol \(P_0\) below, and its marked slots do not vanish. Let \(\textsf{B}^{\text{r}}\) be its rear film (transposed, with reversed momenta). The sign of \(\textsf{B}\) cancels in the two products \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\) and \(\textsf{B}^{\text{r}} \textsf{B}\), which are the only places where \(\textsf{B}\) enters. That these slots record \(D_{\exp z}\) on every torus, and the corresponding statement for products, rears and inverses, is proved in Lemma 11.27.

channel local film regulator film
vector film of \(l + V\), plus \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\) \(R_{\text{vec}, \mu}\)
ghost \(Q_{\text{gh}} = \textsf{B}^{\text{r}} \textsf{B}\) \(R_{\text{gh}, \mu}\)

The regulator films are constructed in the trajectory-bound paragraph. The response whose second derivative the annulus estimate concerns is one half of the vector-channel total film's two-leg figure minus the ghost-channel one; with \(U, L\) the faces (11.46) of each channel,

\(\displaystyle \kappa (x) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{1}{2} (U_{\text{vec}}'' (0) - L_{\text{vec}}'' (0)) - (U_{\text{gh}}'' (0) - L_{\text{gh}}'' (0)) \right) .\) (11.54)

For each selected row, the path bounds give caps for the tab, the one-leg insertions, and the two-leg insertion, together with the two soft contacts. The regulated reference path supplies its own bounds. Combining the paths gives the total stationary denominator and its scale-\(T^2\) lower bound. The derivation of (F6) below shows explicitly how these data yield the required bounds. In particular, no scale-weighted estimate is inferred from analyticity alone: the rates come from the path budget, its one-leg weight \(T^{- 1}\), and its two-leg weight \(T^{- 2}\); the extra shell estimates use subtraction identities.

For a centered-box track, each coordinate has modulus at most \(\pi\), so \(\|x\|_2 \leqslant \sqrt{4 \pi^2} = 2 \pi\); the regulator satisfies \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\). The all-centered scale is

\(\displaystyle T = \|x\|_2 + \mu, \qquad 0 < T \leqslant 1 + 2 \pi = : r_{\ast} . \) (11.55)

Thus (11.44) and (11.45) hold and (11.51)'s upper estimate gives the first two-wave bound after combining it with the lower-face estimate. On the shell \(\mu \leqslant \|x\|_2\), reset the scale to \(T = \|x\|_2\). Then \(0 < \mu \leqslant T \leqslant r_{\ast}\) and \(T \leqslant \|x\|_2 + \mu\), so (11.44) and (11.45) hold again, and the same package gives both the upper estimate and (11.52). The shell population is the subfamily of the same centered records with \(\mu \leqslant \|x\|_2\); only the scale is changed, not the response, bed, color or regulator. Use the halo identity, the second identity in (11.67) of Subsection 11.13, to replace the halo by the continuum pole. Since \(T \leqslant r_{\ast}\),

\(\displaystyle T^{- 2} \leqslant r_{\ast} T^{- 3}, \qquad T^{- 2} \leqslant r_{\ast}^2 T^{- 4}, \qquad \frac{\mu^2}{T^6} \leqslant \frac{1}{T^4}, \qquad \frac{\delta}{T^4} = \frac{1}{T^3} + \frac{\mu^2}{T^6} .\)

Indeed, the response is given by (11.54). The upper estimate and lower-face estimate in (11.51), applied in both channels with \(\mu \leqslant T\), therefore give \(| \kappa (x) | \leqslant aT^{- 4}\). For the shell discrepancy, the exact algebra is

\(\displaystyle \kappa (x) - \Pi_G (x) = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{1}{2} U_{\text{vec}}'' - U_{\text{gh}}'' + (L_{\text{gh}}'' - H_{\text{gh}}'') - \frac{1}{2} (L_{\text{vec}}'' - H_{\text{vec}}'') \right), \) (11.56)

all second derivatives being taken at \(t = 0\), where the halo combination \(\frac{1}{2} (H_{\text{gh}}'' - \frac{1}{2} H_{\text{vec}}'') = \Pi_G\) is the halo identity just cited. The two upper terms are bounded by \(C / T^2 + C \mu^2 / T^6\), and the lower remainders by \(C \delta / T^4\); the displayed scale inequalities give the two powers in (11.57). Enlarge constants once to cover the bounded part of the centered box outside the unit ball. We obtain constants \(a, b\) independent of \(x\) and \(\mu\) such that

\(\displaystyle | \kappa (x) | \leqslant a (\|x\|_2 + \mu)^{- 4}, \qquad | \kappa (x) - \Pi_G (x) | \leqslant b \|x\|_2^{- 3} + b \mu^2 \|x\|_2^{- 6} \quad (\mu \leqslant \|x\|_2 \leqslant 1) . \) (11.57)

The constants \(a, b\) depend only on \(G\), \(V\), \(\eta\), \(\epsilon\), \(C\), \(D\), \(A\). In particular the same \(a, b\) serve every local record, every color \(c\) and every regulator \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\), and they are not affected by the record's own strip width \(\sigma\). These are the uniform local estimates consumed in Subsection 11.13.

11.12.4The local row bridge to the interacting family

Here are the exact row identities behind the local contact conditions. For a selected bed \(B_i\), write \(M_i (p) = B_i . \text{metric} (p)\) and let \(M_{\ast} (p)\) be the constant leader metric. Let \(D (p)\) be the first edge symbol, with components \(q_{\nu} (p)\), and let \(A_0 (p)\) be the zero-form symbol. The color-diagonal projection of the direction matrix is denoted by \(\textsf{T}\). Put

\(\displaystyle G_i (p) = \text{the two-point row of the interacting film}, \qquad J_{i, b} (p, k) = \text{its marked two-leg row} .\)

For a local record, the three layers of \(l\) and of \(V\) have the common fee strip of width \(\eta\); the record satisfies the denominator matching identity on its own strip of width \(\sigma\),

\(\displaystyle (\text{vector local film} + \text{vector regulator film})_{\text{tab}} (p) = D^{\text{bed}} (p), \) (11.58)

where the bed denominator \(D^{\text{bed}}\) is displayed in the derivation below; the floor at every torus side (in particular the zero-momentum normalization \(M_i (0) = I = M_{\ast} (0)\) of the metric row, which is one clause of the floor at any one side); the soft order-three condition on the local correction; and the vacuum matching condition (greyness of \(V\)). These properties all concern the same bed and the same combined response \(l + V\). The regulator satisfies \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\), and the tab of the vector regulator film is the scalar multiple \(s_{\mu} (p) I\) on the strip of width \(\mu / 128\), where, with \(\omega\) as below,

\(\displaystyle s_{\mu} (p) = \mu^2 \left( \frac{\mu^2}{\omega (p) + \mu^2} \right)^9 .\)

They are properties of the local record, rather than consequences of analyticity alone.

Let \(\omega (p) = \sum_{\nu} q_{\nu} (- p) q_{\nu} (p)\). The exact row and free identities use the color-diagonal extension \(\textsf{T} (A)_{(i, c), (j, d)} =\boldsymbol{1}_{c = d} A_{ij}\) of a direction matrix; this extension preserves matrix sums and products. They are

\(\displaystyle G_i (p) - |p|^2 I = \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T (M_i (p) - M_{\ast} (p)) P_1 (p)] + (\omega (p) - |p|^2) I, \) (11.59)
\(\displaystyle J_{i, b} (p, k) - v_b (p, k) = J_{i, b}^{\text{loc}} (p, k) + (J_{i, b}^{\text{vac}} (p, k) - v_b (p, k)), \) (11.60)

and

\(\displaystyle \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T M_{\ast} (p) P_1 (p) + P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T] = \omega (p) I. \) (11.61)

Here \(v_b\) is the linear vector insertion determined by the first radial coefficient of the vacuum marked row.

Derivation of (11.59)–(11.61). Write \(P_0 (p)\) and \(P_1 (p)\) for the zero-form and first-edge symbols, so \(A_0 = P_0\) and \(D = P_1\). The selected denominator matching equates the tab of the interacting film plus its regulator film to the prescribed bed denominator. The two sides expand as

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} (\text{interacting} + \text{regulator})_{\text{tab}} (p) & = G_i (p) + s_{\mu} (p) I,\\ (\text{bed denominator}) (p) & = \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T M_i (p) P_1 (p) + P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T] + s_{\mu} (p) I. \end{aligned}\)

The regulator tab is scalar on the slot space, so equality cancels that term entrywise and gives, on real momenta,

\(\displaystyle G_i (p) = \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T M_i (p) P_1 (p) + P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T] .\)

The first summand is the metric cross product; the second is the zero-form product. The cancellation is exact, so it introduces no regulator remainder. For the leader bed, the degree-zero exterior identity gives

\(\displaystyle \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T M_{\ast} (p) P_1 (p) + P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T] = \omega (p) I.\)

Indeed, expand the first product by direction. The longitudinal rank-one part of the zero-form product cancels the complementary part of the first-edge product, leaving \(\sum_{\nu} q_{\nu} (- p) q_{\nu} (p)\) times the identity. The exact degree-zero exterior identity is \(P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T + P_1 (- p)^T M_{\ast} P_1 (p) = \omega (p) I\), since \(\omega (p) = \sum_{\nu} q_{\nu} (- p) q_{\nu} (p)\) by definition. Since \(M_{\ast}\) is the constant identity metric, this proves (11.61). Subtracting the leader identity from the row-to-film identity proves (11.59), with the lattice-dispersion term \(\omega (p) - |p|^2\) retained explicitly.

For the marked row, the vector local film is the sum of the film of the local correction \(l\), the film of the vacuum response \(V\), and the fixed gauge-fixing film \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\). Accordingly \(J^{\text{loc}}_{i, b}\) in (11.60) is the one-mark slot of \(l\), and \(J^{\text{vac}}_{i, b}\) is the sum of the one-mark slots of \(V\) and of \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\). Linearity of the marked coefficient gives (11.60). The soft-3 hypothesis assigns radial order two to the one-mark layer; composing that layer with the linear two-momentum map gives the required second-order vanishing. The slot of \(V\) is identified by greyness with the Wilson three-leg coefficient. Greyness gives this equality at the grid momenta of every torus side; both sides are continuous on the real momenta and the union of the grids is dense, so the equality holds at all real momenta. An identity on one fixed grid would not suffice for the radial expansion. Let the mark be \((a, b)\), with direction \(a\) and color \(b\), let the two other legs have directions \(i, j\) and colors \(x, y\) and momenta \(- (k + p)\) and \(k\), the mark carrying momentum \(p\), and write

\(\displaystyle \tau (u)_a = iu_a, \qquad c_{bxy} = \langle e_x, [e_b, e_y] \rangle\)

for the structure coefficient in the orthonormal color basis; it is totally antisymmetric. After summing over all positively oriented plaquette types, the contribution to the linear part of the three-leg coefficient in which a chosen leg is differentiated in the curl and the two others in the commutator is \((\tau (u)_{i'} \delta_{a' j'} - \tau (u)_{j'} \delta_{a' i'})\) times the structure coefficient with the color of the chosen leg in the middle, where \(u, a'\) are the momentum and direction of the chosen leg and \(i', j'\) the directions of the others. Choosing in turn the mark, the leg \((i, x)\) and the leg \((j, y)\), and reordering the color indices by antisymmetry, gives the three contributions

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} & [- \tau (p)_i \delta_{aj} + \tau (p)_j \delta_{ai}] \hspace{0.17em} c_{bxy},\\ & [- (\tau (k)_a + \tau (p)_a) \delta_{ij} + (\tau (k)_j + \tau (p)_j) \delta_{ia}] \hspace{0.17em} c_{bxy},\\ & [- \tau (k)_a \delta_{ij} + \tau (k)_i \delta_{ja}] \hspace{0.17em} c_{bxy} . \end{aligned}\)

Their sum is the first radial coefficient of the Wilson slot,

\(\displaystyle [- (2 \tau (k)_a + \tau (p)_a) \delta_{ij} + (\tau (k)_j + 2 \tau (p)_j) \delta_{ia} + (\tau (k)_i - \tau (p)_i) \delta_{ja}] c_{bxy} .\)

The marked slot of the gauge-fixing film \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\) has first radial coefficient

\(\displaystyle [- \tau (k)_j \delta_{ia} - \tau (k + p)_i \delta_{ja}] c_{bxy} .\)

Adding these expressions and simplifying gives

\(\displaystyle v_{b, ij} (p, k) = [- (2 \tau (k)_a + \tau (p)_a) \delta_{ij} + 2 \tau (p)_j \delta_{ia} - 2 \tau (p)_i \delta_{ja}] c_{bxy} .\)

This is homogeneous of degree one. Thus the vacuum marked row minus \(v_b\) has zero value and zero first radial coefficient; analyticity implies that it has radial order at least two. With the soft local term, (11.60) has the required quadratic contact. The common strip bounds then give uniform compact derivative bounds by Cauchy's formula.\(\Box\)

The ghost channel.
The ghost local film \(Q_{\text{gh}} = \textsf{B}^{\text{r}} \textsf{B}\) is a fixed film, independent of the record. Its tab is \(\omega (p) I\) on the color space, so the analogue of (11.59) is \(Q_{\text{gh}, \text{tab}} (p) - |p|^2 I = (\omega (p) - |p|^2) I\), of radial order three. Its one-mark slot at the mark \((a, b)\) has entries analytic in the two momenta and the linear part
\(\displaystyle w_b (p, k)_{xy} = - (2 \tau (k)_a + \tau (p)_a) \hspace{0.17em} c_{bxy},\)

obtained from the explicit slot of the product by expanding the two lattice symbols to first order; the remainder has radial order two. These are the two contacts of the ghost channel, with \(w_b\) in the place of \(v_b\) as the linear leg of its halo. Its compact derivative caps are those of a fixed analytic periodic film. The tab of the ghost total film is \((\omega (p) + s_{\mu} (p)) I\) on the strip of width \(\mu / 128\).

Lemma 11.20. (Row data give the priced insertion family) For the family of all local records with the common fee bounds, denominator matching, floor at every torus side, soft order-three local correction and grey vacuum response just described, the vector local film has uniform compact derivative bounds through order three. Its two-point row has a cubic radial remainder after subtraction of \(|p|^2 I\), and its marked row has a quadratic radial remainder after subtraction of \(v_b\). Consequently it supplies the compact caps and the two contact orders used in the ordinary (non-halo) part of (F6b)–(F6c).

Proof. For each row layer and each fixed pair of matrix entries, the common strip fee and Cauchy's estimate bound derivatives of every fixed order on a compact real ball. The tab, spot and arch are fixed finite linear combinations of these entries after fixed linear maps of momentum; the chain rule therefore gives uniform compact derivative caps through the orders used here. A finite sum of these caps bounds the matrix operator norm, since the matrix dimension is fixed.

For the two-point contact, (11.59) and the zero-momentum normalization supplied by the floor give \(M_i (0) - M_{\ast} (0) = 0\). Analyticity makes this metric difference \(O (|p|)\). Each edge symbol vanishes linearly, \(q_{\nu} (p) = ip_{\nu} + O (|p|^2)\), so the metric term in (11.59) is \(O (|p|^3)\). Expanding each product \(q_{\nu} (- p) q_{\nu} (p)\) shows \(\omega (p) - |p|^2 = O (|p|^3)\) as well.

For the marked row, the local soft condition contributes \(O ((|p| + |k|)^2)\) and the vacuum-minus-\(v_b\) term has the same order by the preceding first-coefficient calculation. Thus (11.60) has quadratic contact. To convert these radial orders into derivative bounds, note that if an analytic scalar entry \(g\) has radial order at least \(m\), then each homogeneous Taylor polynomial of degree \(j < m\) vanishes on every real direction and hence vanishes identically. Taylor's formula with integral remainder applied to \(D^n g\) on the segment from \(0\) to the base point \(a\) gives

\(\displaystyle \|D^n g (a)\| \leqslant K \|a\|^{m - n} / (m - n) !, \qquad n < m,\)

when the \(m\)th derivative is bounded by \(K\) on that segment. For \(\|a\| \leqslant T\), this gives \(T^n \|D^n g (a)\| \leqslant KT^m\). At orders above the contact order use the compact derivative cap and the uniform upper bound \(T \leqslant r_{\ast}\). This proves the local compact and contact bounds. The trajectory-bound argument below derives the total-field bounds in (F6a) and the shell-separation bounds in (F6d) from these contacts and the reference path budgets.\(\Box\)

From a centered record to the row package.
Every centered record contains one bed, clip, regulator, and local correction. Its denominator matching is the identity (11.58), its fees supply the uniform analytic strip, and its floor supplies the zero-momentum normalization. Its local correction is soft of order three. Applying the row argument to the single combined response \(l + V\) gives the vector local film used by the annulus. The free halo comparison is \(|k|^2 I\) with the linear legs \(v_b\) (vector) and \(w_b\) (ghost). Since the vacuum response \(V\) is grey and has the required fees, the finite-volume Wilson matching above identifies its marked vertex and hence its linear term. These statements establish the row contacts from the data of a centered record. The scalar regulator term and its positive floor are then included in the total denominator; the next bound calculation uses the free comparison only on the shell, where its inverse exists.

Recall \(\epsilon > 0\). The denominator lower bound is also tied to this same bed. At a real momentum \(p\), write

\(\displaystyle D_i^{\text{bed}} (p) = \textsf{T} \hspace{-0.17em} [P_1 (- p)^T M_i (p) P_1 (p) + P_0 (p) P_0 (- p)^T] + s_{\mu} (p) I\)

for the denominator matrix in (11.58), and put

\(\displaystyle \text{massiveSymbol} (p, \mu) = \sum_{\nu} |q_{\nu} (p) |^2 + \mu^2 .\)

Assume (11.44) and (11.45) and let \(p = x\) be the background. On the centered box

\(\displaystyle |q_{\nu} (x) | = 2 | \sin (x_{\nu} / 2) | \geqslant \frac{2}{\pi} |x_{\nu} | \geqslant \frac{|x_{\nu} |}{4}, \qquad \text{so} \qquad \sum_{\nu} |q_{\nu} (x) |^2 \geqslant \frac{\|x\|^2}{16} .\)

With \(T \leqslant \|x\| + \mu\) this gives \(T^2 \leqslant 2\|x\|^2 + 2 \mu^2 \leqslant 32 \sum_{\nu} |q_{\nu} (x) |^2 + 2 \mu^2\), hence

\(\displaystyle T^2 \leqslant 64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{massiveSymbol} (x, \mu) .\)

The bed-floor estimate for this matrix, which uses the floor at every torus side, the metric strip bound and \(\epsilon > 0\), gives at every real momentum \(p\)

\(\displaystyle \frac{\min (\epsilon, 1)}{2^{10}} \hspace{0.17em} \text{massiveSymbol} (p, \mu) \|z\|^2 \leqslant \text{Re} \langle z, D_i^{\text{bed}} (p) z \rangle .\)

Using the denominator matching (11.58) at the real momentum \(x\) to identify this matrix with the vector total denominator, we obtain

\(\displaystyle cT^2 \|z\|^2 \leqslant \text{Re} \langle z, S_i (0) z \rangle, \qquad c = \frac{\min (\epsilon, 1)}{2^{16}} .\)

For the ghost channel the total tab at real momentum is the scalar \(\omega (p) + s_{\mu} (p)\), and \(\omega (p) + s_{\mu} (p) \geqslant 2^{- 10} \hspace{0.17em} \text{massiveSymbol} (p, \mu)\): if \(\omega (p) \leqslant \mu^2\) then \(s_{\mu} (p) \geqslant \mu^2 / 2^9\), and otherwise \(\omega (p) \geqslant \frac{1}{2} \text{massiveSymbol} (p, \mu)\). The same cold lower bound therefore gives the ghost denominator bound with \(c = 2^{- 16}\). For either square matrix \(S\), the coercive estimate implies \(cT^2 \|z\|^2 \leqslant \text{Re} \langle z, Sz \rangle \leqslant \|z\| \hspace{0.17em} \|Sz\|\). Hence \(\|Sz\| \geqslant cT^2 \|z\|\); in finite dimension this makes \(S\) invertible with \(\|S^{- 1} \|_{\text{op}} \leqslant (cT^2)^{- 1}\).

Trajectory bounds.
We derive (F6) from the row contacts and the regulated reference field. At scale \(T_i\), a path estimate with tab budget \(L_i\) consists of three component bounds. The tab derivatives satisfy \(T_i^j \|M_i^{(j)} (0)\|_{\text{op}} \leqslant CL_i\) for \(j = 0, 1, 2\); the one-leg slot has budget \(L_i / T_i\), and the two-leg arch has budget \(L_i / T_i^2\). These are separate components of the path estimate; the spot and arch bounds do not follow from the tab bound alone. For the interacting row, the compact derivative caps come from the common strip and selected fees. Along \(x_i + te_i\), each derivative is a momentum derivative contracted with \(e_i\), whose norm is at most one. If an analytic entry has radial order \(m\), all Taylor tensors of degree below \(m\) vanish: their diagonal polynomial vanishes in every real direction, hence the polynomial itself is zero. Taylor's integral remainder applied to its \(j\)th derivative therefore bounds it by \(C \|x_i \|^{m - j}\) for \(j < m\); for \(j = m\) use the compact derivative cap. Since \(\|x_i \| \leqslant T_i \leqslant r_{\ast}\), this gives the path budget \(CT_i^m\) for \(j \leqslant m\). Thus the trajectory transfer with tab budget \(L_i\) gives
\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_{T_i} (\text{tab} ; CL_i), \qquad \textsf{P}_{T_i} (\text{spot} ; CL_i / T_i), \qquad \textsf{P}_{T_i} (\text{arch} ; CL_i / T_i^2) . \) (11.62)

Thus the interacting transfer uses \(L_i = T_i^2\), while the regulated reference transfer uses \(L_i = \mu_i^2\).

For the interacting row, (11.59) says the tab is \(|p|^2 I + O (|p|^3)\); therefore its path has tab budget \(CT_i^2\). For a marked leg, (11.60) says it is the linear vector term plus a quadratic remainder. Along the path, the linear term has size \(O (T_i)\), first derivative \(O (1)\), and zero second derivative. The quadratic remainder has \(T_i^j\)-weighted \(j\)th derivative \(O (T_i^2)\) for \(j = 0, 1, 2\); since \(T_i \leqslant r_{\ast}\), this is at most \(O (T_i)\). Thus the leg has budget \(CT_i\). The uniform arch cap gives budget \(C\) at unit scale. The regulated reference field has the following three trajectory budgets, which we derive from its actual construction below:

\(\displaystyle C \mu_i^2, \qquad C \frac{\mu_i^2}{T_i}, \qquad C \frac{\mu_i^2}{T_i^2} .\)

These weights follow from its construction. The regulator films of both channels are built from the ghost film \(Q_{\text{gh}} = \textsf{B}^{\text{r}} \textsf{B}\), not from the vector film:

\(\displaystyle C_{\mu} = Q_{\text{gh}} + \mu^2 I, \qquad H_{\mu} = \mu^2 C_{\mu}^{- 1}, \qquad R_{\text{gh}, \mu} = \mu^2 H_{\mu}^9, \qquad R_{\text{vec}, \mu} = \sum_{a = 0}^3 \textsf{D}_a^{\text{r}} R_{\text{gh}, \mu} \textsf{D}_a,\)

with products and inverses of films, including their marked slots, and with \(\textsf{D}_a\) the fixed constant matrix selecting the direction \(a\), regarded as a film without marked slots. Begin with the massive ghost film \(C_{\mu_i}\). The ghost film has tab budget \(T_i^2\): its tab \(\omega (p) I\) has a quadratic zero, while its compact derivatives are uniformly bounded. Its marked leg has the linear first term \(w_b\) and a quadratic remainder, hence budget \(T_i\); its two-leg arch has a uniform unit-scale cap. The added constant mass has tab budget \(T_i^2\) since \(\mu_i \leqslant T_i\), and has zero spot and arch. The massive film therefore has the three-component path budgets \(T_i^2, T_i, 1\). Its scalar tab is the free dispersion plus \(\mu_i^2\); the cold geometric inequality bounds it below by \(T_i^2 / 64\). Thus its inverse has norm at most \(64 T_i^{- 2}\) and its inverse path has tab budget \(T_i^{- 2}\). If \(C = A^{- 1}\), \(B_i\) is the original one-leg slot, and \(D_{ij}\) is the original two-leg slot, the inverse film's insertion formulas, with \(\hat{B}_i\) and \(\hat{D}_{ij}\) denoting the inverse film's slots, are

\(\displaystyle \hat{B}_i (p, q) = - C (q + p) B_i (p, q) C (q),\)
\(\displaystyle \hat{D}_{ij} (p, q, r) = - [\hat{B}_j (q, r + p) B_i (p, r) + \hat{B}_i (p, r + q) B_j (q, r) + C (r + p + q) D_{ij} (p, q, r)] C (r) .\)

The one-leg term has size \(T_i^{- 2} \hspace{0.17em} T_i \hspace{0.17em} T_i^{- 2} = T_i^{- 3}\). Each of the three two-leg summands has size \(T_i^{- 4}\): the first two use \(T_i^{- 3} \hspace{0.17em} T_i \hspace{0.17em} T_i^{- 2}\), and the last uses \(T_i^{- 2} \hspace{0.17em} 1 \hspace{0.17em} T_i^{- 2}\). Thus the inverse film has tab, one-leg, and two-leg budgets \(T_i^{- 2}\), \(T_i^{- 3}\), and \(T_i^{- 4}\). The reference construction multiplies this inverse film by \(\mu_i^2\), giving budgets \(\mu_i^2 / T_i^2\), \(\mu_i^2 / T_i^3\), and \(\mu_i^2 / T_i^4\). The same bounds hold for derivatives of orders \(j = 0, 1, 2\) along every front or rear path: apply the product rule to each displayed slot formula and use the corresponding Week estimate for each differentiated factor. The shifted momenta have path velocities among \(e_i, - e_i\) and their sums, whose norms are bounded by \(2\); this changes only fixed constants. Each derivative of total order \(j\) costs \(T_i^{- j}\), which is canceled by the \(T_i^j\) factor in the path bound. Since \(\mu_i^2 / T_i^2 \leqslant 1\), the inverse-reference film has the Week estimate with tab budget \(1\), and consequently spot and arch budgets \(T_i^{- 1}\) and \(T_i^{- 2}\).

The reference insertion is formed from nine copies of this inverse-reference film and then weighted by \(\mu_i^2\). For two films with tab/spot/arch budgets \((L, L / T, L / T^2)\) and \((M, M / T, M / T^2)\), the product has tab budget \(LM\). Its spot formula has two summands, each bounded by \(LM / T\); its arch formula has four summands, each bounded by \(LM / T^2\). Iterating through the nine factors changes only fixed numerical constants: the tab, spot, and arch rates remain \(1\), \(T_i^{- 1}\), and \(T_i^{- 2}\). The final weight gives \(\mu_i^2\), \(\mu_i^2 / T_i\), and \(\mu_i^2 / T_i^2\). This is the ghost regulator film \(R_{\text{gh}, \mu_i}\). Each directional sandwich in the vector regulator film \(R_{\text{vec}, \mu_i}\) has constant unit-budget factors on both sides, so it preserves these bounds. Summing the four directions changes only the common constant. This proves the displayed regulator budgets, in both channels, from the construction.

Because \(\mu_i \leqslant T_i\), adding the local and reference tab budgets gives \(C (T_i^2 + \mu_i^2) \leqslant 2 CT_i^2\). The total denominator has the same \(T_i^2\) tab budget on both the unshifted and boosted path. The bed floor proved above gives its inverse norm at \(t = 0\) at most \(C / T_i^2\). Differentiating the inverse with (11.42) gives both inverse bounds in (F6a). The reference arch budget is exactly \(C \mu_i^2 / T_i^2\), and the interacting arch has unit-scale budget \(C\), proving (F6b). The two total marked legs have budget at most \(C (T_i + \mu_i^2 / T_i) \leqslant 2 CT_i\), which proves (F6c). All constants here are uniform over the family of all centered records because the fees, compact caps, regulator bound, floor constant and centered-box radius are uniform.

On the shell, set \(T_i = \|x_i \|_2 > 0\). The unregulated reference denominator along the path is

\(\displaystyle R_i (t) = \|x_i + te_i \|_2^2 I, \qquad R_i (0) = T_i^2 I.\)

Its path budget is \(CT_i^2\) and its inverse has bound \(C / T_i^2\) by (11.42). Let \(S_i (t)\) be the total regulated denominator. Its difference from \(R_i\) splits into the local tab minus the free quadratic tab, which has budget \(CT_i^3\) by (11.59), and the regulator tab, which has budget \(C \mu_i^2\). Thus

\(\displaystyle \textsf{P}_{T_i} (S_i - R_i ; C (T_i^3 + \mu_i^2)) = \textsf{P}_{T_i} (S_i - R_i ; C \delta_i T_i^2) .\)

Both inverse paths have bound \(C / T_i^2\); the identity

\(\displaystyle S_i^{- 1} - R_i^{- 1} = - S_i^{- 1} (S_i - R_i) R_i^{- 1}\)

and the product rule therefore give the two inverse differences in (F6d) with bound \(C \delta_i / T_i^2\).

For the marked legs, subtract the unregulated free leg, which is the linear vector coefficient \(v_b\) (respectively \(w_b\) in the ghost channel). By the explicit coefficient calculation above the local marked row has this linear part. Their difference has radial order two. Hence the local marked row minus the halo leg has bound \(CT_i^2\) along the shell path. The regulated reference leg has the one-leg budget \(C \mu_i^2 / T_i\) from its path spot component. Since

\(\displaystyle T_i^2 \leqslant \delta_i T_i, \qquad \frac{\mu_i^2}{T_i} \leqslant \delta_i T_i,\)

their sum has bound \(C \delta_i T_i\), proving the leg part of (F6d). This derivation requires \(T_i > 0\) and is used only on the shell. At \(x_i = 0\) the unregulated reference path is singular, so no halo inverse is asserted there.

11.12.5Uniform path bounds from the selected records

For completeness, we describe how the path hypotheses used above are obtained uniformly for the selected family. Each selected local action has the same fee bound and positive strip width, and the external vacuum has its own common fee bound on that strip. Their sum therefore has a uniform fee bound. Cauchy's integral formula on a smaller common strip bounds every fixed momentum derivative of each matrix entry uniformly; for the tab use derivatives through order three, and for the spot and arch through order two. The momentum maps entering the three components are fixed linear maps, so the chain rule multiplies these estimates only by their fixed operator norms. The matrix dimension is fixed, hence the entrywise bounds imply operator-norm bounds. The additional fixed gauge-fixing film \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\) is not a polynomial in the momenta: its three slots are finite sums of products of lattice exponentials. They are entire and periodic, so their derivatives of every fixed order are bounded on the centered box. Adding these bounds gives the compact path caps used in (11.62).

The selected action's soft hypothesis is componentwise: its unmarked layer vanishes to radial order three, its one-mark layer to order two, and its two-mark layer to order one. The local marked insertion uses the one-mark layer composed with a linear two-momentum map, so it has the second-order vanishing required for the local contact. The grey vacuum's marked row, together with that of the gauge-fixing film, has the linear term \(v_b\): the three plaquette placements give the displayed Wilson coefficient, and the gauge-fixing film supplies the complementary part. Their difference has order two. Thus the selected combined row has exactly the tab and marked-leg contacts used in (11.59)–(11.60), with uniform compact caps.

Finally, the reference field is constructed from the same regulator \(\mu\). Its trajectory tab budget is \(\mu^2\), and its two other components carry the factors \(T^{- 1}\) and \(T^{- 2}\). The local field has tab budget \(T^2\). Since \(\mu \leqslant T\), their sum has tab budget at most \(2 T^2\). The floor of the record and the cold geometric bound give the total tab a coercive lower bound \(cT^2\); the elementary inverse calculation preceding (11.62) then yields the inverse estimates for both the unshifted and boosted paths. On the shell, the free comparison is positive because \(T = \|x\|_2 > 0\), and the same uniform strip/cap constants apply after resetting the scale. This verifies that the family-level path bounds used in the face proof are common to all selected rows and all regulators in the stated range.

11.13The annular pole and the two-wave estimate

Lüscher and Weisz [LW95, §1] compute perturbative coupling matching for the Wilson \(\text{SU} (N)\) action. The calculation below identifies the logarithmic coefficient for the present real Lie-algebra inner product and estimates the regulated finite-volume response used in the scale iteration.

The selected one-step construction needs two facts about one finite-volume response. Its logarithmic coefficient is an integral of the response over four-dimensional momentum space, and that response is close to the continuum quadratic pole on an annulus. This section gives the exact polar calculation and expands the finite-volume two-channel estimate that supplies the two radial bounds. The weighted-face estimates that feed it are proved in Subsection 11.12; they are stated here at their precise input interface.

11.13.1From the finite response to a momentum integral

Let \(E_4 = (- \pi, \pi]^4\) be the fundamental momentum cube in Euclidean coordinates, with normalized measure \(c_{\text{wt}} \hspace{0.17em} dx\) and \(c_{\text{wt}} = (2 \pi)^{- 4}\). It has finite volume and contains the unit ball. Two different objects occur, and the coefficient functional is defined on the second.

A response kernel \(F\) has entries \(F_{\xi \zeta} (p, x)\), indexed by two marks (direction and color) and depending on an external momentum \(p\) and an internal momentum \(x\). We assume that every entry is jointly holomorphic, periodic and bounded on a common strip of positive width in \((p, x)\). Its associated picture is one half of the normalized integral over the internal momentum,

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_{\xi \zeta} (p) = \frac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} c_{\text{wt}} \int_{E_4} F_{\xi \zeta} (p, x) \hspace{0.17em} dx.\)

For a two-leg picture \(\mathcal{Y}\) and a color \(a\), the coefficient functional is twice the real part of the quadratic Taylor coefficient along the axis \(e_0\) of the component with both marks equal to \((1, a)\):

\(\displaystyle \alpha_a (\mathcal{Y}) = 2 \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} [t^2] \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Y}_{(1, a) (1, a)} (te_0) = \text{Re} \frac{d^2}{dt^2} \mathcal{Y}_{(1, a) (1, a)} (te_0) |_{t = 0} .\)

For the kernel put

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}_a (x) = \frac{1}{2} \frac{d^2}{dt^2} F_{(1, a) (1, a)} (te_0, x) |_{t = 0}, \qquad g_a (x) = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{K}_a (x) .\)

Differentiation under the integral gives, for the picture \(\mathcal{Y}\) associated with \(F\),

\(\displaystyle \alpha_a (\mathcal{Y}) = c_{\text{wt}} \int_{E_4} g_a (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx. \) (11.63)

The common analytic bound and Cauchy's estimate bound the second \(t\)-derivative of the integrand uniformly in \(x\), which justifies the differentiation and shows that \(g_a\) is bounded and continuous on the closure of \(E_4\). Equation (11.63) is an identity for the analytically assembled response picture. The comparison of that picture with the finite sampled pole test is a separate recording estimate with an error term; it is not an equality and is not asserted by (11.63).

For \(x \in \mathbb{R}^4 \setminus \{0\}\) let \(r = |x|\) and define the gauge pole

\(\displaystyle \Pi_G (x) = 4 C_G \left( \frac{x_1^2}{r^6} - 4 \frac{x_1^2 x_0^2}{r^8} - \frac{1}{r^4} \right), \) (11.64)

where the gauge group is simple and \(C_G\) is its adjoint Casimir in the normalization of the colour inner product on the Lie algebra. For a group of unitary \(r_G \times r_G\) matrices this inner product is

\(\displaystyle \langle u, v \rangle = - \frac{1}{r_G} \text{Retr} (uv),\)

the colour basis \((e_b)\) used below is orthonormal for it, and

\(\displaystyle - \text{tr} (\text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} u \hspace{0.17em} \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} v) = C_G \langle u, v \rangle, \qquad C_G = - \frac{1}{\dim \mathfrak{g}} \sum_b \text{tr} \left( (\text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} e_b)^2 \right) .\)

Simplicity is what makes the left side a multiple of the inner product. The normalization matters for the constant \(\lambda_G\) below: with the trace form \(- \text{tr} (uv)\) in place of \(\langle u, v \rangle\) the constant \(C_G\) would be divided by \(r_G\). For \(SU (N)\) in its defining representation, \(C_G = 2 N^2\). The spherical area of \(S^3\) is \(2 \pi^2\). Rotational invariance gives

\(\displaystyle \int_{S^3} \theta_1^2 \hspace{0.17em} d \theta = \frac{2 \pi^2}{4}, \qquad \int_{S^3} \theta_1^2 \theta_0^2 \hspace{0.17em} d \theta = \frac{2 \pi^2}{24} . \) (11.65)

For the second identity, set \(A = \int \theta_0^4\) and \(B = \int \theta_0^2 \theta_1^2\). Coordinate permutations make these independent of the distinct indices. From \(\sum_i \theta_i^2 = 1\), \(4 A + 12 B = 2 \pi^2\). Rotating the \(0\)-axis to \((e_0 + e_1) / \sqrt{2}\) gives \(A = (2 A + 6 B) / 4\), hence \(A = 3 B\) and \(B = 2 \pi^2 / 24\). The first identity follows by integrating \(\sum_i \theta_i^2 = 1\).

Consequently the angular mean of the parenthesis in (11.64) is

\(\displaystyle r^{- 4} \left( \frac{1}{4} - \frac{4}{24} - 1 \right) = - \frac{11}{12} r^{- 4} .\)

Let \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\). Since the Euclidean polar Jacobian is \(r^3 dr \hspace{0.17em} d \theta\),

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mu < |x| \leqslant 1} |x|^{- 4} \hspace{0.17em} dx = 2 \pi^2 \int_{\mu}^1 \frac{dr}{r} = 2 \pi^2 \log \mu^{- 1} .\)

Using \(c_{\text{wt}} = (2 \pi)^{- 4}\), we obtain the exact pole law

\(\displaystyle c_{\text{wt}} \int_{\mu < |x| \leqslant 1} \Pi_G (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx = - \frac{11 C_G}{24 \pi^2} \log \mu^{- 1} = - \lambda_G \log \mu^{- 1}, \qquad \lambda_G = \frac{11 C_G}{24 \pi^2} . \) (11.66)

This fixes the sign and normalization of the logarithm; in particular the pole is not defined only up to an angular constant.

11.13.2The finite-volume two-channel calculation

We now state the two scale receipts used by the finite-volume construction. Let \(x \in [- \pi, \pi]^4\) be a centered momentum, \(r = |x|\), and \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\). The response kernel is the one of Subsection 11.12: for a local record with color \(b\) and regulator \(\mu\), it is one half of the two-leg figure of the vector-channel total film minus that of the ghost-channel total film. Write \(\mathcal{K} (x) \in \mathbb{C}\) for its coefficient \(\mathcal{K}_b (x)\) defined before (11.63), and \(g (x) = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{K} (x)\). With the faces \(U, L, H\) of (11.46) in that section, formed in each of the two channels with the same background \(x\), the same mark \((1, b)\), the same boost \(e_0\) and the same regulator \(\mu\), the six complex second-derivative traces are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} D = U_{\text{vec}}'' (0), & d = L_{\text{vec}}'' (0), & d_0 = H_{\text{vec}}'' (0),\\ Y = U_{\text{gh}}'' (0), & c = L_{\text{gh}}'' (0), & c_0 = H_{\text{gh}}'' (0) . \end{array}\)

The halo traces \(c_0, d_0\) and the pole \(\Pi_G\) are defined only for \(r > 0\). The exact identities are

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}= \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{1}{2} (D - d) - (Y - c) \right), \qquad \Pi_G = \frac{1}{2} \left( c_0 - \frac{1}{2} d_0 \right),\) (11.67)
\(\displaystyle \mathcal{K}- \Pi_G = \frac{1}{2} \left( \frac{1}{2} D - Y \right) + \frac{1}{2} \left( (c - c_0) - \frac{1}{2} (d - d_0) \right) . \) (11.68)

The first identity holds at every centered \(x\), including \(x = 0\): the two-leg figure of a film is the trace of its upper face minus that of its lower face, and the response kernel is one half of the vector figure minus the ghost figure. The second is the free halo identity, with marked direction and color fixed; it and (11.68) hold for \(r > 0\) and are used only on \(\mu \leqslant r\). The third is their algebraic difference; the real-valued pole \(\Pi_G\) is embedded in \(\mathbb{C}\) in these formulas. None of the three involves a torus side.

For clarity, the second identity follows directly from the two free propagator numerators, that is, from the linear legs \(w_b\) (ghost) and \(v_b\) (vector) of Subsection 11.12 and the free inverses \(|k|^{- 2} I\). In the selected unit color direction \(e_b\), use the normalization \(\text{tr} (\text{ad} (e_b)^2) = - C_G\). Put \(\rho = |x|^2\), \(c_{\text{col}} = - C_G\), and \(L = - 4 x_1^2\). Along the marked displacement \(te_0\), the common denominator is \(\Delta (t) = \rho + 2 x_0 t + t^2\). The ghost numerator is the scalar orbital factor \(L\) times the color trace, giving \(Lc_{\text{col}} / \rho\). For the vector numerator, write \(p = - 2 ix_1\) and let \(A (t)\) have entries \(A_{01} (t) = - 2 it\), \(A_{10} (t) = 2 it\), and all other entries zero. Then \(p^2 = L\) and direct multiplication gives

\(\displaystyle \text{tr} [(pI + A (- t)) (pI + A (t))] = 4 p^2 - 8 t^2 = 4 L - 8 t^2 .\)

Multiplying by the same color trace and scalar denominator factor gives the vector numerator \(\rho^{- 1} (4 L - 8 t^2) c_{\text{col}}\). Differentiating the quotients twice at \(t = 0\) gives

\(\displaystyle \left( \frac{Lc_{\text{col}} / \rho}{\Delta} \right)''_{t = 0} = - \frac{2 Lc_{\text{col}}}{\rho^3} + \frac{8 Lc_{\text{col}} x_0^2}{\rho^4}, \qquad \left( \frac{\rho^{- 1} (4 L - 8 t^2) c_{\text{col}}}{\Delta} \right)''_{t = 0} = - \frac{16 c_{\text{col}}}{\rho^2} - \frac{8 Lc_{\text{col}}}{\rho^3} + \frac{32 Lc_{\text{col}} x_0^2}{\rho^4} .\)

Thus the orbital trace minus one half of the vector trace is

\(\displaystyle \frac{8 c_{\text{col}}}{\rho^2} + \frac{2 Lc_{\text{col}}}{\rho^3} - \frac{8 Lc_{\text{col}} x_0^2}{\rho^4} = 8 C_G \left( \frac{x_1^2}{r^6} - 4 \frac{x_1^2 x_0^2}{r^8} - \frac{1}{r^4} \right) = 2 \Pi_G (x),\)

which proves the halo identity in (11.67). (11.68) is then obtained by subtracting the just-computed free identity from the first identity and collecting terms.

The first family of estimates is the all-centered-record receipt, with \(T_1 = r + \mu\) and \(R_0 = 1 + 2 \pi\):

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant r, \quad T_1 = r + \mu \leqslant R_0, \qquad |\mathcal{K}| \leqslant A_1 T_1^{- 4} . \) (11.69)

The second is the shell receipt, available only on \(\mu \leqslant r \leqslant 1\) and using \(T_2 = r\):

\(\displaystyle |D| \leqslant ur^{- 2} + v \mu^2 r^{- 6}, \qquad |Y| \leqslant u_{\text{ref}} r^{- 2} + v_{\text{ref}} \mu^2 r^{- 6}, \) (11.70)
\(\displaystyle |c - c_0 | \leqslant A_0 (r + \mu^2 r^{- 2}) r^{- 4}, \qquad |d - d_0 | \leqslant B_0 (r + \mu^2 r^{- 2}) r^{- 4} . \) (11.71)

All constants \(A_1, u, v, u_{\text{ref}}, v_{\text{ref}}, A_0, B_0\) are nonnegative and are chosen before the local record: they do not depend on its regulator, color, bed, clip or strip width. The subscript ref marks the ghost channel. These two receipts are kept separate: the all-centered estimate uses scale \(r + \mu\), while the shell comparison uses scale \(r\). They are the conclusions (11.51) and (11.52) of Proposition 11.19, applied in the two channels to the family of all centered records with the bounds derived there (Lemma 11.20 and the trajectory-bound paragraph): (11.69) follows from (11.51) at scale \(r + \mu\) and the first identity in (11.67), (11.70) is the upper bound in (11.51) at scale \(r\), and (11.71) is (11.52) at scale \(r\).

Proposition 11.21. (Two wave bounds from the finite response) Suppose (11.67)–(11.71) hold uniformly. Then

\(\displaystyle |g (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_1}{(r + \mu)^4}, \qquad |g (x) - \Pi_G (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_2}{r^3} + \frac{A_2 \mu^2}{r^6} \quad (\mu \leqslant r \leqslant 1), \) (11.72)

where

\(\displaystyle A_2 = \max \{u + u_{\text{ref}} + A_0 + B_0, v + v_{\text{ref}} + A_0 + B_0 \} .\)

Proof. The first estimate follows from (11.69) and \(| \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} z| \leqslant |z|\). On the annulus, use (11.68) and (11.70)–(11.71):

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{K}- \Pi_G | \leqslant (u + u_{\text{ref}}) r^{- 2} + (v + v_{\text{ref}}) \mu^2 r^{- 6} + (A_0 + B_0) (r^{- 3} + \mu^2 r^{- 6}) . \) (11.73)

Here \(\tfrac{1}{2}\) factors are bounded by one, and \((r + \mu^2 r^{- 2}) r^{- 4} = r^{- 3} + \mu^2 r^{- 6}\). Since \(r \leqslant 1\), \(r^{- 2} \leqslant r^{- 3}\). The displayed \(A_2\) bounds (11.73) by \(A_2 (r^{- 3} + \mu^2 r^{- 6})\). Taking real parts gives \(|g - \Pi_G | \leqslant |\mathcal{K}- \Pi_G |\), proving (11.72).\(\Box\)

11.13.3The two applications of the finite-volume receipts

The common family of local records is used twice, with different scale parameters. Each record supplies a centered momentum \(x\), a positive regulator \(\mu \leqslant 1\), and the same complex response coefficient \(\mathcal{K} (x)\). The centered-box condition \(|x_j | \leqslant \pi\) gives \(|x| \leqslant 2 \pi\).

For the all-centered estimate, use scale \(T_1 = |x| + \mu\). The whole-frame bound from the two channels is

\(\displaystyle |D - d| \leqslant A_{\text{int}} T_1^{- 4}, \qquad |Y - c| \leqslant A_{\text{ref}} T_1^{- 4} .\)

The exact first identity in (11.67) then gives \(|\mathcal{K}| \leqslant (A_{\text{int}} / 4 + A_{\text{ref}} / 2) T_1^{- 4}\). This is the first radial estimate; it applies also at \(x = 0\).

For the annular comparison, restrict the same records to \(\mu \leqslant |x|\) and use the second scale \(T_2 = |x|\). The upper face of the vector channel is split into a principal term and a tadpole term; their charged bounds are \(uT_2^{- 2}\) and \(v \mu^2 T_2^{- 6}\). The same split for the ghost channel has bounds \(u_{\text{ref}} T_2^{- 2}\) and \(v_{\text{ref}} \mu^2 T_2^{- 6}\). The lower-channel remainders after subtracting their respective halos are bounded by \(A_0 (T_2 + \mu^2 / T_2^2) T_2^{- 4}\) and \(B_0 (T_2 + \mu^2 / T_2^2) T_2^{- 4}\). The free quotient calculation gives \(\Pi_G = \frac{1}{2} (c_0 - \frac{1}{2} d_0)\), so these four estimates feed exactly into (11.68) and yield (11.70)–(11.73).
