These scale receipts are thus two separate applications of the finite-volume receipts to the common family of local records: the first applies the frame estimate to every centered record, while the second restricts to the subfamily \(\mu \leqslant |x|\) before applying the shell estimate. They use the same response coefficient and common constants. The annulus proof therefore does not interchange a limit with a volume-dependent integral. The comparison of this analytic coefficient with the finite sampled pole test is a separate recording estimate, with an error equal to the site count times a quantity tending to zero; exact equality with the finite sampled coefficient is not asserted, and nothing of the kind is inferred from (11.63).

Scope.
This section proves the normalized pole integral, the halo identity and the algebraic conversion from the two scale receipts to the radial bounds. The receipts themselves are proved in Subsection 11.12 for the family of all centered records. The recording estimate is proved in Subsection 11.14. The order of choices there differs from the one for the radial constants: the fee strip of the response and the constants \(A_1, A_2\) are fixed before the blocking side \(L\), whereas the strip width \(z_L\) and the bound \(K_L\) of the regulated recording kernel are obtained after \(L\) is fixed, with \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), and may depend on \(L\). For fixed \(L\) the recording error is the site count times \(\mathfrak{r} (z_L, K_L) e^{- z_L \ell / 4}\), in the notation of that section, which tends to zero along every sequence of sides \(\ell \to \infty\).

11.14Finite-volume recording and uniform drift

The continuum pole calculation controls a coefficient of the analytically assembled response. The finite-volume argument needs two additional steps: an exact determinant identity at each torus size, and a quantitative bound on the difference between a torus mean and its zero Fourier mode. This section proves that bound, explains the determinant calculation and positive-matrix domain, and gives the exact scale order in the final annulus output. The local estimates used to feed this construction are those of Sections 11.12 and 11.13.

Write \(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma} (A)\) for the class of periodic functions that are holomorphic when each imaginary momentum coordinate has modulus less than \(\sigma\) and have supremum norm at most \(A\) there. The coefficient of a translation-invariant two-leg picture is its mixed second derivative; its finite-volume realization at a conserving pair is \(\ell^4\) times that coefficient. Write \(\text{Err}_{\ell} (\mathcal{Y}, \mathcal{P}; \delta)\) when every mixed two-leg coefficient of the finite test \(\mathcal{P}\) differs from this finite-volume realization for \(\mathcal{Y}\) by at most \(\ell^4 \delta\).

11.14.1A torus mean differs from the zero mode by an exponential tail

Let \(\ell \geqslant 1\) be an integer and let \(f\) be a periodic function of four real momenta, holomorphic and bounded by \(A\) on the complex strip of width \(\sigma > 0\). Write its absolutely convergent Fourier series as

\(\displaystyle f (\theta) = \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}^4} c_n e^{in \cdot \theta}, \qquad |n|_1 = \sum_{j = 0}^3 |n_j | .\)

The normalized mean over the side-\(\ell\) grid is

\(\displaystyle \langle f \rangle_{\ell} = \ell^{- 4} \sum_{q \in (\mathbb{Z}/ \ell \mathbb{Z})^4} f (2 \pi q / \ell) .\)

Character orthogonality in each coordinate says

\(\displaystyle \ell^{- 4} \sum_q e^{2 \pi in \cdot q / \ell} =\boldsymbol{1}_{n \in \ell \mathbb{Z}^4} .\)

Consequently \(\langle f \rangle_{\ell} - c_0\) is the sum of precisely the nonzero Fourier coefficients whose indices lie in \(\ell \mathbb{Z}^4\). Every such index satisfies \(|n|_1 \geqslant \ell\). Cauchy's integral formula, applied after shifting each coordinate contour by \(\pm i (\sigma / 2)\), and the geometric-series bound in four coordinates give

\(\displaystyle \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}^4} e^{(\sigma / 4) |n|_1} |c_n | \leqslant A \left( 2 \frac{1 + \sigma / 4}{\sigma / 4} \right)^4 . \) (11.74)

For completeness, the one-coordinate sum used here is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} e^{- h |k|} = 1 + 2 \sum_{k \geqslant 1} e^{- hk} \leqslant 2 \frac{1 + h}{h}, \qquad h = \sigma / 4 ;\)

the last inequality follows from \(e^h \geqslant 1 + h\). The contour shift gives the factor \(e^{- (\sigma / 2) |n|_1}\) for an individual coefficient, leaving the weight \(e^{(\sigma / 4) |n|_1}\) in (11.74). Therefore

\(\displaystyle | \langle f \rangle_{\ell} - c_0 | \leqslant \mathfrak{r} (\sigma, A) e^{- \sigma \ell / 4}, \qquad \mathfrak{r} (\sigma, A) = A \left( 2 \frac{1 + \sigma / 4}{\sigma / 4} \right)^4 . \) (11.75)

This estimate is for the actual full grid at each \(\ell\); no subsequence selection is involved.

Lemma 11.22. (Recorded second-order coefficients) Let \(F_{ij} (p, q)\) be two-momentum response entries in \(\mathcal{H}_{\sigma} (A)\) jointly in \(p, q\). Let \(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\) be a real finite-volume test and assume its exact local coefficient identity: for every pair of colored sites \(q, q'\), the mixed second coefficient of \(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\) is zero when \(q + q' \ne 0\), and otherwise is

\(\displaystyle \ell^4 \hspace{0.17em} \langle F_{ij} (\text{mon} (q'), \hspace{0.17em} \cdot \hspace{0.17em}) \rangle_{\ell} . \) (11.76)

Define the internal zero mode and limiting picture by

\(\displaystyle \hat{F}_{ij, 0} (p) = \int_{\mathbb{T}^4} F_{ij} (p, q) \hspace{0.17em} dq, \qquad \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p) = \tfrac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} \hat{F}_{ij, 0} (p),\)

where Haar measure on \(\mathbb{T}^4\) is normalized to one. We use the translation-invariant two-leg convention in which the mixed second derivative is the polarized coefficient (there is no factorial in the definition of that coefficient). Thus the finite-volume realization at a conserving pair is \(\ell^4\) times this coefficient, namely \(\ell^4 \hspace{0.17em} 2\mathcal{Y}_{ij}\). Then \(\mathcal{Y}\) is interpreted as the translation-invariant two-leg picture: its mixed coefficient at external momenta \(p, p'\) is zero unless \(p + p' = 0\), and in the conserving case is \(2\mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p') = \hat{F}_{ij, 0} (p')\). \(\mathcal{Y} \in \mathcal{H}_{\sigma / 2} (A)\), and for every side \(\ell\),

\(\displaystyle \text{Err}_{\ell} (\mathcal{Y}, \mathcal{P}_{\ell} ; \mathfrak{r}(\sigma, A) e^{- \sigma \ell / 4}) . \) (11.77)

In the normalized convention \(C_2 = D^2 / 2\)! used for coefficient pictures elsewhere (see (11.91)), (11.77) says that for unit Fourier waves \(v, w\) of momenta \(p, p'\) and marks \(i, j\)

\(\displaystyle \left| C_2 (\mathcal{P}_{\ell}) [v, w] -\boldsymbol{1}_{p + p' = 0} \hspace{0.17em} \ell^4 \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p') \right| \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} \ell^4 \hspace{0.17em} \mathfrak{r} (\sigma, A) e^{- \sigma \ell / 4} ; \) (11.78)

in particular \(\mathcal{Y}\) records \(\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\) in the sense of (11.91) with error \(\ell^4 \mathfrak{r} (\sigma, A) e^{- \sigma \ell / 4}\).

Proof. The normalized Haar integral defining \(\hat{F}_{ij, 0}\) is holomorphic in \(p\) on the width-\(\sigma\) strip: on each compact substrip its integrand and derivatives are uniformly bounded, so differentiation under the integral is valid. Its norm is at most \(A\), hence the half-scaled picture is bounded by \(A\) on the width-\(\sigma / 2\) strip. For each fixed real \(p\), apply (11.75) to the one-variable function \(q \mapsto F_{ij} (p, q)\); its Fourier zero mode is exactly \(\hat{F}_{ij, 0} (p)\). This gives

\(\displaystyle | \langle F_{ij} (p, \cdot) \rangle_{\ell} - \hat{F}_{ij, 0} (p) | \leqslant \mathfrak{r} (\sigma, A) e^{- \sigma \ell / 4}\)

on the real external momentum torus. If \(q + q' \ne 0\), both mixed coefficients in the definition of \(\text{Err}_{\ell}\) vanish by momentum conservation. If \(q + q' = 0\), the second Taylor coefficient of \(\mathcal{Y}= \frac{1}{2} \hat{F}_{ij, 0}\) has the factor \(2! = 2\), so it equals \(\hat{F}_{ij, 0}\); subtracting (11.76) and multiplying the mean estimate by the number \(\ell^4\) of sites gives (11.77). Dividing by \(2\)! gives (11.78).\(\Box\)

For a finite side \(\ell\), set

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_{\ell, ij} (p) = \tfrac{1}{2} \hspace{0.17em} \ell^{- 4} \sum_{q \in (\mathbb{Z}/ \ell \mathbb{Z})^4} F_{ij} (p, 2 \pi q / \ell)\)

at each external grid momentum. By (11.76), its second finite-volume coefficient is \(2 \ell^4 \mathcal{Y}_{\ell, ij} (p)\). The limiting picture also has the exchange and reality symmetries

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p) =\mathcal{Y}_{ji} (- p), \qquad \overline{\mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p)} =\mathcal{Y}_{ij} (- p) . \) (11.79)

At each finite side, the logarithmic determinant is a real scalar function of real field coordinates. Equality of its mixed derivatives gives exchange of the two external legs, while reality of its Hessian gives conjugation under Fourier inversion. Thus the finite-volume normalized pictures have both relations at every allowed grid momentum. Estimate (11.75) says \(\mathcal{Y}_{\ell, ij} (p) \to \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p)\) uniformly over the external grid points as \(\ell \to \infty\). Thus their difference from \(\mathcal{Y}\) tends uniformly to zero on these grids as the side tends to infinity. The union of the full side grids is dense in the momentum torus, and \(\mathcal{Y}\) is continuous by its analytic strip bound. Passing along cofinal sides and then using density proves (11.79) everywhere. This supplies the Hessian symmetry and reality of the half-scaled zero-mode picture; it does not follow from factorial normalization alone.

The factor \(1 / 2\) in \(\mathcal{Y}\) is essential: the finite-volume mixed second derivative of \(\frac{1}{2} \hat{F}_{ij, 0}\) contributes \(2! \hspace{0.17em} \frac{1}{2} \hat{F}_{ij, 0} = \hat{F}_{ij, 0}\). This is why (11.76) compares the normalized grid mean to the continuum coefficient and why the error is the per-site Fourier error multiplied by the site count exactly once.

11.14.2Determinant identity and positive-matrix domain

In the application, the pole test is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P}_{\ell} (x) = \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det P_{\ell} (x) - \log \det V_{\ell} (x),\)

where \(P_{\ell}\) is the positive rank matrix and \(V_{\ell}\) the positive reference matrix. The finite ticket identifies the zero-, one-, and two-leg Taylor layers of \(P_{\ell}\) with those of the interacting response, and those of \(V_{\ell}\) with the reference response. The floor and positive-regulator hypotheses make both matrices positive definite at \(x = 0\); by continuity their real logarithmic determinants are smooth nearby. Ordinary matrix differentiation gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} D \log \det P [H] & = \text{tr} (P^{- 1} DP [H]),\\ D^2 \log \det P [H, K] & = \text{tr} \hspace{-0.17em} (P^{- 1} D^2 P [H, K] - P^{- 1} DP [K] P^{- 1} DP [H]) . \end{aligned} \ \) (11.80)

and the same formulas for \(V\). Write \(A (r)\) for either matrix family, \(B_i (p, r)\) for its first variation (from background momentum \(r\) to \(r + p\)), and \(C_{ij} (p, q, r)\) for its mixed second variation (from \(r\) to \(r + p + q\)). The shifted fiber block of the mixed derivative of the logarithmic determinant at the ordered pair of legs is the trace of

\(\displaystyle A (r + p + q)^{- 1} C_{ij} (p, q, r) - A (r + p + q)^{- 1} B_i (p, r + q) A (r + q)^{- 1} B_j (q, r) . \) (11.81)

This follows by differentiating \(D \log \det A [H] = \text{tr} (A^{- 1} DA [H])\) and using

\(\displaystyle D (A^{- 1}) [K] = - A^{- 1} DA [K] A^{- 1} .\)

There is one ordered product for the specified ordered pair; exchanging the legs gives the corresponding other entry, not an extra copy in (11.81). Sum this expression over internal momentum and apply the weights in the finite ticket. Formula (11.81) is the shifted fiber block of the Hessian. Translation invariance makes the full trace entry zero unless the external momenta conserve; in the conserving case summing the fiber blocks gives the normalized torus mean multiplied by \(\ell^4\). This finite-volume determinant calculation precedes the Fourier tail limit.

The Fourier estimate is entrywise. Any closed-cone passage for finite reflection matrices and the smooth-word approximation to point configurations belong to the separate reflection argument.

11.14.3Order of choices in the all-period output

Here is the exact downstream implication, with its inputs exposed. Fix \(\sigma, \epsilon, \tau > 0\) and \(C, Z, A \geqslant 0\). Assume a baseline analytic family \(H\) has strip fee \((k, E)\) with \(k > 0\), \(E \geqslant 0\), and matches the free Wilson response at every volume. Assume the response service chooses \(v > 0, M \geqslant 0\), depending only on these external data, with the following property: for every sequence of positive integer sides \(\ell_j \to \infty\), every bed \(B\), clip \(p\), path \(R\), and budget \(\Lambda\) satisfying the path bound \((\tau, A, \Lambda)\) in \(p\), attachment of \(B\) to \(R\), metric strip bound \((\sigma, C)\), direct-term bound \(Z\), the floors \((\epsilon, C)\) of \(B, p\) at every torus side (not only at the sides \(\ell_j\)), and the sworn condition at every \(\ell_j\), it provides a family \(T\) with fee \((v, M)\), whose zero-, one-, and two-leg layers vanish to orders three, two, and one respectively, and exact analytic cancellation of the target response against the Wilson term at every \(\ell_j\). Require also the exact finite-layer realization: for every \(\ell_j\), the interacting and reference matrices are real analytic on the real momentum torus, Hermitian positive definite at the origin with a positive lower eigenvalue bound uniform in \(j\), and their Taylor layers of orders zero, one and two are the layers specified by the response ticket. The resulting shifted-fiber kernel \(F_{c, \ell_j} (p, q)\) is the restriction of one common periodic kernel \(F_c (p, q)\), holomorphic and bounded by some \(K\) whenever every imaginary momentum coordinate has modulus less than some \(z > 0\), uniformly in \(j\) and color \(c\). This is the common-strip and common-layer content needed for the full-grid limit; positivity at the origin alone does not imply it. Assume also the uniform two-wave output: for every combined family below, every color \(c\), and every regulator \(0 < y \leqslant 1\), the same constants \(a, b \geqslant 0\) give

\(\displaystyle | \kappa_{y, c} (x) | \leqslant a (|x| + y)^{- 4}, \qquad | \kappa_{y, c} (x) - \Pi_{G, c} (x) | \leqslant b |x|^{- 3} + by^2 |x|^{- 6} \quad (y \leqslant |x| \leqslant 1) \) (11.82)

on the centered momentum box. This states the uniform local estimate needed from the next producer; it is not deduced from the finite-volume recording argument.

Theorem 11.23. (All-period recording and drift) Under these hypotheses, for every admissible path datum, every odd integer \(L \geqslant 1\), and every side sequence \(\ell_j \to \infty\) there are a picture \(\mathcal{Y}_L\), constants \(z_L > 0\), \(K_L \geqslant 0\), and numbers \(e_{L, j} \geqslant 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_L \in \mathcal{H}_{z_L / 2} (K_L), \qquad e_{L, j} =\mathfrak{r} (z_L, K_L) e^{- z_L \ell_j / 4} \longrightarrow 0, \qquad \text{Err}_{\ell_j} (\mathcal{Y}_L, \mathcal{P}_{\ell_j} ; e_{L, j}), \) (11.83)

and, for every color \(c\),

\(\displaystyle | \alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}_L) + \lambda_G \log L | \leqslant D_0, \quad D_0 = c_{\text{wt}} \left[ a \left( \frac{\pi^2}{2} + |E_4 | \right) + 3 \pi^2 b \right] . \) (11.84)

The constant \(D_0\) is independent of \(L\) and of the side sequence.

Proof. Choose the baseline before the response family. Put \(w = \min (k, v) > 0\); restrict both fee bounds to \(w\) and add the families, obtaining fee \((w, M + E)\). Now fix \(L\) and set

\(\displaystyle y = L^{- 1}, \qquad s = \min \{\sigma, w, y / 128\} > 0. \) (11.85)

Then \(0 < y \leqslant 1\), and \(s \leqslant y / 128\) is the phase condition in the local determinant-floor estimate. At every \(\ell_j\), the response ticket matches the target minus the Wilson term, while the baseline ticket matches the Wilson term. Adding their zero-, one-, and two-leg Taylor identities gives the target ticket. The finite determinant formula (11.80) turns this into the exact local coefficient identity (11.76), and the floor and positive regulator bounds ensure both determinants are nonzero at zero. The floors at every torus side and the metric assumptions identify the regulated interacting table with the tested local determinant table; the reference table is identified by its explicit regulator formula. These are the two tab identities required by the local positivity/floor argument at scale \(s\). The exact finite-layer and common-strip receipt in the hypotheses supplies \(z_L > 0\), \(K_L \geqslant 0\) and the common analytic two-momentum kernel in \(\mathcal{H}_{z_L} (K_L)\). Applying Lemma 11.22 yields

\(\displaystyle e_{L, j} =\mathfrak{r} (z_L, K_L) e^{- z_L \ell_j / 4}, \qquad \text{Err}_{\ell_j} (\mathcal{Y}_L, \mathcal{P}_{\ell_j} ; e_{L, j}) . \) (11.86)

The positive \(z_L\) and \(\ell_j \to \infty\) imply \(e_{L, j} \to 0\) for this fixed \(L\). No uniformity of \(z_L, K_L\) in \(L\) is required or asserted.

The same response satisfies (11.82), uniformly in color \(c\), at regulator \(y\). The coefficient-integral identity (11.63) of Subsection 11.13 identifies \(\alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}_L)\) with the normalized integral over \(E_4\) of \(g = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} \kappa_{y, c}\), which is bounded and continuous, and (11.66) gives the pole integral \(c_{\text{wt}} \int_{y < |x| \leqslant 1} \Pi_G = - \lambda_G \log y^{- 1}\). These are the hypotheses of Lemma 11.32, with \(A_1 = a\), \(A_2 = b\) and \(\mu = y\); its conclusion is (11.84), with the same \(D_0\) for every color. Explicitly, split \(E_4\) into the ball \(|x| \leqslant y\), the annulus \(y < |x| \leqslant 1\) and the rest. On the ball the first bound in (11.82) gives \(|g| \leqslant ay^{- 4}\) and the volume is \(\pi^2 y^4 / 2\). Outside the unit ball \(|g| \leqslant a\). On the annulus \(\int |x|^{- 3} dx = 2 \pi^2 (1 - y)\) and \(y^2 \int |x|^{- 6} dx = \pi^2 (1 - y^2)\), so the second bound in (11.82) contributes at most \(3 \pi^2 b\). Finally \(\log y^{- 1} = \log L\), while \(a, b\) were fixed uniformly before choosing \(L\). Taking \(L_{\min} = 1\) supplies the all-odd-side clause needed by the outer construction; oddness is not otherwise used in this estimate.\(\Box\)

Scope.
This proves the finite Fourier tail, the exact conversion of local coefficient identities into a site-count-scaled recording error, and the downstream assembly from the stated baseline, response-service, local-positivity/floor, and uniform two-wave outputs. Of these four hypotheses, the finite-layer realization with its common strip is obtained in Lemmas 11.24 and 11.25. The fixed-film identities are proved in Lemma 11.27, and the Wilson and correction response services in Lemma 11.26 and its applications. The uniform two-wave output (11.82) is conclusion (11.57) of Subsection 11.12. Their combined statement is Proposition 11.28, with the path, metric and floor hypotheses displayed there. No finite-volume coefficient is identified with the continuum integral without first passing through (11.86).

11.15The finite-volume and annulus estimates use one response

The finite-volume recording and the annulus bound must be attached to the same analytic response. This is a short compatibility step, but it prevents an invalid substitution of a different family after taking the Fourier limit. We state the join explicitly.

Fix a simple gauge group, a clip \(p\) and path \(R\), a bed \(B\), and a sequence of torus sides \(n_i\) tending to infinity. Responses, their fees, their three marked layers and greyness are as defined in Subsection 11.12. We say that a response has the tickets of an analytic function \(\mathcal{U}\) of the link field on a torus if its zero-, one- and two-mark layers record exactly, on that torus, the marked derivatives of \(\mathcal{U}\): the derivatives at the origin of \((y, z) \mapsto \mathcal{U} (e^y e^z)\) of order two in \(y\) and of order zero, one and two in \(z\). Let \(W\) be one fee-bounded analytic Wilson response which is grey: it has the tickets of the Wilson action at every torus side, not only at the sides \(n_i\). This is the form in which the face estimates use it. Let \(Q\) be one fee-bounded correction response, with soft orders three, two, and one in its zero-, one-, and two-mark layers, which at every chosen side \(n_i\) has the tickets of the finite action of \(p, R\) minus the Wilson action. Assume, for this same bed and path: the path bounds of \(R\) in \(p\); the attachment of \(B\) to \(R\); a metric strip bound for \(B\); the floor of \(B, p\) with a constant \(\epsilon > 0\) at every torus side; and the sworn condition at every chosen side \(n_i\). Finally, the local-record family of Subsection 11.12 is formed with \(V = W\) as its vacuum response. Assume \(W\) and \(Q\) have a common positive response strip width \(\eta\) and finite fee fixed before the odd side \(L\) is chosen. This is the width used for the uniform radial constants; it is distinct from the possibly smaller strip \(z_L\) of the regulated recording kernel. For the logarithmic estimate, fix \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\) before applying the radial bound, with \(\mu = L^{- 1}\) for the requested odd side \(L\). The Wilson fee, correction fee, floor parameter, and metric bound are common to the family; the bed, clip, and side sequence are the same in the recording and face estimates. The strip width and bound for the recorded kernel may depend on this fixed \(L\).

Lemma 11.24. (Same-response compatibility) Under these assumptions set \(F = Q + W\). Then the following conclusions concern this one \(F\) and the same selected data:

  1. For every chosen torus side, the zero-, one-, and two-mark tickets of \(F\) are exactly the corresponding finite-action tickets. These are the three marked response layers used to identify the action variations in the finite test. If the regulated rank and reference matrices have their positive floors, the finite pole test is their logarithmic-determinant difference, and its conserving mixed coefficient is the site count times the normalized mesh mean of the shifted-fiber kernel of \(F\).

  2. The analytic kernel obtained from those shifted-fiber coefficients is independent of the torus side. Define the analytic picture by

    \(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p) = \frac{1}{2} \int_{\mathbb{T}^4} F_{ij} (p, q) \hspace{0.17em} dq,\)

    with normalized Haar measure. At a conserving pair the exact finite-volume mixed derivative of the pole test is \(2 n_i^4 \mathcal{Y}_{n_i, ij} (p)\), where

    \(\displaystyle \mathcal{Y}_{n, ij} (p) = \frac{1}{2 n^4} \sum_{q \in (2 \pi / n) (\mathbb{Z}/ n\mathbb{Z})^4} F_{ij} (p, q)\)

    is formed with the mesh mean; it is not \(2 n_i^4 \mathcal{Y}_{ij} (p)\), the quantity with which \(\text{Err}\) compares it. The difference between the mesh picture and \(\mathcal{Y}\) is controlled as follows. If the common kernel has bound \(K\) on a strip of width \(z > 0\), the picture \(\mathcal{Y}\) satisfies the recorded estimate

    \(\displaystyle \text{Err}_{n_i} (\mathcal{Y}, \mathcal{P}_{n_i} ; \mathfrak{r}(z, K) e^{- zn_i / 4})\)

    for every chosen side \(n_i\).

  3. For each color \(c\), the radial bounds obtained from the same local-frame family and the same Wilson reference give

    \(\displaystyle | \alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}) + \lambda_G \log \mu^{- 1} | \leqslant D_0 .\)

    Here \(D_0\) is fixed before the odd side parameter is chosen, while the strip constants \(z, K\) and hence the recording error may depend on \(L\).

Proof. The fee bounds on \(Q\) and \(W\) restrict to their common smaller strip and add. The exact response identities add as well: at each chosen side the zero-, one-, and two-mark ticket of \(Q\) is the finite-action ticket minus the Wilson ticket, while the corresponding ticket of \(W\) is the Wilson ticket. Hence these three tickets of \(F\) are exactly the finite-action tickets. The order-zero remainder construction fixes the normalization; no extra factor of the number of sites is inserted here.

For each side, the positive floors make the two determinant matrices invertible at zero. Differentiating their logarithmic determinants gives one direct second variation and the single ordered product of first variations for each specified ordered pair. The ticket identity therefore identifies that finite mixed coefficient with the conserving mesh sum of the common shifted-fiber kernel. Nonconserving entries vanish by momentum conservation. The fiber trace contributes a site count exactly once. That the kernel is one function of the momenta, independent of the side, is immediate, since the same \(F\) is used. That it has a strip bound is not: the kernel contains the inverses of the two regulated denominators. The strip width \(z\) and the bound \(K\) are supplied by Lemma 11.25 below. The full-mesh Fourier estimate of Lemma 11.22 now applies to these exact finite identities, giving the displayed error at every side, without a subsequence extraction.

For the third conclusion, apply the all-centered and shell face estimates to the same selected local-frame family. The interacting and regulated reference paths are the ones formed from \(F\) and the fixed Wilson response; the shell comparison uses the same bed and only momenta with positive unregulated scale. The resulting two radial estimates are uniform in the color and were fixed before choosing the odd side. Integrating over the inner ball, the annulus, and the part outside the unit ball gives the logarithmic estimate by Lemma 11.32. The analytic coefficient in that integral is \(\alpha_c (\mathcal{Y})\) for the picture defined above: both it and the finite recorded coefficients are obtained from the same shifted-fiber kernel, with the factor \(1 / 2\) canceled by the two-leg mixed derivative. This proves that the finite recording and the radial drift belong to one response.\(\Box\)

11.15.1The common strip of the regulated kernel

Fix \(L\), put \(\mu = L^{- 1} \in (0, 1]\), and let \(F = Q + W\) be as above, with all three layers holomorphic and periodic on a strip of width \(w > 0\). Let the metric strip bound of \(B\) hold at width \(\sigma > 0\) and put

\(\displaystyle s = \min \{\sigma, w, \mu / 128\} .\)

In the notation of Subsection 11.12, the vector total film is the film of \(F\) plus \(\textsf{BB}^{\text{r}}\) plus \(R_{\text{vec}, \mu}\), and the ghost total film is \(Q_{\text{gh}} + R_{\text{gh}, \mu}\). For a film with tab \(a\) and slots \(b, c\), the two-leg figure is

\(\displaystyle \text{fig}_{\xi \zeta} (p, q) = \text{tr} \left[ a (q)^{- 1} c_{\xi \zeta} (- p, p, q) - a (q)^{- 1} b_{\xi} (- p, q + p) \hspace{0.17em} a (q + p)^{- 1} b_{\zeta} (p, q) \right],\)

and the response kernel is one half of the figure of the vector total film minus the figure of the ghost total film. This is the kernel \(F_{\xi \zeta} (p, q)\) of Lemma 11.22: by the determinant formula (11.81), the conserving mixed coefficient of the pole test on the torus of side \(n_i\) is the site count times the grid mean over \(q\) of this kernel, once the tickets of the two total films are known. The tickets of the vector total film are those of \(F\), proved in the first conclusion of Lemma 11.24, together with the tickets of the fixed films \(\textsf{B}\), \(\textsf{D}_a\) and of their products, rears and inverses. The required fixed-film identities are proved below in Lemma 11.27.

The one fact about the selected path that enters is the value of the zero-mark layer. By Lemma 11.10, under the path bounds, attachment, the floor at the side \(n\) and the sworn condition at the side \(n\), the Hessian at zero of the quadratic part of the finite action of \(p, R\) on the torus of side \(n\) is the kinetic form of the bed, \(\sum_f \langle (d_1 x)_f, (M_n d_1 y)_f \rangle\) with \(M_n\) the metric of \(B\) on that torus. The cubic and quartic atom terms of the action vanish to third order at zero and do not contribute to the Hessian. In Fourier variables: at every grid momentum \(k\) of the torus of side \(n_i\),

\(\displaystyle a_F (k) = \textsf{T} [P_1 (- k)^{\textsf{T}} M (k) P_1 (k)],\) (11.87)

where \(a_F\) is the tab of the film of \(F\), \(M (k)\) the metric row of \(B\) and \(\textsf{T}\), \(P_1\) are as in (11.59).

Lemma 11.25. (Common strip) Under the standing assumptions of this section, with \(L\) fixed, there are \(z > 0\) and \(K \geqslant 0\) such that every entry of the response kernel is holomorphic and periodic in \((p, q)\) on the strip of width \(z\) and bounded by \(K\) there. The numbers \(z, K\) do not depend on the torus side; they may depend on \(L\), on the bed and on the path.

Proof. Step 1: the denominator matching (11.58) on the strip of width \(s\). Write \(\hat{P}_j = \textsf{T} (P_j)\) for the color-diagonal extensions of the exterior symbols, which act on colored forms. The tab of the vector total film is \(a_F (k) + \hat{P}_0 (k) \hat{P}_0 (- k)^{\textsf{T}} + s_{\mu} (k) I\). Indeed tabs multiply under products of films and invert under inversion, so the tab of \(R_{\text{gh}, \mu}\) is \(s_{\mu} I\) on colors, and the directional sandwiches sum to \(s_{\mu} I\). This function is holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(s\): \(a_F\) by the fees, and \(s_{\mu}\) because \(\omega (k) + \mu^2\) does not vanish on the strip of width \(\mu / 128\). Indeed \(\omega (k) = \sum_{\nu} (2 - 2 \cos k_{\nu})\), and for \(k_{\nu} = u + i \theta\) with \(| \theta | \leqslant \mu / 128 \leqslant 1\) one has \(\text{Re} (2 - 2 \cos k_{\nu}) = 2 - 2 \cos u \cosh \theta \geqslant - 2 (\cosh \theta - 1) \geqslant - 2 \theta^2\), so \(\text{Re} (\omega (k) + \mu^2) \geqslant \mu^2 - 8 (\mu / 128)^2 > 0\). The bed denominator \(D^{\text{bed}} (k)\) displayed in Subsection 11.12 is holomorphic and periodic on the same strip, by the metric strip bound. By (11.87) the two functions agree at every grid momentum of every side \(n_i\). Since \(n_i \to \infty\), these grids are dense in the real momentum torus, and both functions are continuous; they agree at all real momenta. A holomorphic function on the strip which vanishes at all real points vanishes identically, so they agree on the strip. This is (11.58).

Step 2: invertibility at real momenta. Let \(k\) be real and \(x\) a vector. The floor at every torus side gives, after passing from the grids to all real momenta by density and continuity, \(\text{Re} \langle v, M (k) v \rangle \geqslant \epsilon |v|^2\) for the metric row. With the exterior identity \(| \hat{P}_1 (k) x|^2 + | \hat{P}_0 (k)^{\ast} x|^2 = \omega (k) |x|^2\) for a colored one-form \(x\), and \(s_{\mu} (k) \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \text{Re} \langle x, D^{\text{bed}} (k) x \rangle \geqslant \epsilon | \hat{P}_1 (k) x|^2 + | \hat{P}_0 (k)^{\ast} x|^2 + s_{\mu} (k) |x|^2 \geqslant \min (\epsilon, 1) (\omega (k) + s_{\mu} (k)) |x|^2 .\)

As shown in Subsection 11.12, \(\omega + s_{\mu} \geqslant 2^{- 10} (\omega + \mu^2) \geqslant 2^{- 10} \mu^2 > 0\). Hence the tab of the vector total film is invertible at every real momentum. The tab of the ghost total film is the scalar \(\omega + s_{\mu}\) on colors and is invertible there as well.

Step 3: a complex neighborhood. Let \(a\) be either tab. The set of points of the strip of width \(s\) at which \(\det a \ne 0\) is open and contains the compact real cell \([- \pi, \pi]^4\). It therefore contains all \(k\) with \(\text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} k \in [- \pi, \pi]^4\) and \(| \text{Im} \hspace{0.17em} k_j | \leqslant r\) for some \(0 < r \leqslant s\), and by periodicity the whole closed strip of width \(r\). Take \(r\) valid for both tabs.

Step 4: the kernel. On the strip of width \(r\) the inverse tabs are holomorphic and periodic. The figure is a polynomial in the entries of \(a^{- 1}\), \(b\) and \(c\) evaluated at \(q\), \(q + p\), \((- p, q + p)\), \((p, q)\) and \((- p, p, q)\). If \((p, q)\) lies in the strip of width \(r / 2\), all these arguments and their partial sums lie in the strip of width \(r\), where the three layers of both total films are holomorphic and periodic. Hence every entry of the response kernel is holomorphic and periodic on the joint strip of width \(r / 2\). Put \(z = r / 4\). The closed strip of width \(z\) is, modulo periods, the compact set \([- \pi, \pi]^8 \times [- z, z]^8\), on which the continuous entries are bounded. Let \(K\) be the sum of these bounds.\(\Box\)

Nothing in this proof is uniform in \(L\): the lower bound \(2^{- 10} \mu^2\) degenerates as \(\mu \to 0\), and the radius \(r\) comes from a compactness argument. This is why the recording strip is chosen after \(L\), while the radial constants, which never use this strip, are chosen before it.

11.15.2The annulus estimate

We can now state the output of Sections 11.12–11.15 in the form consumed by the one-step construction of Subsubsection 11.21.2, where it is denoted \(\text{Ann} (G, \sigma, \epsilon, C, Z, \tau, A)\). Its remaining response inputs are supplied by the following finite coefficient calculation.

Lemma 11.26. (Coefficient pictures give priced marked responses) Suppose analytic actions \(U_n\) have \(DU_n (0) = 0\) and exact normalized Taylor coefficient pictures of orders \(j = 1, 2, 3\), with one strip width \(s > 0\) and component bound \(A \geqslant 0\). There is one marked response with fees \((s / 25, AC_{\ast})\), recording the required two-, three- and four-slot mixed derivatives at every side for which the input records hold. Here \(C_{\ast}\) is finite and depends only on the fixed gauge-coordinate sets. If the input pictures have ray orders \(q, q - 1, q - 2\), with nonnegative orders understood as zero when necessary, the response layers have these same respective ray orders.

Proof. Retain the intermediate mixed pictures in the construction preceding Proposition 11.14; no division into spatial response arrays is needed here. At layer \(r = 0, 1, 2\) there are \(r + 2\) slots, two for the variation and \(r\) for the background. For every partition \(\pi\) of these labelled slots into \(b\) nonempty blocks, the chain rule for \(D (X, Y) = \log (\exp X \exp Y)\) contributes

\(\displaystyle b! \sum_{a_1, \ldots, a_b} P_{b - 1} \left( a_1, \ldots, a_b ; \left. \sum_{j \in \pi_1} p_j, \ldots, \sum_{j \in \pi_b} p_j \right) \prod_{h = 1}^b c_{\pi, h} (a_h) . \right.\)

The coefficients \(c_{\pi, h}\) are the components of the derivatives of \(D\) on that block, in the finite link and color bases. The \(b = 1\) term is zero by \(DU_n (0) = 0\). The factor \(b\)! changes the normalized Taylor picture into the raw derivative; it is not an additional enumeration of partitions. The pointwise analytic map \(D\) is the same at every link and side, so these finitely many derivatives through order four have finite bounds independent of \(n\). Let \(C_r\) be the sum of the absolute multipliers \(b! \prod_h |c_{\pi, h} (a_h) |\) over partitions and coordinate assignments, enlarged to cover each output component, and put \(C_{\ast} = 1 + C_0 + C_1 + C_2\).

The external momenta have one conserving relation. On width \(s / (r + 3)^2\), each momentum sum in a block lies in the original width \(s\): there are at most \(r + 2\) slots and the eliminated momentum has at most \(r + 1\) independent summands. Thus the displayed finite sum is holomorphic and periodic on that strip with bound \(AC_r\). The common width \(s / 25\) works for all three layers. Linear substitution of block momenta preserves ray vanishing. The input of index \(b - 1\) vanishes to order at least \(q - (b - 2) \geqslant q - r\), since \(b \leqslant r + 2\); hence the output has the asserted ray order. Multiplication by the local coefficients and finite summation preserve it.

At a mesh momentum the exact input picture contributes zero unless its block momenta sum to zero; this is the same total momentum condition as for the external slots. In the conserving case its contribution is the site count times the displayed coefficient. The ordinary finite chain rule therefore identifies the sum with the exact mixed derivative of \(U_n \circ D\). This proves all three marked records, with precisely one site-count factor. Every operation used the same pictures for every side, so the resulting response is side independent.\(\Box\)

Apply the lemma to the Wilson pictures of Proposition 11.1, taking the sum of their three finite bounds and a common positive strip. The Wilson action has zero first derivative at the identity. We obtain a response \(W\) with fees \((k, E)\), \(k > 0\), \(E \geqslant 0\), whose records hold at every torus side. This is exactly the grey Wilson response used above.

Next apply it to \(U_{n_i} = I_{n_i} -\mathcal{W}_{n_i}\). Under (11.88), Proposition 11.12 supplies its exact pictures, one positive strip and finite bound depending only on the displayed scalar parameters, and ray orders \(3, 2, 1\). The first derivative vanishes by Lemma 11.10 and the Wilson identity. The lemma gives a correction response \(Q\) with fees \((v, M)\) and soft orders \(3, 2, 1\), recording \(I_{n_i} -\mathcal{W}_{n_i}\) on every selected side. Its constants are fixed before the bed, path, side sequence and odd block side are chosen. Thus both responses required in Lemma 11.24 have now been constructed.

Lemma 11.27. (Exact fixed-film records) The gauge-fixing film, the constant directional films, and their sums, products, rears and regulated inverses record their finite-torus matrix families and the first two background derivatives at every side.

Proof. For a wave \(h (x) = e^{ip \cdot x} v\), the covariant difference is

\(\displaystyle (D_{\exp z} h) (x, a) = e^{ip \cdot x} \{v - e^{ip_a} e^{\text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} z (x, a)} v\} .\)

At \(z = 0\) its tab is \((1 - e^{ip_a}) I\), namely the negative zero-form symbol in the conventions of Subsection 11.12. For a marked background \(z (x, a) = t \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{a = b} e^{ik \cdot x} c\), the first derivative is the wave of momentum \(p + k\) with coefficient \(-\boldsymbol{1}_{a = b} e^{ip_a} \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} c\). For a second mark \(s \hspace{0.17em} \boldsymbol{1}_{a = d} e^{il \cdot x} f\), the mixed second derivative has momentum \(p + k + l\) and coefficient

\(\displaystyle - \tfrac{1}{2} \boldsymbol{1}_{a = b} \boldsymbol{1}_{a = d} e^{ip_a} (\text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} c \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} f + \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} f \text{ad} \hspace{0.17em} c) .\)

These follow directly from the first three terms of the exponential series. Complex waves are obtained by complexifying these real multilinear identities. They are precisely the tab and marked slots of the covariant-difference film as defined there. The direction selectors are constant matrices, so their marked slots are zero.

For clarity write a film as \((A, B_i, C_{ij})\), where \(B_i (k, p)\) sends incoming momentum \(p\) to \(p + k\) and \(C_{ij} (k, l, p)\) sends it to \(p + k + l\). For a second film \((E, H_i, J_{ij})\), the product slots are

\(\displaystyle AE (p) = A (p) E (p), \qquad (FH)_i (k, p) = B_i (k, p) E (p) + A (p + k) H_i (k, p),\)
\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} (FH)_{ij} (k, l, p) = & C_{ij} (k, l, p) E (p) + B_i (k, p + l) H_j (l, p)\\ & + B_j (l, p + k) H_i (k, p) + A (p + k + l) J_{ij} (k, l, p) . \end{array}\)

They are the ordinary Leibniz rule, with the outgoing momentum of the right factor used as the incoming momentum of the left factor. Thus finite-torus recording is preserved by products. Sums are immediate. Transposing the real finite matrix reverses the wave momenta and interchanges its incoming and outgoing indices; applying this to each of the three displayed derivatives proves the rear rule.

If the zero-background matrix is invertible, its inverse is analytic near zero, by the finite-dimensional Neumann series. Put \(K (p) = A (p)^{- 1}\). Differentiating the identity \(F^{- 1} F = I\) gives inverse slots

\(\displaystyle \hat{B}_i (k, p) = - K (p + k) B_i (k, p) K (p)\)

and

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \hat{C}_{ij} (k, l, p) = & K (p + k + l) B_i (k, p + l) K (p + l) B_j (l, p) K (p)\\ & + K (p + k + l) B_j (l, p + k) K (p + k) B_i (k, p) K (p)\\ & - K (p + k + l) C_{ij} (k, l, p) K (p) . \end{array}\)

These identities prove the inverse record including both orders of the cross term. The massive ghost matrix \(D_1^{\textsf{T}} D_1 + \mu^2 I\) is positive definite for \(\mu > 0\). Hence its inverse, its powers, scalar multiples and directional sandwiches have these exact records. These are precisely the ghost and vector regulator films displayed in the trajectory-bound paragraph. The positive regulated floors similarly justify every further inverse used for the determinant coefficients. All identities are finite-dimensional and hold at each torus size; no limit or uniform inverse bound is needed for their exactness.\(\Box\)

Proposition 11.28. (The annulus estimate) Let \(G\) be simple, let \(\sigma, \epsilon, \tau > 0\) and \(C, Z, A \geqslant 0\), and use the Wilson and correction responses and the fixed-film identities just constructed. There is \(D_0 \geqslant 0\) such that the following holds for every odd integer \(L \geqslant 1\), every sequence of torus sides \(n_i \to \infty\), every bed \(B\), clip \(p\), path \(R\) and number \(\Lambda\) such that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda), \qquad \text{Attached} (B, R), \qquad B. \text{metric.strip} (\sigma, C), \qquad p. \text{direct} \leqslant Z,\\ \text{Floor} (B, p, n, \epsilon, C) \text{for every torus side } n, \qquad \text{Match} (B, n_i, G) \text{for every } i. \hspace{3cm} \text{(11.88)} \end{array}\)

There are a two-leg picture \(\mathcal{Y}\), numbers \(z > 0\), \(K \geqslant 0\) and a sequence \(e_i \to 0\) such that

  1. every component of \(\mathcal{Y}\) is holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(z / 2\) and bounded by \(K\) there;

  2. for every \(i\), \(\mathcal{Y}\) records the pole test \(\mathcal{P}_{n_i}\) of \(p, R\) with regulator \(L^{- 1}\) on the torus of side \(n_i\), in the normalized sense (11.91) with \(n = 2\), with error \(n_i^4 e_i\); here \(e_i =\mathfrak{r} (z, K) e^{- zn_i / 4}\);

  3. \(| \alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}) + \lambda_G \log L| \leqslant D_0\) for every color \(c\).

The constant \(D_0\) depends only on \(G, \sigma, \epsilon, C, Z, \tau, A\); the picture \(\mathcal{Y}\) and the numbers \(z, K\) may depend on all the data, including \(L\). No limit in \(i\) is part of the assertion.

Proof. Take \(W, k, E\) and \(v, M\) from the two applications of Lemma 11.26; put \(w = \min (k, v)\), so that \(W\) has fees \((w, E)\). Apply the conclusion (11.57) of Subsection 11.12 with vacuum response \(V = W\), fee width \(\eta = w\), floor constant \(\epsilon\), bed bound \(D = C\) and correction fee \(A = M\). It gives \(a, b \geqslant 0\), and we put \(D_0 = c_{\text{wt}} [a (\pi^2 / 2 + |E_4 |) + 3 \pi^2 b]\). All of this precedes \(L\) and the path data.

Now let \(L\) and the data (11.88) be given, let \(Q\) be the correction response just constructed, and put \(F = Q + W\), \(\mu = L^{- 1}\) and \(s = \min \{\sigma, w, \mu / 128\}\). The first conclusion of Lemma 11.24 gives the tickets of \(F\), and Lemma 11.25 gives \(z, K\). Let \(\mathcal{Y}\) be the picture associated with the response kernel. The second conclusion of Lemma 11.24, that is Lemma 11.22 in its normalized form (11.78), gives the first two assertions; \(e_i \to 0\) because \(z > 0\) and \(n_i \to \infty\).

For the third, observe that \((s, \mu, c, B, p, Q)\) is a local record in the sense of Subsection 11.12, for every color \(c\): \(Q\) has fees \((w, M)\) and the required soft orders; \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\) and \(0 < s \leqslant \mu / 128\); the layers of \(F = Q + W\) are holomorphic on the strip of width \(s \leqslant w\) and the metric bound holds at width \(s \leqslant \sigma\); the denominator matching on the strip of width \(s\) is Step 1 of the preceding proof; and the floors hold at every side. This is the same response \(F\) and the same strip \(s\) as in the recording. Hence (11.57) holds for its response coefficient with the constants \(a, b\) chosen above. The coefficient identity (11.63) applies to \(\mathcal{Y}\) because the kernel has the strip bound of Lemma 11.25, and the radial integration in the proof of Theorem 11.23 gives the third assertion with \(\log \mu^{- 1} = \log L\).\(\Box\)

Proposition 11.28 is Theorem 11.23 with its hypotheses on the finite-layer realization, the common strip and the two-wave bounds discharged. Lemmas 11.26 and 11.27 supply the remaining response and finite-film records; Lemma 11.10 supplies the quadratic coefficient under the stated path hypotheses.

Selected construction of the common data.
In the selected construction, the baseline \(W\) is the fee-bounded Wilson response. The correction \(Q\) is the response produced from the attached path and bed. Its finite tickets are the difference between the intended action and the Wilson action. Combining them before evaluating any side gives \(F = Q + W\). The fee, ticket, floor, and metric conditions used above are all on this combined response and the original selected bed.

The local-frame bounds are produced from that same Wilson reference: the finite-volume vector and ghost responses, together with their one-leg and two-leg extensions, are obtained from one family of local frames. The all-centered estimate is applied at scale \(|x| + \mu\); the shell estimate is applied to the restriction \(\mu \leqslant |x|\), where the unregulated reference inverse exists. The two applications give the upper face and the lower face-minus-halo bound, respectively. These are precisely the hypotheses used in the preceding face calculation; no change of bed, reference, or finite-volume sequence is made between the face estimates and the ticket identity.

In summary, the proof of Proposition 11.28 composes the Wilson baseline \(W\), the path correction \(Q\), the addition \(F = Q + W\) of their tickets, the finite determinant recording of Lemma 11.22, and the uniform radial evaluation of Theorem 11.23.

11.16Symmetries of the selected stationary germs

The Taylor construction of the output action uses the symmetries of three finite-volume functions: the classical stationary value, the completed determinant correction, and the inherited loop evaluated on the stationary fine field. We prove these symmetries for the selected stationary branch. In particular the determinant argument includes the moving frame and its regularization.

Fix a block step, its spanning forest, a positive regulator \(\mu\), and the analytic stationary branch \(U (V)\) in the original rooted tree chart. The bound gap is strictly positive. The standing hypotheses, which are inputs to this section, are precisely the following: the patch satisfies the branch compatibility of the kernel; the incoming path satisfies its path bounds in the incoming clip; the branch vanishes at zero, is analytic, and is stationary near the origin for the tested action of every choice of block representatives, not only of one; the Hessian of every tested action in the tree-chart variables at the origin has one positive lower bound; \(\mu > 0\); and a local Haar chart with its density is fixed. Write \(t\) for a choice of block representatives. For a coarse field \(V\) and a joint fine field and frame \(U\), let \(S_t (V, U)\) be the classical action with the blocking penalty. Let \(H_t (V, U)\) be its Hessian in the joint left coordinates, and let \(F_t (U)\) be the completed gauge-fixing derivative. In the notation of the determinant construction, put

\(\displaystyle M_t (V, U) = H_t (V, U) + F_t (U)^{\ast} F_t (U), \qquad \mathcal{B}_t (V, U) = \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det M_t (V, U) - \log \det \Gamma (U) .\)

Here \(\Gamma (U) = d_U^{\ast} d_U + C_{\mu} (U)\) is the same scalar gauge-orbit operator, with \(C_{\mu} = \mu^2 P_{\mu}^9\). All matrices in this section are real. The positive bound gap and positive regulator make the relevant determinants positive near the stationary origin, so these expressions define analytic real germs there.

11.16.1Transport of the completed determinant

The two determinant identities below are stated at base points \((V, U)\) satisfying the following side conditions, for representatives \(t\).

    (a)

    Frame regularity. The block frames of the incremented joint field \(\exp (X) U\) are real-analytic functions of the increment \(X\) at \(X = 0\).

    (b)

    Analyticity. The action \(X \mapsto S_t (V, \exp (X) U)\) is real-analytic at \(X = 0\).

For the signed identity we also use

    (c)

    Stationarity. The first derivative of \(X \mapsto S_t (V, \exp (X) U)\) vanishes at \(X = 0\).

The precise requirements are stated with each identity; \(\mu > 0\) throughout. All three conditions hold at every point of the selected stationary branch near the trivial joint field. Condition (a) holds on a neighbourhood of the trivial field, because the frame logarithm and the nearest-group map are analytic near the identity. Condition (b) holds there because the action is a finite composition of analytic maps. Condition (c) is the stationarity of the branch, assumed above for every choice of representatives. The transformations considered below fix the trivial field and map the stationary equation to itself, as shown in the next subsection. These identities are used only along the branch. We do not claim the signed identity without condition (c).

A fine gauge transformation \(k\) and coarse gauge transformation \(u\) act on the joint field and frame. Their tangent maps in joint, fine-link and scalar coordinates are orthogonal: they are products of adjoint colour rotations and coordinate permutations. Denote the joint tangent map by \(T\) and the scalar map by \(A\). Equivariance of the block ribbon and of its frame logarithm gives

\(\displaystyle F_t (U') T = AF_t (U) .\)

We verify why this identity includes both parts of the gauge fixing. For its frame part, the logarithm near the identity obeys \(\log (khk^{- 1}) = \text{Ad}_k \hspace{0.17em} \log h\); differentiating the block-frame identity gives the intertwining of its derivative. For its link part, the covariant difference intertwines the scalar and link adjoint maps, and transposition gives the corresponding identity for the adjoint difference. The scalar resolvent and hence its ninth power intertwine because multiplication and inversion preserve an intertwining relation. These are the two summands of \(F_t\).

The classical action is gauge invariant. In left increment coordinates the transformed action composed with \(T\) equals the original action near the increment origin. Twice differentiating this identity yields

\(\displaystyle T^{\ast} H_t (V', U') T = H_t (V, U) .\)

There is no derivative of \(T\) in this calculation: the base fields are fixed while differentiating the increment variable. Combining this congruence with the gauge-fixing identity and \(A^{\ast} A = I\) gives

\(\displaystyle T^{\ast} M_t (V', U') T = M_t (V, U) .\)

Similarly \(\Gamma (U')\) is orthogonally conjugate to \(\Gamma (U)\). Taking determinants and using \((\det T)^2 = (\det A)^2 = 1\) proves, when \((V, U)\) and \((V', U')\) satisfy (a) and \((V', U')\) satisfies (b),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_t (V', U') =\mathcal{B}_t (V, U) .\) (11.89)

The same transformation preserves \(S_t\) and the inherited loop.

Signed lattice transformations require a slightly different tangent map. For a transformed link \(e\), let \(e'\) be the original link it reads. If its orientation is preserved, the tangent reads \(X_{e'}\); if reversed, it reads \(- \text{Ad}_{U_{e'}^{- 1}} \hspace{0.17em} X_{e'}\). This map is orthogonal and satisfies the exact increment identity

\(\displaystyle s^{\ast} (\exp (X) U) = \exp (T_U X) \hspace{0.17em} s^{\ast} U.\)

The scalar tangent is the coordinate permutation; the joint tangent also includes the corresponding frame permutation. The block representative changes from \(t\) to the transported choice \(r\). Equivariance of the ribbon, frame logarithm and covariant difference therefore gives, with these tangent maps,

\(\displaystyle F_t (s^{\ast} U) T_U = AF_r (U), \qquad T_U^{\ast} H_t (s^{\ast} V, s^{\ast} U) T_U = H_r (V, U) .\)

For the penalty term this uses invariance of the group bound under inversion and conjugation: reversing both links in its relative product leaves its bound unchanged. The same resolvent argument handles \(C_{\mu}\). Taking determinants as above gives, when \((V, U)\) satisfies (a) for \(r\) and \((s^{\ast} V, s^{\ast} U)\) satisfies (a)–(c) for \(t\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_t (s^{\ast} V, s^{\ast} U) =\mathcal{B}_r (V, U) .\) (11.90)

This is the completed moving determinant. It does not assert full signed covariance of the separate auxiliary fine-lattice pole symbol.

11.16.2Returning a moved solution to the original chart

A transformed stationary solution need not lie in the original tree chart. Gauge it back using the tree gauge map. This map is analytic near the trivial joint field, sends that field to itself, and leaves the coarse input fixed when the coarse gauge transformation is the identity. The transformed stationarity equation is still zero: under the invertible tangent map its first derivative is the original zero first derivative. The local uniqueness in the original chart, proved by the stationary implicit-function construction, now identifies the regauged solution with the selected solution at the transformed coarse input. We use uniqueness only in that chart.

The classical value, the determinant correction by (11.89), and the inherited loop are unchanged by this regauging. Consequently their values along any analytic stationary probe through the trivial field equal their values on the selected branch at that probe's coarse input. The argument also applies to parameter-dependent gauge transformations: freeze the parameter when comparing the action and matrices, and then use analyticity and the local branch uniqueness on a common neighborhood.

We must also remove the changed representative \(r\) in (11.90). The tested classical actions for \(t\) and \(r\) agree as germs in both the coarse and tree-chart variables, by the local branch construction of Subsection 10.1. Therefore their stationary values and their tree-chart Hessians agree. The moving Haar identity of Subsection 11.10 expresses each thermal value as that tree Hessian determinant with the same Haar factor, or equivalently as the same inherited loop plus \(\mathcal{B}_t\) or \(\mathcal{B}_r\). Equality of the tree Hessians and cancellation of the common inherited loop give equality of \(\mathcal{B}_t\) and \(\mathcal{B}_r\) along the selected branch. Thus both tested functions are independent of the representative as germs. This uses equality on a neighborhood of the moving stationary point, which follows from equality of the joint germs; equality at the stationary value alone would not identify their Hessians.

11.16.3The identities used by the Taylor coefficients

Apply the preceding argument first to a signed lattice transformation, then to constant colour conjugation, and finally to a small variable coarse gauge transformation. For the last operation the transformed coarse logarithm is its actual nonlinear gauge action; transform the joint frame accordingly and gauge the resulting stationary solution back to the original chart. Each of the three analytic stationary functions is unchanged as a germ under all these operations. The inherited loop has the same invariances directly from its gauge invariant atoms and finite signed average.

In summary, for every choice \(t\) of block representatives each of the three functions of the coarse link field, the classical stationary value \(E_t\), the completed determinant correction \(\mathcal{B}_t\) along the branch, and the inherited loop along the branch, is real analytic at zero and is unchanged, as a germ at zero, by every signed lattice transformation, by every constant colour conjugation, and by the nonlinear action of small coarse gauge transformations. Moreover, for two choices \(t, r\) of representatives the germs of \(E_t\) and \(E_r\) coincide, and so do those of \(\mathcal{B}_t\) and \(\mathcal{B}_r\) along the branch. All statements are for one forest. The comparison of the stationary data of two different forests is a separate statement, which is not proved in this section.

Differentiating these germ identities supplies the signed and colour symmetries and the nonlinear gauge identities of the finite-volume Taylor coefficients in Subsection 11.17. When the lower positive-degree coefficients vanish, the differentiated nonlinear gauge identity reduces to its leading linear Ward identity, as proved there. All three functions here use the same stationary branch, regulator and finite torus; no symmetry claim is imported from a separately chosen stationary solution.

11.17Constructing the stationary output action

We now apply the curvature lift to the Taylor coefficients of the stationary action. The construction has two separate parts. Cubic and quartic atoms match the classical action through degree four. The quadratic response determines one scalar change in the kinetic coefficient and an invariant loop remainder with vanishing moments. Both parts use the same coarse bed and the same sequence of tori.

11.17.1Recovering Ward data from finite-volume coefficients

For an analytic real function \(F\) of the link coordinates, write \(C_n (F) = D^n F (0) / n\)!, extended complex multilinearly to complex link fields. A unit Fourier wave has a specified momentum, coordinate direction, and colour basis vector. A common coefficient picture \(P\) for functions \(F_k\) on tori \(\Lambda_k\) means that

\(\displaystyle \left| C_n (F_k) [v_1, \ldots, v_n] -\boldsymbol{1}_{\sum p_j = 0} \hspace{0.17em} | \Lambda_k | \hspace{0.17em} P (p_1, \ldots, p_{n - 1}) \right| \leqslant | \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\) (11.91)

for every such tuple of unit waves; the zeroth momentum in the picture is minus the sum of the other momenta. Direction and colour indices in \(P\) are understood. We assume a common positive strip width and finite component bound, and \(\varepsilon_k \to 0\). The side lengths tend to infinity. Exact recording is the case \(\varepsilon_k = 0\).

Suppose \(F_k\) is invariant, as a germ at the identity, under gauge transformations and under simultaneous adjoint colour rotations. Assume its positive-degree derivatives below degree \(n\) vanish. Differentiate the gauge-invariance identity once in the gauge parameter and \(n - 1\) times in the link variables. The term where all link derivatives fall on \(F_k\) gives its \(n\)th derivative with one argument equal to the linear gauge variation \(d_0 h\). Every other term contains a positive-degree derivative of \(F_k\) of degree less than \(n\) and is zero. Consequently

\(\displaystyle C_n (F_k) [v_1, \ldots, d_0 h, \ldots, v_n] = 0.\)

On Fourier waves this is exactly the contraction by \(q (p_j)\) in factor \(j\). It holds in every factor by symmetry of the derivative. This is also the leading case of the finite gauge-variation identity in Subsection 11.3.

Approximate any tuple of real independent momenta by finite-torus frequencies and choose the zeroth frequency to make their sum zero. Representatives may differ by \(2 \pi\) multiples; both the picture and the symbols \(q\) are periodic. Divide (11.91) by \(| \Lambda_k |\). The error tends to zero, while continuity of the common picture and of \(q\) permits passage to the limit in the finite contraction sum. Thus the picture has all continuous-momentum Ward identities. Conjugating real Taylor coefficients and reversing every wave gives \(\overline{P (- p)} = P (p)\) by the same limit. Differentiating the constant colour-rotation invariance and passing to the limit makes the real and imaginary colour tensors invariant. These are precisely the hypotheses of Subsection 11.4.

For \(n = 2\), only the first derivative of \(F_k\) needs to vanish. This follows from colour invariance when the gauge Lie algebra is simple: an invariant linear functional kills every bracket, and the brackets span the algebra. The constant value of \(F_k\) need not be zero.

11.17.2Matching a leading Taylor coefficient

Suppose \(n \in \{3, 4\}\), the two cases used below, each \(F_k\) is invariant under signed coordinate permutations as a germ, \(F_k\) vanishes to order \(n\), and \(P\) records its \(n\)th coefficient exactly, at strip cost \(A\). The curvature lift produces invariant real atoms of degree \(n\) with total exponential budget \(KA\), at a positive width chosen before \(A\) and \(P\). Let \(S\) be their translation sum and then their average over the finite group of signed coordinate permutations.

Here is the normalization check. Each transported plaquette slot has zero constant term and linear term equal to its discrete curl. The leading coefficient of the product of \(n\) slots is therefore the product of these curls. The normalized coefficient \(C_n (S)\) is the symmetrization of that ordered product, namely the average over the \(n\)! permutations of its arguments. On unit Fourier waves, summing the anchor over the torus gives zero unless the momenta sum to zero, and gives \(| \Lambda_k |\) otherwise. The remaining absolutely convergent displacement sum is exactly the curvature symbol contracted in every factor. It is \(P\) by the lifting identity. Since \(C_n (F_k)\) is symmetric, symmetrizing this identity retains it. Averaging orientations also retains it, because \(F_k\) is invariant under those orientations. Reflections may multiply a wave by a unit-modulus edge phase; these phases are included when applying the same identity to the transformed wave. Thus \(C_n (S) = C_n (F_k)\) on every tuple of unit Fourier waves.

These waves span the complexified link space, by finite Fourier inversion. Multilinearity gives equality on arbitrary arguments and hence on real fields. Both functions are analytic, and each atom vanishes to order \(n\); the exponential budget permits termwise differentiation on a common complex link ball as in Proposition 11.5. It follows that

\(\displaystyle F_k - S = O (|x|^{n + 1})\) (11.92)

along every real ray. In the approximate version of (11.91), permutation and orientation averages are convex averages, so the same argument retains the coefficient error \(| \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\) on each tuple of unit waves. It does not insert a factorial or a further volume factor.

11.17.3The cubic and quartic construction

Fix the outgoing clip \(q\), with direct coefficient \(q_{\text{direct}} \leqslant Z\), and the coarse bed \(C\), with strip bound \((w, M_H)\) for its metric and root rows, with its floor in the clip \(q\) at every torus side, and with the sworn condition at every coarse side of the sequence. Let \(E_k\) be the classical stationary action in the chosen chart, that is, the tested action of the incoming clip and path along the stationary branch. Let \(R_C\) be the path whose row family is the root row of \(C\) and whose loop, cubic and quartic families are zero, and let \(J_k\) be the complete quadratic action of \(q, R_C\) on the \(k\)th coarse torus: the average over signed lattice transformations \(s\) of

\(\displaystyle \sum_f \left[ q_{\text{direct}} \hspace{0.17em} \varpi (\text{hol}_f (s^{\ast} \exp x)) + \tfrac{1}{2} | \ell_{R_C} (s^{\ast} \exp x ; f) |^2 \right],\)

the sum being over plaquettes, with \(\varpi\) the Wilson plaquette bound and \(\ell_{R_C}\) the row field. It contains the direct Wilson term of the clip as well as the squared row term. Both \(E_k\) and \(J_k\) are analytic, gauge invariant, and invariant under signed coordinate permutations. Their values and first derivatives at zero are zero. Their Hessians at zero agree, and equal the coarse Gaussian form \(\mathcal{K}_C (x, y) = \sum_f \langle (d_1 x)_f, (M_C d_1 y)_f \rangle\) of the metric of \(C\). For \(J_k\) this is Lemma 11.10, applied to \(R_C\) at the coarse side; \(R_C\) is attached to \(C\) by construction and satisfies the path bounds by the root strip bound, and the lemma uses the floor and the sworn condition of \(C\) at that side. For \(E_k\) it is the reduced Hessian (heat) identity of the stationary branch for this same bed \(C\),

\(\displaystyle D^2 E_k (0) =\mathcal{K}_C,\)

which is one of the chart receipts treated in Subsubsection 11.21.2. It is an input here, as is the Hessian gap \(\eta\) common to all \(k\) in Proposition 11.29: it holds for the coarse bed matched to the fine bed and the installed tower by the two-scale identities, not for an arbitrarily chosen coarse bed. Thus

\(\displaystyle F_k = E_k - J_k\)

has order three. Suppose \(E_k\) has exact common coefficient pictures of degrees three and four with component bound \(M \geqslant 1\) at width \(\sigma\). The row estimate of Proposition 11.3 for the squared row term, and the Wilson pictures of Proposition 11.1 multiplied by \(q_{\text{direct}} \leqslant Z\) for the direct term, supply such pictures for \(J_k\) at positive widths and fixed bounds \(P_3, P_4\), which depend on \(w, M_H, Z\). Choose the minimum of the three widths. Both residual pictures then have cost at most

\(\displaystyle M (1 + P_3 + P_4) .\)

Apply (11.92) at \(n = 3\) to obtain an invariant cubic atom action \(S_3\) of budget \(K_3 M\) and with \(F_k - S_3\) of order four.

Its fourth coefficient must also be subtracted. In general an atom of degree three has nonzero higher Taylor terms. The uniform atom-picture estimate gives an exact common degree-four picture for \(S_3\), of cost at most \(K_3 MB_4\) at a positive width. Subtract it from the degree-four picture of \(F_k\), and take the minimum width. The resulting cost is at most a fixed multiple of \(M\). Apply the leading-removal construction at \(n = 4\) to \(F_k - S_3\). This produces an invariant quartic action \(S_4\), again with budget bounded by a fixed multiple of \(M\), and

\(\displaystyle E_k - J_k - S_3 - S_4 = O (|x|^5) .\) (11.93)

The two atom families and their common positive width are chosen independently of \(k\). They consequently match the action on every torus in the sequence. Taylor expansion on rays followed by polarization proves equality of the full derivatives of orders zero through four. The chart's tag-independence is an equality of germs, so the same derivatives agree for every allowed choice of tag, without choosing a different path for each tag.

11.17.4Removing the unique quadratic kinetic coefficient

We need one further consequence of the Ward and lattice symmetries. Let \(f_{ab}^{uv} (p)\) be an analytic quadratic coefficient picture, where \(a, b\) are coordinate indices and \(u, v\) colour indices, with momenta \((- p, p)\). Suppose it comes from the common recording of analytic, gauge-invariant, signed-coordinate-invariant real functions as above. It then has the exchange symmetry

\(\displaystyle f_{ab}^{uv} (p) = f_{ba}^{vu} (- p) .\)

The leading homogeneous terms transform as ordinary coordinate tensors under signed permutations: the reflected-edge phases have constant term equal to the relevant coordinate sign. Once all lower homogeneous terms vanish, the higher terms of these phases do not contribute at the order being considered.

First \(f (0) = 0\). Indeed the first-order part of the Ward identity for a constant tensor is \(\sum_b p_b f_{ab}^{uv} (0) = 0\) for all \(p\), and coordinate vectors detect every entry. For its linear term write \(K_{ab}^{uv} (p)\). The left and right Ward identities give

\(\displaystyle \sum_b p_b K_{ab}^{uv} (p) = 0, \qquad \sum_a p_a K_{ab}^{uv} (p) = 0.\)

They imply \(K_{ab}^{uv} (e_b) = K_{ab}^{uv} (e_a) = 0\) and \(K_{ab}^{uv} (e_j) + K_{aj}^{uv} (e_b) = 0\). If \(a, b, j\) are distinct, the coordinate permutation exchanging \(b\) and \(j\) identifies the two terms, so both vanish. When indices coincide the preceding two identities already imply the same conclusion. The linear term is zero and \(f\) has order two.

Let \(Q_{ab}^{uv} (p)\) be its quadratic homogeneous term. Fix \(a \ne b\) and one unit colour vector \(e_u\). We claim that

\(\displaystyle Q_{aa}^{uu} (e_b) = 0\) (11.94)

forces \(Q = 0\). At fixed \(a, p\), the colour form \(Q_{aa}^{uv} (p)\) is real, symmetric, and adjoint invariant. Reality follows from momentum reversal and even homogeneity; symmetry follows from the exchange identity. Such a form is a scalar multiple of the invariant inner product. Indeed its self-adjoint representing operator commutes with every adjoint operator; each eigenspace is an ideal, so simplicity leaves only one eigenvalue. Therefore (11.94) kills that whole colour form. Coordinate permutations transfer the conclusion to \(Q_{xx}^{uv} (e_y)\) for all \(x \ne y\). The case \(x = y\) follows directly from the leading Ward identity.

Polarize \(Q\), writing \(T_{xy}^{uv} (e_k, e_l)\) for its symmetric bilinear coefficient in momentum. Reflection in coordinate \(r\) multiplies this entry by \((- 1)^{\boldsymbol{1}_{r = k} +\boldsymbol{1}_{r = l}}\) on the momentum side and by \((- 1)^{\boldsymbol{1}_{r = x} +\boldsymbol{1}_{r = y}}\) on the tensor side. Unless these parities agree for every \(r\), the entry is zero. For \(x = y\) this eliminates every mixed momentum coefficient; the diagonal coefficients were just shown to vanish. For \(x \ne y\) it leaves only the mixed pair \(\{k, l\} = \{x, y\}\). Finally insert \(p = e_x + e_y\) in the Ward identity with first tensor index \(x\). All other entries have vanished, so it kills this last mixed coefficient as well. Thus \(Q = 0\) in every colour component.

The picture now has order three. Reversal of all coordinate signs acts by \(+ 1\) on its two tensor indices but by \(- 1\) on a homogeneous cubic polynomial. Its cubic term is therefore zero. We have proved that the single test (11.94) gives order four.

Let \(H (p)\) be the coarse metric row, with \(H (0) = I\), and let \(b_H\) be the exactly recorded quadratic picture of its quadratic path action. Its quadratic homogeneous term is

\(\displaystyle (b_H)_{ab}^{uv, [2]} (p) = \tfrac{1}{2} (|p|^2 \delta_{ab} - p_a p_b) \delta_{uv} .\)

This follows by replacing both discrete curls by their linear terms \(ip \wedge\) and using \(H (0) = I\); the action has coefficient \(1 / 2\) and its two waves have opposite momenta. In particular the transverse unit test is \(1 / 2\). Fixing distinct axes \(0, 1\) and any unit colour basis vector \(e_u\), define

\(\displaystyle \alpha = 2 \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} f_{11}^{uu, [2]} (e_0) .\)

Then \(f - \alpha b_H\) passes the zero test. It inherits the Ward, reality, exchange, and lattice symmetries by subtracting the actual quadratic action, so it has order four. This establishes the normalization of \(\alpha\) before any curvature lift is chosen.

11.17.5The loop remainder and its real-wave estimate

Let \(U_k\) denote the local determinant and Jacobian response and \(V_k\) the inherited loop response. Suppose their common pictures are \(f\) and \(g\), with strip bounds \(\Phi\) and \(R\) on width \(\sigma\), respectively. Assume \(f\) records \(U_k\) with error \(| \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\), while \(g\) records \(V_k\) exactly and has order four. Assume also that \(J_k\) has the exact picture \(b_H\), of bound \(B_H\), at that width. All three functions have the invariances used above. Then

\(\displaystyle h = f - \alpha b_H + g\)

has order four, strip cost at most \(\Phi + | \alpha | B_H + R\), and records \(U_k - \alpha J_k + V_k\) with the same error. The two-factor lift, with retained order two, gives an invariant loop atom family of budget

\(\displaystyle D (\Phi + | \alpha |B_H + R) .\) (11.95)

It has zero total coefficient and zero first displacement moments. Let \(S_2\) be its orientation-averaged action. The normalization argument above shows that the difference between \(C_2 (S_2)\) and \(C_2 (U_k - \alpha J_k + V_k)\) on every pair of unit complex Fourier waves is bounded by \(| \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\).

Put \(d = 4 \dim \mathfrak{g}\), the number of one-form direction and colour coordinates at a site. A complex wave with all component amplitudes bounded by one is a sum of \(d\) unit basis waves, so bilinearity bounds the coefficient difference by \(d^2 | \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\). A real wave is half the sum of that complex wave and the opposite-momentum wave with conjugate amplitude. Expanding two real waves gives four terms, each with factor \(1 / 4\), and hence the same bound. Finally \(D^2 = 2 C_2\):

\(\displaystyle | D^2 (S_2 - U_k + \alpha J_k - V_k) (0) [w_p, w_q] | \leqslant 2 d^2 | \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k .\) (11.96)

The momenta \(p, q\) are arbitrary; no opposite-momentum restriction has been added.

11.17.6One common output path

Take the row family to be the coarse bed roots and take the loop, cubic, and quartic families just constructed. Their exact row attachment is unchanged by adding the other three families. The metric identity follows from that root attachment and the coarse bed identity. Invariance and the two loop moment identities hold separately for the constructed atoms. At any positive width below the common construction width, the row and cubic/quartic budgets are satisfied whenever

\(\displaystyle A' \geqslant \max \{\mathcal{B}_{\text{row}} (w, M_H), KM\}, \qquad \Lambda' \geqslant D (\Phi + | \alpha |B_H + R) .\)

Here \(M_H\) is the root-strip bound and \(M\) the classical Taylor bound. Width reduction only decreases each weighted absolute sum. Thus this is a path with all the required summability, metric, invariance, and moment properties, not merely a list of formal jets.

Its classical part is \(J_k + S_3 + S_4\) and obeys (11.93). Its response part is \(\alpha J_k + S_2\). The stationary determinant identity identifies the actual thermal response, as a germ, with \(U_k + V_k\). Consequently (11.96) is the required Hessian comparison for the actual response. If

\(\displaystyle 2 d^2 \varepsilon_k \leqslant \Theta \Omega e^{- g \hspace{0.17em} \text{side} (\Lambda_k)},\)

its right side is at most \(| \Lambda_k | \Theta \Omega e^{- g \hspace{0.17em} \text{side} (\Lambda_k)}\). The stationary gap gives positivity of the stationary fluctuation Hessian, so its determinant remains positive near zero; the analytic chart gives the required smooth response germs. These conclusions all concern the same output path. Production of the common determinant picture, the inherited picture with its fourth-order bound, and the displayed exponential error is still required when this construction is used in a transition.

We record the result with its order of choices.

Proposition 11.29. (The stationary output path) Let \(G\) be simple and fix \(\sigma > 0\), \(w > 0\), \(M_H \geqslant 0\) and \(Z \geqslant 0\). There are \(s\) with \(0 < s \leqslant \min (\sigma, w)\) and \(K, D \geqslant 0\), depending only on these data, with the following property. Let coarse sides \(c_k \to \infty\) and fine sides \(f_k\) be given, and for every \(k\) a block step from the coarse to the fine torus whose patch satisfies the branch compatibility of the kernel, whose fine side is at least one hundred times the macro radius of the patch and whose coarse side exceeds one, a forest, a choice \(t_k\) of block representatives and a map \(\rho_k\) into the tree chart. Let \(p, q\) be clips with \(q_{\text{direct}} \leqslant Z\), let \(B, C\) be beds, \(U\) a path, \(\mu > 0\), \(\eta > 0\), and fix a local Haar chart. Assume:

  1. \(U\) has path bounds in \(p\) and is attached to \(B\); the floor of \(B, p\) and the sworn condition of \(B\) hold at every \(f_k\);

  2. for every \(k\), \(\rho_k\) is a stationary branch in the sense of Subsection 11.16 with Hessian gap \(\eta\), and it satisfies the reduced Hessian identity for the bed \(C\);

  3. the metric and root rows of \(C\) have strip bound \((w, M_H)\), the floor of \(C, q\) holds at every torus side, and the sworn condition of \(C\) holds at every \(c_k\);

  4. the classical stationary actions \(E_k\), formed with \(t_k\), have exactly recorded common pictures of degrees three and four, with components holomorphic on the strip of width \(\sigma\) and bounded by \(X \geqslant 1\);

  5. there are two-leg pictures \(f\) and \(g\) with components holomorphic on the strip of width \(\sigma\) and bounded by \(\Phi\) and \(R\), such that \(f\) records the completed determinant correction \(\mathcal{B}_{t_k}\) along \(\rho_k\), with regulator \(\mu\), with error \(| \Lambda_k | \varepsilon_k\), where \(\varepsilon_k \geqslant 0\) and \(\varepsilon_k \to 0\), and \(g\) records the inherited loop of \(U\) along \(\rho_k\) exactly and has order four at zero momentum.

Fix a color \(u\) and put \(\alpha = \alpha_u (f)\). Then there is a path \(T\) such that

    (a)

    the row family of \(T\) is the root row of \(C\), and \(T\) is attached to \(C\);

    (b)

    \(T\) has the path bounds \((s', A', \Lambda')\) in \(q\) whenever \(0 < s' \leqslant s\), \(A' \geqslant \max \{\mathcal{B}_{\text{row}} (w, M_H), KX\}\) and \(\Lambda' \geqslant D (\Phi + | \alpha | \text{quadBound} (\min (\sigma, w), M_H) + R)\);

    (c)

    for every \(k\), every \(j \leqslant 4\) and every choice of block representatives, the tested action of \(p, U\) along \(\rho_k\) and the main term of \(T\) in the clip \(q\) on the \(k\)th coarse torus are analytic at zero and have the same \(j\)th derivative there;

    (d)

    for every \(k\) and all \(\Theta, \gamma, \Omega\) with \(2 d^2 \varepsilon_k \leqslant \Theta \Omega e^{- \gamma c_k}\): for every choice of block representatives the determinant of the tree-chart Hessian of the tested action \(\mathcal{T}\) is positive along \(\rho_k\) near zero, the thermal function \(\vartheta (x) =\mathcal{L}_U (x, \rho_k (x)) + \frac{1}{2} \log \det D_z^2 \mathcal{T} (x, \rho_k (x)) - \log \prod_b J (\rho_k (x)_b)\), formed with the loop term \(\mathcal{L}_U\) of \(U\) and the density \(J\) of the Haar chart, and the function \(\alpha \mathcal{K}_{2, T} +\mathcal{L}_T\), formed from the quadratic term and the loop term of \(T\) in \(q\), are analytic at zero, and their second derivatives at zero on any two real plane waves with complex amplitudes of modulus at most one differ by at most \(| \Lambda_k | \Theta \Omega e^{- \gamma c_k}\).

The path \(T\) does not depend on the choices of representatives in (c) and (d), nor on \(\Theta, \gamma, \Omega\).

Proof. The preceding subsections prove this. The cubic and quartic families are those of (11.93), constructed from the pictures in (4) and from the pictures of \(J_k\), which exist by (3); the equality of Hessians used there is the lemma cited for \(J_k\), which uses (3), and the reduced Hessian identity in (2). The symmetry and gauge properties of \(E_k\), of the determinant correction and of the inherited loop are those of Subsection 11.16, whose standing hypotheses are (1) and (2). The loop family is the one constructed from \(h = f - \alpha b_H + g\), with budget (11.95). The path is assembled in the present subsection, which gives (a) and (b). Assertion (c) for the representatives \(t_k\) is (11.93) with polarization; for other representatives it follows from the equality of germs proved in Subsection 11.16.
