For (d), the moving Haar identity (11.25) identifies the thermal function, as a germ, with the inherited loop plus the completed determinant correction, up to the choice of representatives, which again does not change the germ; (11.96) is then the stated comparison.\(\Box\)

The stationary data in (2) and the pictures in (4) and (5) are inputs to this proposition. The hypotheses are extracted from the standing data of the one-step construction, in which the incoming path \(U\) is attached to the fine bed \(B\) and the output path to the coarse bed \(C\). In the one-step construction the pictures in (4) are those of Proposition 11.18, the inherited picture \(g\) is that of Proposition 11.30, and the determinant picture \(f\) with its exponentially small error is the subject of Subsection 11.9.

11.18Transporting the inherited loop response

The loop term entering a stationary step already has zero total coefficient and zero first displacement moments. These cancellations make its quadratic momentum picture vanish to fourth order. We prove that the stationary pullback retains this order and gains \(L^{- 2}\) in its strip bound, where \(L\) is the blocking side. The order statement and the quantitative strip statement require different arguments.

11.18.1The fine-lattice picture

Group the loop coefficients by the two plaquette directions, the two colour indices, and the relative displacement \(z\) of their bases. Denote the resulting real coefficient array by \(r (z)\). Its exponential norm is bounded by the loop budget \(\Lambda\): the norm of the relative displacement is no greater than the sum of the two slot lengths. Absolute summability permits grouping all atoms with the same displacement. The path's moment identities give

\(\displaystyle \sum_z r (z) = 0, \qquad \sum_z z_a r (z) = 0.\)

Consequently its Fourier series \(\hat{r} (p)\) vanishes to order two at zero. The quadratic coefficient of the loop action is the sum over face indices of

\(\displaystyle d_1 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} \hat{r} (p) d_1 (p),\) (11.97)

with its two field arguments symmetrized and its lattice orientations averaged. Each curl vanishes to first order. Thus every component of the resulting common picture \(S\) has order four. Its strip bound is at most \(C_{\tau} \Lambda\) at the original path width \(\tau > 0\): each curl entry costs at most \(4 (e^{\tau} + 1)\), there are finitely many face pairs, and the signed orientation average has the fixed reflection cost already estimated in Proposition 11.5.

Formula (11.97) records the actual finite-volume quadratic coefficient. At zero total momentum, the two slot offsets appear only through their relative displacement; the anchor sum gives the number of fine sites. At nonzero total momentum it is zero. This verifies the identity before taking a large-volume limit. Invariance and the finite Ward identity give

\(\displaystyle S (p) d_0 (p) = 0, \qquad d_0 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} S (p) = 0.\) (11.98)

All tensors below use this unconjugated bilinear convention. On real momenta, \(d_0 (- p)^{\textsf{T}} = d_0 (p)^{\ast}\).

11.18.2A strip bound with four finite differences

Put \(\mu = L^{- 1}\). Let \(W_i\) be the installed matrix of the step on the \(i\)th torus and let \(X\) be its common coarse-to-fine Fourier bridge, the function denoted \(\Phi\) in Subsection 11.7: on every torus of the family, \(X\) at the grid momenta is the Fourier representation of \(W_i\). By that section \(W_i\) is the fine tangent at zero of the gauged-back, recentered stationary representative; it is not the tangent of the branch in the original tree chart, which differs from it by an infinitesimal gauge transformation. Assume that, on a fixed positive coarse strip of width \(\sigma\), \(X\) is holomorphic and periodic and, for every ordered word of at most two fine coordinate differences, of length \(j \leqslant 2\),

\(\displaystyle | \Delta^j X| \leqslant D \mu^{1 + j} .\) (11.99)

This is the bridge bound (11.29); for the retained tower it is proved in Subsection 11.7.

Apply the finite-difference division of Lemma 11.13 to the order-four picture \(S\). It writes \(S\) as a finite sum of products of four symbols \(e^{ip_a} - 1\) times Fourier series having a common exponential budget \(C \Lambda\) on a positive width \(w\) depending on \(\tau\). Distribute two differences to each of the two field arguments. Moving a difference from momentum \(p\) to \(- p\) uses

\(\displaystyle q_a (p) = - e^{ip_a} q_a (- p) .\)

The extra phase is a unit lattice translation of the remaining coefficient; its cost is absorbed in \(C\). Thus every term has exactly two differences on each bridge leg.

Pull this representation back by \(X\). The width \(\sigma\) is not changed. Instead the blocking side is restricted: we require

\(\displaystyle \mu \sigma \leqslant w,\) (11.100)

so that for coarse momenta in the strip of width \(\sigma\) all fine phases lie in the coefficient strip of width \(w\). Here \(w\) and the constant \(C\) depend on \(\tau\) only and were chosen before \(L\). The exponentially weighted coefficient sum bounds every displaced contraction uniformly. For each pair of legs, (11.99) contributes

\(\displaystyle D^2 \mu^3 \mu^3 = D^2 \mu^6 .\)

Summing the fine anchor within one coarse block contributes \(L^4 = \mu^{- 4}\). Hence the complete common coarse picture \(H\) has, on the width \(\sigma' = \sigma / 4\), component bound

\(\displaystyle |H|_{\sigma'} \leqslant C' \Lambda \mu^2 .\) (11.101)

The finite numbers of colour, direction, and difference addresses are included in \(C'\), which depends on \(G\), \(\sigma\), \(\tau\) and \(D\), not on \(L\), the torus, or \(\Lambda\). Normal convergence of the coefficient sums proves holomorphy. Their covariance under integer momentum shifts and that of \(X\) prove periodicity of the common picture. Expanding the same absolutely convergent sums on a finite torus gives its exact recording identity for the linear pullback of the fine loop action.

The nonlinear stationary pullback has the same second derivative. Indeed the fine loop action \(V\) has \(DV (0) = 0\). Let \(\psi_i\) be the fine component of the gauged-back stationary representative on the \(i\)th torus; it satisfies \(\psi_i (0) = 0\) and \(D \psi_i (0) = W_i\). The chain rule therefore reads

\(\displaystyle D^2 (V \circ \psi_i) (0) [a, b] = D^2 V (0) [W_i a, W_i b] + DV (0) [D^2 \psi_i (0) [a, b]] = D^2 V (0) [W_i a, W_i b] .\)

The loop action is gauge invariant, so \(V \circ \psi_i\) equals, as a germ, the inherited loop evaluated on the stationary branch in the original tree chart; and that function does not involve the choice of block representatives. Thus \(H\) records the actual inherited response, with no additional error.

11.18.3The alias formula and the coarse–fine equation

To prove the order at zero, use the real-momentum alias representation. For \(l \in (\mathbb{Z}/ L\mathbb{Z})^4\), let

\(\displaystyle k_l (p) = \frac{p + 2 \pi l}{L}\)

with any fixed integer representatives; periodicity removes the choice of representatives. Let \(N_l (p)\) be the alias component of \(X\) in the normalization for which a coarse unit wave pulls back into fine waves of amplitudes \(N_l (p)\). With this normalization of the amplitudes the common picture satisfies

\(\displaystyle H_{ab} (p) = L^4 \sum_l N_{- l} (- p)_a^{\textsf{T}} S (k_l (p)) N_l (p)_b .\) (11.102)

For clarity, this equality follows first at each finite coarse Fourier grid. Expand both pulled-back waves in the finite set of aliases. Fine translation invariance kills every pair except opposite aliases; the ratio of fine to coarse site counts is \(L^4\). Approximate an arbitrary real \(p\) along grids whose side lengths tend to infinity. The bridge and both common pictures are continuous and the alias set is finite, so the identity passes to every real \(p\). The same argument works with per-volume errors tending to zero. Here both recording identities are exact.

Write \(K_f (k) = d_1 (k)^{\ast} B (k) d_1 (k)\) for the fine Gaussian form and \(K_c (p)\) for the coarse one, formed with the metric rows of the fine bed and of the coarse bed. The order statement uses, as an input, the two matching equations of the installed matrices for this pair of beds: with \(Q_i\) the block mean and \(\kappa\) the penalty coefficient, on every torus of the family

\(\displaystyle K_{f, i} W_i = Q_i^{\textsf{T}} K_{c, i}, \qquad Q_i W_i = I - \kappa^{- 1} K_{c, i} .\) (11.103)

These are the diagonal two-scale identities of the fine bed, the coarse bed and the tower; they are hypotheses of the one-step construction (Subsubsection 11.21.2). Stationarity of the branch and its Hessian gap alone do not identify an independently chosen coarse form; compare Subsection 11.10. The first equation of (11.103) implies, in the block Fourier coordinates,

\(\displaystyle K_f (k_l (p)) N_l (p)_a = A_l (p) K_c (p) e_a,\) (11.104)

where \(A_l (p)\) is the corresponding component of the adjoint block-mean map and the inverse block Fourier change of coordinates. It is analytic near zero for fixed \(L\). To obtain (11.104), write \(K_f X = Q^{\ast} K_c\) at the grid momenta and diagonalize the fine Gaussian form in its alias blocks. This identity then follows on arbitrary real momenta from its exact finite-grid versions by the preceding density argument, using that the coarse sides tend to infinity and that both metric rows are continuous on the real momenta. Since \(K_c\) contains a curl on each side,

\(\displaystyle K_c (p) = O (|p|^2) .\)

11.18.4Suppressing the nonprincipal aliases

Fix \(l \ne 0\) and real \(p\) near zero. Then \(k = k_l (p)\) stays away from zero modulo \(2 \pi\). Let \(D (k) = d_0 (k)\) and \(\omega (k) = \sum_a |q_a (k) |^2 > 0\). Project the bridge component off its longitudinal part:

\(\displaystyle N_l^{\perp} (p) = (I - D (k) \omega (k)^{- 1} D (k)^{\ast}) N_l (p) .\)

The matrix \(D (k)^{\ast} D (k)\) is \(\omega (k) I\) on colours, so \(D (k)^{\ast} N_l^{\perp} (p) = 0\). The fine kinetic form kills pure gauge vectors. Combining this fact with (11.104) gives

\(\displaystyle (K_f (k) + D (k) D (k)^{\ast}) N_l^{\perp} (p) = A_l (p) K_c (p) .\) (11.105)

The matrix on the left is invertible at \(p = 0\). Here the fine floor is required for the same bed on every finite period, with one positive lower constant. Passing its Fourier inequality to real frequencies gives

\(\displaystyle \langle v, K_f (k) v \rangle \geqslant c \|d_1 (k) v\|^2, \qquad c > 0.\)

The coordinate exterior identity is

\(\displaystyle \|d_1 (k) v\|^2 + \|D (k)^{\ast} v\|^2 = \omega (k) \|v\|^2 .\)

It follows either by expanding both squares or from the anticommutation of exterior multiplication and contraction. A vector in the kernel of \(K_f (k) + D (k) D (k)^{\ast}\) must make both nonnegative terms zero, and hence must be zero. Finite dimensionality gives invertibility. The matrix depends continuously on \(p\), so its inverse is bounded near zero for this fixed \(l, L\). Equation (11.105) now proves

\(\displaystyle N_l^{\perp} (p) = O (|p|^2) .\)

This is a qualitative order statement. No uniform lower bound in \(L\) is asserted for this inverse.

The Ward identities (11.98) permit replacing both \(N\) factors in each alias term of (11.102) by their transverse projections. For \(l \ne 0\), each contributes order two; \(S (k_l (p))\) is bounded near \(p = 0\). Every nonprincipal term therefore has order four. For the principal alias, \(k_0 (p) = \mu p\), and \(S (\mu p)\) already has order four. Its two bridge factors are analytic and bounded near zero. This term has order four as well. There are finitely many aliases, and multiplication by the fixed factor \(L^4\) does not change Taylor order. Hence \(H\) has order four.

Together with (11.101) and the exact recording identity, this supplies the inherited loop picture used in Subsection 11.17. Its strip bound is uniform in the blocking side under the stated bridge bounds; its order-four property uses the all-period floor and the exact coarse–fine stationary equation. In particular it does not assume that the longitudinal alias amplitudes themselves are small.

Proposition 11.30. (The inherited loop picture) Fix \(\sigma > 0\), \(D \geqslant 0\) and \(\tau > 0\). There are \(w > 0\) and \(C' \geqslant 0\), depending only on \(G, \sigma, D, \tau\), such that the following holds for every patch with blocking side \(L\) satisfying \(\sigma / L \leqslant w\) and the branch compatibility of the kernel, every sequence of coarse sides \(m_i \to \infty\) with fine sides \(n_i = Lm_i\) and block steps with that patch, every clip \(p\), path \(R\), fine bed \(B\), coarse bed \(T\) and bridge \(X\). Assume:

  1. \(R\) has path bounds \((\tau, A, \Lambda)\) in \(p\) for some \(A\) and some \(\Lambda \geqslant 0\);

  2. \(X\) is holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\sigma\) and satisfies (11.99) there, and for every \(i\) it is the common Fourier representation of a matrix \(W_i\);

  3. for every \(i\) there are a forest and an analytic map \(\rho_i\) into its tree chart with \(\rho_i (0) = 0\), stationary near the origin for the tested action of every choice of block representatives, these tested actions being real analytic near the origin, such that the derivative of \(t \mapsto \rho_i (tx)\) at \(t = 0\) is the linear prediction of the tree chart from \(W_i\); and the matching equations (11.103) hold for \(W_i\), \(B\) and \(T\);

  4. the metric row of \(T\) has a strip bound at some positive width, the metric row of \(B\) has a strip bound at width \(\sigma / L\), and the floor of \(B, p\) holds at every torus side with one constant \(\varepsilon > 0\).

Then there is a two-leg picture \(H\) such that, for every \(i\), \(H\) records exactly the second Taylor coefficient of the inherited loop of \(R\) along \(\rho_i\) on the \(i\)th coarse torus; every component of \(H\) is holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\sigma / 4\) and bounded there by \(L^{- 2} \Lambda C'\); and every component has order four at zero momentum along real rays.

Proof. This is the content of the present section. The picture is the pullback by \(X\) of the fine picture \(S\) of the first subsection, which needs (1). The strip bound is (11.101), proved under (11.100) and (2). The recording identity for the nonlinear pullback uses the installed tangent in (3). The order at zero is proved in the last two subsections from the alias formula, the first equation of (11.103), the strip bounds and the floor in (4), and the Ward identities (11.98).\(\Box\)

In the one-step construction the hypotheses of this proposition are part of the standing data of the step. These contain, in addition, the following data, none of which plays a role in the argument above:

  • a positive Hessian gap of the branches;

  • the response data of Subsection 11.8 and their two recording conditions;

  • the identification of the response map at each side with the one built from the installed matrix \(W_i\);

  • the vanishing of the frame component of that response map.

The proposition does not assume them, and so applies to the standing data of the step whether or not these further data are present.

11.19Transporting the pole coefficient through the block bridge

The annulus calculation concerns a fine-lattice determinant, whereas the stationary output uses its pullback to coarse fields. We prove that their leading quadratic coefficients agree. This uses both quadratic matching equations for the selected bridge, including its normalization at zero momentum.

Let \(\Pi_f\) be a common quadratic picture for the fine pole functional

\(\displaystyle P_f (x) = \frac{1}{2} \log \det \left( K (x) + d (x) d (x)^{\ast} + \sum_a s_a^{\ast} C (x) s_a \right) - \log \det (d (x)^{\ast} d (x) + C (x)) .\)

The notation is that of Subsection 11.9. Suppose its finite-volume recording error is at most the fine site cardinality times \(e_m\), with \(e_m \to 0\) along the selected periods. Let \(\Pi_c\) be the common picture of the same functions pulled back by the installed linear bridge \(W\), with coarse per-site errors tending to zero. Both pictures are holomorphic on positive strips. Fix the block side \(L\) throughout the argument.

The standing data are those of one stationary step, and all of them refer to the same step. The gauge group is simple. The coarse sides \(m_i\) tend to infinity and the fine sides are \(n_i = Lm_i\). The path \(R\) has path bounds in the clip \(p\) and is attached to the fine bed \(B\); the metric row of \(B\) has a strip bound at width \(\sigma / L\), the floor of \(B, p\) holds with one constant \(\varepsilon > 0\) at every torus side, and the sworn condition of \(B\) holds at every \(n_i\). The metric row of the coarse bed \(T\) has a strip bound at width \(\sigma\). The bridge \(\Phi\) is holomorphic and periodic on the strip of width \(\sigma\) and is the common Fourier representation of the installed matrices \(W_i\), which satisfy both matching equations (11.103) for \(B\) and \(T\), with block mean \(Q_i\) and penalty coefficient \(\kappa\). The frame and kinetic response data of Subsection 11.8, from which the rank response is built, are formed with these same \(W_i\). The pole functional \(P_f\) is formed with the regulator \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), and the fine pole picture \(\Pi_f\) records it at the sides \(n_i\). Fix also the block representatives of the step at each side. The functional \(P_f\) and its pullback by \(W_i\) are defined without them; they enter only the recording of the pulled-back functions by the common picture \(\Pi_c\). The argument of this section does not use the stationary branch or its Hessian gap, although a stationary step also carries both of them.

The block determinant matrix \(M_{\text{rank}}\) is the direct sum of the fine pole matrix and an identity on coarse scalar coordinates, after the coordinate permutation separating the two kinds of variables. Thus its determinant equals the fine pole determinant. The scalar determinant is the same in both presentations. The Fourier matrix identities proved above therefore identify the coarse rank picture with the picture of \(P_f \circ W\); this is an equality for the actual selected finite-volume functions.

The pole function is invariant under local gauge changes, constant color rotations and unsigned coordinate permutations. For gauge changes this follows by conjugating every factor: the fine Hessian in left coordinates, the covariant difference, its resolvent regulator and the component maps all transform by the corresponding orthogonal adjoint actions. Determinants are unchanged. The same argument, with coordinate permutations whose direction signs are all positive, proves this permutation invariance. Its first derivative at zero vanishes by the simple-color argument in Subsection 11.10. Passing the finite-volume identities to the common picture, as in Subsection 11.17, shows that its bilinear matrix \(S (p)\) has both Ward identities

\(\displaystyle S (p) d_0 (p) = 0, \qquad d_0 (- p)^T S (p) = 0,\)

The first-order part of the Ward identities forces \(S (0) = 0\). To prove that the linear part also vanishes, fix the two color indices and write it as \(S_{ab} (p) = \sum_c T_{ab, c} p_c + O (|p|^2)\). The two Ward identities, at quadratic order, give

\(\displaystyle T_{ab, b} = T_{ab, a} = 0, \qquad T_{ab, c} + T_{ac, b} = 0.\)

These equations also kill the case \(a = b\): then \(T_{aa, c} = - T_{ac, a} = 0\). If \(a, b, c\) are distinct, the unsigned permutation interchanging \(b, c\) and fixing \(a\) gives \(T_{ab, c} = T_{ac, b}\); the displayed antisymmetry makes both zero. All repeated-index cases are already zero. Thus \(S\) vanishes to order at least two. This uses only unsigned permutations; no reflection symmetry of the pole functional is assumed. The finite-volume errors disappear after division by the site cardinality. No rate uniform in \(L\) is used in this step.

Write \(H (p)\) for the bilinear matrix of \(\Pi_c\) and \(N_l (p)\) for the fine alias amplitudes of \(W\). The finite-wave expansion from Subsection 11.18 gives

\(\displaystyle H (p) = L^4 \sum_l N_{- l} (- p)^T S (k_l (p)) N_l (p), \qquad k_l (p) = \mu (p + 2 \pi l) .\) (11.106)

We spell out the role of errors here. A pulled-back coordinate wave is a finite sum of fine alias waves with amplitudes \(N_l (p)\). For fixed \(L\), these amplitudes remain bounded as the real momentum approaches a specified value. Multilinearity bounds the error on each pair by the original per-site error times the finite sum of the products of its amplitudes. The ratio of fine to coarse site cardinalities is \(L^4\). Hence the error still tends to zero. Opposite aliases are the only surviving pairs, by fine translation invariance; all other pairs tend to zero. Continuity and a dense sequence of external grid momenta prove (11.106) at every real momentum.

For \(l \ne 0\), the transverse projection of \(N_l (p)\) has order two at \(p = 0\), by the all-period kinetic floor and the primal matching equation proved in Subsection 11.18. The two Ward identities of \(S\) remove the longitudinal parts of both amplitudes. Thus every nonprincipal term of (11.106) has order at least four. This argument does not require \(S\) to vanish at the nonprincipal alias.

It remains to determine the principal amplitude at zero. The dual matching equation, the second equation of (11.103), passes from the grids to all real momenta by the density argument already used. With \(Q (p)\) the symbol of the block mean it reads

\(\displaystyle Q (p) \Phi (p) = I - \kappa^{- 1} K_c (p) .\)

Since \(K_c (0) = 0\), its value at zero is \(Q (0) \Phi (0) = I\). Each nonprincipal alias amplitude at zero is a pure gauge vector, because its transverse projection vanishes there. The block mean commutes with the gauge difference, so it maps this vector to a coarse gradient at momentum zero, which is zero. Hence those aliases contribute nothing to \(Q (0) \Phi (0)\). On the principal constant alias, the normalized transverse weights sum to one and the straight block path has \(L\) edges; therefore the mean acts as multiplication by \(L\). We conclude

\(\displaystyle N_0 (0) = \mu I.\) (11.107)

In particular this normalization is a consequence of the actual two matching equations, rather than a choice of Fourier convention made after the comparison.

Analyticity gives \(N_0 (p) = \mu I + O (|p|)\). Since \(S\) vanishes to order two and \(k_0 (p) = \mu p\), expanding the two principal amplitudes yields

\(\displaystyle H (p) - L^4 \mu^2 S (\mu p) = O (|p|^3)\)

along every ray. The nonprincipal terms have already been shown to be of order four. If \(S_2\) is the homogeneous quadratic term of \(S\), the quadratic term of the expression on the right is

\(\displaystyle L^4 \mu^2 \mu^2 S_2 (p) = S_2 (p) .\)

Thus \(H\) and \(S\) have identical quadratic Taylor terms, and in particular identical drift coefficients in every color component.

We state the result and its use.

Proposition 11.31. (Transport of the pole coefficient) Under the standing data listed at the beginning of this section, let \(\Pi_f\) be a two-leg picture, with components holomorphic, periodic and bounded on a strip of positive width, which records the fine pole functional \(P_f\) of \(p, R\) with regulator \(L^{- 1}\) at every fine side \(n_i\) with error \(n_i^4 e_i\), where \(e_i \to 0\). Let \(\Pi_c\) be the picture associated, as in Lemma 11.22, with the rank response kernel, and assume that this kernel is jointly holomorphic, periodic and bounded on a strip of positive width. Then for every color \(c\)

\(\displaystyle \alpha_c (\Pi_c) = \alpha_c (\Pi_f) .\)

Proof. By the block identity for the determinant matrix recalled above and Lemma 11.22, \(\Pi_c\) records \(P_f \circ W_i\) on the \(i\)th coarse torus with error equal to the coarse site count times a quantity tending to zero. The argument of this section then shows that the bilinear matrices \(H\) of \(\Pi_c\) and \(S\) of \(\Pi_f\) have the same quadratic homogeneous term at zero momentum. The coefficient \(\alpha_c\) is twice the real part of one entry of that term.\(\Box\)

In the one-step construction three coefficients occur, and the proposition relates two of them. Let \(\mathcal{Y}_{\text{det}}\) be the picture of the completed determinant correction \(\mathcal{B}_t\) of Subsection 11.16 along the stationary branch, with regulator \(\mu = L^{- 1}\). The scalar of the output path is \(\alpha = \alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}_{\text{det}})\) (Proposition 11.29). The inherited loop does not enter \(\alpha\): by the moving Haar identity (11.25) the thermal function is the inherited loop plus \(\mathcal{B}_t\) along the same branch, and the inherited picture has order four (Proposition 11.30), so its quadratic coefficient is zero. The comparison of the completed determinant response with the rank response is the subject of Sections 11.9 and 11.10: it is to supply a constant \(C_{\text{cmp}}\), fixed before \(L\), with

\(\displaystyle | \alpha_c (\mathcal{Y}_{\text{det}}) - \alpha_c (\Pi_c) | \leqslant C_{\text{cmp}} .\)

This is an input here. Finally Proposition 11.28 supplies a fine pole picture \(\Pi_f\) for the same clip, path, fine bed and regulator, recorded at the same fine sides \(n_i\), with

\(\displaystyle | \alpha_c (\Pi_f) + \lambda_G \log L| \leqslant D_0 .\)

The three statements concern the same data, and the triangle inequality gives

\(\displaystyle | \alpha + \lambda_G \log L| \leqslant D_0 + C_{\text{cmp}} .\)

The hypotheses (11.88) of the annulus estimate, for the fine bed and the fine sides, are among the standing data of this section together with the bound on the direct coefficient of \(p\) and the strip bound of \(B\) at a fixed width.

11.20Convergence of the free precision horizon

We prove the retained-horizon convergence used for the stationary paths. The cutoff tends to infinity while the scale and block side remain fixed. All coefficients are kept on the lattice cover. Finite-torus matrices are used to identify their limits; they are not substituted for the cover coefficients.

11.20.1Compactness with an exponential row bound

A row \(K\) between finite coordinate sets \(I, J\) has coefficients \(K_{ab} (z)\), \(z \in \mathbb{Z}^4\). Suppose, eventually in \(N\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_z e^{\sigma |z|_1} |K_{N, ab} (z) | \leqslant C \qquad (a \in I, b \in J), \qquad \sigma > 0.\) (11.108)

A subsequence of any given cutoff sequence converges in every coefficient: enumerate the countably many entries, extract successively in their bounded real intervals, and take the diagonal subsequence. If its limit is \(K\), pass to the limit in each finite subsum of (11.108). The nonnegative sums remain bounded by \(C\), so the full sum has the same bound.

For every \(0 \leqslant \tau < \sigma\) this coefficient convergence gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_z e^{\tau |z|_1} |K_{N, ab} (z) - K_{ab} (z) | \longrightarrow 0.\) (11.109)

Indeed its summands are at most \(2 Ce^{- (\sigma - \tau) |z|_1}\), and

\(\displaystyle \sum_{z \in \mathbb{Z}^4} e^{- t |z|_1} = \left( \frac{1 + e^{- t}}{1 - e^{- t}} \right)^4 < \infty \qquad (t > 0) .\)

First make the tail of this majorant small, and then use convergence on its finite complement. This also proves convergence of the Fourier symbols on real momenta and of the periodized matrices on every fixed torus, since their entries are sums of subfamilies of the same coefficients. The argument applies to a cross row by including its finitely many block residues in \(I\).

Two ways of identifying the resulting cluster limits will be useful. If the Fourier symbols have a prescribed limit on a dense subset of the real momentum torus, any two cluster rows have the same symbol there. Their symbols are continuous by absolute summability, so they agree everywhere. Integrating against \(e^{- ip \cdot z}\) recovers the coefficient \(K_{ab} (z)\), proving equality of the rows. Alternatively, it suffices that the cluster rows have equal matrices on an unbounded sequence of torus sides. For a fixed displacement \(z\) the appropriate periodized entry is

\(\displaystyle \sum_{h \in \mathbb{Z}^4} K_{ab} (z + nh) .\)

Its term \(h = 0\) is \(K_{ab} (z)\). All other terms have displacement at least \(n - |z|_1\) and their sum tends to zero by absolute summability. Thus these matrices recover every cover coefficient as \(n \to \infty\). In either case the cluster limit is unique. If the original sequence did not converge to it in some coordinate, a subsequence staying a fixed distance away would have a convergent further subsequence, a contradiction. Hence the full sequence converges, with (11.109) at every smaller width.

11.20.2The primitive multipliers along the actual scale schedule

Fix a scale \(m\), and let \(v_{d, m}\) be the kernel history consisting of the next \(d\) prescribed blocking steps, with a top kernel history at its fine end. Its mass side \(M_{d, m}\) tends to infinity, since each block side is at least three. The actual path at terminal cutoff \(N\) uses precisely \(v_{N - m, m}\); for fixed \(m\), its depth \(N - m\) tends to infinity. Consequently each depth limit below also applies to the selected terminal cutoff sequence. For real momentum \(p\) and a direction \(a\), put

\(\displaystyle u_{d, m, a} (p) = D_{v_{d, m}, a} (p / M_{d, m}) D_{v_{d, m}, a} (- p / M_{d, m}),\)

using the cumulative drive of Subsection 3.6. A single step with patch \(P_m\) and side \(L_m\) gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle u_{d + 1, m, a} (p) = | \text{pullLine}_{P_m, a} (p / L_m) |^2 u_{d, m + 1, a} (p / L_m) .\)

Each rail and centered plank is an average of unit complex numbers on real momenta, up to a unit phase. Its modulus is at most one. Thus the factor in front lies in \([0, 1]\). Starting with \(u_{0, m, a} = 1\), induction on \(d\) in the displayed recurrence shows

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant u_{d + 1, m, a} (p) \leqslant u_{d, m, a} (p) \leqslant 1.\)

This proves convergence for every real \(p\), also after any fixed reciprocal translation of \(p\).

The noise list of \(v_{d + 1, m}\) is the list for \(v_{d, m}\) with one additional kernel history appended. This follows by induction from the list rule under one hop: both old lists undergo the same hop, with the same top element prepended. Each additional real-momentum feed is nonnegative. The accumulated noise \(N_{d, m, a} (p)\) is therefore nondecreasing. The uniform noise estimate of Subsection 3.6 bounds it above, independently of \(d\) for \(p\) in the base cube. At depth one it is one. Its limit \(N_{m, a} (p)\) consequently satisfies

\(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant N_{m, a} (p) < \infty .\)

We also need the high-alias part \(H_{v_{d, m}, a} (p)\). Extend its finite sum to \(\mathbb{Z}^4\) by setting it to zero outside the centered representatives of the nonzero residues of \(M_{d, m}\). Each fixed \(z \ne 0\) is represented for all sufficiently large \(d\). Put \(q = p + 2 \pi z\). The corresponding summand is then exactly

\(\displaystyle \frac{u_{d, m, a} (q)}{d_{M_{d, m}} (q)} .\)

The numerator has a limit by the preceding monotonicity. The identity

\(\displaystyle M (e^{iq_a / M} - 1) \longrightarrow iq_a\)

gives \(d_M (q) \to |q|^2\). For \(p\) in the base cube and \(z \ne 0\), \(q \ne 0\), so its denominator has a nonzero limit. The uniform alias bounds already proved give one summable majorant

\(\displaystyle C \prod_{a = 1}^4 (1 + |z_a |)^{- 2}\)

for these extended summands. The sum of this product is finite by the one-dimensional inverse-square series. Dominated convergence for the series proves convergence of the high-alias part. At \(z = 0\) the extended summand is identically zero; no denominator at the origin is used in this argument.

For \(p \ne 0\) the full directional covariance multiplier is

\(\displaystyle L_{d, m, a} (p) = \frac{u_{d, m, a} (p)}{d_{M_{d, m}} (p)} + H_{v_{d, m}, a} (p) + k^{- 1} N_{d, m, a} (p), \qquad k > 0.\)

It has a finite real limit \(L_{m, a} (p) \geqslant k^{- 1} > 0\). The pulse and dual-pulse symbols are the rational matrix expressions in these multipliers given in Subsection 3.5. Their directional denominators have just been shown to have positive limits. The remaining scalar denominator has the form

\(\displaystyle \sum_a \frac{|e^{ip_a} - 1|^2}{L_{m, a} (p)} .\)

It is strictly positive off the origin in the base cube: every term is nonnegative and some numerator is nonzero. Consequently every entry of both symbols converges there. At zero their normalized values are the identity for every cutoff. The uniform strip bounds of the free-kernel estimates and the first subsection now give convergence of both rows on the cover, with a retained exponential bound.

11.20.3The adjacent one-form and two-form transports

Fix the block side \(L = L_m\). Unlike the mass side in the preceding high-alias sum, \(L\) does not change with the terminal cutoff. For \(p \ne 0\) in the base cube, all the finitely many adjacent aliases \(q_l = (p + 2 \pi \ell_L (l)) / L\) are nonzero real momenta in that cube. Apply the directional limit above at the coarse momentum \(p\) and the fine momenta \(q_l\). The principal and nonprincipal transport formulas of Subsection 3.7 then have convergent factors.

We spell out the additional denominator check in those formulas. The finite alias denominator is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{V}_N (p) = \sum_l |m_{P_m} (q_l) |^2 \sum_a L_{N, a}^{\text{scaled}} (q_l) .\)

Here \(L_{N, a}^{\text{scaled}}\) is the fine directional multiplier with the fixed positive scaling \(L^{- 2}\). Every summand has a nonnegative real limit, each inner sum has a strictly positive limit, and the principal patch mass \(m_{P_m} (p / L)\) is nonzero on the base cube. Hence \(\lim_N \mathcal{V}_N (p) > 0\). All other divisions in the transport formulas are by the positive directional or scalar denominators just checked. Finite matrix products, sums and division by these nonzero limits prove convergence of every alias transport. Fourier reconstruction over the finite set of \(L^4\) aliases proves convergence of the adjacent transport symbols. Their uniform row bounds again identify unique cover limits. Possible exceptional momenta at the origin are removed by continuity of the cluster symbols, not by taking a quotient there.

11.20.4Convergence of amendments, exterior lifts and roots

Several operations preserve the cover convergence just established. Sums, transposes and scalar multiples do so directly. For products, take the minimum of two positive available widths; the convolution of rows has a uniform bound there, multiplied by the finite middle coordinate count. Its symbol is the product of the two symbols, so the first subsection identifies its unique limit. For an inverse row with a uniform bound, suppose \(K_N^{- 1} K_N = I\) on every torus. Every cluster limit \(Q\) of the inverse rows satisfies \(QK = I\) there by finite matrix multiplication. A square matrix with a left inverse is invertible, so that inverse is unique. Recovery from all periods then identifies \(Q\) on the cover, and proves convergence.

The exterior lifts in Subsection 3.10 are finite combinations of coordinate evaluation at \(p_a = 0\), fixed exterior matrices and divided differences

\(\displaystyle \frac{\hat{K}_N (p) - \hat{K}_N (p|_{p_a = 0})}{e^{ip_a} - 1} .\)

Their uniform bounds at a smaller positive width were proved there. For \(p_a \ne 0\) the displayed expression converges, because both numerator terms do and the denominator is fixed. Thus any two cluster symbols agree off that coordinate hyperplane, and by continuity everywhere. This proves convergence of the divided row. Repeating the argument through the finitely many axes proves convergence of the one-sided and two-sided exterior lifts, with all their fixed matrix factors.

It remains to justify the positive square roots; convergence of an arbitrary matrix factor would be false. Suppose \(P_N\) have a common exponential row bound, their torus matrices are positive semidefinite, and \(P_N^{\textsf{T}} P_N\) converge on every torus. Two cluster limits \(P, Q\) have positive semidefinite torus matrices, since symmetry and nonnegativity of each quadratic form pass to the limit. Their squares are equal. A positive semidefinite square root is unique: if \(A \geqslant 0\) and \(A^2 = B\), then \(A\) commutes with \(B\); on each eigenspace of \(B\), diagonalizing the symmetric restriction of \(A\) forces all eigenvalues to be the nonnegative square root of that eigenvalue of \(B\). Hence \(A\) is fixed on every eigenspace. Apply this to \(P\) and \(Q\) on each torus, then recover the cover coefficients. The unique-cluster-limit argument proves convergence of \(P_N\) itself.

The amended beds have exactly these properties. If \(B_N\) is the dual-pulse row, its amended inverse precision is

\(\displaystyle A_N = B_N + l (d_2 B_N)^{\textsf{T}} (d_2 B_N), \qquad l \geqslant 0,\)

and its precision is \(G_N = A_N^{- 1}\). Products and the inverse argument give their limits. The root row has square \(G_N - cI\), where \(c\) is the fixed direct clipping coefficient. Its uniform strip bound and positive semidefinite torus values are supplied by Subsection 3.8, so the positive-root argument applies. The original pulse, dual pulse, amended precision, its inverse and the root row therefore converge together.

For the curvature root, use the explicit amended row of Subsection 3.10:

\(\displaystyle R_N = D_N^{\textsf{T}} (C_N + lI - hS_N^{\textsf{T}} S_N) D_N, \qquad D_N = (I + ld_2 B_{c, N} d_2^{\textsf{T}})^{- 1} .\)

Here \(l, h\) are the fixed coarse and fine amendment coefficients; \(C_N, S_N\) are the two exterior lifts constructed there. Their convergence follows from the operations just proved. The positivity and uniform strip bound of \(R_N\) and its positive root are already established. Since that root's square is \(R_N\), it too converges. All of these statements concern the actual chosen positive roots; no new choice of signs or bases has been made.

11.20.5Completing the retained tower

The tower considered here is not an arbitrary completion of the converging beds. It is the tower of the retained horizon produced in Subsection 3.10 from this same recursively generated bed horizon, with the nonnegative amendment coefficients used above. That construction keeps the beds, the one-form predictor and the two-form transport of the original horizon. For every cutoff \(N > m\) it chooses, together with the three blocks below, a curvature root row \(P_N\) with these properties: a strip bound whose width and constant depend on the stage but not on \(N\); positive semidefinite torus matrices; the identity \(P_N^{\textsf{T}} P_N = R_N\) on every torus, where \(R_N\) is the amended curvature row displayed above; and the block formulas below on every torus of the schedule. The convergence assertion of this subsection is for this jointly chosen tower and uses each of these four properties.

The full precision factors \(J_{f, N}, J_{c, N}\) consist of the fixed direct block and the root rows, so they converge on every torus. Write \(G_{f, N}, G_{c, N}\) for the corresponding precisions and \(R_{f, N} = G_{f, N}^{- 1}\), \(R_{c, N} = G_{c, N}^{- 1}\). With the two-form transport \(T_N\) and curvature root \(P_N\), the exact tower blocks are

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} M_{F, N} & = J_{f, N} R_{f, N} T_N J_{c, N}^{\textsf{T}},\\ M_{N, N} & = k^{- 1 / 2} d_{1, c}^{\textsf{T}} J_{c, N}^{\textsf{T}},\\ M_{O, N} & = \left[ \begin{array}{c} I - J_{c, N} R_{c, N} J_{c, N}^{\textsf{T}}\\ P_N d_{2, c} R_{c, N} J_{c, N}^{\textsf{T}} \end{array} \right] . \end{array}\)

These are the same formulas as (3.53), on each selected physical torus of side \(n_m (p)\), with its prescribed fine partner. Here \(n_m (p)\) denotes the period sequence fixed at scale \(m\) by the schedule. Here \(p\) is fixed while \(N\) increases. The committed transition identities apply on precisely these tori. Every finite matrix factor has a limit there, hence so does each block. Their uniform strip bounds were purchased in the retained tower construction. The period-recovery criterion from the first subsection gives convergence of their cover coefficients. Use the selected sides above, which tend to infinity as \(p\) does; no assumption on convergence of an unrelated periodic realization is needed.

Together with the one-form predictor and two-form transport, these are precisely the five retained tower rows. Thus at every fixed scale all five bed rows and all five tower rows converge, and each family keeps some positive exponential width and finite budget. The purchases of widths and budgets are made at the fixed stages, before choosing the later terminal cutoff. The eventual normalization changes discussed in Subsection 14.21 preserve these limits. This supplies the free-horizon convergence input of that section from the actual recursively selected free kernels.

11.21Attaching finite-cutoff paths on the selected stationary route

This section records the selected stationary route from its horizontal annulus estimate through finite-cutoff path attachment. It separates three different jobs: producing the annulus estimate, using it in a one-step path construction, and assembling the resulting transitions. Only the last job is a finite induction. The quantitative inputs to the one-step construction are stated at their actual interface and are not inferred from that induction.

11.21.1Bounds for the one-step stationary path

The background quadratic data \(B\) (called a bed below) comprise the curvature metric and its root coefficient array. The parameter list \(p\) (the clip) specifies the quadratic test action, including its direct coefficient \(p. \text{direct}\). A path is a collection of row, loop, cubic, and quartic coefficients. For a parameter list \(p\), strip width \(\tau\), coefficient bound \(A\), and loop bound \(\Lambda\), the predicate \(R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda)\) is the path budget: each exponentially weighted row family has sum at most \(A\); the loop atom-budget family has sum at most \(\Lambda\); and the cubic and quartic atom-budget families each have sum at most \(A\). In addition, every atom is invariant, the row symbols satisfy the metric identity, row-symbol terms are summable, and the loop coefficient and its first moment have summable terms and sum to zero. The attachment predicate \(\text{Attached} (B, R)\) says that the path coefficients at each plaquette and cover offset sum to the bed's root entry. In particular, these are simultaneous global summability conditions, not merely pointwise bounds.

The precise annulus estimate, denoted \(\text{Ann} (G, \sigma, \varepsilon, C, Z, \tau, A)\), has the following quantifiers. It supplies an integer \(L_{\min}\) and \(D_0 \geqslant 0\). For every odd side \(L \geqslant L_{\min}\), every side sequence \(m_i \to \infty\), every bed \(B\), clip \(p\), path \(R\), and \(\Lambda\), impose the hypotheses of Proposition 11.28:

\(\displaystyle R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda), \quad \text{Attached} (B, R), \quad B. \text{metric.strip} (\sigma, C), \quad p. \text{direct} \leqslant Z,\)

along with \(\text{Floor} (B, p, n, \varepsilon, C)\) for every \(n\) and \(\text{Match} (B, m_i, G)\) for every \(i\). Then there are one Fourier kernel \(Y\), nonnegative strip constants \(v, E\), and a single error sequence \(e_i \to 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \text{Strip} (v, E, Y_{ij}) \quad \text{for every } i, j, \qquad \text{Rec} \hspace{-0.17em} (Y, \text{Pole} (p, R, L^{- 1}), m_i^4 e_i) \quad \text{for every } i,\)

and \(| \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (Y) + \lambda_G \log L| \leqslant D_0\) for every color \(j\). Here \(\text{Strip}\) means a bounded periodic holomorphic extension with width \(v\) and bound \(E\), and \(\text{Match}\) denotes the finite-volume quadratic matching condition for \(B\). The pole Hessian \(\text{Pole}\) and its finite-mesh recording condition \(\text{Rec}\) are specified in Subsection 11.14. The latter is a finite-mesh identity up to the stated error; no passage to a limit in \(i\) is part of this assertion.

Proposition 11.28 supplies this estimate on the same fine bed, clip and path. Its Wilson reference and its Wilson-subtracted correction are added before any finite-period recording is compared. The fee bounds give one common response strip; the recording error is then the exponentially small torus-mean error. The face estimates of Subsection 11.12 give the two radial inequalities

\(\displaystyle |g (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_1}{(\|x\|+ \mu)^4}, \qquad |g (x) - h_G (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_2}{\|x\|^3} + \frac{A_2 \mu^2}{\|x\|^6} \quad (\mu \leqslant \|x\| \leqslant 1) .\)

Their constants precede the blocking side. The following elementary integration is the last step giving its logarithmic drift constant.

Lemma 11.32. (Radial logarithmic coefficient bound) Assume \(0 < \mu \leqslant 1\), \(A_1, A_2 \geqslant 0\), and let \(E_4 \subset \mathbb{R}^4\) be a measurable finite-volume region containing the unit ball. Let \(g\) be real measurable and integrable on \(E_4\), and let \(h_G\) be integrable on the annulus \(I_{\mu} = \{x : \mu <\|x\| \leqslant 1\}\). Assume the radial estimates

\(\displaystyle |g (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_1}{(\|x\|+ \mu)^4} \quad (x \in E_4), \qquad |g (x) - h_G (x) | \leqslant \frac{A_2}{\|x\|^3} + \frac{A_2 \mu^2}{\|x\|^6} \quad (x \in I_{\mu}) .\)

Assume also the coefficient and pole identities

\(\displaystyle \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (F) = c_{\text{wt}} \int_{E_4} g (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx, \qquad c_{\text{wt}} \int_{I_{\mu}} h_G (x) \hspace{0.17em} dx = - \lambda_G \log \mu^{- 1}, \quad c_{\text{wt}} > 0.\)

For the productive picture \(F\), set

\(\displaystyle C_{\text{rad}} (A_1, A_2) = A_1 \left( \frac{\pi^2}{2} + \text{vol} (E_4) \right) + 3 \pi^2 A_2 .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle | \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (F) + \lambda_G \log \mu^{- 1} | \leqslant c_{\text{wt}} C_{\text{rad}} (A_1, A_2) .\)

Proof. Write \(E_4\) for the finite-volume four-dimensional integration region, \(B_1 = \{x : \|x\| \leqslant 1\}\), \(B_{\mu} = \{x : \|x\| \leqslant \mu\}\), \(I = \{x : \mu <\|x\| \leqslant 1\}\), and \(Q = E_4 \setminus B_1\). Polar coordinates give \(\text{vol} (B_r) = \pi^2 r^4 / 2\). On \(B_{\mu}\), the first radial estimate implies \(|g (x) | \leqslant A_1 \mu^{- 4}\), so \(| \int_{B_{\mu}} g| \leqslant A_1 \pi^2 / 2\). On \(Q\), one has \(\|x\| \geqslant 1\), and hence \(|g (x) | \leqslant A_1\); therefore \(| \int_Q g| \leqslant A_1 \text{vol} (E_4)\).

On \(I\), integrate the second estimate in radial coordinates. The two radial integrals are

\(\displaystyle \int_I \|x\|^{- 3} \hspace{0.17em} dx = 2 \pi^2 (1 - \mu), \qquad \int_I \|x\|^{- 6} \hspace{0.17em} dx = 2 \pi^2 \frac{\mu^{- 2} - 1}{2} .\)

Thus

\(\displaystyle \int_I |g - h_G | \leqslant 2 \pi^2 A_2 (1 - \mu) + \pi^2 A_2 \mu^2 (\mu^{- 2} - 1) \leqslant 3 \pi^2 A_2 .\)

The pole integral is exactly the logarithmic term: \(c_{\text{wt}} \int_I h_G = - \lambda_G \log \mu^{- 1}\), by the assumed pole identity. The Fourier formula for \(\alpha^{\text{ann}}_j\) is \(c_{\text{wt}} \int_{E_4} g\). Splitting the integral over \(B_{\mu} \cup I \cup Q\) and using the three absolute bounds proves the claim. The radial integrals were evaluated above; the pole identity is a separate hypothesis. No uniformity beyond the displayed radial estimates is used.\(\Box\)

11.21.2The stationary chart receipts

The chart data consumed by the path interpolation is produced at each fixed finite volume. It is useful to give this construction separately: the positive Hessian gap, critical chart, and installed-matrix approximation enter the later path theorem as supplied data, but their source is an implicit-function argument, not an additional limit assumption.

Throughout this subsection a kernel choice with penalty \(K_{\text{pen}} > 0\), an odd blocking side \(L\) and a phase \(j\) are fixed, and \(R_{\text{macro}} = (j + 3) \ell + 4 \ell\) denotes the macro radius, where \(\ell = L. \text{val}\). A one-step datum consists of a coarse side \(c\) and a fine side \(f = c \ell\) together with the blocking maps of the kernel. It is called larger when its patch satisfies the branch compatibility hypothesis of the kernel and

\(\displaystyle 100 R_{\text{macro}} \leqslant f, \qquad 1 < c.\)

All statements below are for a larger one-step datum, an allowed forest \(F\) of its blocking ratio, and a local choice at the parent of each fine edge. Configurations are taken in the forest slice, and forest directions are extended by zero on the tree edges.

Start with the planted comparison for a coarse site \(i\) and a fine edge \(e = (x, a)\), where \(i\) is the parent of \(e\). Write \(\ell = L. \text{val}\) as above; let \(p_i\) be the planted root frame and \(P_i\) the local predictor. The planting estimates give nonnegative constants \(C_0, D_0\) and, with

\(\displaystyle \alpha_0 = 2 \max (1, \text{predictorCut}^{- 1}) (2 C_0 + D_0), \quad u_0 = (8 \ell + 1) D_0 \ell, \quad v_0 = C_0 \ell^2 + \alpha_0 \ell^{- 2} + C_0 \ell^2,\)

the finite-volume edge estimate

\(\displaystyle \text{chord} \hspace{-0.17em} \left( U_e (\text{replica} \hspace{0.17em} V)_e^{- 1} \right) \leqslant (u_0 + v_0) \hspace{0.17em} \text{port}_i (U) +\boldsymbol{1}_{\{i \ne i + \text{parentStep} (e)\}} \text{wire} (P_i, V, e) .\)

Here the first term is obtained by following the forest root walk: its length is at most the patch radius, and the planted bound charges each walk edge by \(D_0 \ell\) times the port. If the edge stays in its parent cell, the lifted path closes at the root, so this is the entire estimate. If it crosses to a neighboring parent, the endpoint root-to-frame chords each cost at most \(C_0 \ell^2 \text{port}\), while the predicted edge has chord at most \(\alpha_0 \ell^{- 2} \text{port}\). The triangle inequality adds these three terms, and the residual is precisely the displayed wire term. This is the two-case proof of the planted comparison; the tree walk and crossing endpoint costs are not hidden in a generic locality claim.

For a fixed step, summing the squared port terms over coarse sites uses Cauchy–Schwarz twice: first over the box offsets and plaquette types, then over the box offsets in the site sum. Injectivity of the map from a coarse site/plaquette pair to its embedded fine plaquette prevents extra multiplicity. The resulting factor is exactly the product of the box cardinality, the plaquette count, and the site count appearing in the curl factor. The probe wire is summed over coarse bonds and paid by the second factor, the probe factor. Combining the two with the floor bound yields

\(\displaystyle \sum_e \|v_e \|^2 \leqslant \beta_{\ell} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{D} (v) + \gamma_{\ell} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{P} (v),\)

where the coefficients are

\(\displaystyle \beta_{\ell} = (2 C^2) | \text{Site} (L) | \hspace{0.17em} | \text{Axis} | (|X| \hspace{0.17em} | \text{PlaqAxes} |) \hspace{0.17em} |X|, \qquad \gamma_{\ell} = 2 | \text{Site} (L) |,\)

with \(X\) the fixed patch-offset set and \(C\) the edge-comparison constant. Thus \(\ell\) is the blocking side cardinality, not the coarse torus side. Here \(v\) is a forest direction, extended by zero on the tree edges, \(\mathcal{D}\) is the squared physical color derivative and \(\mathcal{P}\) the squared predictor penalty. Here is the Hessian identity used in this comparison. Let \(h\) be a coarse variation and \(v\) a forest-coordinate variation. Write \(e (h, v)\) for the fine variation obtained by differentiating the forest lift at zero, and write \(Q\) for the linearized block predictor. The linearized predictor residual is \(c (h, v) = h - Qe (h, v)\). For two pairs \((h, v), (h', v')\), the tested action has second derivative

\(\displaystyle D^2 \mathcal{T} (0) [(h, v), (h', v')] = \sum_f \langle d_1 e (h, v), M_n d_1 e (h', v') \rangle_f + \text{penalty} \hspace{0.17em} \langle c (h, v), c (h', v') \rangle .\)

Indeed the Hessian of the fine main action is the first displayed bilinear form by Lemma 11.10, while its first derivative vanishes, so the second derivative of the forest lift does not contribute. The predictor penalty vanishes together with its first derivative at the identity; its quadratic Taylor term is one half of \(\text{penalty}\) times the squared norm of the predictor residual. The second-derivative chain rule therefore gives the second term and no higher derivative of the predictor. Taking \(h = h' = 0\) gives the forest Hessian used above, whose first term is bounded below by \(a\mathcal{D} (v)\) by the all-period floor. Assume \(a > 0\), \(\text{penalty} > 0\), and \(\beta_{\ell}, \gamma_{\ell} \geqslant 0\). Set

\(\displaystyle \eta = \frac{\min (a, \text{penalty})}{1 + \beta_{\ell} + \gamma_{\ell}} .\)

Then \(\eta > 0\), \(\eta \beta_{\ell} \leqslant a\) and \(\eta \gamma_{\ell} \leqslant \text{penalty}\), and the preceding two inequalities give the strict bound gap

\(\displaystyle \eta \sum_e \|v_e \|^2 \leqslant D^2 \mathcal{T} (0) [v, v] .\)

This is the quantitative Hessian lower bound used by the chart construction.

The remaining chart receipts follow in four steps. First, the exponential coefficient budgets in the path bound make each uncut row and atom series converge uniformly on a complex neighborhood of the physical field. Termwise analyticity and summability therefore give smoothness of the finite-volume main action. The chart compatibility hypothesis supplies the required analytic composition along every smooth field chart. Second, in a forest chart the lifted field agrees near the origin with the ordinary field coordinates; the tested action is the flat action composed with this lift. Differentiating this germ identity and using the finite-volume Hessian identity gives the prismatic second variation and the comptroller bilinear form.

Third, fix one allowed choice of local test and write \(F (x, z)\) for the tested action in coarse coordinates \(x\) and forest coordinates \(z\). At \((0, 0)\), \(F = 0\) and \(DF = 0\). The bound gap implies that the vertical Hessian \(D_z^2 F (0, 0)\) is positive definite, hence invertible in the finite dimensional forest space. The implicit-function theorem applied to \(D_z F (x, z) = 0\) gives a smooth branch \(z = \rho (x)\) with \(\rho (0) = 0\). By the branch compatibility hypothesis, the tested actions for any two local choices agree on a neighborhood of \((0, 0)\) in the full variables \((x, z)\), not merely on the graph of \(\rho\). Their vertical derivatives therefore agree near zero, so the same \(\rho\) is critical for each local choice.

Fourth, differentiate the stationarity equation at zero:

\(\displaystyle D^2 F (0, 0) [(x, D \rho (0) x), (0, v)] = 0 \quad \text{for every forest direction } v.\)

The comptroller formula identifies this bilinear form as the sum of the bed metric term and the penalty pairing of the predictor residual. The installed tower matrix satisfies that same linear equation by the diagonal two-scale trial. Uniqueness, from invertibility of the vertical Hessian, therefore yields \(D \rho (0) x = \text{relative} (W, x)\), the guessed-chart identity. Finally, the chain rule at the stationary branch removes the term involving \(D_z F (0, 0)\); the same comptroller formula and the diagonal tower identity give the target bed metric as the Hessian of the reduced action. This is the heat identity: for every local choice, the tested action along \(\rho\) and one half of the kinetic quadratic form of the coarse bed metric are both real-analytic at the origin and have the same second derivative there. It is an equality, not an estimate. These arguments prove the fixed-period stationarity, installed-tangent, and reduced-Hessian identities used by the transition.

The uniformity is as follows. The number \(\eta\) displayed above depends only on the kernel, the blocking side \(L\), the phase \(j\) and the floor constant \(a\): the factors \(\beta_{\ell}, \gamma_{\ell}\) involve only \(L\), the patch-offset set \(X\) of radius \(R_{\text{macro}}\) and the edge-comparison constant \(C\) of the planting estimate, which is itself chosen for \((L, j)\) and the kernel. Hence the same \(\eta\) is a gap for every larger one-step datum with these \(L, j\) and kernel, every coarse side, every forest, and every bed, clip and path satisfying the hypotheses. In particular it is uniform in the period. By contrast the neighborhoods of the origin on which the implicit-function theorem gives the branch \(\rho\) may depend on the finite volume, and no uniformity is asserted for them.

The exact interface for the one-step construction is the following. Fix a kernel choice, a phase \(J\), positive \(\varepsilon, w, \delta\), and nonnegative \(D, Z, C, Z'\). The one-step construction proved in Proposition 11.34 chooses a reel \(R_1\) satisfying \(R_1 . \text{Bound} (s)\), nonnegative \(\kappa\), and positive \(s\). For every \(\tau > 0\) and \(A \geqslant 0\), assume \(\text{Ann} (G, w, \varepsilon, D, Z, \tau, A)\). It then chooses \(d \geqslant 0\), \(\gamma > 0\), a period threshold \(u\), and functions \(\beta (L)\), \(\Lambda_0 (L)\). For every patch with phase \(J\) and odd side at least \(u\), every pair of side sequences \(m_i, n_i\) with \(m_i \to \infty\), every path and beds/towers, the following hypotheses are required:

  • an armada whose every constituent step is a larger step, and the two bed hulls (metric and root strip bounds, floors, and sworn conditions);

  • an incoming path \(R\) attached to the fine bed \(B\), with \(R. \text{Bounds} (p, \tau, A, \Lambda)\) for the fine clip \(p\), the diagonal two-scale trial of the fine bed \(B\), the coarse bed \(T\) and the tower, and the stated header bounds on the installed tower column;

  • for each forest chart \(F_i\), the exact receipts

    \(\displaystyle \text{HessLower} (F_i, p, R, \eta), \qquad \text{Critical} (F_i, p, R, \rho_i), \qquad \text{LinMatch} (F_i, W_i, \rho_i),\)

    and the heat identity for the coarse bed \(T\);

  • the local-ratio chart and the specified direct-coefficient cutoffs for both clips. The annulus picture is produced inside this proof from \(\text{Ann}\); it is not an additional input.

The remaining scalar hypotheses are \(1 \leqslant \Lambda\), \(0 < \eta\), the phase identity, the period threshold, and the two direct clip bounds. The armada must have the selected kernel and all its patches must be branched. These conditions are part of the one-step input, as are the hypotheses used to define each forest chart.

The content of these hypotheses is precise. \(\text{HessLower}\) is the positive lower bound \(\eta \sum_e \|v_e \|^2 \leqslant D^2 \mathcal{T} (0) [v, v]\) for the tested action \(\mathcal{T}\). \(\text{Critical}\) asserts that the chart \(\rho\) is smooth, vanishes at zero, and is stationary for every tested action. \(\text{LinMatch}\) says that the derivative of \(t \mapsto \rho (tx)\) at zero is the chart's linear prediction from the installed matrix. The preceding subsection proves these receipts at each fixed finite volume by the planted sensitivity estimate, the Hessian comparison, and the implicit-function theorem. The output \(\text{ClassicalMatch}\) is instead a conclusion: for every Taylor order \(j \leqslant 4\) and every local choice, the tested action along \(\rho\) and the main action of the new path have equal \(j\)th derivatives at the origin.

The one-step construction gives a path \(Y\) and scalar \(\alpha\) with

\(\displaystyle Y. \text{row} = \text{rowPath} (T), \qquad \text{Attached} (T, Y),\)

for the coarse bed \(T\) and the coarse clip \(q\); thus the output path is attached to the coarse bed, while the incoming path was attached to the fine bed. All four components of \(Y. \text{Bounds} (q, \eta', A', \Lambda')\) obey the requested row/loop/cubic/quartic budgets whenever \(0 < \eta' \leqslant s\), \(\kappa \leqslant A'\), \(\Lambda_0 (L) \leqslant \Lambda'\) and \(\Lambda / 128 \leqslant \Lambda'\). Under the same four conditions the loop, cubic and quartic coefficient arrays of \(Y\) are the images, under the fixed encodings of the reel \(R_1\), of elements \(Q, P, W\) of its three canonical coefficient subspaces for the coarse side sequence, with

\(\displaystyle \|Q\| \leqslant \Lambda', \qquad \|P\| \leqslant A', \qquad \|W\| \leqslant A' .\)

The reel consists of three coefficient Hilbert spaces with encoding widths \(\delta_2, \delta_3, \delta_4\), and \(R_1 . \text{Bound} (s)\) means that \(s\) is strictly smaller than each of them. The canonical subspaces are the orthogonal complements constructed in Subsubsection 11.21.3. The construction also gives

\(\displaystyle | \alpha + \lambda_G \log L| + 1 \leqslant d,\)

with the number \(d\) chosen above. It gives the order-four classical matching identity and, whenever \(\beta (L) \leqslant \Theta\) and \(c' \leqslant \gamma\), the finite-cutoff bound with amplitude \(\Theta\) and decay rate \(c'\). The latter is the following statement at every index \(i\), for the coarse side \(c = m_i\) and the forest \(F_i\). The vertical Hessian determinant of the tested action is positive along \(\rho_i\) near the origin. The thermal function

\(\displaystyle \vartheta (x) =\mathcal{L}_R (x, \rho_i (x)) + \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det D_z^2 \mathcal{T} (x, \rho_i (x)) - \log \prod_b J (\rho_i (x)_b),\)

formed with the loop term \(\mathcal{L}_R\) of the incoming path, the tested action \(\mathcal{T}\) and the Jacobian \(J\) of the local-ratio chart, is real-analytic at the origin, and so is the outgoing comparison function \(\alpha \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{K}_{2, Y} +\mathcal{L}_Y\) built from the quadratic term and the loop term of \(Y\) in the coarse clip. For every local choice, every two coarse sites and every two polarization amplitudes with complex entries of modulus at most one, the second derivatives at the origin of these two functions on the corresponding pair of real plane-wave directions differ by at most

\(\displaystyle | \text{Site} (c) | \hspace{0.17em} \Theta \hspace{0.17em} e^{- c' c} .\)

The order of the choices matters: the reel, \(\kappa\) and \(s\) are chosen before \(\tau, A\); the numbers \(d, \gamma, u\) and the functions \(\beta, \Lambda_0\) after them; and the path \(Y\) and the scalar \(\alpha\) only after the incoming path, beds, tower and stationary data are fixed. The proof constructs the classical match by reducing the tested action to the new pole test and invoking fourth-order exactness of the local response; the exponential estimate comes from the funded Fourier ticket and the exponential rate comparison below. The bounded path is obtained by adding the blank path to the produced row and higher-degree coefficients, then applying the sum-budget estimate to each degree separately.

The intermediate scalar comparison uses the determinant estimate of Subsection 11.9, the contact cancellation of Subsection 11.10, and the alias comparison of Subsection 11.19. These estimates are applied to the same selected bridge. In particular the transverse primal identity allows each nonprincipal nose to be replaced, in the annulus pairing, by its horizontal part, which vanishes to second order. This replacement, and not a second-order bound on the full nose, is the one used below.

The scalar \(\alpha\) is the local quadratic coefficient of the constructed picture. Its normalization is compared with the annular coefficient \(\alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (Y_{\pi})\) by a finite response identity. The response identity uses two determinant pictures. The first is the half-medal response minus the reference determinant; the second is the half-rank-up response minus the same reference determinant. The determinant comparison proves an estimate which bounds the difference of their quadratic coefficients by

\(\displaystyle | \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (\text{medal} - \text{reference}) - \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (\text{rank-up} - \text{reference}) | \leqslant B_{\text{det}}, \)

where \(B_{\text{det}}\) is the displayed finite reserve bound \(\text{biteCost} (\eta / 2, \text{reserveCost} (\cdots))\). The rank-up picture is recorded against the pulled-back pole test at the coarse meshes; the annulus picture is recorded against the pole test itself at the cofinal fine meshes, with error tending to zero. These are different finite-volume tests, so their quadratic coefficients cannot be identified by comparing a single test value. The exact comparison is an alias argument.

Let \(L\) be the blocking ratio between the two periods, \(\mu = L^{- 1}\), \(X (p)\) the analytic bridge between the two period tests, \(S\) the annulus picture, and \(H\) the rank-up picture. For a picture \(P\), write \(\mathcal{R}_P (p)\) for its matrix of quadratic test coefficients. The finite-mesh recording identities and period-stride approximation imply, for every real momentum \(p\),

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_H (p) = L^4 \sum_{\ell \in \mathbb{T}_L} \mathcal{S}_S \hspace{-0.17em} (\text{alias}_{\ell} (p), N_X (- p, - \ell), N_X (p, \ell)),\)

where \(N_X\) are the bridge's nose maps and \(\mathcal{S}_S\) is the annulus matrix pairing. To prove this identity, choose actual mesh momenta \(p_i \to p\) and stride representatives for each alias. The normalized recording error for the annulus test tends to zero; the pulled-back rank-test error also tends to zero after its period normalization. The bridge factorization expresses each coarse test as the finite sum over its fine aliases. At a fixed pair of aliases the recorded mixed coefficient converges to the corresponding entry of \(\mathcal{S}_S\). If the aliases do not pair as \(\ell = - k\), the two mesh centers remain distinct and the coefficient tends to zero. The paired case gives the displayed entry. There are finitely many aliases at this fixed period, so the limits pass through the sum. This is the content of the finite-mesh transfer; it uses both recorded families, not equality of their unsmeared tests.

The hypotheses used in this transfer are the ones present in the selected rank/pole comparison: \(X\) is analytic and has the required uniform strip; the same matrix maps satisfy the exact coarse-to-fine premise; both metric rows have their stated strip bounds; the fine bed has the required floor and sworn conditions and the incoming path is attached to it; the rank response is screened; the cofinal recorded errors tend to zero; and the period meshes are related by an injective replication map. These assumptions give the normalized pulled-back recording and the noise-free alias identity. For the Taylor step below, additionally use the exact second-bed identity (\(N_X (0, 0) = \mu I\)), the transverse primal identity, and the annulus picture's second-order profile. The nonprincipal alias estimate uses the contractibility, strip bounds and horizontal-nose estimate proved in Subsection 11.19; it does not follow from the pole recordings alone.

The factor \(L^4\) is essential. Here \(L\) is the blocking ratio between the two periods, not the coarse physical period. Let \(\ell = 0\) denote the principal alias. The second bed identity fixes the zero-momentum bridge nose at \(N_X (0, 0) = \mu I\). The annulus picture has a second-order Taylor remainder, so its principal pairing differs from \(\mu^2 \mathcal{R}_S (\mu p)\) by \(O (|p|^3)\). For a nonprincipal alias, the full nose need not vanish to second order. The high-alias estimate in that comparison controls its horizontal and transverse components; contractibility then replaces the full nose by its horizontal component inside the pairing, without changing that pairing. The transverse primal identity and the floor bound make this horizontal representative vanish to second order. Only after this replacement does pairing with the annulus quadratic profile give an \(O (|p|^4)\) contribution. Summing the finitely many aliases therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{R}_H (p) - L^4 \mu^2 \mathcal{R}_S (\mu p) = O (|p|^3) .\)

Both sides are analytic near zero. Their Taylor coefficients through order two must agree: along each ray, a nonzero coefficient of degree at most two cannot be \(O (|p|^3)\); polarization recovers the full quadratic form. The second derivative of the rescaled principal term is \(L^4 \mu^4 D^2 \mathcal{R}_S (0)\), and \(L^4 \mu^4 = 1\). Thus

\(\displaystyle \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (\text{rank-up} - \text{reference}) = \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (Y_{\pi}) .\)

Thus \(| \alpha - \alpha^{\text{ann}}_j (Y_{\pi}) | \leqslant B_{\text{det}}\). If the logarithmic coefficient bound from the annulus is \(D_0\), then

\(\displaystyle | \alpha + \lambda_G \log L| + 1 \leqslant B_{\text{det}} + D_0 + 1.\)

The path construction chooses \(d = B_{\text{det}} + D_0 + 1\) (or a larger bound). This is the kinetic normalization step: it controls the finite-volume quadratic coefficient by comparison with the continuum logarithmic coefficient at the actual annulus scale. No unquantified limit in the cutoff is used here.

The exact order-four match is proved from the fifth-order remainder ticket. Let \(f (x)\) be the tested action along the critical chart and let \(g (x)\) be the main term of the new path. The response construction gives smoothness near zero and a ray estimate \(f (tx) - g (tx) = O (|t|^5)\) for each direction \(x\). Taylor's theorem in the single real variable \(t\) then forces the coefficients of \(t^j\) to agree for \(j = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4\). Those coefficients are the diagonal values of the symmetric \(j\)-linear derivatives at zero. The polarization identity for symmetric multilinear forms shows that equality on all diagonals implies equality of the full derivatives. This proves \(\text{ClassicalMatch}\) at every requested order; the input critical chart and the bound gap are used to obtain the smooth response and the response ticket, and are not deduced from the path identity.

For the path budget, the row component is the bed root array itself. Its exponentially weighted row sum is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{B}_{\text{row}} (s', M) = | \text{PlaqAxes} | \hspace{0.17em} Me^{4 s'} \leqslant \mathcal{B}_{\text{row}} (w, M), \qquad s' \leqslant w,\)

because a row entry at offset \(y\) is charged by the bed root strip and the walk used to encode the slot has length \(|y|_1 + 4\). The length convention is the following. A slot consists of a walk \(w\) on the cover and a plaquette type; its length is \(|w| + 4\), the four counting the edges of its plaquette. An atom with \(r\) legs is an \(r\)-tuple of slots, and its length is the sum of the lengths of its legs. Thus an atom whose legs have walks of total length \(|z|_1\) has length \(|z|_1 + 4 r\), and the weight of an atom at width \(\sigma\) is \(e^{\sigma}\) to the power of this length. The remaining three components are the loop, cubic, and quartic atom families constructed by the response step. Their weighted absolute budgets are separately bounded by the three output bounds; invariance, zero loop jets, and exact metric normalization are preserved by the construction. The sum-budget axiom implies each family is summable, and the row-layout identity plus the floor bound gives the metric clause. This is the explicit content behind the claimed \(Y. \text{Bounds}\), not an appeal to pointwise coefficient control.

The finite-cutoff ticket is an absolute bound on the weighted remainder, with original rate \(k\) at least the requested rate \(s\). If its unweighted amplitude is bounded by \(\Gamma\), and the final amplitude budget is \(\Lambda_1 \geqslant 1\), then for every cutoff \(a \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle |wR| e^{- ka} \leqslant \Gamma e^{- ka} \leqslant \Gamma e^{- sa} \leqslant \Gamma \Lambda_1 e^{- sa} .\)

The proof uses only \(s \leqslant k\) and nonnegative amplitudes. This is the finite-cutoff exponential-rate comparison used in the one-step conclusion.

The path budget in the one-step construction is built in degree-specific steps. First, the blank row path copies the target bed roots; if those roots have strip bound \(M\), its row budget is \(\mathcal{B}_{\text{row}} (w, M) = | \text{PlaqAxes} | Me^{4 w}\). Second, the cubic and quartic coefficients are those of the classical action restricted to its stationary graph, that is, of the tested action along the critical chart; no determinant enters them. The cubic coefficient is the cubic response of the tested action divided by \(3\)!. The quartic coefficient is the quartic response, minus the three contractions of two cubic responses through the inverse of the vertical Hessian, one for each pairing of the four external legs, divided by \(4\)!. This is the usual formula for the fourth derivative of a stationary value. If the cubic response bound is \(A'\), the quartic response bound is \(C'\), and the inverse vertical Hessian is bounded at bound \(D_{\ast}\), assume the hypotheses of Proposition 11.18, with its constants \(C_3, C_4, F\) equal to \(A', C', D_{\ast}\) respectively. That proposition gives, at its specified strip widths and with its Taylor normalization, the estimates

\(\displaystyle \frac{A'}{3!}, \qquad \frac{C' + 3 A' D_{\ast} A'}{4!} .\)

The factors \(3\) and \(3\)!, \(4\)! arise from the three pairings of four derivatives and the Taylor factorials; each contraction is bounded by \(A' D_{\ast} A'\). These coefficients are recorded against the third- and fourth-order Taylor coefficients of the tested action along the critical chart, respectively. The determinant of the vertical Hessian enters only the one-loop thermal function, hence the loop correction below. Third, the loop correction is obtained from the Fourier coefficient of the quadratic response after subtracting its scalar quadratic term. The row path and these three atom families are assembled into one path. Its metric identity follows from the row-layout sum and the floor condition; loop normalization and invariance are preserved coefficientwise. This is why the one-step construction has a separate row bound and a separate total higher-degree bound, rather than charging all coefficients to a single norm.

The final budget invoice in that estimate is elementary but essential. Let \(\gamma \geqslant 0\), \(V, \Lambda \geqslant 0\), and suppose \(\Lambda / 128 \leqslant \Lambda'\), the terms arising before the annulus correction obey

\(\displaystyle 2 (C + (d + |y|) V) \gamma \leqslant \Lambda', \qquad |x + y| + 1 \leqslant d, \qquad B \gamma \leqslant \tfrac{1}{256} .\)

Then

\(\displaystyle (C + |x|V + B \Lambda) \gamma \leqslant \Lambda' . \) (11.110)

Indeed \(|x| \leqslant d + |y|\), by \(x = (x + y) - y\), and

\(\displaystyle (C + |x|V + B \Lambda) \gamma \leqslant (C + (d + |y|) V) \gamma + B \gamma \Lambda \leqslant \Lambda' / 2 + \Lambda' / 2.\)

The final term uses \(B \gamma \leqslant 1 / 256\) and \(\Lambda / 128 \leqslant \Lambda'\), so \(B \gamma \Lambda \leqslant \Lambda' / 2\). This is the only step where the auxiliary width of the horizontal picture is traded against the outer row budget.

The exponential cutoff invoice is similarly explicit. If \(\Lambda_1 \geqslant 1\) and \(|wR| \leqslant \Gamma\), with \(s \leqslant k\) and \(a \geqslant 0\), then

\(\displaystyle wRe^{- ka} \leqslant \Gamma \Lambda_1 e^{- sa} . \) (11.111)

The bound \(|wR| \leqslant \Gamma\) gives \(wR \leqslant \Gamma\), while \(e^{- ka} \leqslant e^{- sa}\); multiplying the latter by the nonnegative \(\Gamma \Lambda_1\) and using \(\Lambda_1 \geqslant 1\) proves the claim. This is the rate comparison used to preserve a finite-cutoff tail when the controlling rate is weakened.

11.21.3Canonical representatives for all-period path jets

The path realization uses a closed-subspace projection to choose one representative of prescribed jets at every tested period. Let \(H\) be a real Hilbert space, \(S \subset H\) a closed subspace, and for each integer \(n \geqslant 0\) let \(J_n : H \to E_n\) be a continuous linear map into a real Hilbert space \(E_n\). Define the joint invisible subspace

\(\displaystyle K = S \cap \bigcap_{n \geqslant 0} \ker J_n, \qquad \mathcal{R}= S \cap K^{\perp} .\)

Both are closed: each kernel is closed, and intersections of closed subspaces are closed. Given \(C \in S\), orthogonally project onto \(K\) and put \(C_{\ast} = C - \Pi_K C\). Since \(\Pi_K C \in K \subset S\), one has \(C_{\ast} \in S\); projection orthogonality gives \(C_{\ast} \in K^{\perp}\), and hence \(C_{\ast} \in \mathcal{R}\). As \(J_n (\Pi_K C) = 0\), every prescribed jet is preserved, \(J_n (C_{\ast}) = J_n (C)\), and the Pythagorean identity gives \(\|C_{\ast} \|^2 = \|C\|^2 - \| \Pi_K C\|^2 \leqslant \|C\|^2\).

This representative is unique. If \(C_{\ast}, D_{\ast} \in \mathcal{R}\) have the same jets for every \(n\), then \(C_{\ast} - D_{\ast} \in S\) and all its jets vanish, so it lies in \(K\). It also lies in \(K^{\perp}\), whence \(\|C_{\ast} - D_{\ast} \|^2 = 0\) and \(C_{\ast} = D_{\ast}\). Apply this with the admissible correction series as \(S\) and the derivatives at the tested periods as the maps \(J_n\). For this application assume the admissible subspace is closed; the joint invisible subspace is the common kernel used in the orthogonal-complement selection. When the tested side sequence exhausts all physical periods, this proves all-period jet injectivity on the chosen orthogonal complement. When it does not, the conclusion is exactly for the specified test sequence. This is the canonical realization argument: it uses the orthogonal complement of the common jet kernel, not an assumed injectivity of the jet map on the original space.

11.21.4Changing the forest without changing the stationary action

Lemma 11.33. (Forest-independent stationary germs) Fix one larger block step and its incoming path and clips. Let \(F, F'\) be two allowed forests, and let \(\rho_F, \rho_{F'}\) be their critical branches with positive vertical Hessians at zero. Their reduced classical actions agree as germs of the coarse variables. Their thermal functions agree up to a constant. In particular their derivatives of every positive order agree on a sufficiently small common neighborhood of zero.

Proof. A configuration near the trivial configuration has a unique rooted gauge transformation putting it into the slice of \(F'\): start with the identity at each root and solve successively along its tree edges. Each new gauge value is a product of the already determined value and the intervening link or its inverse. Thus the gauge transformation is analytic near the trivial configuration, fixes the roots, and equals the identity there. Applying it to a configuration in the \(F\) slice defines an analytic change of vertical coordinates \(z' = \phi (x, z)\), with the coarse variable \(x\) fixed. Repeating the construction with \(F\) gives its inverse. Shrinking the neighborhoods if necessary, both transformations and their inverses are defined. Rooted gauge invariance of the tested action gives

\(\displaystyle T_F (x, z) = T_{F'} (x, \phi (x, z)) .\)

The same holds for the inherited loop, since each loop atom is invariant.

The vertical chain rule shows that \(\phi (x, \rho_F (x))\) is a critical point for \(T_{F'}\). It equals zero at \(x = 0\). The positive vertical Hessian makes the implicit-function branch unique near zero; hence \(\phi (x, \rho_F (x)) = \rho_{F'} (x)\) as germs. This proves the assertion about the classical action and identifies the stationary representatives before comparing determinants.

For completeness, the measure on either forest slice is the product Haar measure on its free edges.
