This follows by integrating out the rooted vertex gauges in order of tree height: at each stage one replaces a tree link by a vertex gauge variable by a left or right multiplication, which preserves probability Haar measure. The remaining free links are likewise changed only by left and right multiplications. Fubini applies to this finite product of probability measures. Applying this calculation first to \(F\) and then to \(F'\) shows that the two slice measures are the same measure on rooted gauge orbits. In local coordinates, if \(j_F, j_{F'}\) are their positive density factors, the ordinary change-of-variables formula therefore gives

\(\displaystyle c_F j_F (z) = c_{F'} j_{F'} (\phi (x, z)) | \det D_z \phi (x, z) |,\)

where the positive constants \(c_F, c_{F'}\) are the coordinate normalization constants, independent of \(x, z\). Equality of continuous densities follows from equality of their integrals over all compact coordinate sets. The coordinate transformations may depend on \(x\); this does not affect the formula, which is on each fixed coarse fiber.

At the critical point, the second-derivative chain rule has no term from \(D_z T_{F'}\), and so

\(\displaystyle H_F = J^T H_{F'} J, \qquad J = D_z \phi (x, \rho_F (x)) .\)

Both Hessians are positive on a sufficiently small neighborhood. Taking determinants, logarithms and the preceding density identity gives

\(\displaystyle \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det H_F - \log j_F (\rho_F) = \tfrac{1}{2} \log \det H_{F'} - \log j_{F'} (\rho_{F'}) + \log (c_F / c_{F'}) .\)

The inherited loop agrees on the two representatives. Adding it proves the thermal assertion. In particular the second derivatives used in the finite-cutoff comparison do not depend on the forest. No identification of vectors in the two different forest coordinate spaces has been made.\(\Box\)

11.21.5Purchasing all components of the one-step path

We now supply the one-step construction stated above. All widths in this paragraph are widths in coarse momentum coordinates. The fine strip required by the coefficient estimates is obtained by dividing them by \(L\).

Fix the kernel, the phase, the two bed bounds, the positive floor, the header bound and the two direct-coefficient ceilings in the one-step interface. The selected bridge identities of Subsection 11.7 and the weighted frame and penalty estimates give one common positive coarse strip and fixed bridge and penalty constants. The weighted inverse construction of Subsection 11.6 narrows that strip and bounds the needed inverse Hessians there. These choices depend only on the listed fixed data, and precede the incoming path width \(\tau\) and bound \(A\). The all-period bed floor supplies the real weighted coercivity in that inverse construction. The forest gap of Subsubsection 11.21.2 separately gives each critical branch; its possibly \(L\)-dependent numerical lower bound is not substituted for the uniform weighted coercivity. Take the minimum of the finitely many resulting widths and denote a further smaller positive width by \(\sigma_0\).

For any subsequently fixed \(\tau, A\), the residual coefficient bounds of Proposition 11.12 have a positive fine width and finite bounds. Enlarge the block-side floor until \(\sigma_0 / L\) lies in that fine width. The classical vertex bounds are \(a + L^{- 1} \ell\) and \(b + L^{- 1} r\), where \(a, b\) were fixed before \(\tau, A\), and \(\ell, r\) are fixed after them. Enlarge the floor until \(\ell / L, r / L \leqslant 1\). Proposition 11.18 now gives the exact cubic and quartic stationary pictures on one fixed coarse strip with the bound \(M_{\ast}\) displayed there. Both that strip and \(M_{\ast}\) precede \(\tau, A\). The data used in that proposition are the actual bridge and the actual inverse just described, so its three quartic contractions are the ones of the critical branch of this step.

The selected kinetic response and differentiated matrix calculus of Sections 11.8 and 11.9 give the determinant picture \(f_L\) on a fixed smaller coarse strip. Its absolute bound \(\Phi (L)\) may contain the number \(O (L^4)\) of fine variables; no bound independent of \(L\) is asserted for it. Finite Fourier inversion identifies the normalized finite-volume Hessian with the grid mean of this same kernel. Lemma 11.22, applied entrywise and then to the finite color and direction sum, gives

\(\displaystyle \varepsilon_i \leqslant E (L) e^{- \gamma c_i}, \qquad \gamma > 0,\)

where \(c_i\) is the coarse side. The common coarse strip fixes \(\gamma\) before \(L\); the amplitude \(E (L)\) may depend on \(L\). The moving Haar identity (11.25) identifies this picture with the determinant and Haar part of the actual stationary thermal function. It is therefore not a recording for a different coordinate action.

For the same incoming path, Proposition 11.30 gives an exactly recorded picture \(g_L\) on a fixed coarse strip, of order four and bound \(C' L^{- 2} \Lambda\). Its constant \(C'\) may depend on \(\tau\), but not on \(L\), \(\Lambda\), the path or the periods. The extra fine-strip condition in that proposition is met by increasing the same block-side floor. The classical, determinant, inherited and outgoing bed pictures are now all available on one smaller coarse strip independent of \(\tau, A\) and \(L\); only their thresholds and bounds have the dependence just stated.

Apply Proposition 11.29 to these pictures and the outgoing bed. Its coefficient-lift constants and widths consequently precede \(\tau, A\). The scalar is the quadratic coefficient \(\alpha\) of \(f_L\). The core–rank comparison has an \(L\)-independent nuclear bound, and the contact identity removes the actual–core quadratic difference. Cauchy's estimate on the fixed strip bounds the resulting quadratic coefficient difference by a constant \(C_{\text{cmp}}\) independent of \(L\). Proposition 11.31 identifies the rank coefficient with the fine annulus coefficient for this same bridge. The annulus estimate of Proposition 11.28 thus gives

\(\displaystyle | \alpha + \lambda_G \log L| + 1 \leqslant d := D_0 + C_{\text{cmp}} + 1.\)

The constant \(d\) is chosen after \(\tau, A\) and before \(L\), as required. In particular \(| \alpha | \leqslant d + \lambda_G \log L\).

Write \(B_H\) for the fixed outgoing quadratic-picture bound and \(D\) for the coefficient-lift bound multiplier, enlarging it also to pay for the canonical coefficient realization described below. The loop budget before canonical projection is at most

\(\displaystyle D \{\Phi (L) + (d + \lambda_G \log L) B_H + C' L^{- 2} \Lambda\} .\)

Increase the block-side floor once more so that \(DC' L^{- 2} \leqslant 1 / 256\), and put

\(\displaystyle \Lambda_0 (L) = 2 D \{\Phi (L) + (d + \lambda_G \log L) B_H \} .\)

If \(\Lambda' \geqslant \Lambda_0 (L)\) and \(\Lambda' \geqslant \Lambda / 128\), the first two terms cost at most \(\Lambda' / 2\) and the last costs at most \(\Lambda / 256 \leqslant \Lambda' / 2\). This proves the loop bound with precisely the allowance in the one-step interface. The row, cubic and quartic bounds are fixed before \(\tau, A\), since the row bound and \(M_{\ast}\) are. Let \(\kappa\) be their maximum, including the same realization multiplier. The real-wave expansion in (11.96) changes the recording amplitude only by \(2 (4 \dim \mathfrak{g})^2\); absorb it in \(\beta (L)\). The resulting classical match and finite-cutoff inequality are exactly those of the one-step interface.

Hilbert space of coefficients and canonical representatives.
Here is an explicit Hilbert realization of the coefficient arrays, which also fixes its choice before the incoming budgets. For each of the three degrees use the canonical displacement arrays of the curvature lift, together with their finitely many orientation transforms. A slot is encoded by its displacement, its direction and color indices and a fixed coordinate walk to that displacement. The number of such addresses of length at most \(h\) is bounded by a polynomial in \(1 + h\): the displacement lattice has fixed finite dimension and the other index sets are finite. All coefficient families produced by the lift are supported on these addresses. Choose widths \(0 < s < \delta < \delta' < s_{\ast}\), where \(s_{\ast}\) is a common width at which the three lifted families have their proved absolute budgets. These widths precede \(\tau, A\).

For an address \(a\) of length \(|a|\), give its real coefficient space the Hilbert norm

\(\displaystyle \|c\|_H = Z \left( \sum_a e^{2 \delta |a|} |c_a |^2 \right)^{1 / 2}, \qquad Z = \left( \sum_a e^{- 2 (\delta - s) |a|} \right)^{1 / 2} < \infty .\)

Finite-dimensional coefficient tensor norms are included in the address set. The encoding is the coefficient array itself. Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_a e^{s |a|} |c_a | \leqslant \|c\|_H, \qquad \|c\|_H \leqslant Z \sum_a e^{\delta' |a|} |c_a | .\)

The second inequality uses \(\delta < \delta'\) and \(\ell^2 \leqslant \ell^1\). Thus a fixed enlargement of the previously obtained lift constants pays for the Hilbert norm, whereas a Hilbert norm bound implies the desired path budget at width \(s\). The same construction with an intermediate width in place of \(s\) makes the encoding width strictly larger than the final path width. This is the reel required in the interface.

The real, invariant and moment constraints define a closed real linear subspace of this Hilbert space. Reality and invariance are intersections of kernels of continuous coordinate functionals. The zero loop coefficient and first moment are continuous sums, by Cauchy–Schwarz with the extra exponential margin. At a fixed finite torus and fixed Taylor order, the realization map is also continuous: derivatives of a fixed-order product of transported plaquettes grow at most polynomially in its walk lengths, and that polynomial is absorbed by the same exponential margin. Consequently the invisible space for all requested jets at all the chosen periods is closed. Project as in Subsubsection 11.21.3. The projection preserves the real invariance and moment constraints and every recorded jet, and decreases the Hilbert norm. For cubic atoms keep both the third and fourth jets in this projection, for quartic atoms the fourth jet, and for loops the second jet. Thus the same classical match and the same thermal comparison survive simultaneously; changing the cubic fourth jet would not have sufficed. All norm multipliers have been included in \(D\) and \(\kappa\) above before choosing the last block-side floor. This supplies the canonical representatives with the exact budgets claimed in the one-step interface.

The terminal path.
At a terminal bed the tested quadratic kinetic form is the Wilson kinetic form: its unamended diagonal is the identity, and the amendment identity (3.48) gives \(Gd_1 = Hd_1 = d_1\). Thus the amended metric acts as the identity on exact curvatures, although it need not be the identity on all two-forms. Take its root row as the row component of the path and denote its quadratic action, including the direct clip term, by \(J\). The floor and sworn identities and Lemma 11.10 identify \(D^2 J (0)\) with this kinetic form. Thus the Wilson action \(W\) and \(J\) have the same value, first derivative and Hessian. The Wilson coefficient pictures and the row-quadratic pictures supply exact common third- and fourth-order pictures of \(W - J\), with a width and bound depending only on the terminal stage's root bound and direct-coefficient ceiling. Apply the leading-removal construction of Subsection 11.17 first at degree three and then at degree four. In the second step subtract the fourth coefficient of the cubic atoms as well. It gives cubic and quartic invariant arrays with \(W - J - S_3 - S_4 = O (|x|^5)\) at every tested period. Give the path these arrays and zero loop coefficients. It is attached to the terminal bed, its four path budgets hold below one fixed positive width and above one fixed stage bound, and polarization gives equality of every derivative through order four. No period-dependent selection or limiting argument enters this terminal construction.

Proposition 11.34. (Production of the one-step and terminal paths) Under the bed, tower, floor, attachment and header hypotheses of the one-step interface, the annulus estimate supplies all its conclusions, with the stated order of choices and the loop contraction \(\Lambda / 128\). The terminal identity bed has the terminal base path required in (B) below. The classical match and thermal Hessian estimate hold for every allowed forest.

Proof. The preceding construction verifies each input of Proposition 11.29 on the same beds, bridge, critical branches and torus sequence. Its estimates, the explicit split of the loop allowance, and the canonical projection give the one-step path with all the required norms. The terminal construction gives the base path. For each period choose any allowed forest to make the output pictures. Lemma 11.33 transfers both matching assertions to every other forest, without changing the output path or scalar. This proves the proposition.\(\Box\)

11.21.6The arbitrary-input factor-64 envelope

The numerical factor used by the selected global caller is an allowance amplification at the input to the transition supplier. We give both layers of the finite reindexing, since the two maxima/minima use different pairs.

For each fixed output index \(i\), the one-step theorem is first called at both neighboring input indices \(j = i\) and \(j = i + 1\). If the two calls return widths \(w_{i, i}, w_{i + 1, i}\) and row/cubic/quartic incoming-bound thresholds \(v_{i, i}, v_{i + 1, i}\), the common parameters are

\(\displaystyle s_i = \min (b_i, w_{i, i}, w_{i + 1, i}), \qquad A_i = \max (E_i, v_{i, i}, v_{i + 1, i}) .\)

Here \(b_i, E_i\) are the width and bound supplied by the blank-path estimate. For each of the two calls, \(s_i\) is no larger than its width and its bound threshold is no larger than \(A_i\), so the same pair of finite-volume estimates can be used at either adjacent input scale. The flux theorem is universal in the admissible incoming path, bed, tower, armada, and chart; therefore this uniformization does not choose a favorable incoming object.

Next fix the requested positive width sequence \(\tau_j\) and nonnegative incoming path-bound allowance \(C_j\). For each fixed input index \(j\), the one-step calls indexed by the two possible output indices \(i = j\) and \(i = j - 1\) are unified by a second set of extrema: take the maximum of their drift bounds, period thresholds, and side-dependent cutoff amplitudes, and the minimum of their positive decay rates. At \(j = 0\), the predecessor is interpreted as \(0\), so the single available call is used twice. These choices make one set of output data valid whether the selected transition uses the same-scale receipt or the preceding-scale receipt. This is the second neighbor reindexing in the construction; it is distinct from the width/bound extrema at fixed output index above.

In the public application, the arbitrary allowance sequence \(C_j\) is passed as \(64 A'_j\), where \(A'_j\) is any nonnegative sequence dominating the base path-bound sequence. Thus the one-step conclusion is established for an incoming path budget \(64 A'_j\); the factor 64 is part of the input allowance, not an output estimate obtained from a smaller path. At the selected level, \(A'_j\) is the profile path bound, so the row, cubic, and quartic incoming budgets are \(64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{paths}_j\). The loop halo is separately defined with budget \(64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_j\). The selected schedule must satisfy the following loop charges used by the transition. For the transition from level \(m + 1\) to level \(m\), let \(j = J_{m + 1}\) be the stage of the incoming level and \(L_j\) the block side selected at stage \(j\); the stage of the output level is \(J_m \in \{j, j - 1\}\). The side-dependent threshold \(W_j\) of the one-step construction at stage \(j\), namely the function \(\Lambda_0\) of its interface, and the loop capacities satisfy

\(\displaystyle W_j (L_j) \leqslant \text{loopCap}_m, \qquad \frac{64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_{m + 1}}{128} = \frac{\text{loopCap}_{m + 1}}{2} \leqslant \text{loopCap}_m, \qquad 1 \leqslant \text{loopCap}_m .\)

Thus the threshold is indexed by the incoming stage and the capacity by the output level. These three inequalities are properties of the scale schedule; they are inputs here. An incoming path with ordinary profile bounds already satisfies the halo bounds by componentwise monotonicity, since each budget is nonnegative and \(1 \leqslant 64\). That monotonicity only converts an already constructed path; the arbitrary-input transition itself comes from the pairwise flux calls and the two finite extrema operations. Its horizontal receipt and local response bounds remain the explicit inputs described in the one-step section.

11.21.7From adjacent one-step paths to one finite history

Let \(a\) be the scale schedule, and write \(J_m = a. \text{clock} .J (m)\). Its slow-step property gives

\(\displaystyle J_m \leqslant J_{m + 1} \leqslant J_m + 1.\)

Suppose that for every pair \((j, i)\) with \(j = i\) or \(j = i + 1\) the one-step receipt is available, with width \(w_{j, i} > 0\), bound \(A_{j, i}\), and path \(Y_{j, i}\). Define the common scale width and bound by

\(\displaystyle s_i = \min \{b_i, w_{i, i}, w_{i + 1, i} \}, \qquad A_i = \max \{E_i, A_{i, i}, A_{i + 1, i} \} .\)

Taking minima and maxima preserves positivity and nonnegativity. At either adjacent index \(j \in \{i, i + 1\}\), one has \(s_i \leqslant w_{j, i}\) and \(A_{j, i} \leqslant A_i\). Thus both adjacent-stage one-step constructions meet the common width and bound requirements of output stage \(i\). For each actual transition the construction of the corresponding stage pair is applied to its fixed incoming path and quadratic data. The extrema unify the admissible parameters; they do not assert that the paths returned by the two constructions coincide. The common bound is the maximum of the two one-step bounds, and the common cutoff rate is the minimum of the two one-step rates. This finite neighbor maximum/minimum is the entire unification step in the global construction; no limit in the scale index is taken here.

The next finite construction is where attached paths are assembled. Branch rows and admissible paths are defined in Definition 11.35, and the transition rule on the actual history is constructed in Proposition 11.37. Assume that for each terminal index \(N\) there is a branch row \(R_N\), and for each \(m < N\) there is a transition rule which, from any admissible path at \(m + 1\), returns a path at \(m\), an amplitude \(\alpha_m\), and a stationary coefficient \(\rho_m\), together with a bubble identity and the wing bound. Then for each fixed \(N\) one constructs rows \(Y_m\) for \(0 \leqslant m \leqslant N\) by finite downward induction. Set \(Y_N = R_N\). Given \(Y_{m + 1}\), apply the transition rule to obtain \(Y_m\) and its amplitude/stationary data. Induction preserves admissibility at the next stage because the previous step furnished exactly the attachment and bound required as input. The resulting finite table has all adjacent bubble identities and all wing bounds.

Doing this separately for each \(N\) defines a single horizon of paths: its \(N\)th column is the finite table just constructed. The terminal rows give the branch-row property, the step outputs give the adjacent bubbles, and the one-step row identities give the global row ties and path bounds. Hence one obtains one finite-history path object satisfying all finite-cutoff path conditions, with every cutoff retained on the same horizon. This argument is not a compactness extraction and does not choose a different horizon at different cutoffs. It is the finite descending construction furnished by the transition rule.

11.21.8The stationary plan on the retained horizon

The finite descent above is conditional on a branch row and a transition rule. We now state what these are on the actual history and how the transition rule is obtained there. This is the statement consumed by the later sections; its three analytic inputs are named explicitly.

Fix the scale schedule \(a\). For a level \(m\) write \(J_m\) for its stage, \(p_m, \sigma_m, A_m\) for the clip, width and path allowance of the stage profile, \(\text{loopCap}_m\) for the loop capacity, \(L_m\) for the block side from level \(m + 1\) to level \(m\), and \(n_{m, t}\) for the torus side at level \(m\) and period index \(t\). A horizon \(V\) assigns a bed \(V.H (N, m)\) to \(m \leqslant N\) and a tower \(V.T (N, m)\) to \(m < N\). Fix also a local-ratio chart \(O\) and post costs: positive numbers \(\eta_j, \Gamma_j, c_j\) for each stage \(j\).

Definition 11.35. (Admissible paths and branch rows) A path \(R\) is admissible at \((N, m)\), \(m \leqslant N\), if its row component is the root row of the bed \(V.H (N, m)\) in the walk encoding of the row-bound estimate above, if \(\text{Attached} (V.H (N, m), R)\), and if \(R. \text{Bounds} (p_m, \sigma_m, A_m, \text{loopCap}_m)\). A branch row at \(N\) is a path \(R\), admissible at \((N, N)\), such that at every period the main action of \(R\) in the clip \(p_N\) and the Wilson action have equal derivatives of every order \(j \leqslant 4\) at the identity field, and such that every loop coefficient of \(R\) vanishes.

Definition 11.36. (Stationary plan) A stationary plan on \(V\) consists of paths \(R_{N, m}\) for \(m \leqslant N\), numbers \(\alpha_{N, m}\) for \(m < N\), and maps \(\rho_{N, m}^{t, F}\) for \(m < N\), every period index \(t\) and every allowed forest \(F\) of the one-step datum from level \(m + 1\) to level \(m\) at period \(t\). It is planned at the given post costs if the following hold for all \(N\).

  1. For \(m \leqslant N\) the path \(R_{N, m}\) is attached to \(V.H (N, m)\) and satisfies

    \(\displaystyle R_{N, m} . \text{Bounds} (p_m, \sigma_m, A_m, \text{loopCap}_m) .\)

  2. The terminal path \(R_{N, N}\) has the Wilson derivatives of every order \(j \leqslant 4\) at every period, and all its loop coefficients vanish.

  3. For \(m < N\) and all \(t, F\), with \(\rho = \rho_{N, m}^{t, F}\) and \(j = J_{m + 1}\):

    \(\displaystyle \text{HessLower} (F, p_{m + 1}, R_{N, m + 1}, \eta_j), \quad \text{Critical} (F, p_{m + 1}, R_{N, m + 1}, \rho), \quad \text{LinMatch} (F, W, \rho),\)

    where \(W\) is the installed matrix of the tower \(V.T (N, m)\), and

    \(\displaystyle \text{ClassicalMatch} (F, p_{m + 1}, p_m, R_{N, m + 1}, R_{N, m}, \rho) .\)

  4. For \(m < N\), \(| \alpha_{N, m} + \lambda_G \log L_m | + 1\) is at most the drift allowance of stage \(J_{m + 1}\).

  5. For \(m < N\) and all \(t, F\), the finite-cutoff bound of the one-step interface holds for the incoming path \(R_{N, m + 1}\), the outgoing path \(R_{N, m}\), the scalar \(\alpha_{N, m}\), the chart \(O\) and the map \(\rho_{N, m}^{t, F}\), with the bound \(| \text{Site} (n_{m, t}) | \hspace{0.17em} \Gamma_j \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_{m + 1} \hspace{0.17em} e^{- c_j n_{m, t}}\), \(j = J_{m + 1}\).

The paths of a plan are used twice: \(R_{N, m + 1}\) is the incoming path of the step to level \(m\) and the outgoing path of the step from level \(m + 2\). It is one path with one choice of coefficient arrays.

The proposition below uses the following three constructions. Their producers are given explicitly; the quadratic horizon is constructed in its own chapter.

    (A)

    The annulus estimate. \(\text{Ann} (G, \sigma, \varepsilon, C, Z, \tau, A)\) holds for all \(\sigma, \varepsilon, \tau > 0\) and \(C, Z, A \geqslant 0\). Its producer is Proposition 11.28.

    (B)

    The one-step construction with the interface stated in Subsubsection 11.21.2, for every stage pair \((j, i)\) with \(j = i\) or \(j = i + 1\), and the terminal base path: for every stage, every bed with the root strip bound, the floors and sworn conditions of that stage and with diagonal equal to the identity at the tested periods, and every clip within the stage's direct bound, there is a path whose row is the root row of the bed, which is attached to the bed, has zero loop coefficients, satisfies the path bounds at every width below and every bound above fixed stage values, and has the Wilson derivatives of orders \(j \leqslant 4\) at the tested periods. The one-step and terminal constructions are proved in Proposition 11.34; forest independence is Lemma 11.33.

    (C)

    The quadratic horizons. A free horizon \(X\) and a retained horizon \(V\) on the schedule \(a\), and kernel bounds \((\alpha_j, \Omega_j, \eta^0_j)\), such that \(V\) has the same beds as \(X\) and, for \(m < N\), the same installed column and the same transport column in its towers; and such that \(X\) satisfies, for \(m \leqslant N\): the metric and root strip bounds of \(X.H (N, m)\) at width \(\alpha_{J_m}\) and bound \(\Omega_{J_m}\); the floor of \(X.H (N, m)\) in the clip \(p_m\) with constant \(\eta^0_{J_m}\) at every torus side; the sworn condition at every torus side; the identity \(\text{diag} \hspace{0.17em} X.H (N, N) = 1\) at every side; and for \(m < N\) the header bounds of the tower \(X.T (N, m)\) and the diagonal two-scale trial of \(X.H (N, m + 1)\), \(X.H (N, m)\), \(X.T (N, m)\) at every period.

The conditions on \(X\) in (C) are the bed and flight receipts of the quadratic construction of Subsubsection 3.9.2. The retained horizon is formed from \(X\) in Subsection 13.1, where \(X\) and \(V\) are called \(M^0\) and \(M^1\). Convergence along the cutoff is not a consequence of (C): the conditions there do not determine the remaining tower blocks. For the particular horizons constructed jointly with their curvature roots it is proved in Subsection 11.20; the present proposition does not use it.

Proposition 11.37. (Stationary plan on the retained horizon) Assume the gauge group is simple and (A), (B) hold. Fix a kernel choice, kernel bounds, and bounds \(Z_j \geqslant 0\) for the direct clip coefficients of the stages. There are stage widths \(\bar{\sigma}_j > 0\) and stage path bounds \(\bar{A}_j \geqslant 0\) such that, for all stage widths \(0 < \sigma_j \leqslant \bar{\sigma}_j\) and path allowances \(A_j \geqslant \bar{A}_j\), there are drift bounds \(d_j \geqslant 0\), rates \(\gamma_j > 0\) and block-side floors \(u_j\) with the following property. Let the stage profiles have these widths and path allowances, drift allowances at least \(d_j\), block-side floors at least \(u_j\) and direct clip coefficients at most \(Z_j\). Then, once the block sides are chosen, there are positive gaps \(\eta_j\) and volume thresholds, and once the protection data are chosen there are entry delays, such that for every paid schedule \(a\) beyond these thresholds and delays, every post costs with \(\eta_j\) the given gaps, \(\Gamma_j \geqslant 1\) and \(c_j \leqslant \gamma_j\), all horizons \(X, V\) as in (C) and every local-ratio chart \(O\), the following hold.

  1. There is a stationary plan \((R_{N, m}, \alpha_{N, m}, \rho_{N, m}^{t, F})\) on \(V\), planned at the post costs in the sense of Definition 11.36. Every \(R_{N, m}\) is admissible at \((N, m)\); in particular its row is the root row of \(V.H (N, m)\). The finite-cutoff bound holds even with the factor \(\text{loopCap}_{m + 1}\) replaced by one.

  2. For every level \(m\) there is a reel, fixed before the cutoff and the period, whose three encoding widths are strictly larger than \(\sigma_m\), such that for all \(N > m\) the loop, cubic and quartic arrays of \(R_{N, m}\) are the encodings of elements of its three canonical coefficient subspaces for the side sequence \(t \mapsto n_{m, t}\), of norms at most \(\text{loopCap}_m\), \(A_m\), \(A_m\) respectively.

  3. Extended domain. Let \(m < N\) and let \(R\) be any path attached to \(V.H (N, m + 1)\) with

    \(\displaystyle R. \text{Bounds} (p_{m + 1}, \sigma_{m + 1}, 64 A_{m + 1}, 64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_{m + 1}) .\)

    Then there are a path \(D\) admissible at \((N, m)\), a scalar and stationary maps satisfying items (3)–(5) of Definition 11.36 with \(R\) as incoming and \(D\) as outgoing path, and the arrays of \(D\) lie in the canonical subspaces of the same reel with the same norm bounds as in the previous item.

  4. For every level \(m\) there are \(v_m > \sigma_m\) and \(C_m\) such that the root row of \(V.H (N, m)\) has the strip bound at width \(v_m\) and bound \(C_m\) for all sufficiently large \(N\).

Proof. The stage widths and bounds are those of Subsubsection 11.21.6. Let \(b_i > 0\) and \(E_i \geqslant 0\) be the width ceiling and bound floor of the terminal base path of (B) at stage \(i\), and let \(w_{j, i}, v_{j, i}\) be the widths and bound thresholds of the one-step constructions for the stage pairs \(j \in \{i, i + 1\}\). As there, put \(s_i = \min (b_i, w_{i, i}, w_{i + 1, i})\) and \(A_i = \max (E_i, v_{i, i}, v_{i + 1, i})\), and take \(\bar{\sigma}_i = \min (s_i, \alpha_i / 2)\) and \(\bar{A}_i = A_i\). Thus every admitted width is at most \(b_i\) and every admitted allowance at least \(E_i\), as the terminal rows require; and they are at most \(w_{j, i}\) and at least \(v_{j, i}\) respectively, as the transitions require. The one-step constructions are called with incoming allowance \(64 A_j\), which gives \(d_j, u_j\), rates \(\gamma'_j > 0\) and the functions \(\beta_j, \Lambda_{0, j}\) of the block side; the rates of the statement are \(\gamma_j = \gamma'_j / 2\). The gaps \(\eta_j\) are those of Subsubsection 11.21.2 for the block side \(L_j\), the phase \(j\), the kernel and the floor constant \(\eta^0_j\). The volume threshold at stage \(j\) is the maximum of \(100 (j + 7)\) and of a coarse side beyond which \(\beta_j (L_j) e^{- \gamma'_j n} \leqslant e^{- \gamma_j n}\). The entry delays are those beyond which the three loop-capacity inequalities of Subsubsection 11.21.6 hold.

Terminal rows. By (C) the bed \(V.H (N, N) = X.H (N, N)\) has the root strip bound, floors, sworn condition and identity diagonal. The base path of (B) at stage \(J_N\), taken at width \(\sigma_N\), bound \(A_N\) and loop allowance \(\text{loopCap}_N \geqslant 1\), is therefore a branch row at \(N\).

The transition rule. Let \(m < N\), put \(j = J_{m + 1}\) and \(i = J_m\), so \(j \in \{i, i + 1\}\), and let \(R\) be attached to the fine bed \(B = V.H (N, m + 1)\) with the factor-\(64\) bounds of item (3). We verify the hypotheses of the one-step construction of the stage pair \((j, i)\) for the fine bed \(B\), the coarse bed \(T = V.H (N, m)\), the tower \(V.T (N, m)\), the fine and coarse clips \(p_{m + 1}, p_m\), and the one-step data at all period indices \(t\), whose coarse and fine sides are \(n_{m, t}\) and \(n_{m + 1, t}\). The paid schedule gives that the block side is \(L_j\), the phase is \(j\), the block side is at least \(u_j\), and the sides \(n_{m, t}\) tend to infinity with \(t\). The volume threshold makes every one-step datum larger. The two bed hulls, namely the metric and root strip bounds, all floors and all sworn conditions of \(B\) and of \(T\), are (C) at levels \(m + 1\) and \(m\); here the beds of \(V\) are those of \(X\). The header bounds and the diagonal two-scale trial at every period are (C) as well, since they involve only the beds and the installed and transport columns, which \(V\) and \(X\) share. For every \(t\) and every forest \(F\), the chart receipts of Subsubsection 11.21.2, applied to \(B\), \(T\), the tower and \(R\), give \(\text{HessLower} (F, p_{m + 1}, R, \eta_j)\) and a map \(\rho^{t, F}\) with the critical, guessed and heat properties; their hypotheses are the diagonal trial, the floor of \(B\), the bounds and attachment of \(R\), the sworn condition of \(B\) and the floor of \(T\). Choose one forest \(F_t\) for each \(t\). All hypotheses of the one-step interface now hold with \(\Lambda = 64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_{m + 1} \geqslant 1\). It returns a path \(D\) and a scalar \(\alpha\) with \(D. \text{row}\) the root row of \(T\) and \(\text{Attached} (T, D)\). By the choice of the stage widths and bounds and the first two loop-capacity inequalities, the conditions \(\sigma_m \leqslant s_i\), \(\kappa \leqslant A_m\), \(\Lambda_{0, j} (L_j) \leqslant \text{loopCap}_m\) and \(64 \hspace{0.17em} \text{loopCap}_{m + 1} / 128 \leqslant \text{loopCap}_m\) hold, so \(D\) satisfies \(D. \text{Bounds} (p_m, \sigma_m, A_m, \text{loopCap}_m)\) and has the canonical representatives of item (2). Hence \(D\) is admissible at \((N, m)\). The scalar satisfies the drift bound because \(d_j\) is at most the drift allowance.

The one-step construction gives the classical match and the finite-cutoff bound only for the chosen forests \(F_t\). They hold for every forest by the forest-independence of the stationary data: for two allowed forests \(F, F'\) of a larger one-step datum, with critical maps \(\rho, \rho'\) and positive gaps for the same path, the tested actions along \(\rho\) and along \(\rho'\) agree near the origin for every local choice, and the two thermal functions differ by a constant near the origin. The first statement transfers the classical match, whose right side does not involve the forest. The second transfers the finite-cutoff bound, which concerns second derivatives; positivity of the determinant and analyticity of the thermal function hold for every forest by the gap. This is precisely Lemma 11.33. Finally the amplitude \(\beta_j (L_j)\) and rate \(\gamma'_j\) are replaced by \(\Gamma_j \geqslant 1\) and \(c_j \leqslant \gamma_j\) using the volume threshold. This proves item (3), with the factor one in the finite-cutoff bound.

Assembly. An admissible path at \((N, m + 1)\) satisfies the factor-\(64\) bounds, since all budgets are nonnegative and \(\text{loopCap}_{m + 1} \geqslant 1\). The finite downward induction of the preceding subsection, started from the branch row and using the transition rule just constructed, gives for each \(N\) the paths \(R_{N, m}\), scalars and maps of item (1) together with item (2). The conditions of Definition 11.36 are the recorded outputs of the steps; the finite-cutoff bound is weakened by the factor \(\text{loopCap}_{m + 1} \geqslant 1\). Item (4) holds with \(v_m = \alpha_{J_m}\) and \(C_m = \Omega_{J_m}\), by the root strip bound in (C) and \(\sigma_m \leqslant \alpha_{J_m} / 2 < \alpha_{J_m}\); it holds for every \(N > m\).\(\Box\)

The proposition produces, on the retained horizon itself, the attached paths with their bounds, and consumes the floors, sworn conditions, metric strips and clip bounds of that same horizon. It is a statement about global path attachment. It does not by itself give the local two-bed statement used in the nonlinear step, which also requires the roll identities and is constructed from the same horizon and paths in Subsection 13.3.

11.21.9Selected cutoff limits

The ten retained bed and tower row families must converge on the lattice cover along the actual terminal cutoff sequence. Subsection 11.20 proves this for the recursively selected free horizon, using its uniform strip bounds, positive root choices and committed transition identities. Its proof includes the high-alias and noise limits, the exterior lifts and amendments, and recovery of cover coefficients from the unbounded selected period sequence. Subsection 14.21 then applies these limits to the same stationary construction and shows that the terminal Wilson normalization preserves every fixed-scale limit.

11.21.10Dependency order

The annulus estimate is supplied by the common response and the radial Fourier estimates. The one-step construction above then combines the classical pictures, the determinant and inherited-loop pictures, and the curvature coefficient lift; the terminal path is constructed independently from the Wilson jets. Only afterwards does the retained-horizon proposition perform its finite downward induction. The quadratic horizon and its cutoff convergence remain the separate constructions of Sections 3.9.2 and 11.20. No convergence of the nonlinear paths is used to produce their finite-cutoff attachment or bounds. Their later limits use the canonical all-period representatives and the separate argument of Subsection 14.21.
